C1.1.2
Standardisation involves the establishment of common
practices, methods or formats to ensure consistency
and can occur at a variety of levels, from international
to country, state or company-specific. Our common
systems of weights and measures are prime examples of
standardisation, as they involve establishing and adopting
uniform systems of measurement for quantities like length,
weight and capacity. Using these helps ensure consistency
and comparability across different contexts.
Before standardised systems were developed, different
regions or even individuals might use different units for
the same quantity (e.g., a ‘foot’ could be different lengths
in different places). This led to confusion, difficulties
in trade and inaccuracies in various fields. Through
standardisation, a common language for quantifying
things, facilitating trade, scientific research and many
other activities has been created.
It wasn't until the American Engineers John Hancock
Hall (1816), Simon North (1818) and Thomas Blanchard
(1817) independently introduced advances in machinery
that allowed the mechanised mass production of the
flintlock components and true interchangeability.
Today, standardisation and interchangeability of many
products is commonplace. The standardization of bolt
design, and reduction of variability, for example, has
allowed high confidence in the interchangeability of
nuts and bolts supplied by different manufacturers from
different parts of the world, see Figure C1.1.2e.
Similarly, the standardisation of bolt grade classifications
provides confidence in material properties such as
strength, with the grade (or Property Class) information
typically indicated on the bolt head.

C1.1.3
Alsoknownas ‘built-inobsolescence,planned obsolescence
is a deliberate tactic employed by manufacturers and
designers to create products with a limited lifespan. It
is designed to force consumers to purchase a product
repeatedly, boosting sales while maintaining a competitive
edge by constantly refreshing and improving products.
Adopting a philosophy of planned obsolescence allows
designers to create a desire for product replacement, or
update, rather than repair. Planned obsolescence exists in
such diverse product areas as computer software, mobile
phones and automobiles.
The notion of planned obsolescence is not a new
phenomenon and is documented as ecarly as the 1930s
when General Electric improved the efficiency of flashlight
lamps through a corresponding decrease in their lifespan.
Engineers at the time were encouraged to build items with
alimited lifespan to increase sales and boost the economy.
‘The proposition received mixed responses but proponents
accepted this new ethical paradigm with almost patriotic
vigour.
Design features of planned obsolescence may include:
« low durability
« style obsolescence
« not easily or cheaply repaired
« difficult to disassemble thus restricting maintenance
« regular introduction of newer technologies or
‘improved” models.
The features listed allow manufacturers to cut costs
and boost sales. This approach by manufacturers is also
known as ‘value engineering, however, as markets develop
and competition increases, pressure is brought about
to increase product life spans as consumers migrate
to companies producing more reliable, more durable
products.
Advantages for the consumers, when companies introduce
new versions and generations of a product, include
opportunities to upgrade products as new technologies
become available and access to the latest in fashion trends.
Strength in the consumer market through volume sales
also has a knock-on effect on national economies and
potential job prospects for the local population.
Disadvantages of planned obsolescence are around
the constant need for replacement and associated
inconvenience. Lack of repairability may mean consumers
have to purchase a product before they are ready or
replace a model whose features they are entirely happy
with, New models and new technologies often bring new
ways of operating and this can also be disconcerting for
Some consumers.
Increased consumerism through less durable goods can
also have significant environmental impacts. These are
largely through the generation of more waste, pollution,
energy consumption and consumption of resources
as consumers continuously replace, rather than repair
products.
Style (fashion) obsolescence
Fashion is a general term for a currently popular style
or practice and can therefore be subject to trends. These
may include design features such as shape, form, colour,
style, materials, finish etc., and appear in a diverse range of
fields including but not limited to: vehicles, architecture,
food, clothing, jewellery, footwear.
Perceived value and cost are vastly different and measured
using entirely different criteria. Value for money is
associated with cost and performance, while perceived
value, also incorporating cost, may also include prestige,
rarity and other social implications within a consumer’s
assessment,
While planned obsolescence often uses lesser quality
materials and production values to cut costs, the prestige
of owning a similar item branded differently but priced at
a premium may still draw customers. Labelled the ‘Veblen
effect’ after Thorstein Bunde Veblen, (1857—1929) this
theory describes counter-intuitive consumer behaviour.
Reasons for this behaviour include the thought that pricing
is an indication of quality and conspicuous consumption
of expensive items provides a form of social prestige for
the consumer.
‘The longevity of such trends can be unpredictable. Some
styles may last years before being abandoned, while others
last merely months and are often referred to as ‘fads’
Fashion fads arise primarily due to a combination of:
« social influence
« the desire to fitin
« novelty seeking,
« the power of celebrities and influencers on social media
platforms such as TikTok, YouTube and Instagram
« the rapid dissemination of trends through marketing
and popular culture.
Fads often lead people into adopting a style quickly and
then just as quickly abandoning it once its no longer
considered ‘trendy’
Examples of Style (fashion)
obsolescence:
Zoot Suits (1930s - 1940s): A style of men’s clothing that
included high-waisted baggy trousers with a long watch
chain, and along coat with wide lapels. Often accompanied
by a pork pie hat. They were popular in the 1940s among
African American, Mexican American, Filipino American,
Italian American, and Japanese American men.
The famous American Jazz bandleader of the 1940s Cab
Calloway frequently wore zoot suits on stage calling them
“totally and truly American’, although they were criticised
as unpatriotic by some for wasteful use of material during
WWIL
Paisley Print (1960s): Although the repeating tear-drop
style textile pattern is centuries old (see Figure C 1.1.3a),
the paisley pattern was a key part of hippie culture in
the mid-late 1960s It represented rebellion and non-
conformity and was associated with the psychedelic
style and free love movement. This saw the pattern used
extensively in the youth fashion industry.
Bell-bottom jeans (1960s-1970s): Also known as flared
jeans in which the pant legs characteristically flared out
at the knee. Popular in both men’s and women’s clothing
in the 1960s they fell out of fashion in the late 1970s as
the 1950s fashion of straight jeans became more popular.
Flared jeans periodically returned to favour in the mid-
1990s to 2000s under the name ‘Boot-cut’
Shoulder pads (1930s-1940s, 1980s-early 1990s): Shoulder
pads have been a popular part of women'’s fashion in several
eras including the early-1930s to late-1940s reflecting the
military styling of the time (see Figure C1.1.3b). They
returned again in the 1980s, with their sharp silhouettes
creating a symbol of power and authority, allowing women
to visually project a more assertive presence in traditionally
male-dominated workplaces. They essentially represented
‘power dressing’ by creating a broader, more commanding
silhouette. The style was popularised by prominent female
figures in the media, including characters from American
television shows like Dynasty and Dallas and celebrities
such as Princess Diana, Margaret Thatcher and Michelle
Pfeiffer.
Examples of short-lived Fads:
Troll Dolls (1960s, 1990s)
Pet Rocks (1975)
Beanie Babies (mid-1990s)
Tamagotchi (mid-1990s to early-2000s)
Functional obsolescence
Functional obsolescence is when something becomes
less useful or desirable due to an outdated design or
functionality even if the technology itself is still functional.
This can apply to real estate, technology and other assets.
Functional obsolescence can lead to a loss of value or
depreciation.
This form of obsolescence can be difficult to correct
because the outdated design is not easily updated.
However, sometimes it is possible to make a comeback
from functional obsolescence, For example, a company
may redesign their furniture to conform to the latest
trends.
Examples of Functional
obsolescence:
A property with an outdated design may be less
desirable to buyers and therefore have alower appraisal
value, due to features such as needing to walk through
one bedroom to get to another, or bedrooms and
bathrooms on separate floors
» Dwellings with inadequate electrical systems for
modern appliances
« The absence of modern conveniences such as indoor
plumbing
« New smartphone models make older models less
desirable
» Manufacturers no longer support updates or repairs of
their older products
« Changes in electronics resulting in incompatibility of
older designs.
Technological obsolescence
Technological obsolescence has many features similar to
functional obsolescence and happens when a technology
is no longer useful or efficient compared to newer
alternatives such that technological obsolescence can
occur well before functional obsolescence may arise. This
can happen to hardware and software.
Examples of Technological
obsolescence:
« Time Keeping: The Hourglass and Sundial were both
replaced by more accurate time keeping systems such
as clocks and electronic timers, although still found in
more ornamental situations
« Refrigeration: Ice chests were replaced in the early
20th century by the refrigerator
« Displays: Cathode Ray Tubes (CRTs) were replaced
by Plasma flat screen displays which themselves have
largely been replaced by Liquid Crystal Displays
(LCD) and Organic Light Emitting Diode Displays
(OLED).
Sound recording: Edison’s cylinders were introduced in 1877. These were superseded by Shellac records, known
as 78s (1898). In their turn these were superseded by
Polyvinyl plastic discs which introduced the 45 rpm
and 33 rpm formats (1948). More recently these were
largely replaced by Magnetic tape (1960s) and by the
Compact Disc (1980). At the time of writing it looks
likely that these will be replaced by electronically
stored digital files and streaming services.
Vacuum tubes were replaced by transistors introducing
miniaturisation.
Steel making: The first widely used steel making
method was the Bessemer Converter Steelmaking
Process (1856). It was replaced by Open Hearth
Furnace (OHF) (1860s). This began to be replaced in
1948 by Basic Oxygen Furnace (BOF) and the Electric
Arc Furnace (EAF). The EAF was first introduced
commercially in 1901 and became widely used from
the 1960s due to growing availability of steel scrap.
Computer data storage devices such as the 87, 57 and
3% “ ‘floppy’ discs, commonly used from the 1970s
to 1990s were replaced by the CD-ROMs, DVDs and
USB flash drives shown in Figure C1.1.3c.

C1.2.1
Inclusive design seeks to break down barriers and ensure
that experiences are accessible and usable by everyone,
regardless of physical or cognitive differences. Inclusive
design represents an extension of User-Centred Design
(UCD) in that it aims to include those members of the
community who might otherwise be forgotten during the
product design process, such as those who are pregnant,
elderly, disabled, or injured and through design attempts
to improve the lives of all people.
Inclusive design principles (sometimes called ‘Universal
Design, ‘Universal Usability Design, ‘Accessible Design’
and ‘Design for All') are increasingly being incorporated
into building codes. The term Universal Design was
originally used by Ron Mace, (1998) and was coined to
meet the needs of people of all ages and abilities.
The Centre for Universal Design defines the term as, ..
the design of products and environment to be usable by all
people, to the greatest extent possible, without the need for
adaptation or specialised design. The intent of universal
design is to simplify life for everyone by making products, communications and the built environment more usable
by as many people as possible at little or no cost.
Centre for Universal Design, 2005
Universal design takes into consideration people of all
ages, genders and abilities and through design attempts
to improve the lives of all people. In the past, it has been
misconstrued as ‘one size fits all' This is less than its full
intention. Through legislation and public education
campaigns, designers have come to realise their social
responsibilities. They have recognised that good design
reflects the needs of a variety of users and makes
accommodations for this range.
The main features of Universal Design may be interpreted
as:
« equity in use
« flexibility in service
« allowing tolerance for error
« simple and intuitive operation
« communicate unambiguous information
« need low physical effort to function
« provide space for operators and use
In many industrialised nations, legislation exists covering
and enforcing consideration of people with a disability.
Human factors research drives and informs designs based
on these legislative requirements.
Just some of the countries with legislation requiring
consideration of individuals with a disability include:
Australia, the Disability Discrimination Act 1992
« (Canada, the Ontarians with Disabilities Act of 2001
« US, under the Americans with Disabilities Act of
1990
« South Africa, the Promotion of Equality and Prevention
of Unfair Discrimination Act 2000
« India, Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities,
Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act,
1995.
In developing countries, formal legislation may not be
enacted, but through a series of informal guidelines
and international standards promoted by NGOs and
government agencies, disability issues may be addressed
to some degree.
Instances of everyday items where inclusive design can
be found are in the design of currency, where coins of
varying size, thickness and edge detail allow those with
poor sight to avoid confusion. Similarly, many countries
have adopted notes with differing sizes, colours and
textural details that assist the vision impaired.

C1.2.2
As indicated previously in section A 1.1.3, in statistics, the
50th percentile (or median) is the point below which 50%
of the population falls and represents the ‘average’ adult
or child in terms of a specific body dimension. Designing
solely for this average is, however, often inappropriate
because body dimensions aren’t linearly correlated,
meaning someone with short arms doesn't necessarily
have short legs and vice versa. There is therefore no such
thing as a truly ‘average’ person. Focusing solely on the
50th percentile can therefore exclude a significant portion
of the population, especially at the extremes and when
considering multiple dimensions, potentially creating
accessibility issues for them. This is as true today as it was
in the past.
Historic anthropometric data still available to researchers
today, of the range in height of Union soldiers aged
between 21 and 49 in 1850 and shown in Figure C1.2.2a,
indicates that an average height was 5 foot 8 inches
(~1.73m). While no soldier may have been of this exact
height, all of the information contained in the graph may
have been useful for producing a suitable range of sizes in
clothing or weaponry.
More recently ergonomists have used data obtained
from measuring the human body to collect sufficient
information to allow devices and products to be
manufactured in a way that facilitates their ease of use. To
design for a wider range of users, designers often consider
a range of percentiles, like the 5th to 95th percentile, as
discussed in Section A 1.1.4, to ensure that the product
can accommodate a majority of the population. This is
accomplished by:
« Utilising comprehensive anthropometric ~ data
to understand the full spectrum of human body
dimensions to design products that can accommodate
a wider range of users.
« Incorporating adjustable features into products
whenever possible, allowing users to customise the fit
to their specific needs.
« Depending on the product or service, identifying
specific user groups and tailoring the design to fit
their needs, considering factors like gender, age and
potential physical limitations.

C1.2.3
Designing for extremes can lead to more inclusive,
innovative and future-proof designs.
Advantages:
« Inclusive design. Designs for extreme users can benefit
a wider audience. For example, voice commands for
visually impaired users can benefit everyone who
needs hands-free operation.
« Innovative solutions. Extreme users can provide
unexpected perspectives that lead to new design
opportunities.
« Future-proof designs. Addressing extreme cases today
can prepare designs for future user needs.
« Improveduserexperience. Designing for extreme users
can lead to better user experiences for everyone.
+ Inspiration. Extreme users can inspire designers to
rethink assumptions and break out of their comfort
Zones.
» Improved accessibility. Solutions for extreme users
can make products more accessible and usable for
everyone.
Examples
The Oxo Good Grips products, introduced in the 1990s,
were inspired by Sam Farber seeing his arthritic wife
struggle to hold a vegetable peeler. The OXO peelers
(shown in Figure C 1.2.3a) incorporate a martensitic
stainless steel, half-moon-shaped slotted swivel blade in
a rigid nylon frame. This is fixed to an oversized non-
slip handle made from the thermoplastic elastomer
Santoprene. The large handle improves grip during use
while also reducing hand strain, a particular advantage for
those with arthritis. The handle alse incorporates a unique
‘soft spot’ consisting of a scooped-out region with fins for
the thumb and forefinger, which resulted in the need for
only a light grip during use.

C1.3.1
The ACT model was developed by Trevor Van Gorp and
Edie Adams as a tool to help designers create designs that
intentionally trigger emotional responses. The model was
outlined in their 2012 book Design for Emotion.
The ACT model describes the process through which an
emotional response can be stimulated and some of the
factors that should be considered when designing for
emotion. The model shown in Figure C 1.3.2a, suggests
that product design that produces an emotional response
begins with attracting attention through features such as
colour, texture, sound, etc.
Once the user’s attention has been gained, the product
design must create feelings of control and reliability. In
other words, the user must be able to converse with the
product, it must conform with our sense of usability.
Finally; if the product satisfies the requirements of
usefulness and functionality, the consumer will move to
commit to its continued use. In other words, a transaction
will occur between the user and the product.

Another more recent example where aesthetics have
influenced consumer behaviour is the rise of the digital
Smartwatch (Figure C1.3.1b). This was introduced by
Seiko in 1998. Seiko is now only a minor participant as the
smartwatch market is dominated by Apple and Samsung,
although an increasing number of competitors such as
Huawei, Imoo, Noise, Google, Garmin, Xiaomi, Oppo and
Vivo have appeared.
The smartwatch offers not only traditional timekeeping
but additional wearable tech functionality in the form
of health/fitness tracking and internet connectivity
primarily through cellular (Wi-Fi/5G) or Bluetooth-
based connectivity with a smartphone. Standalone
smartwatches, featuring built-in cellular capabilities, can
function independently from a phone for calls, messages
and data access.
Consumers increasingly view smartwatches not only as a
technology innovation but as a fashion accessory, seeking
stylish designs and diverse options. The electronic nature
of the watches in many instances allows the watch face
to change its appearance to accommodate the user’s
preferences and style.
Figure C1.3.1b Smartwatches with multiple faces
available
42
The four pleasure framework relates to other design
principles, such as Donald Norman’s three levels of design
appeal: visceral, behavioural and reflective outlined in
2003 in ‘Emotional Design. Norman believed that designs
that engaged the emotions improved the user experience.
He suggested such designs should invoke three emotional
responses:
» Visceral (immediate sensory reaction),
« Behavioural (functional usability),
= Reflective (meaning and personal value),
Essentially Norman suggested that a well-designed
product should provide pleasure at each level of interaction
depending on the user’s perception and experience with it;
with physical sensations like touch and aesthetics relating
to visceral, ease of use and functionality to behavioural and
personal connection or societal impact to the reflective
level.
Key points of alignment between the Four Pleasures and
Norman's Three Levels of Design:
« Physio-pleasure (Visceral): Most closely aligns with
Norman’s ‘Visceral’ level, as it focuses on the immediate
sensory experience of a product, like its texture, smell
or visual appeal.
« Psycho-pleasure (Behavioral): Correlates with
Norman’s ‘Behavioural’ level, as it relates to the
pleasure derived from successfully completing a task
or interacting with a product intuitively.
» Socio-pleasure (Reflective): Connects to the
‘Reflective’ level of Norman's design, as it pertains to
the social meaning and personal value a user associates
with a product, like status or belonging within a
group.
» Ideo-pleasure (Reflective): Also aligns with the
‘Reflective’ level, as it encompasses the deeper meaning
and personal values associated with a preduct, like
sustainability or ethical considerations.
Example: A luxury car: « Behavioural level (Psycho-pleasure): While the tapered
« Visceral level (Physio-pleasure): The smooth leather shape and ribbed design is intuitive in its operation and
interior, sleek design and engine sound upon start- allows juice to be directed to a waiting container below,
up. the retro-style rocket ship is notoriously difficult to use
successfully.
« Behavioural level (Psycho-pleasure): The ease of
driving, responsive handling and advanced technology « Reflective level (Socio & Ideo-pleasure): The striking
features that enhance the driving experience. futuristic design has a sculptural quality that suggests
a sophisticated owner, while limited edition and
+ Reflective level (Socio & Ideo-pleasure): The prestige expense, compared with other juicers enhanced its associated with owning a high-end brand, 
reflecting desirability. When fielding criticism of his creation, the user’s success and status. Starck is alleged to have said, ‘My juicer is not meant to squeeze lemons; it is meant to start conversations.
In the postmodern era, the product aesthetic became product.
the driving force. Objects took on creative or arbitrary
forms. Functionality took a back seat to appearance. These © IBO 2025
products were often marketed as ‘unique’ or ‘collectable’
‘The most publicised example of this style is the 1980s
Philippe Starck designed lemon squeezer, or ‘Juicy Salif .
This product soon became an iconic, albeit expensive 'must have' and is hailed as a design classic.

C1.3.2
The ACT model was developed by Trevor Van Gorp and
Edie Adams as a tool to help designers create designs that
intentionally trigger emotional responses. The model was
outlined in their 2012 book Design for Emotion.
The ACT model describes the process through which an
emotional response can be stimulated and some of the
factors that should be considered when designing for
emotion. The model shown in Figure C 1.3.2a, suggests
that product design that produces an emotional response
begins with attracting attention through features such as
colour, texture, sound, etc.
Once the user’s attention has been gained, the product
design must create feelings of control and reliability. In
other words, the user must be able to converse with the
product, it must conform with our sense of usability.
Finally; if the product satisfies the requirements of
usefulness and functionality, the consumer will move to
commit to its continued use. In other words, a transaction
will occur between the user and the product.

A2.1
Today’s consensus is with the theories of Jacob Schmookler,
who in 1962 decided that user needs or market pull were
the most influential drivers of successful innovation. In
other words, successful innovation rests on identifying and
understanding the needs and motivations of consumers
before attempting to meet those needs through the
development and introduction of a new product. Thomas
Edison succinctly summed up this approach when he said,
“I don't want to invent anything that nobody will buy.”
The chances of an invention being developed into a
market-successful innovation may be influenced by the
following factors:
« expectations of demand
« magnitude of user needs
« expectations of profitability
« degree of positivity of market perception
« amount of intellectual property protection afforded by
patents.
User-centered research methodologies play a vital role
in the development of successful products. Integrating
human factors research, its associated methodologies,
and the resulting recommendations is indispensable in
creating functional, effective and sustainable solutions.
It is increasingly evident that addressing complex
design challenges requires more than technological
advancements; it demands a holistic approach employing a
range of techniques and expertise that incorporates social
considerations and cultural contexts. Scholars argue that
the deliberate inclusion of these human factors, historically
overlooked, holds the key to fostering sustainability across
diverse environments. Consequently, heightened attention
is imperative when transferring goods and technologies
within a global marketplace to ensure cultural relevance,
usability and long-term viability.
User-centred design (UCD) requires an iterative
approach in which the consumer is surveyed at a number
of stages throughout the product design process. At each
point, problems are identified and the design modified
until a finished product is arrived at that meets consumer
needs.
The UCD should allow the designer to develop a product
that addresses the whole user experience and makes it
easy for the user to:
determine actions possible at any time
see the options and results of actions
determine current system state
follow intuitively from intention to action.

A2.1
Today’s consensus is with the theories of Jacob Schmookler,
who in 1962 decided that user needs or market pull were
the most influential drivers of successful innovation. In
other words, successful innovation rests on identifying and
understanding the needs and motivations of consumers
before attempting to meet those needs through the
development and introduction of a new product. Thomas
Edison succinctly summed up this approach when he said,
“I don't want to invent anything that nobody will buy.”
The chances of an invention being developed into a
market-successful innovation may be influenced by the
following factors:
« expectations of demand
« magnitude of user needs
« expectations of profitability
« degree of positivity of market perception
« amount of intellectual property protection afforded by
patents.
User-centered research methodologies play a vital role
in the development of successful products. Integrating
human factors research, its associated methodologies,
and the resulting recommendations is indispensable in
creating functional, effective and sustainable solutions.
It is increasingly evident that addressing complex
design challenges requires more than technological
advancements; it demands a holistic approach employing a
range of techniques and expertise that incorporates social
considerations and cultural contexts. Scholars argue that
the deliberate inclusion of these human factors, historically
overlooked, holds the key to fostering sustainability across
diverse environments. Consequently, heightened attention
is imperative when transferring goods and technologies
within a global marketplace to ensure cultural relevance,
usability and long-term viability.
User-centred design (UCD) requires an iterative
approach in which the consumer is surveyed at a number
of stages throughout the product design process. At each
point, problems are identified and the design modified
until a finished product is arrived at that meets consumer
needs.
The UCD should allow the designer to develop a product
that addresses the whole user experience and makes it
easy for the user to:
determine actions possible at any time
see the options and results of actions
determine current system state
follow intuitively from intention to action.

A2.1.1
The term user-centred design (UCD) or human-centred
design (HCD) has its origin in the 1980s with the US
cognitive scientist Donald Norman. The concept became
widely known through his publications, ‘User-Centred
Design: New Perspectives on Human-Computer Interaction’
and ‘The Psychology of Everyday Things’, later renamed “The
Design of Everyday Things. In developing the concept of
UCD, Norman took issue with the multitude of consumer
devices that were not only difficult to use but also often
incorporated design style changes solely for style’s sake,
reducing usability. Functions not required by consumers
may lead to:
« increased cost
« increased complexity
« reduced efficiency of use.
The foundation of UCD is that good design requires that
the needs and capabilities of the users are determined
and incorporated into the design process from the start
through to the finish.
Inclusion of a wide variety of functions may be
accomplished relatively cheaply, however, their inclusion
can lead to confusion and ultimately reduce rather than
increase usability and customer satisfaction, particularly
when things go wrong.
This is particularly the case with electronic interfaces.
Many electronic interfaces from consumer products such
as microwave ovens to TV remotes and smartphones have
complex functionality that can far exceed the requirements
of most consumers. This increased functionality is also
often achieved at the expense of intuitive operation,
leading to confusion, particularly on the part of the casual
user. In most instances, such confusion is little more than
frustrating; however, if it is associated with critical
equipment, the potential for errors can be dangerous.
Interfaces intended for use by a wide range of users such
as Automatic Teller Machines
(ATMs) need to be of a relatively
standardised lay-out, have
large displays and incorporate
limited functionality to avoid
confusion. By comparison,
the design of more complex
consumer products needs to
have those functions used often
displayed prominently and
intuitively accessed or in the
case of modern electronic devices such as smartphones,
the programming options need to be intuitive and allow
easy customisation of the interface.
Similarly, designs need to consider the environment in
which they are used. Most electronic equipment includes
information regarding the temperature range in which it
should be operated and the need to shield devices from
strong magnetic fields to avoid damage. Of possibly
greater danger is the use of standard functions in
unintended situations, such as the practice of texting while
walking or driving.
Over one-third of young people are
reported to have had an accident texting ;
while walking (The Guardian, World :
News, Australia, 23 Jan 2014), and F nEgt]
deaths due to texting while walking are O] ,_-"' AL
reported to be increasing (KTLA-Los =
Angeles, 9 August 2013, see QR code). Increased use of
speech recognition software may obviate the effect of such
distracted walking practices.
If the designer has a deeper understanding of user
requirements, tasks and environments, only those features
truly required and desired by the customer will be
incorporated into the product. In addition, instructions
for use will be written in clear, simple language.

A2.1.2
Research: This stage involves defining the problem and
establishing the criteria the new design needs to fulfill.
Often undertaken by psychologists, ethnographers and
anthropologists, these activities record how humans
undertake tasks and interact with products. Focus groups
and market research strategies are used to determine likely
market opportunities and challenges.
Concept: The initial design concept is typically developed
by a team of industrial designers, psychologists and
engineers. They aim to create a design concept that will
fulfill user needs identified earlier during the research
phase.
Design: Drawings and models of products are generated
from the initial concept design, allowing factors such as
scale, aesthetics and feel to be accessed. At this point,
modifications can be made quickly and cheaply to allow a
variety of iterations to be considered.
Implementation: User testing of the model with a broader
range of end users is undertaken, often under the guidance
of psychologists who monitor the user experience. This
information is again fed back into the design process and
the writing of instruction manuals.
Launch: The product is introduced to the general market,
accompanied by advertising, and sales begin. At this stage,
customer feedback, in addition to questionnaires, designed
to gain a wider response to usability issues and ideas for
improvement, may be sought. In his book Invention by
Design: How engineers get from thought to thing, Henry
Petroski recounts the release of the easy-opening stay-on-
tab can top in the 1970s. Because it was an innovation,
beverage companies required the conducting of consumer
studies to assess acceptance before adopting the change
from the throw-away tab design. In addition, early cans
were imprinted with instructions on how to open the
top.
Advantages of UCD processes
« accommodate a broader audience
intuitive, easy to use, and accessible
« generate market-competitive products
« produce greater levels of user satisfaction
« solve problems earlier in the design process, thus
saving time and is cost-efficient.
Products developed through User-Centered Design
(UCD) methodologies are characterised by their ability
to accommodate a diverse audience, ensuring inclusivity
and broad accessibility. These products exhibit intuitive
functionality, ease of use and seamless accessibility,
addressing user needs effectively. UCD processes also drive
the creation of market-competitive solutions by aligning
design decisions closely with user expectations, fostering
enhanced satisfaction levels. Identifying and addressing
potential design challenges earlyin the development cycle,
UCD can be cost-efficient, optimise resource allocation
and reduce the likelihood of costly redesigns.
Disadvantages of UCD processes
User-Centered Design (UCD) processes can be inherently
time-intensive, particularly during the phases dedicated
to gathering and analysing user data. These activities
often necessitate a considerable upfront investment of
both time and resources. The iterative nature of UCD,
coupled with the variability of user feedback, may also
result in shifts to the project’s focus, thereby requiring the
reassessment and potential repetition of earlier design
stages. The complexity of interpreting extensive user
data, compounded by its often conflicting nature, further
presents significant challenges in determining the most
appropriate and effective direction for the project.

A2.1.3
Professor Don Norman is an expert in design, usability
engineering, and the interdisciplinary field of cognitive
science. He argues that, only by incorporating into the
product development process, the concerns and expertise
of the target user and production teams that manufacture,
distribute, maintain and market the product can a good
design emerge. Therefore, the UCD process represents a fundamental change in the traditional design process,
where designs were developed largely in isolation.
Adriana Chammas, Manuela Quaresma and Claudia
Mont’Alvdo, in their paper A Closer Look at the User
Centred Design (2015), also make this supporting
observation:
“the User-centred design approach encourages the
use of multidisciplinary expertise in order to provide
creative and collaborative ideas between team members,
benefiting the project with different perspectives and
skills”
Employing interdisciplinary groups empowers design
teams to develop products, systems and environments
that transcend functionality and aesthetic appeal,
while fostering inclusivity and generating substantial
environmental, societal and practical impacts.
Critical to the success of multi-disciplinary teams is the
identification of multiple stakeholders, end users and a
range of multidisciplinary experts. Several perspectives
and data sources may, however, provide challenges to
managing UCD teams and may produce multiple design
iterations, but should eventually lead to well-considered
results that have the potential to uncover solutions
previously not considered.
Multidisciplinary teams bring together a diverse range
of professionals from various disciplines, specific fields
of expertise and distinct perspectives. These teams offer
important benefits, including:
Enhanced Creativity and Innovation: Diverse skills and
expertise bring unique perspectives and ideas that may
produce ideas previously not considered or spark an
innovative line of consideration based on other fields of
knowledge represented.
Improved Problem-Solving: Tackling challenges from
multiple angles leads to holistic solutions.
Faster Development Cycles: Collaboration speeds up
the design cycle and reduces dead-end investigations
with multiple ideas or error-checking from different
perspectives
Improved Product Quality: All team members and their
specific experiences contribute to refining every aspect of
the potential solution.
Greater Market Understanding: A range of end-user and
market insights is critical to product market acceptance
and ultimate success.
Because no single discipline could conceivably meet all
the needs of specific target users, multidisciplinary teams
from a diverse range of fields need to be carefully curated to
generate effective, efficient and inclusive designs. Typical
contributions from a range of disciplines are documented
below.
Psychologists: Observing and understanding human
behaviour, cognition and perception is critical to gaining a
deeper understanding of the behaviours and motivations
of users. Interpreting how users process information
assists design teams in understanding the decision-
making process of user groups.
Sociologists: Knowledge of cultural norms, group
dynamics and social behaviours, assists sociologists to
offer critical insights into the ways designs are perceived,
adopted and utilised within communities. This approach
is essential to the development of systems and products
accommodating a diverse range of users, ensuring that
design solutions resonate across various sociocultural
groupings.
Anthropologists: Examining natural environments and
their impact on individuals and groups through tools such
as field research and interviews is important to gaining
insights into cultural and contextual influences that may
mould user behaviours. In reverse, it assists designers in
developing concepts that may resonate with specific target
user groups.
Designers and visual artists: This group offers expertise
in aesthetics, visual communication and the eliciting of
emotional responses. Ensuring that products are both
visually captivating and distinctly positioned within the
marketplace is often pivotal in shaping consumers’ initial
impressions. The application of graphic design strategies—
integrating market differentiation with functionality
and intuitive usability—may serve as a decisive factor in
influencing consumer decision-making processes.
Materials Engineers/Manufacturing: Materials engineers
and manufacturing specialists play a pivotal role in the
selection and processing of materials, ensuring that
products achieve optimal functionality, durability and
manufacturability. Engineering disciplines play a pivotal
role in ensuring technical feasibility and fostering
innovation. The economic aspects, including the costing of
raw materials, tooling and production processes, directly
influence product pricing and market competitiveness.
Balancing these often conflicting requirements demands
a comprehensive and intricate understanding of material
properties, production techniques and cost optimisation
strategies inherent to the discipline.
Software, hardware and systems engineers: Systems
engineers are engaged to organise and implement effective
human-artefact interactions. They work to translate
conceptual designs into practical solutions, focusing on
efficient implementation. This process involves addressing
critical constraints such as scalability, security and
system performance, ensuring that the final design meets
functional and operational requirements.
Human factors engineers: Human factors engineers
specialise in ergonomic design, a discipline focused on
comprehensively analysing the interactions between
humans and systems, products or environments, This
approach seeks to optimise safety, efficiency, comfort and
overall effectiveness in engagement. Ergonomic principles
are particularly critical when designing tools, machinery
and workspaces, as they play a key role in mitigating
strain, preventing injuries and enhancing user experience
and performance.
Data analysts: Systematically examining and interpreting
diverse datasets derived from user tests, questionnaires,
surveys and focus groups, data analysis yields quantitative
insights into user behaviour. These insights facilitate the
identification of patterns and trends, which can better
inform and refine design decision-making processes.
Consequently, specific design trajectories maybe validated
or excluded based on evidence drawn from the analysis.
Marketing and business experts: A deeper understanding
of market trends and target market demographic profiles
aligns design decisions with organisational objectives
and the requirements of specific user groups. This
strategy establishes a cohesive framework that integrates
commercial goals, brand identity and the needs of the
intended audience.
Environmental scientists: This group provides critical
expertise in minimising environmental impacts,
optimising resource utilisation, and promoting
sustainability. Their work ensures product designs align
with user requirements and critical environmental
imperatives. This approach encompasses a comprehensive
analysis of ecological consequences throughout the
product life cycle, addressing key aspects such as materials
choices, energy consumption, life cycle assessment (LCA),
end-of-life considerations, and adherence to regulatory
and safety standards. Through this lens, environmental
science enables the integration of sustainable principles
into the design process, fostering innovation and
environmental responsibility.
Multi-disciplinary design team
example
The development of early spacesuits benefited from the
gathering of scientists from a variety of disciplines charged
with developing a suit for astronauts. A multidisciplinary
team was crucial to developing one of the earliest spacesuits
in the late 1950s for the Mercury space program. The group
consisted of experts from engineering, materials science,
medicine, design and biology. They were asked to freely
express their ideas. Interestingly, the most appropriate
concept was proposed by a biologist.
The collaboration of these experts led to innovative
solutions that a single-discipline team might not have
achieved. Some examples of their efforts are documented
below.
« Temperature Control: Biologists and engineers
combined to create a suit that effectively managed
the external temperature extremes while maintaining
consistent body temperature through creating an outer
suit shell made of aluminum-coated nylon.
+ Maintaining Life Support: Contributions from
experienced biologists, medical experts and engineers
led to an understanding of the needs of the individual
and the development of a dependable and robust
closed-loop breathing system.
+ Data management: A ‘biomed’ flap was introduced
to allow biomedical sensors to connect with the
spacecraft’s telemetry systems.
urability and Safety: Material scientists and
engineers worked together to create a suit that could
protect astronauts from space debris and temperature
extremes.
In the case of the Mercury astronaut program, use of a
multidisciplinary team ensured a holistic appreach to
suit design, considering the needs of the user and the
interrelated nature of several competing demands. The
result was a highly functional spacesuit that met the needs
of the ambitious Mercury space program.

A2.1.4
By integrating the variety of methods documented below,
design teams can create detailed profiles for identified
user groups. Each approach provides insights that, when
compiled, form a nuanced view of consumer behaviours.
This depth of understanding better informs design teams
and helps to create products that users identify with and
appreciate.
mploying a range of methodologies such as those
outlined below, design teams can construct comprehensive
profiles for identified user groups. Each method yields
valuable insights that, when synthesised, offer a nuanced
perspective on consumer behaviours. This depth of
understanding equips design teams with the knowledge
necessary to develop products that resonate with and are
valued by users.
Field Research: Observing users within authentic
environments enables researchers to collect comprehensive
data and contextual insights, uncovering behaviour-
influencing factors that might otherwise remain
undetected. This process can expose cultural influences,
offering a deeper understanding of societal norms and
traditions that shape user behaviour. Additionally,
researchers can identify setting-specific factors—
including environmental, social, and physical elements—
that affect user interactions with products, services or
environments, allowing for an assessment of their relative
impacts. Furthermore, habitual behaviours, patterns and
routines that subconsciously drive user actions can also be
identified, providing valuable insights into the underlying
mechanisms of user behaviour.
Supermarkets frequently undertake field research across
multiple locations to observe customer behaviour. Key
areas of focus include: customer navigation patterns,
product interaction dynamics and time-with-product
studies. The subsequent analysis of this data yields
valuable insights into customer traits, product placement
strategies and effective store layout designs, which attempt
to enhance product exposure, operational efficiency and
the consumer shopping experience.
Task analysis: Breaking down tasks into small individual
steps allows researchers to focus on discrete components
instead of the totality of the task. This granular level of
investigation may reveal micro-interactions within
otherwise complex tasks. This process allows for
examination of thought processes, revealing the mental
processes and cognitive load required at every stage
leading to task completion. Identifying prior knowledge
or levels of skill/dexterity of the various tasks required
for success may also be revealed. Reflecting on user
pain points and identifying common errors/obstacles
ultimately leads designers to develop successful task
completion activities.
Task analysis employs a methodical, stepwise approach
to deconstruct complex operations into discrete stages,
providing a deeper understanding of user behaviour.
This process unveils underlying characteristics, traits
and competencies of user populations by identifying
points of challenge or confusion across the operational
sequence. Insights from the analysis highlight areas where
users encounter difficulties, such as unclear instructions,
redundant steps, or non-intuitive workflows. By exposing
these patterns, task analysis reveals the true nature of the
user population, including their capabilities, preferences
and ultimately informs more user-centric design and
process imprnvements.
User Observation: Researchers observe authentic user
behaviour without any unwanted influence from the
observer. In this environment, researchers may observe
preferences and behaviours that users might not articulate
in other circumstances. Without any additional support
(deliberate or otherwise), users demonstrate challenges
and frustrations when attempting to use the product
independently.
Researchers observe authentic user behaviour in an
environment free from any external influence or bias
introduced by the observer. These conditions allow
researchers to discern user preferences and behaviours
that might remain unexpressed or undetected in different
contexts. The absence of intentional or unintentional
support helps users naturally reveal the challenges
and frustrations they encounter when independently
interacting with the product, offering valuable insights
into usability and design limitations.
Example
Gaming companies regularly observe player interactions
to analyse how users engage with various aspects of
a game. This includes identifying points of difficulty,
frustration and engagement, as well as determining which
features are most frequently used. Such observations
enable researchers to pinpoint usability challenges and
refine gameplay mechanics. The insights gained from
this process inform the development of games that are
more engaging, appropriately challenging and ultimately
enjoyable.
Interviews
Interviews predominantly generate qualitative data
through direct interaction with users, designed to capture
their personal experiences with products. Interviewers
have the flexibility to pose follow-up questions, thereby
uncovering specific details regarding user expectations
and preferences,
Telehealth represents a relatively new
and rapidly evolving service. As this
domain progresses, researchers are
eager to investigate user motivations,
including hesitancy to utilise the
service. Interviews with users can yield
valuable insights into how telehealth can be enhanced to
better meet patient needs and expectations.
Surveys are widely favored by researchers for their ease
of distribution to large and diverse groups. They generate
quantitative data, often in numerical form using Likert
scales, which can be statistically analysed to reveal
consumer behaviours, beliefs, attitudes and trends. The
customisation of surveys also allows for fine-tuning of
questions to target specific user groups. As a research tool,
surveys are efficient and cost-effective to construct and
administer, particularly for large-scale studies.
Example
A kitchen whitegoods manufacturer distributes a survey to
gather user feedback. The survey encompasses questions
regarding product functionality, ease of use, satisfaction
levels and the likelihood of recommending the product.
Upon collection and analysis, the data can guide product
enhancements, such as the incorporation of requested
features, and identify areas of concern or dissatisfaction.
Focus groups: Focus groups cnmprise carefufly SE]ECted
subsets of target audiences, engaging them in structured,
open-ended discussions designed to foster interaction
and encourage the exchange of diverse perspectives.
Such qualitative methodologies offer insights into user
behaviors by eliciting nuanced feedback. The inclusion
of a diverse participant group enhances the potential to
capture a broad spectrum of market insights, ensuring
a more comprehensive understanding of audience
dynamics.
The conversational nature of focus groups facilitates the
gathering of in-depth qualitative data, encompassing a
range of perspectives and sentiments. Expert moderation
plays a pivotal role in steering discussions, employing
follow-up questions to probe deeper into responses,
clarify ambiguities and expand upon critical points.
This process enables a more thorough exploration of
participants’ motivations, expectations, behaviours and
areas of dissatisfaction. Additionally, these sessions reveal
both collective experiences and individual interactions
with products or services.
he data derived from focus groups provides a foundation
for the iterative design and development of products and
services, ensuring alignment with the genuine needs and
preferences of the target audience. This targeted approach
enhances the likelihood of market resonance and user
satisfaction.
A coffee machine manufacturer
convenes a focus group to collect
data on a forthcoming, unreleased
model. The group comprises a
diverse collection of potential buyers
assembled to evaluate the product
and engage in discussions about their
expectations, opinions, preferences
and concerns. These deliberations
are expected to uncover both
recurring patterns and distinctive viewpoints, providing
valuable insights that enable the manufacturer to refine
the product’s design and features. This feedback process
ensures a closer alignment with customer expectations,
manufacturing and market demands.
Figure A 2.1.4a shows the Education and Public Outreach
(EPO) Team hosting a focus group to collect feedback
from educators and students about the formal education
products being developed by the EPO team.

A2.1.5
Primary Persona
While personae are fictional, they are not hypothetical;
they encapsulate the findings of thorough ethnographic
research and field trials, thus enabling researchers to
concentrate on the behaviors of user groups with shared
objectives and to identify overarching issues and themes.
Personae enable designers to remain focused on the
specific needs of identified users. Through comprehensive
research and meticulous data collection, designers can
cultivate a deep empathy for the target audience, facilitating
the creation of personalised and nuanced solutions.
Stereotyping can pose significant challenges if the initial
data collection lacks diversity and inclusivity. As a result,
these stereotypes may not accurately reflect the authentic
needs of the targeted user group. Additionally, personae
can rapidly become obsolete due to evolving fads, trends,
fashion, materials and technologies. External biases
may also influence the construction and synthesis of
personae.
Scenarios
Scenarios inform design decisions and help visualise
the user’s world more vividly during the design and
development cycle, keeping everyone focused on building
the product for the user. Scenarios provide physical and
social context for different personae. To make the personae
seem real and build empathy for them within the design
team, a physical and social context is typically produced,
often including a photograph intended to put a ‘face’ to
the persona.
Scenarios serve as a tool in informing design decisions,
representing the user’s environment throughout the
design and development process. By concentrating on the
user’s perspective, scenarios ensure sustained focus on
creating user-centric solutions. They provide physical and
social contexts for various personae, enriching the design
process with nuanced, relatable insights. To humanise
personae and foster empathy within the design team, these
contexts are often augmented with visual elements, such as
photographs, that symbolically ‘put a face’ to the persona,
thereby enhancing engagement and understanding during
development.
Information included in the profile of a persona may be:
« age
health
« gender
+ interests
« activities
« life goals
« education
« motivation
« employment
« expectations
+ marital status
organisations/affiliations.
Scenarios provide authentic contextual environments
for user/product interactions. This allows researchers to
consider realistic responses, both positive and negative,
from users. Tasks critical to the success of the product may
be identified. Scenarios also help designers to empathise
with users more closely, leading to more nuanced design
decision-making.
Scenarios offer authentic contextual environments for
user-product interactions, enabling researchers to evaluate
realistic user responses, both favorable and unfavorable.
‘This approach facilitates the identification of tasks crucial
to the product’s success. Additionally, scenarios enhance a
designer’s ability to empathise with users, fostering more
sophisticated and nuanced design decisions.
Developing complex or detailed scenarios can be both
time-consuming and costly. Additionally, assumptions
made about users during the construction of these
scenarios may not always be accurate, potentially leading
to erroneous conclusions.
Use case
A use case is a written description of how a user will
interact with a product /design seen from the user’s
perspective. This analysis allows insight into the usability
of the product/design experienced by the user.
While personae have found extensive use in usability
research and design, they are not without their critics,
who point out that the:
» size of the user groups that a particular persona
represents cannot be verified.
« validity of the persona as an accurate representation
of the user population cannot be independently
assessed.
+ persona methods still await peer review evaluation of
validity.
« personae often contain ambiguities, allowing various
interpretations that can lead to team conflict.
+ personae may represent stereotypical images of
user populations, which are damaging to the design
process.
Population stereotypes
Assumptions and associations are made by the population
of a particular culture regarding how equipment and
products operate. Such assumptions include the direction
a handle should turn to open or close, or the direction
a switch moves for on or off. These are not associations
that are intrinsic to the machine but are established as
a factor in machine standardisation within a society.
Because of this, it is often the case that different paths
to standardisation have been taken, leading to different
population stereotypes.
In the UK and Australia, for instance, a light switch is
pushed down for on and up for off, while in the USA, it
is the reverse.
UK (left) and Australian plugs also differ in their
orientation. The Earth pins (safe) are indicated by the red
arrows.
hich is the safer design and why?
Which side of the road drivers use varies globally between
left and right. While over 60% of the world favours the
right, the reason for the variation remains unclear.
Historical references to driving side choice include Roman
charioteers, medieval knight battle tactics, Napoleonic
conquests and even courtesy to female passengers. Some
countries have even made the change from one side to
another: Canada in the 1920s, Sweden (1967), Burma
(1970) and Samoa (2009).
If population stereotypes are incorporated into product
design, operations can often feel more intuitive as they
conform to expectations based on experience. The
displacement of a population stereotype, however, means
that an operation does not conform to user expectations.
Such a displacement might be the turning of a knob
clockwise for ON rather than anti-clockwise. With a little
effort, the alternate operation can be learned; however,
under conditions of stress or tiredness, reversion to the
population stereotype can emerge.
Under conditions of stress, working memory is occupied,
patterns of action learned over a lifetime reassert
themselves and the chances of an error increase, or an
increased delay in response can occur. One such situation
can arise if a motorist has learnt to drive in a vehicle in
which the turn indicator control is on the right-hand
side of the steering column, and the windshield wiper
controls are on the left. If this motorist then occasionally
drives a European vehicle in which these controls are on
the opposite sides of the steering column, confusion will
result in the wipers being activated when a turn signal is
intended.
Designs that align with population stereotypes tend to
be more relatable and effective. Additionally, employing
stereotypes can expedite the design cycle, reducing the
overall time required for completion.
If careful attention is not given to the application
of stereotypes, there is a risk of oversimplification.
Additionally, the use of stereotypes can introduce bias,
especially when addressing minority groups.
Demographics
Through the analysis of user group data encompassing
variables such as age, gender, income, educational
attainment, geographic location and cultural background,
researchers can uncover the characteristics, traditions and
preferences of distinct population segments.
This detailed understanding enables designers to
develop products and services with greater precision and
relevance. Furthermore, demographic insights can inform
the strategic planning of marketing campaigns, product
placement, and promotional activities, thereby enhancing
product effectiveness and reach.
The exclusion of demographic data may result in a
misleading, less diverse, and less inclusive representation
of user groups. Conversely, an exclusive focus on
demographics only maylead to a superficial understanding
of the needs and preferences of these groups.

A2.2
s computer systems have evolved and become integral
to manufacturing plants, advancements in related
computer software have enabled designers to swiftly
create digital models of products, transitioning seamlessly
from initial rough sketches to detailed three-dimensional
visualisations. This optimisation of product development
processes, from conceptual design to market release,
is considered a strategic imperative for manufacturers
seeking competitive advantage. A crucial component of
this strategy is the adoption of CAD/CAM technology
and rapid prototyping, aimed at reducing cycle times,
minimizing duplication of effort, eliminating errors,
enhancing quality control and decreasing labor content.

A2.2.1
In the earliest stages of design development, a fast
response was required to cover the range of potentially
creative solutions to the initial design brief. Depending
on what stage the product development is at, prototypes
may be expressed as either low or high fidelity. These
terms refer to the level of sophistication, detail or
complexity the prototype adopts. During the initial
phases of design development, rapid iteration is essential
to explore a broad spectrum of creative alternatives in
response to the design brief. This is where low-fidelity
prototypes are best explored. These prototypes are
simple expressions of creative solutions to the brief.
They convey the initial concept of the idea proposed and
may be used to obtain early feedback from users/clients,
mostly focusing on basic functionality and simplified
form, without consideration at this point of materials,
manufacturing or the complexities of interactivity.
Low-fidelity prototypes serve as rudimentary expressions
of conceptual solutions to the design brief. They primarily
explore foundational ideas, enabling the procurement of
preliminary feedback from stakeholders such as users or
clients. These prototypes emphasise core functionality
and basic form. Considerations related to materials,
manufacturing processes, or intricate interactivity are not
evident at this early stage.
Low-fidelity prototypes may take the form of simple
conceptual design sketches, cardboard models, or
wireframe CAD models showing the layout of critical
components without aesthetic consideration. The goal
here is to provide early design ideas and concepts for
initial consultation, feedback and evaluation.
These prototypes are distinguished by several critical
attributes: they are cost-efficient, rapidly fabricated and
readily modifiable to accommodate iterative refinements.
Their primary purpose is to emphasise fundamental
functionality while providing preliminary indications
of potential structural organisation. No special skills are
required to develop low-fidelity models. This opens up the
earliest stage of the design process to stakeholders who
may have no design experience.
Low-fidelity prototypes exhibit certain limitations,
primarily stemming from their lack of precision and
comprehensive functionality. This limits their ability to be
effectively evaluated against a user experience in realistic
contexts. Furthermore, these prototypes may inadequately
convey the product’s efficacy to stakeholders, leading to
potential miscommunication. They are also insufficient
for conducting exhaustive usability testing, as they do not
support meaningful user interaction with the model.
High-fidelity models are crafted to look and feel as close
to the finished product as possible. From websites to
physical products, high-fidelity designs incorporate all of
the aesthetic details, functionality and interactivity that
the final production model would contain.
High-fidelity designs are employed in the later stages of
the development stage of the design cycle. High-fidelity
designs often become the template for engineers to
deconstruct. A major benefit at this point is the ability
to test the totality of the design. Usability testing at this
stage also provides users with greater authenticity and
appropriate contextual environments.
High-fidelity prototypes are associated with several
drawbacks, most notably the time investment required
to achieve the necessary level of detail and functionality.
Iterative adjustments at this stage are equally time-
intensive, compounding the challenges. Additionally, the
development of high-fidelity prototypes often necessitates
specialised technical expertise and advanced skills.
A significant challenge is navigating the transition from
low-fidelity to high-fidelity models. This transition
demands that the design team possess a comprehensive
understanding of the implications of the chosen design
direction. Effective communication of this vision, coupled
with securing consensus from key stakeholders, is
imperative before advancing to high-fidelity prototyping.

A2.2.2
A2.2.3
As a part of their everyday operations, designers need to
acquire the skillsassociated with graphical communication.
The quick and easy nature of sketching as a tool provides
the designer with a great deal of flexibility, whether in the
field or a design studio. The only tools required are paper
and a pencil. Used in the early stages of the design process,
pencil sketches allow designers to quickly jot down ideas
that may influence the final design.
Annotations accompany drawings to improve the
communication of information. They also help with the
creative process, documenting fleeting thoughts before
they are forgotten. Designers use annotations in a variety
of ways, including the need to:
» identify problems for future resolution
« clarify obscure or difficult-to-sketch concepts
« note a range of related thematic variations or
alternatives
« record their thoughts about the particular feature for
later reference.
Three-dimensional sketches provide designers with a sense
of form, proportion and aesthetics, while two-dimensional
sketches can isolate and detail more structural features. At
some later stage, these sketches may need to be translated
into a more formal and accurate representation.
Orthographic drawing uses lines of sight that are always
perpendicular to the viewing plane to produce a projected
image, hence the name orthographic projection. Figure
A 2.2.2a shows a representation of third-angle projection
and how the various views of an object are generated.
Varieties of different forms of orthographic projection
are named depending on the positioning of the object
and the observer to the plane or planes of projection.
Different styles, such as first and third angle projections,
are identified by one of the four quadrants or angles they
enclose (Figure A 2,2.2b).
In third-angle projection, the front and horizontal planes
of projection are perpendicular to each other; however,
using the line of intersection of the two projection
planes as a hinge, the top view is swung directly above
the front view. Frequently, the folding line between the
views is drawn on the paper. Above the folding line,
an HP notation indicating a horizontal plane appears,
while below the folding line, the letters VP may appear,
indicating a vertical plane (Figure A 2.2.2¢ ).
The first and third angle projections are both used
extensively throughout the world. When the first quadrant
is used, the lines of sight travel from the observer’s eye to
the object and then to the plane of projection. When the
third angle of projection is used, the lines of sight travel
from the observer’s eye through the plane of projection to
the object (Figure A 2.2.2d). In each type of projection, the
views are obtained similarly, but their relative positions
vary.
In first angle projection, the top view is on the bottom,
drawn below the front view, whereas in third angle
projection, the top view is drawn above the front view.
Australia, Canada and the United States all use the third-
angle of projection. The standard symbol convention
identifying a third-angle orthographic projection drawing
appears on the right, shown in Figure A 2.2.2¢.
In a conventional third-angle orthographic projection,
the views will always appear in the positions shown. The
top view is projected directly onto the horizontal plane of
projection and shows the width and depth dimensions.
The front view is projected directly onto the vertical
frontal plane of projection and shows the width and height
dimensions. Theleft-side viewis projected directly onto the
vertical left plane of projection. The side view of an object
shows the depth and height dimensions. In most cases,
three views convey all of the information to adequately
describe an object. In product design, orthogonal drawing
usually refers to accurate line drawings used to convey
dimensional and/or assembly details. These may be
manually drawn using drafting equipment or now, more
commonly, using CAD software.
General assembly is an orthogonal drawing that
communicates the functional detail of a design by
indicating the size and relative position of all parts,
fastening details and functional relationships between
parts.
Views are carefully selected and sectioning and other
conventional representations are used to successfully
communicate the complete design. Critical overall
dimensions maybe shown along with accompanying notes
explaining details for overall assembly and function.
Sub-assembly drawings are used for large or highly
complex products. Product assembly may also have to
be broken down into sub-assemblies to allow a sufficient
level of detail to be shown. In this situation, a hierarchy of
assembly drawings may be required to relate each part to
the functioning of the whole.
An orthographic drawing (commonly referred to as an
engineering or working drawing) shows precise details
and dimensions. It is most often used as a workshop or
production drawing. Generated in the final stage of the
design process, and fully dimensioned, they provide
valuable information to manufacturers and engineers
relating to: dimensions, tolerances, construction methods,
materials, surface finishes and assembly. A sample
engineering drawing of a worm shaft is shown in Figure
A222f
Isometric drawing is a 3D pictorial style of drawing
where an object is viewed from a corner. Derived from
the Greek ‘equal measurement’ this style of drawing uses
actual measurements taken from the object or orthogonal
drawings. Parallel 30° lines ensure that near and far objects
are given equal emphasis.
As a final touch for realism, sketches may be rendered to
enhance depth perception. Surface textures may also be
added to indicate different materials.
Isometric drawing has found acceptance by product
designers, engineers and architects when choosing a style
of pictorial drawing for: product presentations, technical
drawings, workshop manuals, patent illustrations and
machine components. This is particularly the case when
components require assembly. Figure A 1.3.4g shows an
architectural, digital render of a planning presentation
drawn in the isometric style.
An exploded assembly is usually an isometric drawing
with all parts displaced (exploded) along a central axis.
This type of drawing is more visually descriptive, showing
actual assembly configurations and fastening details.
Exploded drawings serve to identify parts for assembly
and maintenance procedures. Below is a video game
controller digitally rendered and presented in an exploded
form.
Perspective drawing is based on observation from a
single point, the eye, which produces images similar
to those of a camera. Perspective drawings create the
illusion of an object’s size diminishing the further it
retreats from a fixed viewpoint. Early artists employed the
technique of perspective. A contemporary of Leonardo
da Vinci, German artist Albrecht Diirer further perfected
the technique of perspective in use at the time. Diirer
developed several machines designed to ‘capture’ an
image that would maintain spatial relationships of objects,
varying in distance from the observer.
‘Diirer’s window’ used a piece of glass acting as a picture
plane to accurately create a perspective image. These
concepts of picture planes, vanishing points and spatial
relativity are equally relevant today. Commonly used for
architectural drawings, perspective drawings add realism
to the image and provide stakeholders with an image that
is easily interpreted.

A2.2.4
Physical models exist to allow designers to present
tangible embodiments of their designs that are suitable
for visual appraisal, usability testing in-house, and with
authentic user groups to better assess the need for further
development. Physical models have the advantage of
tangibility. They may be physically handled and viewed
from a variety of perspectives. Models can also be
constructed to exhibit particular features of the design.
Unfortunately, they may not always be easily or quickly
modified, and in some cases, may require complete
reconstruction. Models produced through the process of
rapid prototyping may be made from a variety of materials
including: cardboard, polymers, metals, ceramics or wax.
Rapid prototype models provide tactile feedback. They
may even be produced to replicate the look and function
of a real object. Made within hours and easily modified
without the use of extensive tooling, these models may
also be used to demonstrate the evolution of a model
through a range of iterations.
Clay modelling is a very traditional technique used by
car designers to produce physical models. Some design
studios have transferred their modelling activities to
the CAD department, while others maintain that clay is
still the best way to visualise the development of three-
dimensional designs.
Clayscale models such as the one shown in Figure 2A 2.2.4a
may be produced to examine aesthetics, while full-size
models allow for wind tunnel testing, fitting of hardware
and components such as door handles, glass, etc. Physical
models can also be digitised using three-dimensional
scanners. The process of three-dimensional scanning
allows engineers to virtually manufacture panels, add
power plants and drivetrains towards the development of
a simulated working prototype. The process below shows
a half-section cutaway revealing the range of modelling
materials employed in the construction of the prototype.
As a design tool, architectural models are a quick and
inexpensive way for architects to visualise space, consider
the relationship between buildings and landscape/
environment and assist when discussing revisions with
clients. Landscape models of large developments may
use a scale of about 1:100, while for individual buildings
(requiring greater detail), a scale of 1:20 would be more
appropriate. Models such as the one shown in Figure A  224b may use a variety of materials including:
illustration board, foamcore, medium density fibreboard,
corrugated cardboard, balsa wood and acrylic.
Physical models in particular allow designers to
assess dimensional attributes and experiment with the
location, colour, texture and size of function controls.
Dyson is well known for the use of cardboard models
in the design process. Examples of just two of the many
cardboard models constructed by Dyson engineers in the
development of the DC08 vacuum cleaner are shown in
Figure A 2.2.4c and Figure A 2.2.4d.
By using cardboard, prototypes can be created quickly and
cheaply. As design concepts are gradually developed, the
sophistication of the prototype increases with the addition
of smoother lines, textures and colour.
he construction of a physical prototype has distinct
advantages for designers, manufacturers and clients
alike.
‘These include:
« opportunityfor user trials
« client input into developing models
+ speeding up product development cycles
« possibility of testing production methods and materials
before production tooling
« reducing development costs by avoiding costly
mistakes and false starts in production runs
« the functionality of the design becomes more obvious
when reviewing physical models and prototypes
« proof of concept, when examining interacting or
moving components or internal mechanisms.
‘The exclusive use of models in the design process may also
cause problems in the form of:
« performance - models may not always be representative
of the final product
« functionality - models are not always able to replicate
in-service use
« predictions - models may not always provide a true
indication of the final product; materials, performance
or interaction with the environment.

A2.2.5
CAD stands for computer-aided design. CAD drawings
exist as electronic data files that are created using specialist
software.
There are a variety of different types of CAD software,
depending on the application to which they are being
applied. Each of these systems applies its form of internal
logic that must be understood by designers; two-
dimensional CAD software operates in the x and y axes.
Drawings are constructed line by line or through the
creation of basic shapes. This type of CAD is used in place
of traditional drafting techniques and while relying on
two dimensions, can also be used for the construction of
pictorial drawings in the isometric or oblique style.
Advantages of CAD drawings include their ability to be:
« stored in a variety of formats
« corrected or altered very quickly
« created as 2-D or 3-D representations
« rescaled, zoomed, cropped, etc., for detail
« presented as paper printouts, virtual models or rapid
prototypes
« electronically distributed for collaboration as part of a
group project
« used in conjunction with numerical control machinery
for CAD/CAM production.
Disadvantages of CAD drawings include:
. staff training required
initial cost and upgrading of software
high level of skill is required to be competent
compatibility between software and hardware skills regularly require upgrading as the software
updates
