« steep learning curves are associated with the early
stages of uptake
Early design models can be eithervirtual or physical. Virtual
models may be two-dimensional or three-dimensional,
and can be rendered, wireframe, or orthographic.
Physical models, on the other hand can range from simple
cardboard mockups to fully functional prototypes. This
early creative phase introduces the product concept,
encompassing aspects such as appearance, form, fit, size
and, if necessary, function.
The creation of virtual models is typically more cost-
effective than the production of physical prototypes,
particularly during the preliminary phases of design when
numerous iterations may be necessary. The flexibility
inherent in virtual models facilitates rapid modifications,
thereby streamlining the iterative process. A significant
advantage of utilising virtual models lies in their capacity
to be easily shared with collaborators, ensuring that all
parties (including stakeholders) can provide input and
verify that the project is progressing as intended.
Non-technical reviewers often find three-dimensional
virtual models more accessible and intuitive compared
to traditional engineering or workshop drawings. This
mode of visualisation enhances the clarity of proportions,
aesthetics, and textures, thereby providing a more
comprehensive and user-friendly representation of the
design.
Virtual models can be subjected to simulated environments
that replicate various stresses, loads and impacts. Materials
properties may also be allocated to allow for virtual testing
of physical and engineering properties. Animation of
moving and mating components can show areas of non-
alignment or ‘clashing’ Flow simulation software can
analyse effects, such as turbulence and drag, without
recourse to expensive models and wind tunnel tests. Stress
analysis can also be performed on these virtual products
to identify weaknesses and make modifications before the
first physical component is produced.
In the elite world of Formula One motor racing,
mathematical models are employed to design high-
performance fuel mixtures. Using data gathered from
engine tests, mathematical models of molecules are
simulated in virtual engines to determine performance
characteristics before field testing.
Within the auto industry, physical models can also be
digitised using three-dimensional scanners. The process
of three-dimensional scanning allows engineers to
virtually manufacture panels, add power plants and
drivetrains towards the development of a simulated
working prototype.
Surface modelling
Surface modelling software is utilised to shape the
exterior or ‘skin’ of a CAD object, enabling the creation
of highly complex geometries and providing a more
detailed representation than wireframe modelling. The
primary objective of surface modelling is to achieve a
highly realistic effect; however, it does not capture any
information regarding the object’s internal details.
In contrast, solid modelling encompasses the most
comprehensive geometric information among all CAD
model types. Solid models integrate all the wireframe and
surface data to fully define the faces and edges of models,
along with associative information known as topology.
Although surface and solid models may appear similar
based on their external appearance, there are significant
differences. Surface modelling is employed to rapidly
generate intricate shapes and is extensively used in the
consumer product design industry. These shapes, however,
lack thickness and internal information, rendering them
unsuitable for sectioning to reveal interior details or for
use as a basis for rapid prototyping.
Surface modelling finds application in the automotive
industry where the aerodynamic designs are paramount
to reduce drag. Similar constraints apply to aerospace
engineering, where weight, strength, and aerodynamics
are often competing specifications.
Surface modelling is extensively utilised in industrial
design and consumer product development, serving as
a critical tool for conceptualising and refining complex
geometries. In these domains, considerations of aesthetics,
functionality, material selection, and environmental
impact are paramount, ensuring that the final designs not
only meet performance and usability criteria but also align
with sustainability and market demands as well as fashion
trends.
Solid modelling
Solid modelling software produces ‘solid’ representations
of objects that may be further manipulated. Because these
objects have ‘mass; calculations may be performed on the
object within the software, such as volume and centre
of gravity. Changes made to parts or segments within
the drawing are automatically translated throughout the
model, allowing for modifications to be made without the
need for a new drawing. This makes the software suitable
for the fast testing of new design iterations and problem-
solving. Materials properties may also be allocated to
allow for virtual testing of physical and engineering
properties.
Solid modelling is frequently used in conjunction with
rapid prototyping technologies to construct physical
three-dimensional models. Solid modeled objects contain
additional information in the form of primitive geometry,
such as planes, cones, and cylinders.
In manufacturing industries, tool path generation, clash
detection, tolerance examination, and the comprehensive
analysis of mechanical components and assemblies are
integral to ensuring that products meet stringent structural
and functional requirements. These processes are essential
for achieving operational excellence, maintaining quality
standards, and supporting the successful execution of
manufacturing objectives.
Dimensionally accurate models of human implants and
prosthetics for surgical implantation may be developed
and tested in highly accurate anatomical models of
individuals before scheduling surgery.
In the entertainment industry, solid modelling is
extensively utilised within animation and film visual effects
to create highly realistic and detailed three-dimensional
characters and environments.
Generative design
Generative design is a computer-driven iterative design
process. Designers or engineers input specific parameters
that will define the properties of the product, and the
computer generates a response to meet these requirements.
Parameters may include dimensions and loading points
Multiple permutations may be created until the operator
is satisfied with the result. This process allows for much
faster iterations and can create many more permutations
than an individual could think of. Generative design tools
are now commonplace in many CAD packages.
Digital humans / motion capture
Digital humans are computer-based models designed to
assist researchers to simulate human biomechanics and
predict how a real human body would react in a variety
of situations and environments. The use of digital humans
allows for the gathering of information relative to the
range of human motion, forces, fatigue, potential injury,
etc.
Motion capture technology has been utilised to enhance
the comprehension of human factors, assess the physical
limitations of users and facilitate product evaluation. This
technology records, tracks and analyses movement by
capturing a series of high-frequency snapshots. Strategic
markers are often placed on specific points of a moving
human body to identify critical geometric features. The
resulting positional changes of the figures are meticulously
recorded and digitised for subsequent playback or
analysis. Figure A 2.2.4b illustrates this process in action,
demonstrating the capture and transfer of data points
through an intermediary digital skeleton into a digital
human construct.
A motion capture session records the movements of an
actor rather than their visual appearance. These captured
movements are subsequently mapped onto a three-
dimensional virtual human model, created by a computer
artist. This process enables the computer model to
accurately replicate the movements and actions of the
original human subject. Once recorded, motion
animations, such as those used in filmmaking (as depicted
in Figure A 2.2.5b), can be generated. Additionally, the
captured data can be employed directly for motion studies
or to analyse the interaction of human motion with natural
or built environments.
Motion capture technology offers significant advantages
over manual animation, including time savings and
the creation of more natural movements. However, it is
constrained to anatomically feasible motions. By reducing
the need for animators to draw each frame or key frames,
which are then interpolated, motion capture can also
lower the overall cost of animation production.
Digital humans, using software with an underlying
physics engine, may be used to replicate and calculate
muscle force, efficient muscle action or fatigue. They may
also identify effects of clothing on performance, model the
dynamics of walking patterns and utilise the mathematical
relationships between energy produced, heart rate and
oxygen uptake. It is also possible to derive physiological
indices such as the heart rate, breathing rate, carbon
dioxide production rate and core temperature.
All of this data can be gathered automatically and
simultaneously in a range of simulated environments,
what-if scenarios or hazardous situations that would be
either too expensive or dangerous to reproduce using a
human model.
Using statistics and data gathered from anthropemetric
tables and motion capture, digital humans employ complex
algorithms to generate motion, posture, joint force, etc., to
predict responses to new situations or environments that
have not been previously tested.
Digital human models are increasingly used early in
product design to reduce product development time and
costs. Manufacturing industries demand that products be
designed for the comfort and accessibility of consumers
as well as workplaces and the health and safety of
employees, Digital human models assist manufacturers in
examining designs from an ergonomic perspective. They
allow designers to test virtual models and evaluate the
performance of future products before the construction
of a prototype.
Using digital humans early in the design cycle, designers
can:
save time and expensive resources
« test and evaluate products based on ergonomic
factors
« take into account the range of sizes and shapes of
users
« consider user safety factors in a range of
environments
» establish physical use requirements and product
serviceability
« review human factors in design before building
physical prototypes.
Digital human modelling can reduce the need for the
production of physical prototypes. A range of parameters
may be modelled and tested during product development,
not only to optimise individual design factors, but to
allow for the evaluation of the complex interplay between
multiple modifications and their ‘knock-on’ effects. DHM
and simulation in product design enable products to be
developed more quickly, i.e. more design iterations in less
time. This results in higher product quality and meets
human requirements more accurately. Products are safer
as a result of more thorough analysis of safety aspects and
improved productivity results from enhanced automation
of the development process. Digital prototypes are also
cheaper and faster to produce, test and modify than
physical prototypes.
Digital humans enable manufacturing plants to be
developed more quickly and a manual workflow to
be optimised. They improve worker safety and reduce
compensation costs resulting from accidents. Machines
and other equipment can be positioned to optimise cycle
time and avoid hazards. Manufacturing processes can be
designed to eliminate inefficiencies and ensure optimal
productivity. They can be used to ensure that people
can access the parts and equipment needed to assemble
products; check that workers can effectively use any hand
tools needed to perform manual tasks, and check that all
tasks can be performed safely without requiring inordinate
strength or exposing people to the risk of injury.
Using digital humans enables designers to ensure that
there is sufficient space to perform maintenance tasks,
including space for hands, arms and tools, as well as space
to install and remove parts. Designers can check that
technicians can see when they do specific maintenance
tasks and that they can use the requisite hand tools.
Digital humans enable people to be trained in multiple
locations without the need for physical prototypes or
actual equipment, and so reduce the cost of training,
manufacturing and maintenance personnel.
Digital humans can be used in e-commerce to model
clothing products. A customer can produce a model
corresponding to his or her body shape, size and look. The
model can then try on clothing so that the customer can
see what it might look like.
Haptic technology
‘The word ‘haptic’ originates from the Greek, ‘to touch.
Haptic technology is commonly referred to as ‘force
feedback’ or ‘force reflection’ technology because of
the sense of touch or force communicated through the
system. These devices approximate the physical ‘feel’ of the
environment, providing real-time feedback in the form of
pressure and spatial information. This ability allows users
to interact with virtual environments accessed through
computer connections and creates a more immersive,
more engaging user experience.
Based on haptic or force response’ devices, telepresence
systems allow for control of processes remotely. Used in
medicine, robotics and for the operation of industrial
equipment, these systems rely on sensors to feedback
visual and/or tactile information to the operator.
Smartphones already provide a click-like response when
the screen is pressed, and computer game controllers
activate ‘rumble packs’ to pass on vibrations to gamers.
Devices incorporating haptic feedback technology can be
utilised to emulate the functionality of physical keyboards
on touchscreen tablets or to convey other tactile
experiences, such as the sensation of texture.
One of the pioneering applications of haptic technology
can be traced back to the servomechanism systems in
large aircraft. These systems were engineered to replicate
the tactile feedback experienced by pilots as they neared a
stall, thereby providing a critical warning of perilous flight
conditions.
Haptic systems are generally divided inte three main
categories: graspable, wearable and touchable devices.
Each group is characterised by its distinct application and
the type of sensory feedback it delivers.
Graspable Haptic Systems
Graspable devices are those held in the hand and readily
generate feedback transferred to fingers and palms.
Joysticks and digital game controllers are the most
common type of graspable devices. While commonplace
in video games graspable devices also provide a more
realistic experience to pilots training in flight simulators.
Resistance in joysticks and vibrations mimic the effects
pilots would feel when adjusting various aircraft control
surfaces.
Wearable Haptic Systems
‘Wearable haptic devices are often worn on the hand or
wrist. Rings and gloves are the most common of these
devices. Haptic gloves are employed to enhance the gaming
experience by providing physical feedback to interactions
with the digital environment. Virtual reality haptic gloves
increase the immersion experience of suitably equipped
games and applications. By interacting through the sense
of touch rather than controllers or joysticks, users get a
more realistic experience and the ability to perform tasks
requiring more dexterity and faster, more natural, intuitive
responses.
Haptic rings, designed for individuals with visual
impairments, utilise vibrational feedback across various
points on the user’s finger. These devices are intended
to enhance the user’s interaction with touchscreen
interfaces.
Smartwatches and fitness trackers frequently employ
haptic feedback mechanisms. These devices convey
notifications to users through various vibration patterns,
alerting the wearer to events such as goal achievements,
new emails, conveying alarm settings, among others.
Some of the more sophisticated devices interpret GPS data
to inform the wearer of directional changes required.
Touchable Haptic Systems
Touchable systems encompass electronic devices equipped
with touchscreen functionality, such as smartphones,
tablets, and interactive interfaces like ATM terminals and
self-service kiosks.
While not all touchscreen devices integrate haptic
technologies, those that do provide tactile feedback as a
response to user interactions, providing confirmation of
data entry and enhancing the overall user experience.
In addition to gaming, fitness and aircraft applications
already raised, haptic feedback is making positive
contributions in a range of other fields as outlined below.
Healthcare: The rapid advancements in virtual reality (VR)
and haptic technology are significantly transforming the
healthcare sector. Surgeons are increasingly utilising VR
for various surgical procedures. The integration of haptic
feedback within these virtual environments enhances the
tactile perception of surgeons, thereby reducing cognitive
load and contributing to improved patient outcomes.
Retail: Grocery stores are increasingly using self-checkout
to reduce staff and speed up menu navigation and
pricing. Haptic feedback on touchscreens provides some
confidence for the consumer that data has been input or
processes have been completed.
Animation
Computer animation is a way of automating the process
of linking graphic elements that change slightly over time
and, when played consecutively, simulate motion.
Animated presentations add realism to CAD models. In
applications where assembly instructions or movement of
parts is required. These are best supplied visually through
a staged/timed assembly presented through an animation.
This type of presentation would also be the fastest to
produce, generate the smallest file size and be the least
expensive option.
The graphic shows, through animation, the complexity of
movement associated with each rotation of the shaft.
A graphic shows of a simliar assembly shows a great
degree of detail.
Virtual reality simulates environments and may give the
user an opportunity to interact with the environment.
Walk-through or fly-over simulations
are an example of this type of
digital material where the user can
‘walk around’ digital models or
environments and view them as if
they existed in a three-dimensional
space. They are designed to provide
an off-site viewer with an in-context impression of
projected designs that may not even exist. These digital
constructs provide detail, perspectives and interaction not
necessarily available with a physical model without any of
the costs associated with physical models or transport to
remote sites. Virtual technologies are used to test vehicle
aerodynamics, train astronauts and even simulate road
conditions for learning drivers. Mark Zuckerberg says,
“the incredible thing about VR experiences is that you feel
like you're present in another place with other people”.
The ability of computers to convert two-dimensional
drawings or three-dimensional models into virtual
reality environments has enhanced the designer’s ability
to perceive potential design flaws, test for materials
suitability, examine ergonomic compatibility and make
modifications before the first physical component is
produced. Recent developments see CAD programs
incorporating ‘assessment of sustainability algorithms’
that can determine and compare the environmental
impact of a range of viable alternatives.
Virtual reality technologies generate computer models of
real components or systems and are finding applications
in architectural modelling, remote control of robotics,
machining, ergonomic design and vehicle prototyping.
Virtual reality can be used to simulate the interaction
of components on-screen, without incurring the costs
of physical prototypes. Static or dynamic loads may be
modeled to analyse the performance of a particular design
or modification. Virtual reality constructs also allow
designers to predict how the model will perform in use
and when exposed to a variety of real-world forces.
Virtualisation of products allows designers to evaluate the
efficacy of design in a responsive environment. It is often
employed in conjunction with computer-based testing,
such as finite element analysis. In the transport industries
(air, sea and land), designers can use virtual tests to
examine fluid dynamics and review the efficiency and
viability of their designs. Both time and money are saved
in these virtual ‘proof of concept’ simulations. Designers
can test a variety of factors such as lift, drag, turbulence,
fluid flow patterns, heat transfer fields, momentum, etc.
All of this is done using the CAD package’s embedded
mathematical modelling and driven by a physics engine,
maintaining the laws of physics. The required outcome
of the initial brief and anticipated returns will often
determine whether the considerable time and costs
associated with generating a realistic and effective virtual
environment can be justified.
In a practical, consumer-based application, Japanese
customers of Matsushita can submit architectural plans
for processing into a virtual simulation to examine a range
of kitchen design alternatives.
In the entertainment industry, immersive virtual
environments are now commonplace. Four-dimensional
theatre experiences simulate additional environmental
factors through chair and air movement, vibrations, scents
and water misting.
In Figure A 2.2.5g, Goddard engineer Tom Grubb
manipulates a three-dimensional simulation that
animates the speed and direction of four million stars. The
system employs a virtual reality program to obtain a new
perspective on the stars’ motions. The simulation helped
by making the design process for spacecraft, instruments
and repair missions easier, allowing engineers to simulate
experiences before they start manufacturing.
Virtual reality operations save costs, allow for real-time
training, testing and development at considerable cost
savings and reductions in time compared to traditional
approaches to model making, testing and evaluation.
Virtual reality technology has the potential to:
« advance real-time training
« reduce product development cycles
« save resources by removing reliance on physical
modelling.
Augmented reality
Augmented reality (AR) integrates the user’s environment
with digital overlays to provide an enhanced, information-
rich environment. Content provided may be (but not
limited to) haptic, visual, auditory or a combination of
any of the previous. Information may be transmitted
through a variety of media such as smartphones, digital
glasses or headsets. AR differs from VR in that AR adds
enhancements to an environment while VR totally
replaces the environment with a simulated immersive
experience.
The real value of AR lies in its ability to merge a person’s
view of the real world with additional information in such
a way that it presents a cohesive, value-added, easy-to-
interpret environment.
AR may be used by designers and consumers alike.
Interior designers may employ AR to overlay room
views, test furniture stylings and various layout options.
In the fashion industry, clothing and accessories may
be reviewed by the user to check for fit and appearance.
Marketing images and promotional information may be
displayed at live events and on sporting fields.
Example:
Augmented reality appears in map technologies such
as Google Maps. Users may search for a wide range of
destinations, including such things as grocery stores,
restaurants, local attractions etc., and an overlay of
destinations appears on the map marking locations and
relative distances from the user’s location.
Extending this approach, the user may ask for directions.
Using a ‘live view’ function, interactive arrows appear on
the screen overlaid on top of the live camera view of the
current location.
Example:
Augmented reality heads-up-displays, or simply Heads-
up-displays (HUDs), are now becoming common place
additions to domestic vehicles, designed to improve
driver concentration and provide significant customisable
information. These systems are set to replace many of the
traditional features drivers have become accustomed to,
such as mechanical instrument clusters.
HUDs offer several benefits to drivers, such as:
« Provision of safety alerts visually and audibly
« customisation for the individual driver to assess what
information is important for display
« enhanced driver safety by allowing the driver to focus
solely on the road ahead while critical information is
provided within line-of-sight.
HUDs have appeared in an aviation context for many
years, but continue to develop as the technology evolves.
Finite element analysis (FEA)
Most often used for stress calculations, finite element
analysis or FEA is a tool used to test virtual models under
a variety of conditions. It may be used to test the design
of new products or analyse existing products in new
situations.
Several FEA software packages are available. Some include
components to measure thermal, electromagnetic, fluid
and structural dynamics.
Using the structural assessment component, FEA can
simulate how an object would react under service loads or
conditions. Under the FEA process, a virtual model shows
how loads would be distributed within the object. These
reactive forces may be in response to loads generated by:
expansion due to temperature variations, external loads,
pressures or vibration.
Through the action of dividing the model up into a series
of simple but discrete elements joined through nodes, the
program can analyse a complex object by considering
it as a series of resolvable units. Observations are then
made as to how the object may perform under a variety
of conditions. When assessing a structure FEM may be
employed to generate stiffness and strength visualisations.
This data may in turn assist with weight minimisation,
materials selection, manufacturing process choices and
costing.
FEA can be used to study the interactions of assembled
components on-screen, before incurring the costs of
physical prototypes. Static or dynamic loads may be
simulated to evaluate a design’s performance under
extension, bending and twisting to evaluate stress, strain,
and displacement. FEA thus allows design simulations to
evolve before manufacturing. FEA often finds applications
in the car industry, where simulated collisions using
components, assemblies or whole-of-vehicle designs can
show the result of various crash scenarios. The data would
show distributed forces, twisting, buckling, movement
and even failure of various elements of the design.
FEA is very cost and time-efficient. Manufacturing and
physical testing costs far outweigh those of producing and
testing a computer simulation. It substantially decreases
the time from design concept to product reality. FEA
analysis, however, can only inform designs to a point
where physical testing may be required to confirm the
specifications of the final product.
In the context of finite element analysis (FEA), ‘LEM’ is a
term regularly used to examine complex engineering and
physical systems. It involves breaking down a structure into
smaller elements, analyzing their behavior and assembling
these results to understand the entire system.LEM, on the
other hand is just one type of material behaviour that FEA
can analyse; FEA can also handle non-linear, plastic or
time-dependent material models.
Once a digital model is broken into a series of interlinking
triangles, a mesh of finite elements is formed. In simple
terms each triangle is joined by a node forming a complex
geometric mesh. All of the elements contain algebraic
equations linked to material properties of the field.
Globally, the elements and their built-in equations allow
for the solving of physical problems based on numeric
calculations around such inquiries as stress distribution,
temperature, expansion, etc. The precision of the results
delivered is decided by the coarseness of the mesh.
In Finite Element Analysis (FEA), the different colors
show how much an identified parameter changes in
different areas, enabling identification of regions exhibiting
either higher or lower intensity. This helps to review areas
of greater or lesser intensity. An attached colour legend
shows the relative magnitude of a parameter linked to a
colour.
When looking at stress and strain, areas with higher
values may be where the material may break or suffer
plastic deformation. The software can be set to show when
these values go beyond safe limits, such as when stress
is higher than what the material can handle. This helps
in understanding which parts of the model are most at
risk and may require redesigning. FEA may also be used
to predict material failure by assessing expectations of
fatigue. This provides an indication of the durability or
expected lifespan of the object under examination, as
shown in the FEA simulation in Figure A 2.2.5L
The FEA in Figure A2.2.5m shows the complex stress
characteristics between the engaging components of a
snap-fit component. This data helps designers by revealing
the initial stresses formed, but also may be extrapolated
over time to inform issues surrounding product
durability.

A2.2.6
The importance and potential of rapid prototyping
is summed up in statements that proclaim it as the
next Industrial Revolution. Essentially, it is a group of
technologies that are used to construct physical models
(prototypes). Often referred to as three-dimensional
printers, most of the technologies involving rapid
prototyping are additive in nature, but CNC milling
machines have coined phrases such as ‘subtractive rapid
prototyping’ to describe their systems.
‘The basic process for additive rapid prototyping consists
of five steps:
« create a CAD drawing of the object
« convert the CAD file into an appropriate format such
as STL, OBJ, AME 3ME, etc.
« transfer the STL file to the rapid prototyping machine
of choice
« manufacture the product
«+ clean up/assemble the final product.
Stereolithography (SLA)
In this process, computer software sections a 3D computer
model into thin slices (0.05 mm-0.15 mm) before a
computer-controlled UV laser beam or transparent LCD
in conjunction with a UV lamp to ‘build’ a model layer
by layer within a tank of resin. Figure A2.2.6a shows the
stereolithography process.
Printers may use a bottom-up or top-down approach.
Most use the top-down technique. The model-building
process involves the reaction between a bath of liquid
photopolymer and a directed beam that ‘prints out’ the
gradually solidifying object. As each layer is complete, the
object, mounted on a movable table, moves downwards to
expose more photopolymer ready to create the next layer.
This technique allows the production of objects that may
be too costly, time-consuming or difficult to create, using
traditional methods.
The SLA technique can produce high-quality, smooth-
surfaced, complex models quickly, with no visible layering.
A variety of resin types are available. However, the
process does have some drawbacks, including potentially
high costs, models that lack strength, the need for post-
production washing and curing after printing and the use
of non-environmentally friendly materials.
Fused deposition modelling
(FDM)
Fused deposition modelling works on a principle similar
to a hot-melt glue gun (Figure A 2.2.6b). FDM uses
ultra thin filaments, stored on a spool. These filaments
are extruded through a heated chamber that converts
them into a semi-liquid form, before being deposited.
The fusion head travels horizontally across a movable
machine table. FDM meodelling filaments commonly used
include investment casting wax, PETG, ABS, PLA, Nylon,
Polycarbonate and Flexible Thermoplastic Polyamide
(TPA). Accuracies of + - 0.15% may be achieved, and if
support material is required, it may be easily removed
post-production.
There are several advantages associated with FDM
printing that make it a very popular choice. These include
lower costs, the large variety of filaments available, speed
of printing, size of builds and relatively good detail that
may be achieved. However, the process does have some
disadvantages, including the fact that other methods can
provide higher levels of detail without layering and the
need for support materials/structures.
Selective laser sintering (SLS)
Selective laser sintering has its name derived directly
from the manufacturing processes involved. A CO, laser
selectively focuses on a section of a movable table covered
in a heat-fusible powder. The power of the laser is set to
bind the powder without it melting (sintering). Repetitive
scans of the table with the laser build up the model in
layers.
Support structures are not required in this process. As
each layer scan is complete, a new layer of powder is
deposited over the top and pressed flat with a roller. The
act of compressing the powder allows the model to be
constructed without supporting structures. Models may
even be manufactured with moving parts. Once complete,
the model is dusted off, with excess powder recycled.
SLS powders can be manufactured from thermoplastics,
polystyrene, sand wax, ceramics, steel and even stainless
steel.
SLA, SLS, and FDM compared Sample Questions

the ability to form complex geometries, is faster than B. To ensure the product meets the designer’s
FDM and has greater flexibility in its range of materials preferences and goals.

in these technologies have secured greater dimensional C. To reduce production costs by limiting the |15

B2.1.1
The five phases of the Design Cycle identified by the IB
are as follows.
« empathise
+ defining the project
+ ideation and modelling
+ designing a solution
« presenting a solution
ogether, these components form an iterative approach to
problem-solving. Everything flows from understanding
user needs and examining the problem from their
perspective. In more detail, each stage can be identified by
the following functions.
Empathise
To empathise is to begin to understand or share the feelings
of others. This approach is crucial for understanding the
needs of both groups and individuals.
According to CB Insights (a private market intelligence
platform headquartered in New York that specialises
in analysing data on startups, venture capital activity,
and emerging technologies), 42% of startups fail due
to a lack of market need. This indicates that inadequate
research has been conducted to thoroughly understand
user requirements, thereby preventing researchers from
identifying a sufficiently large market segment with
a specific problem or unmet need. A diverse array of
research methodologies, encompassing both quantitative and qualitative techniques, is essential for collecting
comprehensive data,
Qualitative methods such as user interviews, observation,
and empathy maps are used to understand user thoughts,
feelings and motivations. These approaches are often
complemented with quantitative methods, such as
user surveys, analytics data (CTR, drop-off rates), and
customer sentiment analysis, to gauge behaviours and
scale sentiments. Together, these methods provide a
holistic view, revealing both the ‘what’ and ‘why’ of user
experiences to guide design decisions.
Defining the project
This phase builds upon the foundational knowledge
acquired in the ‘empathise stage! Designers must
reconceptualise the problem and need to align with the
users’ perspectives. By employing task analysis, designers
can achieve a deeper understanding of users, their
environments, objectives and any environmental factors
influencing performance.
Ideation and modelling
This stage is characterised by creative idea generation,
wherein designers propose a range of solutions to the
identified problem, progressively refining their concepts
through an iterative process. Initial ideas manifest as rapid
conceptual designs, which subsequently evolve into low-
fidelity physical models using materials such as cardboard
or digital models in simple wireframes, etc. The swift
prototyping process facilitates prompt modifications based
on stakeholder feedback. At times, the interplay between
designs and feedback may necessitate a re-examination
and refinement of the problem or a revisit to the creative
ideation phase.
Designing a solution
Repeated cycles of iteration, aimed atenhancing prototypes
based on specific user feedback and field trials, lead to the
progressive refinement of the solution. User responses
or developmental adjustments may necessitate revisiting
earlier stages of the cycle for further consideration and
refinement.
Presenting a solution
The presentation of a comprehensive, annotated solution
should represent the culmination of an exhaustive
investigation of the design brief from the user’s perspective.
Accompanying annotations should not merely enumerate
features, materials and manufacturing methods but also
explain the design decision-making process. They should
articulate the rationale behind the design, ensuring that
the viewer understands the reasoning underpinning
the proposed solution and where the product would
foreseeably fit within the market alongside competing
designs.
Adherence to the principles behind the design cycle
should yield a refined product.

B2.1.2 and B2.1.3
Primary Sources
These are all original documents and may take many
forms, including, but not limited to, written works such
as letters, diaries, surveys, questionnaires, records of
interviews and original documents. Creative works such
as music, photographs and works of art are also primary
sources.
Using a physical artifact (such as the one shown in Figure
B 2.2.1b) for research, testing and evaluation purposes in
the early phases of preparing a solution to a design brief
would be considered to be utilising the benefits of primary
research.
In relation to design, a drawing or an actual original
product design is a primary source for research if it was
created at the time of the event or subject you are studying,
and serves as a first-hand account or direct evidence.
‘These items are considered primary sources because they
were produced by participants or observers and are not an
interpretation or analysis by someone else.
Primary sources often include raw data and direct
evidence. Raw data refers to unprocessed, unorganised
information collected directly from a source before any
analysis, interpretation or transformation has taken place.
Its the foundational material from which insights are
derived.
Characteristics of Raw Data:
« Unstructured or minimally structured: Often in the
form it was originally recorded (e.g., survey responses,
sensor readings, interview transcripts).
« Untouched by analysis: No filtering, cleaning or
statistical treatment has been applied.
« Context-dependent: Its meaning and relevance often
depend on how and why it was collected.
Examples include:
« Temperature readings from a weather station are taken
every minute.
+ Audio recordings from user interviews.
« Clickstream logs from a website.
« Open-ended responses from a questionnaire.
In user-centred design, raw data is often the starting point
for building empathy and generating insights. It needs to
be sifted through to uncover patterns, pain points and
opportunities.
In Design research, it provides:
« Authenticity: Raw data preserves the original context
and nuance.
« Traceability: Enables researchers to verify findings
and ensure transparency.
+ Flexibility: Can be re-analysed using different methods
or for different questions.
An example of primary-sourced information would be
data collected by researchers gathering anthropometric
measurements from a proposed user group. Because these
measurements are garnered from user groups, the data
relates directly to the intended target market.
Qualitative and quantitative data
Data obtained from both primary and secondary research
can be divided into two broad groups: quantitative and
qualitative.
Qualitative data provides information about some quality
aspects of the subject or product being analysed. These
include preferences or feelings about the product. Such
assessments are often determined using instruments such
as:
» interviews
« observation
« focus groups
+ questionnaires
telephone surveys.
These instruments aim to determine the level of
satisfaction the surveyed population has with an idea,
product or service.
Individuals, however, perceive the world in different ways.
This may be a factor of their upbringing, experience,
culture, etc. Qualitative data is gathered in this context
to elicit meaningful responses from individuals and
groups.
Other examples:
Text messaging and vibrating functions of
smartphones allow people with hearing impairments
to communicate easily over large distances.
Text to Speech (TTS) technology: helps people with
disabilities, particularly those with visual impairments
or learning difficulties like dyslexia, by reading digital
text aloud. This allows them to access information
without needing to read it themselves, which can
significantly improve their comprehension and
engagement with written content.
Language Translation Apps such as Google Translate:
facilitate communication with others who speak
different languages, particularly in situations where
they might not have access to a professional interpreter.
This enables the user to participate more fully in
society and access services like healthcare, education
and government agencies regardless of language
barriers.
‘Rumble Strips’ also known as Audio Tactile Lane
Markings (ATLM): typically placed on the edge lines
and centrelines of roads are designed to generate a
distinet noise and tactile sensation when a vehicle
drives over them, alerting drivers if they are drifting
out of their lane, particularly when drowsy or
distracted, allowing them to correct their course and
avoid potential accidents.
Braille dots on banknotes: Raised dots on the polymer
banknotes of Australia (see Figure C1.2.3b), Canada
and the UK provide a tactile feature that helps the
vision impaired to identify note denominations. Paper-
based banknotes also allow similar tactile functionality
by using intaglio printing.
Figure C1.2.3b ‘Braille’ dot Tactile features on
banknotes
Currency Identification apps, driven by Al such as
CashReader and MCT Money Reader use the camera
in a smartphone to identify bank notes for people with
visual impairments.
Tactile pavements: help people, particularly the
visually impaired, by providing a textured surface on
walkways, which can be detected with a cane or by foot,
alerting them to potential hazards like road crossings,
platform edges, or changes in direction, allowing them
to navigate more safely and independently.
Examples of the type of information required and why the
responses are so varied include:
Temperature - Individuals experience temperature in
different ways. A range of comfort zones will exist based on
body mass, manner of dress or even physiological changes
that can be developed from exposure to a particular
temperature or environment over time.
Space - Space is another part of the environment perceived
differently by individuals. Comfort zones or personal
space vary from culture to culture.
Taste - Responses to taste are also influenced by culture
and experience. Taste can, of course, have at least two
separate meanings, e.g. the sensation of flavour perceived
in the mouth and throat on contact with a substance or
one’s ability to appreciate aesthetics and influences of
cultural variation. Both are valid.
If the product was a new packaged product, such as a
sauce, the first definition is valid. Preferences for sweet,
sour, and salty foods vary significantly by culture,
shaped by factors like cultural exposure to certain foods,
learned associations and differing dietary habits. What is
considered delicious in one culture might be unappetising
in another, as taste preferences are not static but are
moulded by our environment, with cultural norms and
food traditions playing a profound role in developing and
reinforcing these sensory experiences.
In product design, taste operates as a lens through which
aesthetic value is interpreted, often guiding decisions
about form, colour, texture, and symbolic meaning.
It is informed by:
» Personal preferences
» Cultural norms
» Historical context
« Social identity.
Aesthetic taste is important in product design because it
is foundational to:
1. User-Centred Appeal
« Products that align with users’ aesthetic tastes are more
likely to be embraced and loved.
« Taste influences consumer first impressions, which are
critical in competitive markets,
+ It fosters emotional resonance, turning functional
objects into meaningful experiences.
2. Cultural Relevance
« Taste is culturally embedded—what’s elegant in Tokyo
might feel excessive in Stockholm.
« Designers must navigate local aesthetics to ensure
relevance and avoid alienation.
« This is especially vital in global brands, where cross-
cultural sensitivities can make or break a product.
3. Brand Identity & Differentiation
« A brands taste signature (e.g., Apples minimalist
elegance or IKEAs democratic simplicity) becomes a
strategic asset.
« Ithelps carve out a distinct visual language, reinforcing
brand values and positioning.
4. Sustainability & Longevity
e Products designed with ‘good taste’ often have
timeless appeal, reducing the churn of trend-based
consumption.
« Thisapproach aligns with sustainable design principles,
encouraging longer product lifecycles and emotional
durability.
The designer’s challenge is to balance personal expression
with user expectations. They often rely on iterative testing,
mood boards, and cultural research to refine aesthetic
decisions. The ultimate goal is not to please everyone, but
to resonate deeply with the intended audience.
Taste in product design is about crafting aesthetic meaning
that aligns with values, identities, and aspirations. It's
where form meets feeling, and where design transcends
utility to become culture.
Value - may be perceived as a function of cost, features,
prestige, rarity, etc., or a combination of these factors.
Quantitative data are those that are obtained in some
form of numerical data and, as such, determine a quantity.
Such assessments are often determined using instruments
such as:
« datalogging
» scale models
« full-scale tests
« questionnaires
« computer simulations
« controlled experiments.
A quantitative assessment of attitude towards an idea/
product/service can be obtained by determining the level
or intensity of feeling. This is done by using questionnaires
that ask for a rating scale, usually organised from ‘strongly
dislike’ to ‘strongly like' or ‘strongly disapprove’ to
‘strongly approve, etc. Such a scale can have a numerical
value assigned to each choice for later analysis. A scale
with these properties is known as a Likert Scale.
In these questionnaires, a final summation and averaging
of the responses provides an overall determination of the
participant’s evaluation. Participant evaluation can then
be analysed along with other participants using statistical
methods for factors such as age, gender etc.
In the same manner, experimental protocols may be
established to test the viability of materials, components,
assemblies and impacts of design alternatives.
Analysis of primary research
Analysing primary research involves various techniques,
depending on the nature of the data collected. Here are
some common methods:
Surveys and questionnaires are often analysed using
statistical methods. These generate insights through
the determination of a mean, median, mode, standard
deviation, and techniques such as regression analysis.
Regression analysis may be used to determine how
respondent-rated features influence overall satisfaction.
For instance, ease of use and design aesthetics have a
strong positive impact on satisfaction, while price has a
negative impact.
Open-ended responses where answers don't fit into a
standard format may be reviewed to identify user patterns
and themes.
As with questionnaires, interview results and narrative
responses may be reviewed for shared patterns and
themes. Interviews. When examining narrative responses,
Focus Groups present an opportunity to examine the
frequency and context of key words and phrases uttered
in discussions.
Observations provide an opportunity for cultural
insights, behavioural patterns and interactions. Using
predetermined codes, observers can quickly record data
for further analysis.

B2.1.4 and B2.1.5
Secondary Sources
These are often interpretations, analyses, summaries, and
abstracts of primary sources. Academic journal articles,
textbooks, newspaper reports and reference books all
fall under this heading. Secondary sources, such as those
listed, provide interpretation and context for designers.
Papers in academic journals can be either primary or
secondary research, depending on their content. Primary
research papers report original findings, data or discoveries
directly from a study, such as surveys or experiments,
while secondary research papers analyse, synthesise, or
interpret existing primary sources, like review articles that
summarise multiple studies or articles commenting on
others’ findings.
Similar to the data gathered from user groups, secondary data may be collected from a database of anthropometric measurements. Such databases are often national and are more general in form.
Referencing a text such as this would be an example of using a secondary source.

Analysis of secondary research
Analysis of existing data helps inform design decision-
making using one or more of the many strategies outlined
below.
Statistical analysis of secondary data provides information
about connections, correlations, patterns and trends. Data-
driven decisions are a direct result of statistical analysis.
Content analysis involves the examination of text,
graphical or visual, or multimedia content to attempt
to identify patterns, trends, contexts and meanings. It is
beneficial in the interpretation of user feedback in any
of its forms, e.g. surveys, social media, product reviews
and customer complaints. Evaluation of one’s own or
a competitors product may reveal product trends or
suggest improvements. Content analysis may be both
qualitative and quantitative. This dual approach provides
a comprehensive analysis of user needs and a product’s
efficacy in the market.
Literature search/reviews involve the systematic search for
published material to establish current levels of knowledge
and criticism related to the topic of interest and to inform
future research directions. This search is usually performed
using sources of information recognised as being
authoritative, such as peer-reviewed academic journals,
books and published theses, including information
published by consumer magazines, government agencies
and industry.
A properly conducted literature search will uncover the
state of knowledge about a topic, preventing unnecessary
duplication of effort while highlighting areas of potentially
fruitful study. A problem with literature searches,
particularly in the current age of electronic information
retrieval, is the sheer amount of information that can be
retrieved and must be sorted through for relevance to the
specific project.
The search for published resources was traditionally begun
by consulting volumes containing collections of abstracts
from papers published within the targeted field.
A hugely time-consuming process. This process has been
replaced by developments in information technologies.
Literature searches
The rise of the internet and search engines such as Google
Scholar has become an essential tool in locating academic
papers. The provision of access to these resources
anywhere/anytime in the world for purchase, or free
through open access, has refined the literature search
process.
Google Scholar’s size is not fixed; recent estimates from
2018 suggest around 389 million records, while the 2014
PLOS ONE study estimated about 100 million English-
language scholarly documents on the web. There is no
official statement from Google on the exact size of its
index, but it continuously grows. Google Scholar indexes
a wide range of scholarly literature from academic
publishers, professional societies, universities, and online
repositories, not just specific journals, and includes
articles, books, theses, and more.
Artificial intelligence has also significantly changed the
way literature reviews are conducted. Through enhanced
search capabilities, vast amounts of information may be
processed and interpreted. Summaries may be produced
on the fly, advanced search criteria help to screen unwanted
data and analysis tools are applied, all in one action.
Artificial Intelligence (AI) has significantly changed
literature reviews by automating repetitive tasks like
searching and summarising, increasing efficiency, and
helping to identify trends and gaps in existing research. Al
tools facilitate semantic analysis and citation management,
improve accuracy, and can reduce human bias, though
they also raise ethical considerations regarding algorithm
bias and the need for human oversight.
Note: a precautionary note here should be included about
the reliance on AA, as it is still subject to error, although
improving as further developments increase response
reliability. Erroneous results can be provided, and non-
existent references have been found in some studies.
Issues with Primary and
Secondary sources
Primary research methods offer distinct advantages by
facilitating direct engagement with users, thereby enabling
the identification of unmet needs and expectations.
Through this process, designers can uncover frustrations
and obstacles encountered during product use, as well
as collect data on user interactions that may highlight
usability concerns. However, the quality of primary
research is contingent upon the recruitment of participants
who accurately represent the target demographic, a task
that is often challenging. Biases may also arise due to
the phrasing of interview questions, the methodology
of observations, or issues related to the ‘observer effect;
wherein participants alter their behaviour due to their
awareness of being observed. Additionally, conducting
primary research often demands considerable time and
financial resources.
Using data retrieved from such sources as market reports 
and case studies, secondary research methods may be
used to highlight legislative standards, industry and social 
trends. Failures in competitors’ products to fully meet 
user needs may be uncovered when analysing market 
competition. However, issues may arise when assessing
existing data that does not fully synchronise with your
specific design or context. Secondary sources alse quickly
date and, therefore, may not be entirely accurate. In some
cases, the volume of information available may make it
difficult to find, analyse and synthesise an appropriate
focus.

Meta-analysis
Meta-analysis is often used to aggregate the results of
many, often small, primary research studies performed at
different times and places into a larger data group to draw
broader conclusions.
It is therefore considered to be secondary research, 
because it only synthesises and analyses data from existing 
primary studies, rather than generating new primary data
itself. 
‘While meta-analyses involve rigorous statistical methods
and analytical work, they don’t involve collecting new data 
from individuals or experiments but instead repurpose Commons
and combine findings from previously published research
to provide a comprehensive, aggregated conclusion. 
Developlng a Persona
Persona development requires special attention to
detail and gathering enough information to specify an 
appropriate target user group. Relying on demographics
alone for this process is fraught with pitfalls. The following demographic details are gathered about a target user group
examples show how relying on demographics alone may
provide a lack of detail that may lead to an inappropriate 
profile.
Example one: male demographic profile
The following demographic details are gathered about a
target user group: 

« Male
« European nationality
« Born late 19" century 
« Public figures
« Cultural icons
« Leading professionals in their field

Despite their shared backgrounds, this data generates two examples of profoundly different individuals whose tastes in products and services would differ significantly.

« Female
« European origin
« Born late 19" century
« Figures of perseverance
« Leading professionals in their field
« Global impact

Despite their shared backgrounds, this data generates two
examples of profoundly different individuals whose tastes in products and services would differ greatly.

Personae help designers determine exactly ‘who' they
are designing for. Knowing the target users’ behaviour
patterns, likes and dislikes, increases the chances of a
successful design that meets the user’s expectations.
Personae are critical to this understanding of user needs
while allowing designers to empathise with their market
through the gathering of specific and comprehensive
data. The key steps to constructing successful personae
include:
« Conducting user research, gathering data through
interviews, surveys, focus groups and observations.
+ Analysing data for patterns of behaviour, common
areas of dissatisfaction.
« Categorising users in groups based on shared
characteristics obtained from the previous stage.
» Developing a profile for each group.
« Sharing profiles with key team members, clients and
seeking feedback.
« Applying the personae to the design cycle.
Goals and needs demographics, background, Gender,
ageand relationship Goals and needs, likes/dislikes
behaviours.
Personae are crafted to enable designers to empathise
deeply with the user. The primary objective of a persona
is to eliminate any biases or preconceived notions the
designer might hold about the user group, thereby
fostering a genuine connection with the user. This is
especially crucial when users possess expectations and
experiences, including cultural backgrounds and heritage,
that differ significantly from the designer.
By engaging with the persona, designers aim to perceive
the persona as a real individual. This approach significantly
enhances the likelihood that the product will effectively
address the users’ needs and expectations.
User personae are not perfect, and if poorly crafted, they
may create problems for designers and ultimately the
products they create. If the persona leads teams in the
wrong direction, the product may potentially be solving
problems that don't exist.
The Adobe model for design thinking is shown in Figure
B2.1.4, identifying empathy as the critical starting point
for the process.
Common issues and challenges associated with user-centred research
Conducting user-centred research enables researchers
to uncover detailed insights into user behaviours,
motivations and unmet needs. However, despite its value,
the application of user-centred research techniques is
fraught with challenges that can compromise the integrity
and utility of the findings if not carefully addressed.
One of the most persistent challenges lies in recruiting
suitable participants. The validity of user research is
contingent upon engaging individuals who accurately
reflect the target user group. Yet, researchers often
encounter barriers when attempting to access hard-
to-reach populations—such as individuals with
disabilities, older adults or those operating within niche
professional domains. Low response rates can also impact
the representativeness of the data, undermining the
generalisation of insights.
Bias presents another formidable obstacle. Both researcher
and participant biases can subtly infiltrate the research
process, distorting outcomes.
Bias in user-centred design research can subtly distort
findings, misguide design decisions and ultimately
compromise the inclusivity and effectiveness of a
product.
Cognitive biases stem from how researchers and
participants process information:
« Confirmation bias: Researchers may seek or prioritise
data that supports their existing assumptions,
overlocking contradictory evidence. Example:
Preferring feedback that validates a design concept
while ignoring usability concerns voiced by outliers.
« Framing effect: The way questions or findings are
presented can influence interpretation. Example:
Asking “How easy was it to use?” implies a positive
experience, skewing responses toward affirmation.
« Anchoring bias: Early information disproportionately
influences decisions. Example: Initial user feedback
sets expectations, causing later insights to be
undervalued.
« Availability heuristic: Designers may rely on easily
recalled examples rather than representative data.
Example: Assuming a feature is popular because it was
mentioned frequently in one session, despite broader
data suggesting otherwise.
Social and cultural biases arise from interpersonal
dynamics and cultural framing:
+ Social desirability bias: Participants may tailor
responses to please researchers or align with perceived
norms. Example: Users avoid criticising a product in a
moderated session to appear agreeable.
« Hawthorne effect: Participants behave differently
when they know they’re being observed. Example:
Overperforming during usability tasks, giving a false
impression of ease-of-use.
« Cultural bias: Researchers may interpret behaviours
or feedback through their own cultural lens. Example:
Misreading silence as confusion when it may reflect
cultural norms around politeness or reflection.
Researcher biases relate to how studies are designed and
interpreted:
+ Selection bias: Choosing participants who are not
representative of the target user base, Example: Testing
only with tech-savvy users for a general consumer
app.
« Leading questions: Structuring questions in ways that
suggest a preferred answer. Example: “Wouldnt this
feature make your experience better?” nudges users
toward agreement.
« Interpretation bias: Drawing conclusions that reflect
the researcher’s expectations more than the data.
Example: Overemphasising positive feedback while
minimising critical insights.
Addressing bias in product design and user-centred design
is important because it can undermine the very principle
of empathy and responsiveness. It risks designing for the
assumed user rather than the actual user. It can potentially
overlook marginalised voices or edge cases. It also has the
complication of creating products that reflect the designer’s
worldview more than the user’s lived experience.
Mitigating bias requires deliberate strategies: triangulating
data sources, reflexive practice, inclusive sampling, and
transparent reporting. The integration of bias audits or
peer debriefs into research workflows may assist with
maintaining methodological rigour.
Access to contextual environments is also frequently
limited, especially in remote or digital research settings.
Observing users within their natural habitats yields rich,
contextually valid data. However, logistical constraints,
privacy concerns and institutional restrictions often
limit such access. Even when observation is possible,
the Hawthorne effect—where individuals alter their
behaviour due to awareness of being observed, can further
complicate data interpretation.
Comprehensive user studies require significant
investment in planning, execution and analysis. Yet, tight
project timelines and limited budgets often necessitate
shortcuts, resulting in superficial engagement with users
or truncated synthesis of findings. This is particularly
problematic when dealing with qualitative data, which
demands rigorous analysis to determine meaningful
patterns distilled from complex narratives.
Stakeholder alignment poses yet another challenge. In
the product design cycle, a stakeholders is anyone who
has a vested interest in the product’s success or is affected
by its development. Their roles span strategic, technical,
operational and user-centred domains.
Across the Design Cycle, stakeholders’ impact upon:
« Discovery and research: Customers, product
managers and designers collaborate to define the
problem space.
» Ideation and concepting: Designers and engineers
brainstorm solutions, guided by product managers
and user input.
« Prototyping and testing: QA, users, and support teams
validate usability and performance.
« Implementation: Engineers build the product,
with ongoing input from designers and product
managers.
» Launch and feedback: Marketing, sales and support
teams engage users and relay feedback for iteration.
User-centred research must ultimately inform strategic
decisions, yet stakeholders may undervalue qualitative
insights or resist findings that challenge entrenched
assumptions. Misalignment between business objectives
and user needs can lead to selective adoption of research
outcomes, diluting their impact.
Legaland ethical considerations are paramount throughout
the research process. Ensuring informed consent,
participant anonymity, and respecting and maintaining
cultural sensitivities are not merely procedural formalities
but essential components of responsible research
practice.
Legal obligations refer to compliance with formal
regulations such as data protection laws (e.g. EU General
Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), Australian Privacy
88
Act), intellectual property rights and accessibility
standards. These are enforceable and non-negotiable.
Ethical considerations go beyond legality, encompassing
moral responsibility to treat participants with respect,
fairness and transparency. For example, it may be
legally allowable to collect certain data, but ethically, the
question should be asked whether doing so is necessary
or respectful.
In product design, ethical lapses, such as exploiting user
data or misrepresenting research intent, can erode trust
and lead to long-term reputational damage, even where
no laws are broken.
Informed consent ensures that participants understand
the purpose, scope, risks and benefits of the research
before agreeing to take part. It must be voluntary,
comprehensible and revocable at any time. In design
research, this includes clarifying how data will be used
(e.g. for prototyping, persona development), whether
anonymity will be preserved and how feedback might
influence the final product. Consent forms should be
adapted to suit different literacy levels and cultural
contexts to avoid any misunderstanding.
Power dynamics may be evident when researchers
hold implicit authority over participants, especially in
commercial or institutional settings. This imbalance can
influence how openly users share feedback, particularly
if they fear judgment or repercussions. Ethical design
research mitigates this by:
« Using neutral language and inclusive facilitation
techniques.
« Encouraging co-creation and participatory methods
where users are treated as collaborators, not subjects.
« Being transparent about how their input will be used
and credited.
Cultural norms and sensitivities shape how users perceive
products, interact with technology and engage in research.
Insensitivity to these norms can lead to misinterpretation
of data or alienation of key user groups.
Researchers must adapt methods to local customs (e.g.
avoiding direct questioning in collectivist cultures) and
be aware of symbolic meanings in visual design, colour,
gesture and language. They must also avoid imposing
Western-centric design paradigms in global contexts.
Failure to uphold these standards can erode trust and
expose organisations to reputational and legal risks.
inally, translating research insights into actionable design
interventions is complex. Findings may be abstract or
context-dependent, requiring careful interpretation and
collaboration between researchers and designers.
The previous analysis of the complexities and challenges
associated with user-centred research may be summarised
into the following points.
« Recruitment difficulties: finding the right users can be
hard
« Bias: researcher or participant bias can skew results
« Limited access: some environments or user groups are
hard to reach
« Time and budget constraints: deep research takes
resources
« Data overload: synthesising qualitative data can be
overwhelming

B2.1.6
A storyboard provides a visual representation outlining
a chronological sequence of events or actions illustrating
the user’s journey. This tool is instrumental in identifying
user challenges, or pain points, which designers can then
address to develop products that are intuitive, accessible
and highly relatable.
Creating a user journey storyboard
The role of the personas is to allow designers to empathise
with an artificial construct with specifically identified
traits and needs. Once these are identified, designers can
make their initial, informed response when creating an
initial solution to the brief. Users are then employed as
real-life reviewers to examine, evaluate and indicate where
pain points or problems occur in the use or functionality
of the design at its earliest stages of development.
A user journey begins with the user’s initial exposure to
a product, whether through the physical item itself or via
marketing efforts. This journey progresses through the
stages of product evaluation, purchase and ultimately,
usage. Mapping the user journey provides designers with
critical insights into the user experience, emphasising
the user’s needs and challenges throughout the process.
This approach ensures the final design is user-centric,
addressing potential pain points and enhancing overall
satisfaction.
The storyboard commences with a comprehensive
understanding of the user persona, encompassing
behavioural patterns, goals and needs. Each phase of
the user’s interaction with the product is meticulously
deconstructed into a sequence of events. Contextual
scenes are crafted to visually represent the user’s
experience at each stage. Detailed information, including
context, actions undertaken, pain points and emotions,
is documented within the images and accompanying
notes.
A holistic analysis of the storyboard enables designers to
consider various factors such as environment, actions,
functionality and navigation that may pose challenges to
the user. These identified challenges are then viewed as
opportunities for design improvements, enhancing the
overall user experience.

B2.1.7
Product analysis may be conducted using a variety of
techniques. The choice of which combination of analysis
tools is used is based on the product type, application,
market, etc. In many cases, all of these tools listed below
will be used.
Market assessment
This is the examination of current trends and potential
future directions of the market.
It also analyses target user behaviours and their
engagement with existing products, likes and dislikes.
Competition Analysis
A/B comparison is a technique that directly compares one
product with another. It may also be used to test various
iterations of the same product against each other.
Benchmarking involves the setting of measurable,
predetermined performance standards or key performance
indicators.
SWOT analysis assesses the products strengths,
weaknesses, opportunities and threats
Usability testing gathered directly from users,
observations, interviews, surveys and focus groups all
contribute to determining the efficacy and functionality
of the product and the identification of pain points,
preferences and emotional responses.
Market performance evaluates product efficacy through
the analysis of user engagement, repurchasing and
conversion rates from other similar products.

B2.1.8
A well-written design brief is key to aligning all
stakeholders with the project’s objectives. It establishes a
comprehensive framework that not only accelerates the
90
design process but also ensures the final product aligns
with user expectations and requirements,
The critical elements of a design brief follow the procedures
outlined below:
» Overview: a succinct statement outlining the project’s
purpose and anticipated outcomes, accompanied by
detailed information on the target user group.
« Documentation of measurable specifications,
encompassing features, functionalities, and aesthetic
considerations. Clearly defined constraints establish
boundaries and specific parameters.
» Budgetary and time allocations.
» Thorough analysis of market competition.
» Required standards and any legislative requirements
must be specified.
« Form, market segment and production scale of the
outcome
Sample design brief
Luggage identification tag
Project Overview:
A new innovative design for airport baggage tags is
required to accompany a new premium luggage line. The
tags must be appealing, easily identifiable, robust, user-
friendly, sustainable and functional. The design should
embody the company’s established brand identity and
commitment to quality and innovation.
Design Specifications:
» Size: Approximately 70 x 50mm
» Material:
materials
weather and UV-resistant recycled
« Colour: aligns with brand identity.
« Corporate recognition: Logo placed prominently on
front and back of tag
« Identification details: Full name, phone number and
email
« Attaching/detaching: adjustable connection that offers
secure attachment, easily attached and detached
« Visibility: must be recognisable from a distance of
5m
« Anticipated sale price: $10.
Target audience
Frequent flyers, including professionals and holiday
makers.
Market analysis
« The luggage tag market is experiencing steady growth,
driven by several key factors:
« Increasing domestic and international travel continues
to grow
« Travellers are increasingly aware of security, mistaken
identification and lost luggage issues
+ Cost /benefit is significant
« Environmental concerns drive the need for ‘green’
products
« Personalisation continues to be popular
« Price concerns for premium products
« Imitation or copy products dilute the market share.
Timing
« Initial concept designs, sketched renderings
4 weeks
« Final design, three-dimensional virtual design and
physical mockup 2 weeks
« Functional prototype 2 weeks
« Production-ready files in digital form 1 week
Legislative guidelines and standards
« There are no legislative guidelines
« Airlines provide generic paper luggage tags containing
specific barcodes and tracking information.
Scale of production
The final design should be production-ready for mass
production of the initial run of 5,000 pieces.

B2.1.9
Specifications encompass measurable product criteria,
descriptions of the requirements needed to successfully
meet the needs of the design brief, which is heavily based
on user needs data. Specifications cover everything from
dimensions and materials to aesthetic considerations and
functionality. Specifications provide clarity of purpose
by providing measurable outcomes. They ensure quality
control is maintained throughout the design process.
Budgetary constraints are clearly stated to avoid costly
overruns, and compliance with legislative and safety
requirements avoids costly certification problems.
Sample specifications for a range of Apple products are
available at the QR code shown in Figure B2.1.9
The specifications detail the style, scope and quantity of
specifications required to completely define a product.

B2.1.10
he idea generation phase is pivotal in the search for
alternative solutions within the design process. The
creative approach begins with the specification as a
foundational reference. Documentation of creative ideas
is enhanced through sketches accompanied by detailed
annotations. All creative alternatives must be thoroughly
evaluated during this stage to identify the most promising
solution,
Atthisjuncture, designers focus on conceptual frameworks
rather than fully developed solutions. They draw
inspiration from a diverse array of sources and employ
various methodologies to facilitate idea generation. These
methodologies are often categorised under the umbrella
of cognitive organisers. Notable examples of creativity
tools include:
TRIZ: A Russian acronym for the Theory of Inventive
Problem Solving. It is built on two simple principles:
You don't have to reinvent the wheel; solutions may lie
in identifying and adapting existing solutions. TRIZ sees
conflicts as creative opportunities for innovative outcomes
and combines problem-solving with analysis gleaned from
trends in global patent literature.
SCAMPER: Designed as a strategy to encourage
structured problem solving. When solving a problem, a
chosen perspective is used to help generate creative ideas
or to iterate on an existing approach. The letters form an
acronym for a list of tasks from which to select.
« Substitute (analogy), replace the existing component
with another of a similar nature.
« Combine (convergence) adds parts together.
« Adjust identifies ways to modify the product.
« Magnify, minify changes in size to features to rearrange
focus or primary functionality.
« Put to other uses (generate/divergence/connect)
identifies other potential contexts for use, which may
in turn suggest changes.
« Eliminate, remove parts, components and features that
may be redundant or superfluous.
92
« Reverse, invert, turn, mirror or otherwise change the
orientation of parts.
Mindmapping: As a graphic organiser, mind maps can be
created in various formats, including freehand sketches or
computer-generated diagrams. Regardless of the format,
mind maps are fundamentally tree-structured, connecting
a central idea or topic to its associated branches or
subtopics.
rainstorming: Often employed as a collaborative activity,
group participants are encouraged to generate multiple
responses to the problem at hand, prioritising quantity
over quality. During this process, no idea is subject to
criticism or dismissal. The accumulation of contributions
often stimulates the emergence of new ideas, inspired by
the suggestions of others within the group.
Six Thinking Hats: Developed by Edward De Bono, this
approach uses coloured hats as guides for thinking and
focuses the problem-solving approach around a specific
perspective.
By combining the six modes of thinking, sequences may
be created to structure the thinking process towards a
specific goal.
orphological analysis: The tool employed here is often
referred to as a ‘Zwicky box), used to visually organise new
ideas against a predetermined set of parameters, allowing
for examination of combinations of features that may
previously not have been considered.

B2.1.12
The model, test, refine cycle (Plan-Do-Study-Act or PDSA cycle) is a continuous iterative improvement process. It incorporates the following stages:
-Modelling (planning) 
-Testing iterations (doing)
-Refining for improvement (acting)
-Repeating the process where necessary
The PDSA cycle has another element: studying and
analysing test results, which is inferred in the refining
stage of the model, test and refine cycle.
While Design Thinking focuses more heavily on the initial ‘ideation’ phase of empathising with users and defining problems, Agile is an iterative technique that uses a set of principles and values that guide product design and development through repeated cycles of planning, execution and feedback.
By breaking projects into smaller ‘sprints’ or increments,
Agile allows teams to adapt to change, incorporate
customer feedback and continuously improve the product
throughout its lifecycle, rather than relying on a single, A
linear process.
Ideation insights are significantly enhanced by the adoption
of Agile processes due to the inherent adaptability and
