flexibility these techniques provide. Agile’s iterative and
incremental approach to development allows for rapid
cycles of feedback and refinement, which directly benefits
the ideation phase.
Although Agile originated in the software industry, its
iterative, flexible and adaptive framework is now widely
applied across various fields, including marketing, business
development and other non-software projects that benefit
from quick feedback and continuous improvement.
Kaizen is a fundamental part of the Agile methodology, representing the core philosophy of continuous improvement that aligns with Agile principles like iterative development, inspecting and adapting and team empowerment. While Kaizen is a broader philosophy from lean manufacturing, its application in Agile specifically involves incorporating small, incremental changes to processes and products to enhance value and productivity.
The PDSA (Plan-Do-Study-Act) cycleisatool that supports the Kaizen philosophy of continuous improvement and is also compatible with the Agile mindset, particularly through its iterative and experimental nature, but it is not a direct example of Kaizen or Agile itself 
Kaizen is the overarching philosophy of making gradual, ongoing improvements, and PDSA provides the structure method for testing those changes. Agile is a broader
development and project management philosophy that embraces change and iterative development, which PDSA cycles can be used to implement within.

B2.1.13
Low-fidelity (lo-fi) modelling is all about speed, flexibility
and cost-effectiveness. It is a technique adopted for early-
stage prototyping when testing form, function or user
interaction without committing to final materials. A
breakdown of commonly used materials across categories
follows.
Examples of basic structural materials that are ideal
for shaping form and testing ergonomics or spatial
relationships.
Cardboard is frequently selected for the rapid creation of
design prototypes due to its versatility and accessibility.
Various types of recycled cardboard, ranging from cereal
boxes to corrugated cartons, are readily available.
The finished model may be assessed based on such criteria
as:
Aesthetics: overall proportions, appearance
« Functionality: size of hole for bird entry, interior
capacity
« Construction: allowances for material thickness, fit
and finish, manipulation—cutting, folding, joining,
and bending—and can be
» Assembly using a range of techniques.
igh-fidelity prototyping is employed when precision,
realism and stakeholder engagement are critical. These
models are powerful tools used to evaluate product performance with end users. They can be used to generate meaningful performance data due to the fact that they
closely resemble the final product in terms of appearance, functionality and interactivity. They may also include
fully functional interfaces, realistic visuals and near-final
hardware or software components.
Depending on the product, end-users may be employed to generate data based on:
Task completion rates
Time-on-task
Tracking number and location of error rates
user satisfaction survey
Interaction logs that record button presses, menu
navigation paths and feature usage frequency.
« Eye-tracking (if applicable) to show where users focus
their attention
+ Comfort and/ fit ratings
« Control accessibility and functional intuitiveness
« Aspects of aesthetics, including appearance, colour
and textures.
High-fidelity prototyping plays a pivotal role in bridging
conceptual design with tangible realisation, offering a suite
of strategic advantages across the product development
lifecycle.
Foremost, the creation of realistic and visually
refined prototypes significantly enhances stakeholder
engagement. By presenting design concepts in a form that
closely mirrors the final product—both functionally and
aesthetically—teams can facilitate clearer communication,
reduce ambiguity and accelerate decision-making
processes. This fidelity fosters trust and alignment among
cross-functional stakeholders, enabling more confident
approvals and investment in subsequent development
phases.
Equally critical is the role of high-fidelity prototypes in
usability testing. These models allow users to interact
with the product in a contextually authentic manner,
yielding insights that are both nuanced and actionable.
The tactile and visual realism support the identification
of ergonomic, cognitive and experiential issues that may
not surface in low-fidelity or abstract representations. As
such, designers can iterate with precision, grounded in
empirical user feedback.
Finally, high-fidelity prototypes serve as a robust
conduit for developer handoff. The inclusion of precise
specifications, detailed visual assets and functional cues
ensures that engineering teams can translate design intent
into manufacturable solutions with minimal interpretive
loss. This alignment between design and development
mitigates risk, reduces rework and supports a more
efficient transition from prototype to production.
A review of materials and techniques commonly employed
is shown below.

B2.1.14
For a new product design, physical details must be
communicated accurately before a product can be
successfully manufactured. Detailed drawings must be
generated communicating a comprehensive range of
information, including dimensions and tolerancing,
scales, surface finishes, assembly details, contextual notes,
adherence to standards and engineering drawing practices,
materials selections and manufacturing notes.
The Australian Technical
Drawing Standards,
particularly the AS 1100 series, are foundational to
engineering, architecture, and design practice across
Australia. They serve as a shared visual language that
ensures clarity, precision and consistency in technical
communication.
‘The Australian Drawing Standard is just one document,
like many others worldwide, that provides:
« Uniformity across disciplines through the
establishment of a common framework for engineers,
architects, designers and manufacturers. It reduces
ambiguity and misinterpretation in drawings,
especially in multidisciplinary projects.
« Legal and contractual relevance, even though adoption
is voluntary in principle. These standards become
legally binding when referenced in contracts or
regulatory frameworks.
« Compliance and accountability in professional
documentation.
« Streamlined workflows by standardising symbols,
layout conventions, and notation.
« Enhanced interoperability between CAD systems and
manual drafting practices.
« Promotion of safe design practices by ensuring accurate
representation of components and assemblies.
« Support for quality assurance in manufacturing and
construction.
Standards such as these are periodically reviewed and
updated to reflect technological advancements and
industry needs.
Why are drawing standards important to designers?
For designers with expertise in materials science, visual
communication and user-centred design, adhering to
standards such as AS 1100 ensures that technical visuals
are not only accurate and professional but also sound
and inclusive. It's a way of embedding methodological
integrity into every drawing, whether its a chart,
schematic, dimensioned sectional view or a storyboard
for sustainable design.
The following engineering drawings of a bridge design
show adherence to the drawing standard AS1100. These
engineering design drawings show a proposed solution to
an engineering problem.

B2.1.15
Visual representations
When designing and constructing the iconic Sydney
Opera House, the problem of creating the shells was
famously solved by Danish architect Jorn Utzon by
taking sections from a sphere. This ensured that the
components making up the shape of each segment could
be accurately communicated and constructed off-site for
later erection.
Jern Utzon's pivotal insight into the geometry of the
Sydney Opera House's roof shells came after years
of grappling with their complex form. Rather than
continuing to experiment with disparate curves and
irregular surfaces, he conceived a solution rooted in a
single, unifying geometry: the sphere. By extracting each
shell segment from the same spherical volume, Utzon
resolved both aesthetic and engineering challenges. This
approach allowed for consistent curvature across all roof
elements, enabling modular construction and simplifying
the fabrication of precast components. The elegance of the
solution lay not only in its mathematical clarity but in its
capacity to harmonise form, function and feasibility.
What distinguished Utzon’s approach was his ability
to convey this geometric concept with clarity and
conviction. He produced physical models and visual
diagrams that demonstrated how each shell could be
derived from the same spherical source, making the idea
tangible for engineers, builders and stakeholders. These
representations bridged the gap between architectural
vision and technical execution, fostering collaboration
and trust. Utzon's method exemplified how abstract
geometry, when communicated effectively, can become a
practical tool for innovation—transforming a sculptural
ambition into a buildable reality.
Virtual building
The Guggenheim Museum of Modern and Contemporary
Art in Bilbao, Spain, is one of the first examples of CAD
using virtual design and construction tools to design, test
and review an innovative and impactful design solution in
architecture.
Designed by Canadian-American architect Frank Gehry
and opened in 1997, the museum was the building most
frequently named as one of the most important works
completed since 1980 in the 2010 World Architecture
Survey among architecture experts.
In Figure B2.1.15a, Frank Gehrys design philosophy
is shown in initial sketches of the building form and is
explored through a series of design concept sketches.
These drawings show the building in a site context and
explore the relationships between the various components
and how they interact. This approach provides a holistic
overview of the project and bears a striking resemblance
to the finished structure.
The building exterior consists of multiple random,
intersecting curved faces designed to catch the light. The
buildings’ interconnecting shapes integrate limestone,
glass and titanium on a galvanised steel structure.
In 1993, architects at Gehry Partners began to use Dassault
Systéme’s CATIA software for the schematic design phase
of the museum to digitise and model the exterior of the
museum project. The exterior skin of the building was
made from 33,000 titanium plates or ‘scales. Special
laminated panels only 0.4mm thick were created to
establish the greatest strength, stability, heat and weather
resistance. Using titanium meant that stronger, thinner
and lighter panels were used.
The virtual design modelling and the methods used to
cut the complex shapes were a pioneering approach.
However, the true power of computer-aided design in
this instance was leveraged to calculate point by point the
stresses to which materials were subjected. Generating a
three-dimensional model showing the different tensions
in play allowed the values of many structural elements of
the museum to be calculated, These elements included the
steel structure, thousands of unique titanium cladding
panels, glass and foundations. It also helped to facilitate
and automate the cutting of these materials.
The processes here, employed by the Gehry team, were
later to be known as Building Information Modelling
or BIM, which is defined by the US National Building
Information Model Standard Project Committee as:
“a digital representation of the physical and functional
characteristics of a facility. BIM is a shared knowledge
resource for information about a facility, forming a reliable
basis for decisions during its life-cycle; defined as existing
from earliest conception to demolition.”
Previously, building design was largely reliant upon two-
dimensional engineering drawings (plans, elevations,
sections, etc.). BIM transcends traditional drawing
methods and information by incorporating data relating to
project timing, budgets, asset management, sustainability,
manufacturers’ and suppliers’ details, etc. Being of digital
form, BIM is more than just geometry; it enables. a wide
range of collaborative processes relating to the built asset
from initial planning to construction and throughout its
operational life.
Such is the power of this building that it was the
centrepiece of a revitalisation effort for the city. The hope
was to attract tourists and stimulate the local economy.
The overwhelming success of the building and its ability
to attract tourists and international interest has coined the
term ‘Bilbao Effect’

B2.2.1
‘There are many different drawing styles, each with its own
unique characteristics, purpose and application.
Isometric drawing may appear as a quick sketch to
convey a design concept for sharing, feedback and further
development, or it may be in a more technical form, drawn
with instruments to express greater detail and accuracy in
scale, proportions and dimensions. The advantage of this
style is that it provides a sense of realism, allowing the
viewer to see three sides of the object simultaneously. It is
particularly suitable for presentation drawings, especially
for audiences with limited technical knowledge.
Figure B2.2.1a shows an early isometric sketch of the
winning competition entry for the Disseldorf Olympic
Village, Kathy
Orthographic drawings present several two-dimensional
views of an object. Often referred to as orthographic
projection, the name is derived from the fact that each view
is projected from another to create a three-dimensional
relationship ~ between two-dimensional — drawings.
Manufacturers and engineers regularly specify these
drawings to provide precise dimensions and technical
information.,
A single view of an object is sometimes all that is required
for a complete visual explanation. When dimensions,
material and other information are included, an object
requiring only a single view is easy to understand.
Most one-view drawings depict flat objects, typically made
from materials such as sheet metal or plastic. Spherical
objects would require only one view and a note indicating
the material and diameter of the sphere.
Figure B 2.2.1b shows a single-view orthographic drawing.
Only one viewis required, given that the part has a uniform
thickness. Figure B2.2.1c shows a three-view orthographic
projection that maintains the relationship between the
views by the mechanism of ‘projection.
Orthographic projection is a name given to drawings that
usually have three views. Often, the three views selected
are the top, front and right side. It is possible, when
required, to select other views, such as the left side or
bottom. Generally, though, it’s the top, front and right side
that are traditionally seen by the person reading prints.
Another advantage of orthographic projection is that it
allows the person reading the print to ‘see’ the inside or
surfaces of an object which normally could not be seen.
Engineering drawings include orthographic drawings,
assembly drawings, exploded drawings, detail drawings
and more, These provide materials details, assembly
instructions, surface treatments, manufacturing standards
and tolerances information needed for manufacturing or
construction.
Exploded drawings show the assembly of individual
components in detail. The individual parts of the
component are separated but located along ‘axes of
assembly’ to maintain the spatial relationships between
parts. One of the first examples of an exploded view was
created by Leonardo da Vinci in his design drawing of a
reciprocating motion machine, circa 1478-1480, shown in
Figure B 2.2.1d.
igure B 2.2.1e shows Patent drawing figures from U.S.
Patent 4,909,523 ‘In-line roller skate with frame! This
patent, filed in 1987, described key innovations in the 1988
Rollerblade Lightning inline skates, made visible through
the use of an exploded isometric drawing, Through the
use of a half-sectional view, the half-sectional internal
details, otherwise obscured, may be seen.
Figure B2.2.1e Exploded drawing of an inline skate
bearing assembly. Image by Brennan J. Olson, Public
domain, via Wikimedia Commons
Assembly Drawings are used to illustrate how different
components come together into a functional system.
Individual parts are distributed along axes of assembly,
indicating both the sequence of assembly. These drawings
are crucial for manufacturers and technicians working
on complex machinery. Fasteners, joints and attachment
methodsare clearly shown. A bill of materials (BoM) listing
all parts, quantities and materials is often provided.
The three-dimensional (isometric) illustration shown
in Figure B 2.2.1f identifies the mating relationships of
parts, subassemblies and higher assemblies. It shows the
sequence of assembling or disassembling the individual parts
Perspective rendering is a more artistic representation
that shows objects as they appear to the human eye,
using vanishing points and depth perception. Architects
and designers use it to convey realistic visualisations
of buildings and products. This allows less tech-savvy
clients a more easily interpreted visualisation of the final
product.
Common mistakes across all drawing types
« Incorrect scaling: Misjudging dimensions or
proportions can result in drawings that don’t accurately
represent the real-world object.
+ Unclear linework: Using inconsistent line weights
or failing to distinguish between solid, hidden and
centerlines can confuse viewers.
« Insufficient or incorrect dimensions: Omitting
critical measurements or using inconsistent units leads
to fabrication errors.
« Poor annotation: Using vague notes or failing to follow
standard conventions makes interpreting the drawing
difficult.
« Overcrowded or poorly organised layouts: Too much
information crammed together reduces readability.
« Ignoring industry standards: Not following ISO,
ANSI, or other technical guidelines can make the
drawing unusable in professional settings

B2.2.2
Levels of fidelity in prototyping
Fidelity refers to how closely a prototype matches the final
product in terms of appearance, function and detail.
Low-fidelity prototypes are quick, simple and often made
from inexpensive materials (e.g., cardboard, foam, paper).
They're used to explore ideas and test basic concepts.
Medium-fidelity prototypes have more accurate shapes,
proportions and some working features, but may still use
substitute materials.
High-fidelity prototypes look and function almost exactly
like the final product, often using the same materials,
finishes, and manufacturing processes.
Figure B2.2.2a Iterative prototype development, image by
Dyson Australia (modified)
Aesthetic vs Functional Prototypes
Aesthetic prototypes focus on how the product looks and
feels; its form, colour, texture and visual proportions. They
help stakeholders judge whether the design is appealing
and aligned with brand or user expectations.
Functional prototypes focus on how the product works;
its mechanical performance, ergonomics and usability.
They test whether the design can perform its intended
tasks reliably.
In practice, a prototype can be purely aesthetic, purely
functional or a hybrid that balances both.
Constructing prototypes at different levels of
Fidelity
Low-Fidelity
Aesthetic: Simple foam blocks, paper mock-ups or
3D-printed shells to explore shape and proportion.
Functional: Rough mechanical rigs or breadboard
electronics to test movement, mechanisms or basic
interaction.
Advantages: Fast, cheap and
experimentation.
encourages
Limitations: Limited realism — can't fully judge fine
details or final performance.
Medium-Fidelity
Aesthetic: More refined models with accurate proportions,
basic surface finishes and closer colour matching.
Functional: Working parts made from substitute materials
(e.g., acrylic instead of glass) to test ergonomics and
usability.
Advantages: Good balance between cost and realism;
useful for user testing.
Limitations: May still differ from the final product in
weight, feel or durability.
High-Fidelity
Aesthetic: Fully finished surfaces, accurate colours,
textures and branding elements.
Functional: Uses final materials and manufacturing
methods; performs exactly as intended.
Advantages: Closest to the real product; ideal for final
validation and stakeholder approval.
Limitations: Expensive and time-consuming; changes are
harder to make.
Considerations of scale, shape, and space
Scale
Scale affects how users perceive and interact with the
prototype. A scaled-down model can show form but may
not reveal usability issues like reach, grip or weight.
Low-fidelity models may be scaled down to save time and
materials.
High-fidelity prototypes are usually full-scale to test
ergonomics and fit.
Shape
Shape communicates function and brand identity. Even in
low-fidelity form, shape can spark valuable feedback on
recognisability and appeal.
Early prototypes may exaggerate or simplify shapes to test
ideas.
Later prototypes refine curves, edges and proportions for
aesthetics and performance.
Space
Space refers to how the product fits into its environment
and interacts with surrounding objects or users. Context
is always important; a product that works perfectly in
isolation may fail in a cluttered or constrained space.
Mock-ups can be placed in real or simulated environments
to check clearance, visibility and accessibility.
Functional prototypes can test movement paths, storage
fit or user flow.
Steps in reviewing a prototype
Match expectations to fidelity, i.e. a cardboard mock-up
will not feel like a finished aluminium part.
Focussed feedback, atlow fidelity, concept and proportions
are important, while high fidelity prototypes are used to
assess fine details and performance.
Consider form vs function trade-offs, i.e. a beautiful
aesthetic model may hide functional flaws, and a purely
functional prototype may not inspire user engagement or
excitement.

B2.2.3
Computer-Aided Design (CAD) has not only become the
‘go-to’ drafting tool for designers but has also evolved into
an integrated environment for ideation, refinement, and
validation. The advantages of CAD models extend across
the entire design lifecycle, from conceptual exploration to
manufacturing implementation, offering both technical
precision and strategic value.
In its simplest form, CAD ensures dimensional accuracy
and geometric precision. Unlike manual drafting, where
errors can propagate through iterations, CAD models are
parametric and constraint-driven, allowing designers to
embed rules and relationships directly into the geometry.
This ensures consistency across assemblies and enables
rapid recalibration when design parameters change.
Such precision is particularly critical in industries where
tolerances are tight and compliance with standards is
non-negotiable.
The efficiency and speed afforded by CAD allow for
the generation, modification and replication of design
variations. It accelerates the iterative process, enabling
designers to explore a broader range of solutions within
compressed timeframes. Automated features such as
mirroring, patterning and scaling reduce repetitive
tasks. This efficiency translates directly into reduced
development costs and shorter time-to-market.
CADalsofacilitates progressive refinementof designintent.
Early conceptual sketches can be rapidly transformed into
three-dimensional models, which are then developed
into detailed assemblies with manufacturable features.
The parametric nature of CAD allows for iterative
improvement, where modifications to one feature cascade
through the model, maintaining coherence across the
design. This iterative flexibility is essential in collaborative
environments where feedback loops are frequent and
multidirectional.
Another critical advantage lies in visualisation and
communication. CAD models can be rendered
photorealistically, animated to demonstrate motion, or
exploded to reveal assembly logic. These outputs bridge
the gap between technical specialists and non-technical
stakeholders, enabling clearer communication of design
intent. In contexts such as client presentations, funding
pitches, or interdisciplinary collaboration, the ability to
convey both aesthetic qualities and functional principles
is invaluable.
Beyond representation, CAD models serve as the
foundation for analysis and simulation. Integrated tools
for Finite Element Analysis (FEA), motion studies,
and thermal or fluid simulations allow designers to test
performance virtually, identifying weaknesses before
physical prototyping. This predictive capability reduces
reliance on costly trial-and-error methods and supports
evidence-based decision making. By embedding analysis
within the design environment, CAD fosters a more
holistic approach to engineering, where form and function
are evaluated concurrently.
The transition from design to production is also
streamlined through CAD’s manufacturing integration.
Models can be directly translated into technical drawings,
CNC toolpaths, or 3D printing instructions, ensuring
fidelity between digital intent and physical output. This
digital continuity minimises translation errors, enhances
reproducibility and supports advanced manufacturing
strategies such as mass customisation and distributed
production.
Finally, CAD models underpin collaboration and
knowledge management. Digital files can be shared
across global teams, version-controlled, and integrated
into Product Lifecycle Management (PLM) systems.
This facilitates concurrent engineering, where multiple
disciplines contribute simultaneously, and ensures that
design data remains accessible and traceable throughout
the products lifecycle.
Surface Models: A surface model represents only the
outer faces of an object, its ‘skin, without any information
110
about what is inside. It’s essentially a hollow shell. It is
analogous to spray-painting an object, the paint layer
being the surface model.
Curves and mathematical formulas define surfaces, which
are then joined to form a continuous outer boundary.
These models are used for complex, free-form shapes
like car bodies, aircraft fuselages or consumer product
housings. They prove useful when you need to sculpt or
adjust individual faces without affecting the rest of the
model.
These models are limited due to their lack of volume or
mass properties (no calculations may be made to directly
obtain details surrounding weight or the centre of gravity).
Care must be taken to seal the model, ensuring a
continuous outer boundary.
The Scan2CAD article explores the concept of surface
modelling in CAD, a technique used to create complex,
free-form shapes that are difficult to achieve with solid
modelling. It outlines how surface modelling differs
from wireframe and solid approaches by focusing on the
external contours of objects rather than their internal
volume,
The guide introduces key modelling techniques such as
NURBS (Non-Uniform Rational B-Splines), polygon
meshes and subdivision surfaces, and explains their
relevance in industries like automotive design, aerospace
and medical imaging, It also highlights common tools used
in surface modelling—like lofting, sweeping, trimming
and stitching—and discusses how these tools are applied
in popular CAD software, including Rhino, SolidWorks,
and Fusion 360.
Finally, the article offers practical advice for designers,
such as combining surface and solid modelling when
appropriate, keeping geometry manageable, and
understanding the limitations of converting surface
models into solids. These insights help professionals
create accurate, manufacturable designs for advanced
applications.
Solid Models
Built from closed geometry, often using parametric
features like extrusions, revolves and sweeps. A solid model contains both the surfaces and the enclosed volume,
i.e. the software ‘knows’ what's inside and outside.
These models are most commonly used for engineering
and manufacturing because of their ability to calculate
volume, mass, centre of gravity, and simulate machining
or assembly. Solid models can easily generate orthographic
views, section views and detailed drawings.
These models, however, are less flexible for extreme
free-form sculpting than surface modelling.
These models are analogous to a real block of wood that
has an outer surface, but also a solid interior with
measurable weight.
‘The ALI3DP article on solid modelling in CAD introduces
solid modelling as a key technique in computer-aided
design used to create 3D objects with defined volume and
structure. It explains how solid models differ from surface
and wireframe models by offering complete geometric
and physical data, making them ideal for simulations,
manufacturing and rapid prototyping.
The article outlines common solid modelling methods
such as constructive solid geometry (CSG), boundary
representation (B-rep), and parametric modelling. It
also discusses practical applications in industries like
engineering and product design, and highlights how
solid models support processes like 3D printing and CNC
machining.
Virtual Models
Virtual modelling is a broad term for any CAD model
that exists only in the digital environment, whether
surface, solid or a hybrid, and is used to represent a real
or proposed object. *Virtual’ emphasises that the model is
not physical yet, but can be analysed, tested and refined as
if it were.
Virtual models act as a digital prototype for visualisation,
simulation and collaboration. They can integrate with
CAE (Computer-Aided Engineering) for stress analysis,
thermal studies or motion simulation and have the facility
to link to CAM (Computer-Aided Manufacturing) for
CNC machining or 3D printing.
Significant advantages are accrued from this type of
modelling approach in that changes may be made quickly,
MobELLING AND PROTOTYPING
files are easy to share and facilitate collaboration. There
are no costs associated with materials, and a variety of
presentation formats may be selected, depending on the
target audience’s technical knowledge or needs.
Virtual models are analogous to a video game’s 3D model
of a car, i.e. it's not real, but it behaves and looks like the
real thing in the virtual world.

B2.2.4
Interpreting FEA results
‘When evaluating FEA models, the following considerations
should be taken into account.
Check for Yielding: Ensure that yielding is not present
or is within acceptable limits. If yielding occurs, assess if
the plastic strains are acceptable. In the context of Finite
Element Analysis (FEA), yielding refers to the point at
which a material stops behaving purely elastically and
begins to deform plastically — meaning the deformation
will remain even after the load is removed. In FEA, you
often check for yielding to ensure your design stays within
safe limits. If the calculated stress in your model exceeds
the material’s yield strength, the software will indicate that
yielding is likely occurring in that region. Yielding doesn’t
always mean failure; in some designs (like crash zones in
cars), controlled yielding is intentional to absorb energy.
However, in most structural or mechanical components,
yielding is a warning sign that the part may no longer
perform as intended.
Identify high-stress regions and compare them to the
material’s yield strength.
Check plastic strain values if your analysis includes
nonlinear material behaviour.
Decide if yielding is acceptable for the design’s purpose
— if not, adjust geometry, material or loading.
Evaluate stability: Verify if there are any stability issues,
such as buckling, which can affect the integrity of the
structure.
Compare to allowable limits: Compare the calculated
stresses and displacements to allowable limits to ensure
compliance with design criteria.
Figure B2.2.4 shows the contour plot of stresses from a
linear dynamic analysis. A mobile case is dropped from
2m to plot the stresses due to impact. The FEM simulation
was performed using the SimScale FEM software.

B2.2.5
Constructing and interpreting
CAD (Computer-Aided Design)
Modelling for rapid prototyping involves a structured
workflow that ensures the digital design is accurate,
manufacturable, and optimised for speed and efficiency. A
breakdown of the key steps follows.
Steps in constructing CAD models for rapid
prototyping
Define objectives and constraints
« Identify the purpose of the prototype (e.g., functional
testing, visual presentation).
« Set design constraints and specifications such as size,
material, tolerance and manufacturing method (e.g.,
3D printing, CNC).
Sketch initial geometry
« Use 2D sketches to outline basic shapes and profiles.
« Apply geometric constraints (e.g, symmetry,
parallelism) to maintain design intent.
Build 3D features
« Extrude, revolve, loft or sweep sketches to create solid
bodies.
« Use parametric modelling to allow easy updates and
iterations.
Assemble components (if applicable)
« Combine multiple parts into an assembly.
« Define relationships like mates, joints, and motion
constraints.
Apply materials and properties
« Assign material types to simulate weight, strength and
thermal behaviour.
« Include surface finishes or textures if needed for
appearance or performance.
Check manufacturability
« Ensure the model meets the requirements of the
chosen prototyping method.
« Avoid overhangs, thin walls, or unsupported features
that may fail during printing or machining.
Export for prototyping
« Convert the model to a suitable format (e.g., STL for
3D printing, STEP for CNC).
« Verify resolution and mesh quality to avoid errors
during fabrication.
Testing CAD models for rapid prototyping
Geometric Validation
« Watertightness: Ensure the model is a closed solid
with no gaps or holes.
« Non-manifold geometry: Check for overlapping
surfaces or edges that could confuse slicing software.
Dimensional Accuracy
« Confirm that critical dimensions match design
specifications.
« Use measurement tools within the CAD software to
validate tolerances and fit.
Material and Wall Thickness Checks
« Ensure wall thickness meets minimum requirements
for the chosen prototyping method (e.g., 1.2 mm for
FDM).
» Avoid thin features that may warp or break during
printing.
Support and Overhang Analysis
« Identify areas that require support structures (e.g.,
overhangs > 45°).
« Optimise orientation to reduce the need for supports
and improve surface quality.
Simulation and Stress Testing (Optional)
« Run finite element analysis (FEA) to predict how the
prototype will respond to forces, heat or motion.
« Use results to refine geometry before fabrication.
Export and slicing compatibility
« Export the model in a suitable format (e.g., STL, OB]J,
STEP).
» Test the file in slicing software to ensure it loads
correctly and generates toolpaths without errors.

B2.2.6
In contemporary design practice, the strategic deployment
of prototypes serves as a cornerstone for iterative
refinement and stakeholder alignment. Prototypes are
constructed along a scale of fidelity and function, each
tailored to elicit specific forms of feedback and data from
distinct audiences. Key prototype types, the data they help
collect, and who they're designed for are shown below.
Data types required
Quantitative data includes task completion rates, error
frequency, time-on-task, force measurements and thermal
or electrical performance.
Qualitative data encompasses user impressions, emotional
reactions, perceived value and aesthetic preferences.
Technical data documents the gathering of
tolerances, material behaviours, stress/strain data and
manufacturability.
Behavioural data records interaction patterns, decision-
making flow and ergonomic comfort.
The type of prototype and data generated is often governed
by stakeholder needs and preferences.
The specific requirements of these groups are shown
below:
« End Users: To validate usability, comfort and
desirability.
« Clients/Stakeholders: To align expectations, aesthetics
and business goals.
« Engineers/Developers: To test feasibility, integration
and performance.
« Design Teams: For internal iteration, refinement and
concept evolution.
« Manufacturers: To assess production viability, tooling
requirements and tolerances.
At the earliest stages of the design process, conceptual
sketches and low-fidelity mockups are employed to
externalise abstract ideas and explore preliminary
configurations. These artifacts are typically non-
functional and serve as visual prompts for discussion. The
data gathered at this stage is largely qualitative, focusing
on user impressions, aesthetic preferences and conceptual
clarity. Such prototypes are primarily intended for internal
design teams and clients, enabling rapid iteration and
alignment on foundational principles before deeper
investment is made.
As the design matures, digital simulations and parametric
CAD models become instrumental. These prototypes
allow for virtual testing of form, fit and function, often
incorporating performance simulations such as stress
analysis or thermal behaviour. The data extracted here
is technical and quantitative, including dimensional
tolerances, material properties and system interactions.
Engineers, developers and manufacturing partners are
the primary audience, as these models inform feasibility
assessments and downstream production planning.
To bridge the gap between digital abstraction and physical
reality, 3D printed models and tactile mockups are
introduced. These physical prototypes facilitate ergonomic
testing, spatial validation and user interaction studies.
Feedback at this stage includes both quantitative metrics
(e.g., grip strength, reachability, assembly tolerances) and
qualitative insights (e.g., comfort, intuitive use). They are
often presented to end users, industrial designers and
usability experts to ensure the product resonates in real-
world contexts.
Further along the development pipeline, functional
prototypes—which may be partially or fully operational—
are deployed to assess performance under realistic
conditions. These prototypes yield critical data on
reliability, user behaviour and system integration. Beta
testers, engineers and clients engage with these models to
validate core functionality and uncover latent issues prior
to final production.
In parallel, visual prototypes and high-fidelity renderings
are crafted to communicate the intended aesthetic and
brand identity. These are typically non-functional but
polished, designed to evoke emotional responses and
gauge market appeal. Marketing teams, investors and
clients are the primary recipients, as these prototypes
support promotional efforts and strategic decision-
making.
Finally, in service-oriented or experience-driven domains, service blueprints and role-playing scenarios are utilised to map
user journeys and simulate interactions across multiple touchpoints. These prototypes generate behavioural and experiential
data, revealing pain points, emotional triggers and service gaps. UX researchers, ethnographers, and service designers rely
on these insights to optimise holistic user experiences.
Generative Al modelling
Generative design refers to the use of algorithms and
computational geometry to systematically explore a
range of creative solutions, developed around supplied
constraints and or needs. The software can iteratively
refine alternatives based on evolving defined parameters
and objectives. The possible design permutations are vast,
a task often not feasible for human designers. Care is
needed to expertly assess the proposed solutions that the
generative design offers to pursue the most appropriate
line of development.
Generative design uses different types of computer
thinking:
« Rule-Based Systems: These follow set rules to create
designs. For example, “windows must face north” or
“rooms must be square.”
« Optimisation & ”: These use trial-and-error techniques
like genetic algorithms (inspired by evolution!) to
improve designs step by step.
« Machine Learning: This includes advanced tools like
neural networks (CNNs, transformers, GANs) that
learn from data and can generate new ideas based on
patterns.
Figure B 2.2.6a shows a piece from the Samba furniture
collection. The piece was created by Guto Requena with
generative design. The rhythm and melody of traditional
Brazilian music were used as an influence to shape each of
the pieces in the collection.
Two different classic samba songs were chosen. From each
one, parameters such as vocals, bass, treble and medium
were extracted. Curves were then generated, growing in
real-time, following the music. A CNC machine sculpted
the digital file in Carrara marble.
The final result is an organic and smooth surface, the
literal visualisation of Samba songs.
Generative design in architectural design
The design phaseisacritical foundation in the development
of any architectural or construction project. It involves a
range of sophisticated tasks, such as planning site layouts,
configuring interior spaces and shaping exterior forms.
All of these areas must balance the demands of numerous
constraints and preferences. These challenges often require
significant time and cognitive effort from designers.
To address the demand for greater efficiency, researchers
have increasingly focused on automating aspects of the
design process. Generative design, using computational
algorithms to model and explore a wide array of design
possibilities, by systematically generating and evaluating
alternatives based on defined goals and parameters, is
one such approach. Generative architectural design offers
a powerful framework for navigating complex design
problems and enhancing decision-making.
Generative design is useful in architecture and building
design for:
« Site Layouts: Help arrange buildings and spaces for the
best use of land and sunlight.
« Interior Design: Suggests room layouts that are comfy
and practical.
« Exterior Design: Creates building shapes that look
amazing and perform well (like saving energy or
resisting wind).
Because computers can explore so many options so
quickly, generative design helps designers make better
decisions.
The link below leads directly to an article on generative
design modelling in architecture.
Generative design in product design
In 2019, Philippe Starck, Kartell and Autodesk Research
created the first-ever production chair using generative
design.
Starck played a pivotal role in shaping the conceptual
direction and defining the design constraints for this
project. These parameters were then processed by a
generative design algorithm, which produced a range of
viable solutions tailored to Kartell's injection-moulding
production capabilities.
Starck emphasised the limitations of human creativity,
suggesting that even the most imaginative minds are
bound by inherent cognitive and cultural frameworks.
He argued that artificial intelligence introduces a
transformative potential, one that transcends the
boundaries of human design thinking. The resulting A.I.-
generated chair, in his view, marks the onset of a new era
in design freedom, offering possibilities that traditional
human-led innovation can no longer achieve.

C2.1.1
The road to sustainable use of the planet’s resources is
complex, with many interrelated interests involved, and
strategies to achieve such a goal are still developing. Two
commonly discussed strategies taken from the world of
economics are top-down and bottom-up methodologies.
Top-down strategies, which are often implemented
to address global sustainability issues, typically align
with the principles established by the Brundtland
Commission. These strategies prioritise the needs of the
present generation without compromising the ability of
future generations to meet their own needs. Achieving
this goal necessitates comprehensive evaluations of
resource availability, depletion rates, energy consumption,
pollution, waste management and market patterns to
develop long-term sustainability solutions.
Non-renewable resources must be managed judiciously
and substituted with renewable alternatives. Consequently,
the top-down approach focuses on setting sustainability
targets and implementing measures to achieve them.
However, criticisms of this strategy highlight the
absence of clear methodologies for achieving these goals and ambiguities regarding the responsibilities for
implementation and enforcement.
Bottom-up strategies, which are implemented at regional
or local levels, exemplify initiatives such as the Think
Globally, Act Locally campaign. These strategies encourage
local action by temporarily shifting focus away from the
broader scale of the problem to emphasise the impact
of local changes. For instance, the Clean-up Australia
Campaign mobilises local schools and organisations,
including Lions and Rotary clubs, to undertake clean-
up efforts, preserving local bushland and fostering
community pride. Similarly, Landcare initiatives involve
planting trees and grasses to stabilise coastal dunes against
erosion. These grassroots efforts are pivotal in promoting
environmental stewardship and sustainable practices
within communities.
Importance of Decision Making in Sustainable Design
Designers employ a combination of decision-making
methodstoensure that user needs are metand sustainability
outcomes are achieved. These may include:
« Engaging with stakeholders such as consumers,
manufacturers and policy makers and actively
encouraging feedback throughout the design cycle
« Balancing inevitable conflicts and compromises
between cost, functionality and sustainability
« Incorporating long-term considerations into
environmental benefits and financial investment when
transitioning to sustainable practice
« Adopting holistic life-cycle considerations when
assessing environmental impacts.
Incorporating user feedback can promote more sustainable
and practical designs.

C2.1.2
In 2001, Edwin Datschefski published his book The Total
Beauty of Sustainable Products, in which he outlined
five parameters or principles by which he maintained
design should be judged to determine its sustainability
credentials. He developed these principles in response to
the rise in ‘greenwashing’ Greenwashing is promoting
products with minor environmental or green credentials
as sustainable. He felt consumers were being misled by
advertising and media use in general, and subsequently
developed five principles of sustainable design. These
principles are outlined below:
Cyelic: Products should be part of a recycling system,
through natural processes such as composting or recycling
as part of the material stream.
Solar: Energy required to operate the manufacturing and
product use should come from renewable energy sources
in any form.
Safe: Products should not contain hazardous materials or
produce hazardous by-products in their creation.
Efficient: Designs should aim for efficiency of material
choice and operation to use one-tenth of the materials
and energy previously used. Designs that provide multiple
functions reduce the impact of several devices.
Social: Production should incorporate safe work practices
and conditions, and observe fair trade principles that
support human rights and community development.
Using these principles, Datschefski showed how new
designs could be assessed for sustainability using currently
accepted product designs as a benchmark. A selection of
products follows in Figure C2.1.2a to Figure C2.1.2, which
are provided to illustrate Datschefski’s sustainability rating
scale,
Datschefski, a sustainable design consultant, has proposed
a 100-cubed project where 100% of products become
100% sustainable by the year 2100.

C2.1.3
The term ‘triple bottom line’ (TBL) is attributed to John
Elkington (1995) and identifies the need to consider the
economic, environmental and social regions of human activity together to attain sustainability.
The TBL approach forces organisations to deal with environmental and societal issues, not just the economics of profit-making. It has been popularised through several catchphrases such af ‘economy, environment, equity, or "Planet, people, profit"
The interlinked relationship between financial sustainability, social responsibility and environmental sustainability is shown graphically in Figure C2.1.3. The overlapping portion of the diagram represents the concept
of triple bottom line sustainability.
Each of the components is described below.
Environmental sustainability involves maintaining
the integrity of an ecosystem by assessing and working
within its capacity, while at the same time recognising and
respecting biodiversity.
Social sustainability includes empowering the local
population to maintain cultural identities, implementing
equitable principles and practices and creating a stable
environment.
Financial sustainability incorporates
development principles through responsible growth while
improving productivity and efficiency.
Conflict
Conflicts often arise from the need to maximise
shareholder returns and the costs of transitioning to
sustainable practice. These costs may be attributed to
changing materials manufacturing techniques and the
need for new plant and equipment.
Similar conflicts exist between meeting social needs
through such programs as ‘Fair Trade, increasing
operational costs and potentially affecting profit margins.
Conflicts between the environment and economic
components of the program stem from the costs associated
with changes surrounding the meeting of environmental
legislation and pressure from special interest or consumer
groups.
Compromise
A phased approach to incorporating environmentally
sustainable methods over an extended timeframe can
mitigate investor apprehensions and make the transition
more feasible.
Effective communication with, and education of, key
stakeholders, employees and consumers regarding
the advantages of eco-friendly practices is critical. By
explaining the long-term benefits of sustainability while
demonstrating that the business remains financially viable
and competitive, this process can facilitate smoother
adoption and broader support for the transition.
TBL encompasses all the values, attitudes, performances
and processes, companies implement to maximise
financial, social and environmental benefits. Cynics,
however, claim TBL is little more than an opportunity
for corporations to pay lip service to the environment
through a framework for reporting purposes.

C2.1.4
The Triple Bottom Line (TBL) framework encourages
designers to prioritise the social needs of communities,
the financial imperatives of clients and companies, and the
environmental impacts of their creations.
Designers have a unique opportunity to drive sustainable
development by making mindful choices in materials,
assembly methods and design strategies that emphasise
reduction, reuse, repair and recycling. Identifying
low-energy manufacturing methods and prioritising
community well-being, designers foster equitable and
inclusive employment practices, thereby building resilient
communities.
Design approaches include:
» Minimising material usage and waste generation.
« Preventing waste from entering landfill or incineration
streams.
« Enhancing product longevity through upgrade, repair
and reuse pathways.
« Lowering costs and profitability.
« Reducing emissions and pollutants. 
« Selecting materials with low environmental impacts.
« Specification of alternative energy supplies such as solar, wind power, hydro and battery storage options, reducing reliance on fossil fuels, reducing carbon footprints and lowering energy costs.
« Initiating fair pay and equitable employment strategies,
built into their costing plans, designers can ensure safe, 
positive work environments that build and strengthen communities.
« adopting sustainable practices that lower costs in the 
long term, helping companies to achieve long-term
profitability. 
‘This holistic approach ensures that design practices
contribute to sustainable development while balancing
economic, social, and environmental considerations.

C2.2.1
The circular economy refers to an economy based on the
use of renewable sources of energy and the recycling of
materials to create a sustainable economy, as illustrated
in Figure C 2.2.1a. In this way, the circular economy is
intended to mimic the processes of a biological ecosystem
as opposed to the current prevailing relatively linear
economic model in which non-renewable energy sources
are used to create products that, at the end of their life
are buried in landfills or incinerated, characterised as the
‘take-make-consume-dispose’ economy. Examples of the
operation of such an economy can be seen in the use and
reuse through recycling of polyethylene terephthalate
(PET) in the beverage industry and the recycling of paper
and cardboard.
In 2020, the European Commission adopted a new
Circular Economy Action Plan as part of the European
Green Deal initiative, designed to produce sustainable
economic growth. The program aims for the EU to achieve
a climate neutrality target, i.e., an economy with net-zero
greenhouse gas emissions and halt biodiversity loss by
2025.
The new action plan (through legislative and non-
legislative measures) targets how products are designed,
promotes circular economy processes, encourages
sustainable consumption, and aims to ensure that waste is
prevented and the resource losses are kept to a minimum
China is also legislating to improve the effectiveness
and efficiency of its circular economy and sustainability
initiatives. Employing five-year cycles, the Chinese
Government reviews and revises its sustainability goals
and economic growth targets.
Linear and circular models
The linear and circular models of resource management
represent fundamentally different approaches.
The traditional linear system of production and
consumption is frequently encapsulated by the phrase
‘take, make, dispose, emphasising a unidirectional flow of
resources culminating in waste. The key goals here focus
on production efficiencies, market consumption and
profits.
By contrast, the circular economy is encapsulated by
the principles of ‘reuse, recycle, and recover! Kirchherr
et al. (2017) articulated the circular economy as “an
economic system that replaces the ‘end-of-life' concept
with reducing, alternatively reusing, recycling and
recovering materials in production/distribution and
consumption processes”. The circular economy seeks to
develop a closed-loop system that maintains the integrity
of natural resources and environmental ecosystems. This
model emphasises sustainable minimisation of waste and
pollutants while enhancing resource efficiency across
production, distribution and consumption processes.

C2.2.2
‘Design-for’ strategies
One of the most efficient means of reducing waste is to
address the problem at the design stage, following design
for manufacture (DfM) guidelines. These guidelines
include provisions for adopting designs that favour
Biodegradable materials are preferred in a circular
economy because they meet thekey principles of the model,
that is, supporting sustainability objectives, reducing
waste and managing resources for maximum efficiency.
This is done by leveraging biological processes that break
down biodegradables into water, minerals and organic
matter. providing nutrients for soils and reducing waste in
landfills and aquatic environments. The decomposition of
biodegradable materials also generates lower greenhouse
gas emissions in contrast to their non-biodegradable
alternatives. Reductions in waste management and the
use of recycled waste can assist with financial goals.
Governments are implementing regulations that favour
biodegradable materials. Compliance with legislation and
regulations means companies can avoid unnecessary fines
and financial burdens.
the efficient selection of materials, ease of assembly/
disassembly, repair, recovery and recycling.
The DfM process may be deconstructed into three
complementary components incorporating design for:
materials, processes and assembly.
Design for materials is a mechanism by which designers
select appropriate materials, reducing toxic substances,
hazardous waste, polluting emissions and the quantity
of materials required. Where possible, single-component
materials are specified for moulding, and recyclable
materials are marked for later identification. These
measures assist in the later disassembly and recycling
processes. Similar specifications are also applied to
packaging.
Design for process involves reductions in: the amount of
energy consumed, the number of production processes
employed, waste generated and emissions produced.
The number of parts that require additional operations
including: plating, painting, printing, labelling, insertion
of screws, caulking, riveting and welding is decreased.
Design for assembly approaches are used to analyse
components and sub-assemblies, minimising costs
by reducing the number of parts and maximising the
efficiency of assembly processes. Consideration of tools
to be used during assembly may also be important.
A reduction in the number and variety of fasteners and
tool types used during assembly is preferable,
Design for longevity focuses on creating longer-lasting
products, thusreducing waste and improving sustainability.
There are many overlaps with other strategies, but the key
considerations involve designers:
« incorporating
pathways
repairability and upgradability
« specifying high-quality materials to withstand wear
and tear
« creation of classic designs that engender timeless
appeal
«+ efficient use of resources when manufacturing and
disposing of
« soliciting an emotional response, connecting the
owner with the product
« designing modular componentry that allows for
switching out discrete units to allow for technological
upgrades or repairs.
These principles enhance the product’s lifespan and align
with sustainability goals and those of a circular economy.
DEMATERIALISATION
Dematerialisation can be defined as the progressive
reduction in the amount of energy and/or material used to
produce a product, and corresponds with the goal of waste
minimisation. Some of the interrelated factors associated
with dematerialisation are indicated in Figure C 2.2.2b.
Dematerialisation does not mean a decline in materials
usage will occur, as increased production may result in
a growth of material use overall, although the individual
use of material per unit decreases with design changes and
miniaturisation.
‘This phenomenon is sometimes known as Jevons’ Paradox
or the rebound effect. In 1865, the English economist
William Stanley Jevons found that the increased efficiency
of newer steam engines resulted in an increased use of coal
rather than a reduction. This was because the increased
efficiency led to the expansion of steam power. Economic
and population growth, of course, also strongly interact
with many of the factors.
Modern electronics provides many examples of
dematerialisation. Email, for example, has replaced the
Fax and reduced the flow of surface mail, to the extent that
many countries are reducing postal services and reviewing
the need for traditional postal services.

C2.2.3
Biodegradable materials are preferred in a circular
economy because they meet thekey principles of the model,
that is, supporting sustainability objectives, reducing
waste and managing resources for maximum efficiency.
This is done by leveraging biological processes that break
down biodegradables into water, minerals and organic
matter. providing nutrients for soils and reducing waste in
landfills and aquatic environments. The decomposition of
biodegradable materials also generates lower greenhouse
gas emissions in contrast to their non-biodegradable
alternatives. Reductions in waste management and the
use of recycled waste can assist with financial goals.
Governments are implementing regulations that favour
biodegradable materials. Compliance with legislation and
regulations means companies can avoid unnecessary fines
and financial burdens.

C2.2.4
Waste mitigation strategies are designed to reduce
or eliminate the volume of material lost to end-of-life
disposal methods.
Re-use
Re-use involves the repeated use of components or
products employing the same or an alternative purpose
than that originally specified. Examples include: glass
containers, plastic drink bottles, fabric bags, etc.
Repair
Repair involves restoring a product or component to a
good or sound working condition after deterioration or
damage. Repairs may be functional or cosmetic. Examples
include: mending clothes, repairing damage to vehicle
bodywork and replacing faulty computer components.
Increasingly, the cost of repair of consumer products has
become such that replacement is often cheaper. In this
respect, many items today are manufactured based on
planned obsolescence, in which it is expected that they
will have a limited useful life, at the end of which they will
be replaced rather than repaired.
Recycle
Recycling is often defined as the series of activities,
including collection, cleaning, separation and processing,
by which products or other materials are recovered from
the waste stream for use instead of virgin raw materials
when manufacturing new products. Recycled products
may be reprocessed back into their original form, e.g. used
glass jars recycled back into new bottles, or it may involve
materials being reprocessed into a new product, e.g. waste
paper being recycled into cardboard (Figure C 2.2.4a).
This reprocessing excludes the recovery of materials for
energy.
Recondition
Reconditioning or remanufacturing is the process by
which used products may be returned to, or close to,
their original manufactured specification. Reconditioning
extends product life until it is no longer commercially
viable to continue. It may also refer to upgrading
components as new technologies become available.
Reconditioned products generally come with a renewed
warranty guaranteeing their quality. Examples include:
reconditioned car engines, retreaded tyres, upgrading
computers with replacement modules, etc.
Re-engineer
Re-engineering involves revising established designs to
improve cost, performance, safety, manufacturability,
quality, waste reduction, usability metrics, etc.
Pollution and waste
Most production processes create pollution and/or waste
during the various stages of manufacture from raw material
extraction to transport, processing, manufacturing and packaging. Pollution/waste comes in many forms such as
excess heat, exhaust gases, chemical discharges, product
rejects and leftover packaging.
Methodologies for waste
reduction and designing out
waste
To move toward sustainable manufacturing, designers
focus on reducing waste that otherwise finds its way
into landfills or as environmental pollution. Figure
C 2.2.4a provides a schematic representation of the waste
management hierarchy, moving from the least favoured
option of waste disposal through to the prevention of
waste generation.
ethods of waste reduction include:
Product recovery and reuse
« Produce to order, eliminate oversupply
« Production optimisation to reduce waste
« Avoid the use of unnecessary packaging
« Material substitution that favours recycling
« Use energy-efficient equipment and lighting
« Storage is designed to reduce product deterioration
« Quality assurance practices to reduce rejects based on
poor quality.
Take-back legislation
Increasingly, the responsibility for recycling is being
directed towards the manufacturers of the goods.
Regulations are introduced to require manufacturers to collect or ‘take back’ their products at End-of-Life (EOL).
The aims of this legislation are to:
» reduce the amount of hazardous waste sent to
landfills
« increase the availability of scrap, reducing the demand
for virgin new material
« encourage design changes that reduce waste and
improve recyclability.
Collection methods vary depending on the products
under consideration and include:
« drop-off sites
« point-of-purchase collection bins
« curbside collection
« combined/coordinated collection
« permanent collection depot.
End-of-life legislation
In 2000, the European Union (EU) introduced the End-
of-Life Vehicle (ELV) directive (2000/53/EC). This
directive required automobile manufacturers to re-use or
recover, at their cost, over 85% by weight of their vehicles
by 2006, rising to 95% by 2015. In 2007, the legislation
was modified to cover all vehicles produced by a given
manufacturer. In 2022, 4.7 million passenger vehicles, or
5.5 million tonnes of vans and light commercial vehicles
were recovered for scrap.
While the decision as to when EOL has been reached is the
prerogative of the consumer, and the manufacturer must
take back the vehicle. The manufacturer is not required to
physically retrieve vehicles, but must provide a network of
authorised treatment facilities, ensuring:
safe handling and dismantling of vehicles
« hazardous materials such as oils, acids and a variety
of fluids are removed and readied for appropriate
disposal
» recovery of components and materials such as metals,
plastic and glass which are separated, cleaned, sorted
and prepared for reuse or recycling
Vehicle manufacturers must design vehicles with
recyclability in mind and are responsible for financing the
collection and treatment of end-of-life vehicles.
A similar take-back directive was issued by the European
Union (EU) in 2002, concerning waste electrical and
electronic equipment (WEEE). The directive (2002/96/
EC) required that from 2005, the producers of electrical
and electronic equipment had to establish collection
systems for the following product categories:
» Medical devices.
» Lighting equipment.
« Household appliances.
« Automatic dispensers.
« Consumer equipment.
«+ Electrical & electronic tools.
« Monitoring and control equipment.
« Toys, leisure and sports equipment.
« IT and telecommunications equipment.
Collection is typically accomplished through:
» municipal sites
« point of purchase
« producer take-back.

C2.2.5
Renewable and non-renewable
resources
Renewable resources are those that are consumed at a rate
less than they are replaced by natural processes. Renewable
resources may take the form of energy or commodities.
Some resources require careful management, such as
geothermal power, plantation timber, and water.
Wind, solar, and tidal energies are perpetual because
they are deemed inexhaustible. Non-renewable resources
include materials that, due to their rate of depletion and
extreme time scales involved in their formation, are
finite and therefore exhaustible. Fossil fuels such as coal,
oil and natural gas are all non-renewable. Mineral and
ore reserves such as iron, diamond and bauxite are also
considered non-renewable.
Renewable resources are characterised by their
consumption rate being lower than their natural
replenishment rate. These resources exist as energy sources
or commodities. Some resources, such as geothermal
energy, plantation timber, and water, necessitate careful
management to ensure sustainability. Resources like wind,
solar, and tidal energy are considered perpetual due to
their inexhaustible nature.
Non-renewable resources, encompass finite materials that
are exhaustible due to their rapid depletion rates and the
extensive time scales required for their formation. Fossil fuels, including coal, oil, and natural gas, fall into this category. Mineral and ore reserves such as iron, lithium, gold, and bauxite are also classified as non-renewable.
A circular economy fundamentally depends on harnessing renewable energy sources to establish a sustainable economic system. By leveraging inexhaustible and regenerative energy supplies, the following sustainability objectives can be realised: 
« waste minimisation and efficient use of resources 
« significant reductions in environmental impacts gained by the reduction in the use of fossil fuels
« decreases in greenhouse gas emissions and other pollutants, such as Oxides of Nitrogen
« stimulation of economic growth through the creation of employment opportunities within the burgeoning enewable energy sector. 
Use of renewable energy in a circular economy emphasises
the interdependence of ecological sustainability, innovation and economic development.

A3.1.1
Classification systems help us to organise, understand and
utilise materials more effectively, enabling better resource
management, technological advancements and scientific
study. Through the establishment of classification systems,
scientists and engineers can understand how different
materials behave and how their properties relate to their
composition and structure. Such an understanding is
important in developing new technologies and products,
as different materials are better suited for different
applications. A systematic classification system allows
scientists to study materials more effectively, compare
materials and identify patterns and relationships.
Aristotelian Classification System
The Greek philosopher Aristotle developed the first
recorded classification systems (384-322 BCE). One of the
most influential thinkers of all time, Aristotle contributed
foundational work in logic, ethics, political theory and
metaphysics that have shaped Western thought for over
two millennia.
Among his contributions was the concept that all
materials were composed of two components, ‘matter’
and ‘form; to explain their nature. Within this system,
matter (a material's substance) was composed, in varying
proportions, of the four elements (earth, water, air and
fire). While its *form’ was related to its defining shape or
structure, such as a table or statue.
In this system, each element was associated with specific
qualities:
« Earth: Cold and dry.
+ Water: Cold and wet.
Air: Hot and wet.
« Fire: Hotand dry.
In addition, these elements could be transformed
into one another, such as water turning into air. Such
transformations occurred when one of the elements
qualities was replaced by its opposite, leading to a different
element. For example, heating water resulted in a change
from ‘Cold and wet’ to ‘Hot and wet, i.e. a change from
liquid Water to Air in the form of steam.
Heavy materials like metals were explained as being
mainly composed of earth, with a smaller quantity of
the other three elements contributing to their properties,
while lighter objects contained less earth, relative to the
other three elements in their composition. For example,
the solid nature of wood and the fact that it falls when
dropped was attributed to the primary presence of earth
with smaller quantities of the other elements. These
lighter elements explained other attributes, such as
combustibility, attributed to the presence of fire, decay and
growth attributed to the presence of water and porosity
and lightness with air.
While Aristotle’s classification system for materials
is no longer accepted, it did provide a framework for
understanding and categorising the physical world,
along with change and transformation, that influenced
subsequent scientific and philosophical thought. Such was
Aristotle’s influence in the Western world, it was not until
the 17th Century that his classification system for matter
was superseded.
The Three-Ages System
The first and perhaps the most widely known modern
classification of materials was introduced in 1826 by the
Danish archaeologist Christian Jiirgensen (C.].) Thomsen.
The system divided human history into three epochs
of material use: the Stone Age, the Bronze Age and the
Iron Age. Also known as the ‘museum ordering method’
or ‘Chest of drawers system, it allowed archaeologists
to establish a chronological sequence for prehistoric
developments of material use, primarily associated with
the production of tools and weapons. In a similar vein, this
system could be expanded into the Steel Age, Polymer Age,
Age of Composites and the Silicon Age (to acknowledge
the rise of micro-electronics). Such a system is, however,
not particularly useful in understanding how and why
different materials are required in modern design. 
The periodic table, devised by the Russian chemist Dmitri
Mendeleev in 1869, is a materials classification system
because it organises elements based on their chemical
properties. Mendeleev’s table, using atomic mass as the
organising principle, was later revised by the English
physicist Henry Moseley in 1913 to the modern form
based on atomic number (the number of protons in the
nucleus). This was done to reconcile the positions of lodine
and Tellurium, although the basic format and positioning
of elements are largely unchanged. In organising the
elements into a Periodic table, several gaps were identified
thatled scientists to look for and ultimately find the missing
elements. The table, therefore, provided an invaluable tool
for not only illuminating chemical associations, allowing
scientists to understand the behaviour of elements and
their compounds, but also as a predictive tool for elements
yet to be discovered.
The periodic table (shown in Figure A3.1.1b) helps to
classify elements like metals, nonmetals and metalloids,
each with distinct properties.
While the periodic table is an invaluable tool for
understanding the properties of elements and their
interactions, there is no one style of materials classification
that will suit every situation. Many other methods of
classification are possible and depend on the purpose for
which classification is undertaken.
For engineering purposes, materials can be broadly
categorised into seven groups: Wood, Metals, Polymers,
Ceramics, Textiles, Leather and Composites. Significant
sub-groups, such as those shown in figures A3.1.1c and
A3.1.1d, may be created for each category, with each of
these sub-groups being further divided into several finer
categories.
Classification systems can also be broadly arranged into
their material properties:
Physical properties are those properties that can be
observed and measured without changing the material’s
chemical composition and are inherent to a material’s
fundamental state. Examples include mass, density,
melting point, colour, thermal and electrical conductivity,
thermal expansion coefficient, texture and optical
properties.
Mechanical properties include those properties that
describe how a material behaves under stress, strain and
deformation when exposed to applied forces. Examples
include hardness, strength, toughness, ductility, stiffness
and formability.
Chemical properties describe a material’s ability to react
or change chemically and are observed during chemical
reactions, rather than being an inherent property of
the material. Examples include flammability, reactivity,
oxidation, corrosion resistance, toxicity ans acidity/
basicity.
These classification systems allow engineers to assess
a material's properties against the requirements of a
specified manufacturing process or product. They allow
criteria such as cost, ease and efficiency of manufacture,
durability in service, repairability and recyclability to be
evaluated before beginning production.

A3.1.2
Frame, shell and solid structures are fundamental building
blocks in product design, each offering distinct advantages
in terms of strength, weight and functionality. Frame
structures use interconnected parts for support, shell
structures rely on thin, curved layers and solid structures
are made of dense material. Combination structures, like
frame-and-shell, leverage the strengths of multiple types
of materials for optimised performance.
Frame Structures: A frame structure is composed of
interconnected parts (beams, columns, etc.) that are joined
together to form a stable and rigid supporting structure.
The function of a frame structure is to support and carry
loads. Frame structures enabled the construction of
high-rise buildings by providing a strong load-bearing
skeleton. The first such building, consisting of ten floors,
was constructed in Chicago in 1884. In addition to high-
rise building skeletons, frame structures include bicycle
frames, car chassis, truss and rigid-frame bridges and
structures such as the Statue of Liberty and the Eiffel
Tower, shown in Figure A3.2.1a.
Concrete roadway bridge supports and decking, such
as that shown in Figure A.3.2b, also represent a frame
structure because they utilise a network of interconnected
members to distribute loads and resist forces.
Shell Structures: A shell structure has a thin, outer layer
that is hollow and often curved, allowing for large open
spaces while being visually striking, The function of a
shell structure is to distribute loads efficiently and create
lightweight structures that can also often act as a protective
layer. Examples include egg shells, igloos, aircraft fuselages,
boat hulls, car bodies, large domes such as the Mt Palomar
observatory, the US Congress building, modern stadia
roofs such as the Singapore National Stadium and many
cathedrals, temples and mosques around the world. The
roof of the Sydney Opera House, shown in Figure A3.1.2b,
is an example of a shell structure.
Solid Structures: A solid structure is made of dense
material, like rock or concrete and is not hollow and is
often employed to provide a base or foundation. Examples
include mountains, dams, concrete foundations and
archways as shown in Figure A3.1.3c. Arches primarily
work by transferring loads through compression, meaning
the arch’s material is pressed together instead of being
pulled apart. Wedge-shaped blocks are arranged to create
a curve, with the central block (keystone) locking the
structure in place.
hile frame, shell and solid structures are fundamental
building blocks, product design often requires that they be
used in combination to obtain the best mix of properties.
Combination Structures: A combination structure, as the
name implies, includes elements of other structure types
for a specific purpose, leveraging the strengths of each to
achieve the best balance of strength, weight and efficiency.
Examples include: car bodies (solid engine block, frame
chassis, shell exterior), aeroplane fuselages (frame support
with a shell exterior), domed stadiums (solid concrete
walls, metal frame and shell roof).
Combination structures allow designers to create products that are functionally strong and aesthetically appealing. By understanding the different types of structures and their applications, engineers and designers can create products best suited to their environment and intended role.

A3.1.3
The selection, from a range of competing options, of the
most suitable material for a specific purpose can be difficult
and often requires the balancing of competing properties.
By comparing and contrasting the properties of different
materials, designers can make informed decisions that
align with the product’s properties and specific project
requirements.
Professional CAD packages provide tables of material
properties to help users select the most suitable materials
for their designs. These tables typically include information
such as density, hardness, tensile strength, electrical
conductivity, expansion coefficients and other selected
properties that are relevant to the project. This process
provides designers with opportunities to quickly compare
materials against specific criteria as various iterations of
the product are developed.

A3.1.4
Density measures the quantity of mass per unit volume.
It helps us understand how materials behave and interact
with each other. The SI units for measuring density are
kilograms per cubic metre or kg/m’.
Density is an intrinsic (or inherent) property of a material,
as it is independent of the amount that may be present.
‘When amaterial is heated, the thermal energy gained by the
atoms increases atomic vibrations, leading to an increase
