in atomic separation and an increase in the dimensions
of the material overall. This increase in dimensions is
typically quantified as the change in length per unit length
per degree (mm' K™) for the coefficient of linear thermal
expansion (a). The coefficient of thermal expansion for
some materials from 20° to 100 “C is provided in Figure
A3l4a,
Thermal Contraction Problem
A steel rod is taken from a furnace at a temperature of
600 °C, at which time its length is measured to be 250mm.
What will the rod length be after it has cooled to 20 "C

Shrink Fit Problem
A steel pin has been machined to a diameter of 150 mm
and is to be shrunk fit into a steel housing to create an
interference fit of 0.15 mm. To what temperature would
the housing need to be heated to position the pin in
the housing and achieve the required interference? An
insertion clearance on diameter of 1 mm is to be used.
Assume room temperature is 20 °C and the coefficient
of thermal expansion for both the pin and housing is
11 x 10°K™". Assume also uniform heating and no loss
of temperature between removing heat from the housing
and during the insertion of the pin.
Starting conditions at room temperature (20 °C):
Pin diameter @, = 150mm
Shrink fit is to be 0.15mm
.. Housing diameter @, must be machined to 150 - 0.15
=149.85mm
Coefficient of thermal expansion = 11 x 107°K™"
O, xa =149.85x11x10°
=0.00164835 mm K™’
This means that for every degree Kelvin (or degree
Celsius) that the temperature is raised, an expansion of
0.00164835mm should be obtained.
.. To expand the housing by 0.15mm to 150mm plus a
1 mm clearance, an expansion of 1.15 mm is required.
Temperature = 1.15 + 0.00164835
= 698°C
Note that additional complications are introduced if
the interference fit is between materials with different
coefficients of thermal expansion. In these situations, any
changes in operating temperature that may increase or
decrease the interference also need to be considered.
THermAL ConbucTIVITY
When a temperature gradient is present in a material,
heat will flow from the region of higher temperature to
the region of lower temperature. Thermal conductivity
(K) is a measure of the efficiency with which thermal
energy travels through a material. The higher the thermal
conductivity, the greater the rate at which heat will flow.
The imperial unit for thermal conductivity is the BTU.
inhr! “F! ft2 or BTU hr'' "F* ft™'. This unit represents
the amount of heat energy (in BTU) that flows through a
material one foot thick, across one square foot of surface
area, in one hour, when there’s a temperature difference
of one degree Fahrenheit. The SI units for thermal
conductivity are Watts/metre/"K (W m™ K ™).
Metals typically have high thermal conductivity, while
polymers and ceramics have low thermal conductivity and
are insulators rather than conductors of heat. The thermal
conductivity of several materials at room temperature is
provided in Figure A3.1.4b. Note values are indicative
only.
1 WmK'=0.00239 x Cal em™' s ' K
1Btuhr! oF' ft'! = 1.731 Wm 'K’
Figure A3.1.4b Thermal conductivity of some common
materials at room temperature.
MELtinG Point
The melting point (Mp) of a material is the specific
temperature (Tm) at which a solid substance transitions
into a liquid state. It's a characteristic property of a
substance and is usually defined at a standard pressure,
often atmospheric pressure. At the melting point, the solid
and liquid phases of the material coexist in equilibrium.
The melting points of a selected group of materials are
presented in Figure A 3.1.4c.
The melting point of a material is a function of the strength
of the intermolecular forces holding the material together
as a solid. The stronger the forces, the more energy is
required to break them apart and transition the solid into
a liquid, leading to a higher melting point. These forces
can include various types, like ionic bonds, covalent
bonds, metallic bonds, hydrogen bonds and van der Waals
forces.
Water molecules in ice form hydrogen bonds between the
slightly positive hydrogen atoms of one molecule and the
slightly negative oxygen atoms of another. It is the strength
of these hydrogen bonds that contributes to the melting
point of ice (0°C).
Metals, when in solid form, are arranged in a repeating
pattern of metallic bonds, known as a metallic lattice,
where electrons are delocalised and shared throughout the
structure. This delocalisation allows for strong metallic
bonds, leading to properties like high melting and boiling
points. By contrast, mercurys atomic structure and
bonding characteristics result in weaker metallic bonds,
leading to a lower melting point.
ELecTRICAL RESISTIVITY AND
ELecTricAL CoNDucTIVITY
Electrical conductivity (o) and electrical resistivity (p)
are measures of the ease with which free electrons move through a material are inherent properties of a material. As might be expected, conductivity is inversely related to resistivity as indicated below.
a=1/p
Although this relationship is strictly true only when applied to metals.
The electrical reistivity of some common materials are presented below.
Resistivity in metals is described in terms of resistance to
the free flow of electrons. This resistance to motion was
postulated to arise from the scattering of the electrons
due to collisions with the positive ions of the metal lattice.
According to this theory, resistivity arises from any
phenomenon that disturbs the motion of electrons, such
as:
« Collisions with lattice imperfections (eg, caused by
cold working).
« Collisions with solute atoms (e.g. impurities)
« Collisions with thermally-induced lattice vibrations
(e.g. temperature)
Temperature has the greatest effect on resistivity.
As temperature increases, thermally induced lattice
vibrations increase, raising the chance of interaction with
an electron. Therefore, as temperature increases, so does
resistivity. As the temperature approaches absolute zero
(0°K), thermally-induced lattice vibrations also approach
zero and resistivity becomes a function of temperature-
independent factors such as impurity content and
microstructural effects such as lattice imperfections,
precipitates and inclusions.
By contrast, increasing the temperature of semiconductors
causes more electrons to jump from the valence band to
the conduction band, increasing the number of charge carriers and thus enhancing conductivity, as illustrated in
Figure A3.1.4e.

A3.1.5
CoRrROSION RESISTANCE
Corrosion is an electrochemical reaction that results
in the conversion of metallic materials into oxides, salts
or other compounds. Metals undergoing corrosion lose
their strength, ductility and other important mechanical
properties. Corrosion resistance is therefore the property
of a material to avoid or resist such environmental
degradation.
The rate at which corrosion occurs depends on the
reactivity of the material in the environment. For many
materials, this consists of the rate at which a protective
oxide is formed at the surface. If a dense, tightly adherent
oxide is formed, it tends to protect the metal surface from
continuing attack, leading to a condition in which the
surface is said to be passivated.
The corrosion resistance of metal alloys such as stainless
steel is in fact a result of its high reactivity with oxygen, hich results in the rapid formation of a nanometres-
thick chromium oxide surface layer that restricts access
to further oxide attack. The corrosion resistance of
aluminium relies on a similar reactivity with oxygen, in
which a protective layer of aluminium oxide, ALO, is
formed. The reactivity of stainless steel and aluminium
gives them a self-healing property such that when
scratched, the protective oxide is rapidly re-established.
Porous, loosely adhering oxides, that occur on steel, allow
continued corrosion by either allowing corrosive species
through the oxide or by loss of the oxide, exposing the
surface to further attack. A relative measure of corrosion
resistance can be gained from a determination of the
electrical potential of the material in situ.
For corrosion to take place, three conditions must
generally exist simultaneously;
« The presence of an anode and a cathode. This
occurs when two dissimilar metals or two regions of
differential electrolyte concentration create a difference
in electrical potential.
+« A metallic connector between the anode and
cathode.
» An electrolyte, such as water.
Eliminating these three conditions is often difficult.
Dissimilar metal contact cannot always be avoided because
of weight, cost and functional issues. The potential for
corrosion may be minimised by using surface treatments
such as;
« Plating (anodising)
« Painting
« Sealing
Water cannot be avoided, but it can be controlled by
adequate design to provide drain paths, drain holes
and ingress prevention through the appropriate use of
sealants.
Another anti-corrosion preventative measure often
employed is the use of sacrificial anodes such as zinc.
The steel hulls of ships are protected from corrosion by
the placement of Zinc bars over the surface, as shown in
Figure A3.1.5a. These bars protect the steel surface around
them by a process called cathodic protection.
ReAcTIVITY (FOOD SAFETY)
In the context of food safety, ‘reactivity’ can refer to
both an individual’s response to a food, such as allergic
or intolerance reactions and the interaction between
food and its container that can have adverse effects on
the quality of the food and pose potential safety risks if
consumed.
Warnings to individuals subject to specific intolerances
and allergies, such as gluten, nuts or shellfish, etc., are
often included in the contents section of a label, as shown
in Figure A3.1.5a.
ackaging is used to extend the useful life of a foodstuff
(typically referred to as its shelf-life) from deterioration
by protecting it from environmental exposure to
microorganisms. Over time, however, reactivity can
occur between the food and its container that can involve
changes in flavour, texture, colour, nutritional level and
safety. Because of this, labels on packaged food often
include ‘Best Before’ or ‘Use By’ advice as well as storage
recommendations, as shown in Figure A3.1.5b and Figure igure A3.1.5¢ Typical Foed storage advice of food
packaging
Several mechanisms can be involved in adverse reactions
between food and its container. Some of these are outlined
below. All can be accelerated by increased temperatures.
Oxidation: Oxidation, in general, is defined as the
loss of electrons or the addition or removal of oxygen
and/or hydrogen. In the case of food, this involves a
reaction with organic molecules such as fats, proteins
and carbohydrates. The container can either facilitate or
hinder this process. For instance, clear plastic or glass jars
are not recommended for light-sensitive foods because
UV light can accelerate oxidation. For this reason, foods
such as olive oil and some beverages are stored in coloured
glass.
Hydrolysis: Hydrolysis is a chemical process where
molecules in fats, proteins and carbohydrates are broken
down by water. It is a reaction that plays a significant role
in food storage and spoilage issues, affecting food texture,
flavour and even the stability of packaging materials. The
hydrolysis reaction of fats is responsible for the rancidity
of fats such as butter and oils.
Migration: Migration refers to the movement (or leaching)
of chemicals from the container into the food, changing
its flavour or potentially creating health risks. This is why
certain plastics, especially those containing Bisphenol
A (BPA) or phthalates, are not considered safe for food
contact. Metal cans can also react with their contents,
particularly when acidic foods such as citrus fruits and
tomatoes are present, if their inner polymer lining has
been damaged. If this lining is damaged, metal ions can
leach into the food, leaving a metallic taste
HvyGroscopy
Hygroscopy is the phenomenon of a material attracting
and holding water molecules from its surroundings,
typically through absorption or adsorption. In essence, a
substance can ‘seek out’ and retain moisture.
Examples of hygroscopic (hydrophilic) substances include
materials like desiccant packets in electronics packages,
salt, the polymers polyethylene glycol (PEG), poly(vinyl
alcohol) (PVA) and poly(acrylic acid) (PAA). Even
MarteriAL CrassiFicATioN AND PROPERTIES
some natural materials like seeds that have hygroscopic
extensions which help them disperse.
The opposite of hygroscopic is hydrophobic (‘water
fearing’). Hydrophobic substances repel water. Examples
include oils, fats, waxes, the polymers polypropylene
(PP), polyethylene (PE) and fluoropolymers like
polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE, Teflon®), which is
commonly used in non-stick cookware and other water-
resistant applications.
FLAMMABILITY
Flammability refers to a material’s ability to ignite and
burn quickly and is essentially interchangeable with the
older term inflammable.
A similar term is combustibility, which is often used
to describe a materials ability to burn under certain
conditions and so can also be used to describe a material’s
resistance to ignition. In other words, all flammable
materials are combustible, but not all combustible
materials are flammable. For example, a piece of wood is
combustible, but it requires more heat or a longer exposure
to a flame to ignite compared to a flammable liquid like
gasoline.
Note that ‘inflammable’ is not the opposite of ‘flammable’
The correct antonym is nonflammable.

A3.1.6
TENSILE AND COMPRESSIVE STRENGTH
Tensile strength is a measure of a material’s resistance to
plastic deformation from a tensile or stretching type load.
When the tensile strength of a material is quoted, it is the
maximum tensile strength known as the ultimate tensile
strength (ultimate tensile stress). This is one of the most
often quoted mechanical properties for a material.
The ultimate tensile strength (UTS) represents the
maximum applied tensile load that a material can sustain,
divided by the material’s original cross-sectional area.
The tensile strength is therefore typically measured in
kN mm? or Mega Pascals (MPa), where 1Pa=1 N m™,
On the engineering stress-strain curve, the tensile strength
is represented by that position where the maximum stress
is achieved, as illustrated in Figure A 3.1.6a.
Determine the Ultimate Tensile Stress (UTS) of a 15 mm
diameter rod if the maximum load it can withstand is
70kN.
Load =70 x 10°N
Diameter =15x10"m
Area =n(15%107%)2+4
=1.767 x 107'm’
UTS = Load + area
=70% 10’ + 1.767 x 1074
=396 kN m™
=396 MPa
STIFFNESS
Stiffness is the resistance of an elastic body to deflection
by an applied force. There are several measures of material
stiffness depending on the stress state imposed. These
include the modulus of elasticity (also known as Young’s
modulus), the shear modulus (also known as the Modulus
of Rigidity) and the Bulk Modulus.
The most commonly quoted measure is the modulus
of elasticity or Youngs modulus, which is a measure
of stiffness when a body is subjected to axial tensile or
compressive stresses and is visible on the stress-strain
diagram as a straight line. The angle of the line (gradient)
indicates the relative stiffness of the material, such that a
stiff material will have a high Young’s modulus. Young’s modulus is represented by the capital letter E and is
typically expressed in Gigapascals (GPa).
The gradients typical of a number of materials are
presented in Figure A3.1.6b. In this Figure, it can be seen
that a brittle material displays linear elastic behaviour and
fails with little strain, e.g. ceramics and glasses . A soft and
tough material, on the other hand, exhibits a very small
initial slope, but strain hardens and withstands larger
strains before failure, e.g. low carbon steel.
TOUGHNESS
Toughness is the ability of a material to resist the
propagation of cracks. A material’s stress-strain curve can
be used to indicate the overall toughness of the material.
The area under the stress-strain curve, within the plastic
range, is a measure of a material’s toughness as illustrated
in Figure A3.1.6d. The greater the area under the graph,
the tougher the material and the greater the amount of
energy required to cause it to fail.
A commonly employed method of testing the toughness
of a material is to measure its resistance to impact. A
number of standard tests are available, such as the Izod
impact test or the Charpy impact test for metals. In these
tests, a test piece of standardised dimensions is obtained
from the material to be evaluated and a notch is machined
into the surface to a 'V’ or ‘U’ profile, An illustration of the test piece used for the Charpy V-notch test is provided in
Figure A3.1.6e.

The sample is subsequently fractured by impact with a
pendulum and the swing height the pendulum attains
following the sample fracture is used as a measure of the
energy absorbed. An example of a Charpy impact tester is
shown in Figure A3.1,6f.
Tough metals will generally undergo a ductile fracture
in which plastic deformation occurs during crack
propagation. Ductile fractures typically exhibit dull
fibrous surfaces with obvious plastic deformation.
‘The more plastic deformation required to advance the
crack, the more energy is required and hence a tougher
material. Low-toughness metals will generally fracture in
a brittle manner in which the crack can propagate without
the absorption of additional energy. Little work is done in
brittle fracture and hence, the material fails catastrophically
and is usually characterised by bright crystalline features with no evidence of plastic deformation. The toughness
of a material will vary depending on the temperature. If
tested at elevated temperatures, a material will show ductile
fracture behaviour and consequently may be considered
to have good toughness. If tested at low temperature, the
same material may fracture in a brittle manner and may
be considered to have poor toughness.
Typically, a plot of absorbed energy versus test temperature
for metals will produce a graph with a sigmoidal profile,
in which the absorbed energy levels off past a certain
temperature, into what is known as the upper shelf
energy (ductile) or the lower shelf energy (brittle). The
inflection point between these two regions is known as
the transition temperature and marks the position where
one form of fracture begins to dominate over the other.
Because of this, the appearance of the fracture can also
be used to measure toughness by assessing the fracture
appearance in terms of % crystallinity or % fibrosity. A
typical ductile-brittle transition curve obtained by Charpy
V-notch testing of steel is presented in Figure A3.1.6g.
Representative fractures from each region are shown.
Historical note
The British metallurgist Constance Tipper pioneered
research into brittle/ductile transitions. Investigating the
failure of Liberty ships during WWII, she discovered
there was a critical temperature below which fractures
in steel change from ductile to brittle mode. The ‘Liberty
Ships” in the North Atlantic were subjected to such low
temperatures that they would have been susceptible
to brittle failure. These ships were the first all-welded,
mass-produced, prefabricated cargo ships produced by
the United States to assist in the World War II effort. The sudden and catastrophic failure of these vessels was a
major problem for the Allies, see Figure A3.1.6h.
Tipper demonstrated that the type of steel used, rather
than the fact that the ships had been welded, was the
cause of the fractures. The “Tipper test’ soon became the
standard method for determining this form of brittleness
in steel, although the test has now been largely superseded
by the Charpy impact test. FRACTURE TOUGHNESS
The fracture toughness of a material relates to the size
of the crack that may be present before fracture occurs.
Testing is performed using a notched specimen that
is subjected to cyclic loading in tension or three-point
bending, to create a short fatigue crack as illustrated in
Figure A3.1.6i.
his pre-cracked sample is then fractured to determine
a material constant known as the critical stress intensity
or K1C. This value is directly related to the energy for
crack propagation within the material and to other
material constants such as Young’s modulus and Poisson’s
ratio. In this sense, the KIC value provides a more
fundamental measure of material toughness compared
with the Charpy test, which includes not only the energy
of crack propagation to fracture but also the energy of
crack initiation from the notch. The limiting factor in the
general use of fracture toughness tests lies in the difficulty
and cost involved in producing a pre-cracked specimen
compared with the ease of producing Charpy test pieces.
HARDNESS
Hardness refers to the resistance of a material to scratching
or abrasion. It may also refer to resistance to indentation,
penetration or cutting. The number of definitions for
hardness indicates that hardness may not be a fundamental
property of a material, but rather a composite of properties
including yield strength, work hardening, true tensile
strength, modulus of elasticity and others. Hardness is,
however, routinely used as an indication of a material’s
condition. As a general guide, the greater the hardness
of a material, the greater resistance it has to deformation
and wear. As might be expected, a variety of tests has been
developed to measure hardness. These tests fall into three
broad groups, consisting of:
« Scratch Hardness
Pencil
(e.g. Mohs, Bierbaum,
« Static Indentation Hardness (eg Brinell, Rockwell,
Vickers, Knoop, Janka, Durometer, Ultrasonic
Contact impedance)
« Dynamic Hardness (Scleroscope, Leeb)
A selection of commonly used hardness tests is described
below, along with the indentors used for some of these
tests. The requirements for surface preparation vary, but
all necessitate some preparation of the surface to ensure it
is clean and free of debris.
Scratch Hardness
As the name implies, scratch hardness tests involve the
scratching of the test surface with a stylus/indentor using
a constant force, to evaluate the material’s resistance to
permanent indentation or scratching, While the scratch
method invelves a force dragged across a surface, it is not
considered to be a dynamic test in the sense of a rapid
impact like in rebound hardness tests and is therefore
classed as a form of static hardness testing similar to other
static tests that apply a constant load.
The German mineralogist Friedrich Mohs developed
one of the earliest measures of material hardness in 1812,
Moh’s hardness, as it has come to be known, is defined
by how well a substance will resist scratching by another
substance of known or defined hardness and a ranking
from 1 to 10 is assigned. The scale contains ten minerals
that Mohs proposed as exemplars of each position on the
scale, starting with the mineral talc at position 1, which can
be scratched by a fingernail, to diamond at 10, see Figure
A3.1.6j. The Mohs scale, therefore,is not linear; that is, each
increment in the scale does not indicate a proportional
increase in hardness. For instance, the progression from
calcite to fluorite (from 3 to 4 on the Mohs scale) reflects
an increase in hardness of approximately 25 percent; the
progression from corundum to diamond, on the other
hand (9 to 10 on the Mohs scale), reflects a hardness
increase of more than 300 percent. For this reason, the
Mohs test, while greatly facilitating the identification of
minerals in the field, is not suitable for accurately gauging
the hardness of most materials, particularly industrial
materials such as steel or ceramics.
The Bierbaum test uses a standardised diamond indenter
that is dragged across the test surface and the width of
the scratch produced is measured. This test is suited to a
variety of materials, particularly plastics.
The Pencil test (or Wolff-Wilborn test) uses a set of
twenty pencils ranging from grades 9B to 9H to assess the
resistance to scratching of coatings, varnishes and paints,
A pencil is placed in a holder at an angle of 45 ° to the test
surface and the holder is moved across the surface under
a fixed force of 7.5N, shown in Figure A3.1.6k. The test
is repeated until a pencil grade that just scratches/indents
the surface is found and that grade is recorded as the ardness. This test has found use in the testing of coatings
on metals, polymers, wood and ceramics.
The pencil hardness test, while a simple and quick method
for evaluating coating hardness, is not ideal for comparing
coatings due to its subjectivity and lack of precision.
Static Indentation Hardness
Static hardness tests typically involve the penetration of
an indenter into the test surface using low loading rates.
The Barcol hardness test uses a cone-shaped steel
indenter that is pushed into the test surface until a spring
is completely depressed, resulting in the application of a
fixed load. The depth to which the indentor has penetrated
the surface is then read off a dial gauge calibrated from
0 to 100 and recorded as the Barcol number. This test is
often used to determine the degree to which plastic resin
has cured.
The Brinell test uses a hardened steel (S) or tungsten
carbide (WC) ball typically of 10 mm diameter (a 5 mm
indentor is also available) under a standard test load,
ranging from 1 kgf to 3000 kgf, to produce an indentation
in the surface of the material, shown in Figure A3.1.6l.
The diameter of the impression is measured with a small
portable microscope and the Brinell hardness, based on
this diameter, is read from a conversion table.
Test results are expressed showing both the hardness
recorded, the indentor type (S or W for WC), size (e.g.
10 mm) and load conditions (eg 3000 kg) such as:
200 HBS10/3000 or 200 HBW10/3000
While useful for a wide range of material hardnesses,
the Brinell test is not ideal for very hard materials. The
hardness of the material under test must be less than that
of the indenter to avoid deformation and a potentially
inaccurate reading. This puts an effective maximum
hardness of a hardened steel indentor of 444HB and
627HB for a WC indentor.
Because of the use of a spherical indenter, the Brinell test
is particularly effective in testing materials that may have
an inhomogeneous structure, such as castings. This is due
to the larger contact area of the ball-type indenter, which
effectively averages out microstructural variations such as
grain size, phase differences and inclusions, producing a
more representative measure of the material’s hardness.
‘The Brinell test, along with the Rockwell and Vickers tests,
is the most commonly employed test when evaluating the
hardness of metals.
Rockwell tests use either a small steel ball or a diamond
indenter ground to form a cone of 120 “C, to create an
impression, shown in Figure A3.1.6m. Several Rockwell
hardness scales are available (A, B, C, etc) using various
combinations of indenter and applied load, depending on
the material to be tested. The depth of the impression is
related to a Rockwell hardness that is read on a dial gauge
or LCD.
ickers tests use variable loads and a diamond pyramid
indenter, shown in Figure A3.1.6n. Because the same
indentor is used and only the applied load varies, a
comparable hardness is obtained when testing a range of
materials. Because of this, the Vickers test is the standard
method for the reliable measurement of metal and
ceramic hardness. The impression diagonals are measured
and related to a Vickers hardness number by reference toa
table selected based on the applied load used in the test.
Knoop tests explore microhardness by making
rhombohedral indentations (one long and one short
diagonal) with a pyramidal diamond indenter. The aspect
ratio of the impression diagonals allows impressions to
be placed closer together without concerns of previous
impressions affecting later hardness results.
‘The Brinell, Rockwell, Vickers and Knoop hardness testers
use a standardised indenter and fixed load to determine
hardness and are used extensively in the testing of metals.
The Janka hardness test is used for the testing of wood and
is similar to the Brinell test in that a steel ball, this time of
11.28 mm (0.444 inch), is impressed into the surface to a
depth of 5.64mm (half the indenter diameter), leaving an
impression of 10 mm®. The force required to create this
impression is measured in kN and is reported as the Janka
hardness (JH).
The Durometer hardness test (Figure A3.1.60) is
principally undertaken on polymers and involves the
pressing of an indenter (consisting of a hardened steel rod)
into the surface of the test piece. The indentation hardness
is read from a dial gauge on the body of the instrument. If
the indenter completely penetrates the sample, a reading
of 0 is obtained and if no penetration occurs, a reading
of 100 results. This test is not a good predictor of other
properties such as tensile strength, abrasion resistance or
wear resistance and is generally used in concert with other
tests for product specification.
A more recent addition to the static hardness test group is
the Ultrasonic Contact Impedance (UCL) test, which uses
a vibrating rod with a diamond indentor pressed against
the surface of the material under test. The device changes
the frequency of vibration as the indenter penetrates
the surface, with the frequency related to the material’s
hardness, see Figure A3.1.6p. Because this test instrument
relies on a dynamic vibrational interaction between the
rod and surface under test to measure hardness, it could
also be considered a dynamic hardness test.
The UCL test can be used on a wide range of materials,
including metals, ceramics, some plastics and composites.
This instrument is particularly suited to the testing of thin
materials.
Dynamic HARDNESS
The dynamic hardness tests are rebound tests in which an
indenter falls from a standard height, with the change in
rebound measured. These tests depend on elastic recovery
of the test surface and use high rates of loading.
The Shore Scleroscope test measures the loss in kinetic
energy from a falling diamond-tipped metal ‘tup. The tup
is enclosed within a glass-fronted graduated column and the bottom of the column is placed against the surface to
be tested. When the tup is released from the top of the
column, the tup falls, hits the test surface and rebounds.
The height of rebound is recorded as the hardness (HSc).
The test equipment is light and portable, but must be held
vertically and test access is restricted by the height of the
column, see Figure A3.1.6q.
The Leeb hardness test (Figure A3.1.6r) uses a small pen-
shaped device containing a spring-loaded impact body.
During testing, a small permanent magnet within the
impact body passes through a coil in the impact device inducing a voltage proportional to the velocity. The
hardness calculated is a ratio of the velocities before and
after rebound. Values can be displayed as Leeb hardness (HL) or, more usually displayed as a Vickers, Rockwell or Brinell equivalent.
any conversion tables are available that allow hardness
measurements, undertaken with one method, to be
compared with hardnesses obtained with a different
method and to be equated approximately with tensile
strength. These tables are developed from test data for a
particular material, such as steel or aluminium and cannot
be extrapolated to include other materials not tested.
Elasticity of a material is a measure of its ability to stretch
under load and then return to its original dimensions
following the removal of that load. When a material
is uniaxially' loaded within its elastic region, a linear
relationship is obtained in which elongation is found to be
proportional to load. This relationship is known as Hooke’s
Law, named after the 17th-century English Scientist
Robert Hook. This relationship is often restated as ‘stress
is proportional to strain’ and is the straight portion of the
stress-strain curve, see Figure A3.1.6a.
Plasticity
The plasticity of a material is associated with elongation
behaviour that exceeds the elastic region. Continued
deformation past the elastic limit leads to more complex
deformation where the relationship between stress and
strain is no longer linear. On removal of the load, the
material no longer returns to its original dimensions but
instead retains some permanent deformation.
Plastic deformation of metals occurs as a result of atoms
moving relative to each other along crystallographic
planes of the metallic lattice by a process called slip.
Malleability
Although related to plasticity, malleability differs in that it
refers specifically to the abilityof a material tobe hammered
or pressed into different shapes without breaking, while
plasticity is the broader concept of a material’s ability to
deform permanently without breaking.
Ductility
Ductility is another aspect of plasticity, referring broadly
to a material’s ability to deform plastically under tensile
stress without fracturing. This means it can be stretched,
bent or otherwise shaped under tension before breaking.

A3.1.7
A composite represents a class of material whose
properties derive from the combination of two or more
materials that are bonded together such that each of the constituent materials contributes to an improvement in
mechanical, physical, chemical or electrical properties.
Bone and wood represent examples of composites in
nature. Bone is a composite of the ceramic hydroxyapatite
(calcium phosphate) and the natural polymer collagen,
contained within a gel-like matrix of protein. In bone,
the collagen is present as long fibres, which provide a
strong, flexible structure in much the same way that
individual fibres can be formed into a rope. Small crystals
of hydroxyapatite attached to the surface of the collagen
fibres provide stiffness. In addition, the collagen fibres are
arranged in concentric layers arounda central blood vessel,
with each layer of collagen counter-laid, that is, arranged
in opposite directions, as illustrated in Figure A3.1.7a. The
structure formed has many similarities with that found in
wood. Bone consequently exhibits the characteristics of
strength and stiffness while retaining some flexibility. 
One of the earliest man-made composite materials was
straw-reinforced bricks. At a much later time, cement,
gravel and steel bars were combined to form reinforced
concrete. Development of thin fibres and thermosetting
polymers led to the creation of fibre-reinforced materials
such as glass-reinforced polymers (GRP). More recent
examples are carbon-polymer and metal matrix
composites.
Increasingly, composites are being employed to provide
impmved properties, increase eflicienr.'y, reduce costs
and provide additional functionality. Sporting equipment
manufacturers, for example, have employed modern
composite materials for some time for their improved
strength-to-weight ratio and stiffness compared to
conventional materials. Typical applications include
bicycle frames, golf clubs, tennis racquets (see Figure
A3.1.7b), rowing shells, etc.
Similarly, aerospace industries have invested heavily
in composites to reduce weight while maintaining
or improving material performance. The Boeing 787
Dreamliner, for example, contains over 50% composite
material (80% by volume) and for the first time uses
a carbon fibre composite to form the fuselage of a
commercial jet. The fuselage is constructed from carbon
fibre composite barrels, approximately 6 m (20 ft) in
length. An indication of the extent of composites within
the Boeing 787 is provided in Figure A3.1.7c.
‘The incorporation of the materials into a composite can
occur in several ways, but generally speaking, there are
three basic categories of composite:
« particle reinforced
« fibre-reinforced
« laminar
Particle-reinforced composites
These composites consist of a relatively soft matrix in
which hard particles of relatively uniform dimensions
are embedded. As illustrated in Figure A3.1.7d, these
particles may be symmetrical or asymmetrical in shape.
The particle reinforced composites generally exhibit
isotropic properties, that s, their physical and mechanical
properties, such as stiffness, strength, conductivity, etc.,
are the same in all directions.
Composites within this group include:
« Concrete in which gravel is mixed in a cement matrix
(Figure A3.1.7¢)
« Cemented carbide in which a ceramic such as tungsten
carbide (WC) is contained within a cobalt matrix
(Figure A3.1.7f)
« Spheroidal graphite (5.G.) cast iron (Figure A3.1.7g).
This group can also include dispersion-strengthened
materials in which powders are mixed and formed to
near net shape using powder metallurgy techniques under
conditions of high temperature and pressure. An example
of this would be TDNickel superalloy that incorporates
Thorium particles dispersed in a nickel matrix.
Fibre-reinforced composites
Fibre-reinforced composites are composed of fibres
embedded in a matrix. The matrix material, in powder or
liquid form, is combined with reinforcing fibres in a mould
and the combination subjected to heat and pressure until
fusion occurs.
Different materials can serve as the matrix, including
metals, ceramics and plastics, but it is the bond obtained
between the fibre and the matrix that provides the strength
of the composite.
In this respect, the aspect ratio (ratio of length to diameter)
of the fibres plays an important part, with fibres of high
aspect ratio creating a stronger composite.
Fibres are excellent in tension, although individual
fibres cannot support compressive forces. To have fibres
more readily accept compressive loads, they are ‘glued
together’ within a matrix (usually an epoxy resin). In this
structure, the fibres work together to accept compressive
loads without moving apart and yet retain their excellent
tensile properties. As a general rule, strength increases as
the volume of fibres increases, up to approximately 80%.
After this level of fibre reinforcement, the matrix cannot
be guaranteed to fully encase all the fibres.
Fibres within composites may differ in length and
arrangement. They are commonly divided into five
fibre arrangement types as shown in Figure A3.1.7h, a)
continuous unidirectional, b) continuous orthogonal
layers, ¢) continuous multiple fibres, d) discontinuous
unidirectional, e) discontinuous random orientation.
Reinforced concrete represents perhaps the most
commonly utilised composite material of the continuous
fibre, orthogonal orientation type, where ribbed steel
reinforcing bars (rebar) or wire mesh is embedded into 
matrix of concrete. Developed in France in the mid-1800s,
the composite formed results in a material that has both
the compressive strength of concrete with the improved
tensile strength due to the presence of the steel reinforcing.
Reinforced concrete is typically made on-site, where it is
used to form a slab for building foundations, along with
bridge and roadway pylons.
A significant development occurred in the 1920s, again in
France, with the development of ‘prestressed’ concrete.
This material uses continuous, unidirectional rebar or
high-strength wire stands for reinforcement and is made
off-site for later transport. The rebars or wire strands are
placed in tension within a mould before the concrete
is poured and the tensile stress is maintained until the
concrete has cured. When the stress on the tendons is
released, they exert an internal compressive force on the
surrounding concrete matrix, creating a ‘pre-stressed’
state. By introducing compressive stresses into the
concrete before it’s subjected to external loads, the tensile
stresses that the component is to be subjected to are
effectively balanced.
Because it is manufactured off-site, the use of prestressed
concrete allows increased efficiency of construction and
reduced labour costs, see Figure A3.1.7i.
Since the early 1980s, the aerospace industry has made
increasing use of fibre-reinforced polymer composite
parts in aircraft construction. In general terms, advanced
composites have found increasing application in
commercial aircraft structures as a result of the strength,
stiffness, fatigue resistance, corrosion-resistant and weight
benefits they afford. Safety and reliability are high priorities
for integrating composite components into commercial
aircraft structures. In developing the A380 passenger aircraft, Airbus Industries designed an aircraft to carry
555 passengers, using up to 40 tonnes of composite parts
as a means of reducing weight and operating costs. The
twin-deck A380 model even uses advanced carbon fibre
composites in the construction of new wing sections.
Sports equipment has also made extensive use of fibre-
reinforced plastics to increase strength and stiffness while
reducing weight. Figure A3.1.7j shows a section through
a tennis racket that was reinforced with both glass and
carbon fibres.
Concrete composites have also been produced using short
discrete fibres and are known as fibre-reinforced concrete
(FRC). These composites incorporate the fibres randomly
throughout the concrete to increase its structural integrity.
The fibre used can vary from steel to glass, depending on
the properties desired. An example of such a reinforcement
is shown in Figure A3.1.7k, where the natural fibre Sisal
has been used as the reinforcement.
Other composites within the fibre reinforced group
include free machining steels, in which high additions of
sulphur are made in order to promote the formation of
a large number of manganese sulphide (MnS) inclusions
during solidification. During the rolling process, these
inclusions are deformed into fibres (known as stringers)
aligned with the rolling direction, as shown in Figure
A3.1.71
During machining operations, these fibres act as points of
weakness that allow the swarf created during machining
to break into small pieces, keeping the cutting tool free of
tangled swarf.
Flake (also known as grey) cast iron consists of randomly
dispersed flakes of graphite in a metal matrix of iron,
see Figure A3.1.7m. The graphite flakes are not strictly
fibres, but interconnected flakes similar in nature to flat
discs and which appear fibre-like when viewed in a two-
dimensional section.
Laminar-reinforced composites
Laminar materials consist of two or more layers of material
bonded together, usually with an adhesive, to form a new
composite material with improved properties.
The most commonly recognised laminar material is
plywood, although many other materials are produced
through lamination, shown in Figure A3.1.7n.
Plywood is most often manufactured by bonding an
uneven number of thin wood veneer sheets (‘plys’)
together using strong adhesives, typically in an alternating
cross-grain pattern, before being hot pressed to cure the
adhesive bond. The cross-grain pattern results in the
grain direction of each layer being perpendicular to the
grain of the adjacent layers. This construction contributes
significantly to plywood’s superior resistance to bending,
warping and splitting. These properties lead to its use in
applications where high-quality, high-strength and large-
sheet material is required.
Plywood finds uses both indoors and outdoors and may
also be used as an engineering material for architecture
or lightweight stressed-skin applications such as these
required in marine and aviation environments.
Figure A3.1.70 shows a chair by Danish designer Greta
Jalk, manufactured using laminated and bent plywood.
Laminated glass, also known as ‘safety glass’ (introduced
for car windscreens in 1927), consists of a sandwich of
two layers of glass and a polymer interlayer of polyvinyl
butyral (PVB) joined under heat and pressure in a furnace
called an autoclave, as illustrated in Figure A3.1.7p. If
manufactured using annealed glass, laminated glass can be
cut. When broken, the PVB interlayer holds the pieces of
glass together, avoiding the release of otherwise dangerous
shards of glass. The fracture of laminated glass produces
a characteristic pattern of radial and concentric cracks,
often described as a spider-web pattern, represented in
Figure A3.1.7q.
Laminated glass will flex before shattering and is strong
but not as strong as tempered glass which is produced
by heat treatment, during which the glass is heated to a
temperature of appropriately 650 °C and then subjected
to an air quench to rapidly cool the surface, As the surface
cools and contracts, it is placed under compression relative
to the interior as illustrated in Figure 3.1.7r.
Glass in this form is considered to be in the tempered
condition and exhibits two to three times the breaking
strength and impact resistance of ordinary annealed
glass. However, because toughened glass relies on the
integrity of the surface compression zone, it cannot be
cut. All forming, shaping and cutting operations must be
performed before the tempering operation is performed.
Breaching of the compressive layer results in fracture. In
this instance, fracture results in the formation of numerous
small and relatively uniform rounded particles (dice),
greatly reducing the potential for injury. For this reason,
tempered glass is also often referred to as ‘safety’ glass.
The primary disadvantage of this fracture pattern is
that vision can be obscured, making it unsuitable for
windscreens, although it is often used in automobile side
windows.
Laminar composites may also be laminates of different
materials joined together in a ‘sandwich’ fashion. This
sandwich structure may consist of layers of thin uni-
or bidirectional fibres or a metal sheet held apart by a
lightweight core (foam or honeycomb-style structure).
This allows the designer to achieve the required inertia or
stiffness.
Plain bearings®, used in crankshafts and consisting of a
‘white metal’ surface layer bonded to a copper intermediate
layer on a low carbon steel backing plate, also represent a
laminar composite. Figure A3.1.7s shows one of a pair of
plain bearings.
Figure A3.1.7t shows a ceramic composite sandwich
developed by NASA, composed of a ceramic foam core
between two ceramic composite face sheets.
This structural concept provides high strength and
stiffness at low density -1.06 g/cm’. Choice of fibres and
weave structures allow for variable coefficients of thermal
expansion and elasticity.
The integrated structures possible with this composite
could eliminate the need for non-load-bearing thermal
protection systems in addition to structural components,
thus reducing weight and cost for aerospace missions.
The ubiquitous cardboard box is also a laminar composite
as it consists of fluted corrugated sheets of paper
sandwiched between two flat paper liner boards, as
shown in Figure A3.1.7u. Its properties are gained from its structure, in which the honeycomb-like core provides
strength while the liner boards fix the core in position.
Laminar textiles can also be produced by the lamination
process, Textile laminates can be built up using what are
known as ‘prepregs, which consist of a textile that has
been pre-impregnated with resin. These prepregs can then
be stacked in any orientation to each other to build up a
composite with the desired combination of properties.
An example of a laminar textile is the sailcloth shown
in Figure A3.1.7v that consists of layers of carbon fibre
sandwiched between a polymer film and taffeta. Sails
made from this laminate find use in racing yachts due to
their properties of low stretch, strength and durability.
Textiles, along with glass and carbon fibres, are commonly
used to create woven preforms that are later bonded with
resin. An example of woven carbon fibre is provided in
Figure A3.1.7w. By using the weaving process, large
quantities of flexible matting can be produced that can be cut to size and positioned to follow the contours of a
mould to create complicated shapes.
extile-Reinforced Concrete (TRC), with textiles in
woven, knitted or non-woven form replacing traditional
steel reinforcement as shown in Figure A3.1.7x. This
allows for thinner, more flexible concrete structures.

A3.1.8
PiEZOELECTRICITY
The piezoelectric effect and thus piezoelectric materials,
were discovered by the French physicists Pierre and
Jacques Curie in 1880. They observed that when pressure
was applied to certain crystals, like quartz, a small electric
charge would be generated on their surfaces.
Similarly, when an electric current is passed through a
piezoelectric material, a shape change occurs in response.
By reversing the polarity of the electrical signal, the shape
again changes. An alternating current (AC) can therefore
produce a shape change from the rest state of the material.
Piezoelectric materials are widely used as sensors in
different environments. Quartz (SiO,) is a commonly
used piezoelectric material and has been extensively used
in watches. Some ceramics are also used, such as barium
titanate (BaTiO,) and lead zirconate titanate (Pb[Zr Ti,_]
O,), known as PZT.
Piezoelectric materials are widely used for the thickness
testing of pipes and vessels and the detection of internal
flaws by ultrasonic testing.
In ultrasonic testing, an alternating voltage is supplied
to a probe containing a piezoelectric crystal. The voltage
stimulates the crystal to change dimensions (vibrate),
producing sound waves of a defined frequency. When the
probe is placed on the test surface, the sound waves travel
into the material and are reflected from the other side of
the test piece and any internal discontinuities.
‘When these returning sound waves reach the probe surface,
the vibrations stress the piezoelectric crystal sufficiently
to produce an electrical voltage in response. The initial
sound pulse and the returning signal are recorded on an
oscillograph screen, allowing visual interpretation by a
technician. Depending on the material and construction
under test, one or two probes may be used, as illustrated
in Figure A3.1.8b.
 typical ultrasonic test unit is shown in Figure A3.1.8¢
along with an assortment of probes.
Shape Memory
Shape Memory is a property exhibited by certain metal
alloys capable of recovering their initial shape when they
are heated after having been plastically deformed. Shape
Memory Alloys (SMAs) exhibit pseudo-elasticity and the
shape memory effect due to rearrangement of the atomic
lattice.
Discovered in the 1930s, the shape memory alloys
represent a group of materials that are capable of changing
shape or size in a predetermined manner by undergoing a
solid-state phase change at a defined temperature.
Despite creating a great deal of interest, these alloys
remained largely a laboratory curiosity until the 1960s
when the NiTi alloy Nitinol (an acronym of the Nickel
and Titanium Naval Ordinance Laboratory) was
produced. Due to its excellent mechanical properties and
biocompatibility, Nitinol has since become one of the most
widely used shape memory alloys. Nitinol is nonmagnetic
and consists of almost equal amounts of Ni and Ti in a
simple cubic lattice arrangement.
One of the defining characteristics of the shape memory
alloys is pseudo-elasticity.
Pseudo-elasticity
If a shape memory alloy is in the stable lattice (crystal)
arrangement of the austenite phase (representing a high
temperature arrangement of atoms) and is exposed to
mechanical stress, slip can occur, leading to distortion of
the lattice and the formation of martensite.
It should be noted here that while Eek
austenite is commonly a face-centred Ha
cubic (FCC) structure, it can also be i
body-centred cubic (BCC), with the
specific structure depending on the alloy composition and temperature. See the Wikipedia
article linked by the QR code for an explanation of these
terms. For example, Nitinol, a common SMA, exhibits a
BCC austenite phase. Because it is the source of the shape
memory effect, it is often referred to as the ‘mother’ or
‘parent’ phase.
‘The low-temperature phase known as martensite is crucial
for the shape memory effect and usually has a different
lattice structure than austenite, which can be tetragonal,
orthorhombic or monoclinic.
Normally, a reduction in temperature would be required
to achieve the change from austenite to martensite, but
under the influence of an applied stress, martensite
formation can occur without the involvement of a
temperature change and is referred to as deformation
martensite, This change in structure is accompanied by
an apparent non-linear deformation, typically associated
with plastic deformation.
‘When the applied stress is removed, however, the atomic
lattice returns to the austenite phase and the material
returns to its original shape. Figure A3.8.1d illustrates
this phenomenon in which an applied stress essentially
increases the temperature at which martensite will form
and shows the reversible change back to austenite on the
removal of the applied stress.
Pseudo-elasticity can be represented symbolically as
shown below in Figure A3.8.1e.
Austenitic [+fitce Martensitic - focee Austenitic
Structure Structure T Structure
stimulus remove stimulus
Figure A3.1.8e Symbolic representation of Pseudo-
elasticity. Adapted from Dunne, D.P. (2000). Functional
rmemory metals. 
Shape memory effect
Shape memory alloys (SMA) also exhibit pseudo-
elasticity by way of a reversible martensite transformation.
In this case, however, removal of the applied stress does
not result in the material returning to the austenite phase
and its original shape. This change requires moderate
heating as indicated in Figure A3.1.8f.
At elevated temperatures, these alloys have an austenitic
structure that is relatively strong. On cooling, these alloys
undergo a phase transformation to martensite, which
is relatively soft. The unique character of these alloys is
seen when the alloy is formed to shape in the austenitic
condition and then cooled to martensite. If another
forming operation is then performed while it is in the
martensitic condition, changing the shape of the material,
it will maintain that shape until reheated to austenite, at
which time the alloy reverts to its previous shape.
Some alloys can even change back when the temperature
has again been reduced, resulting in a two-way memory
effect. These alloys can therefore cycle between shapes with
changes in temperature as indicated in Figure A 3.1.8h. As
can be seen from this Figure, the transformation between
states has some hysteresis.
Several shape memory alloys have been developed, which
operate at a variety of different temperatures, as indicated
below in Figure A3.1.8g. Precise control of the material
compositionis required to ensure the reliable reproduction
of the transformation temperatures.
The shape memory effect can be illustrated as shown in Figure A3.1.8h, in which the phase changes have been represented at each stage along with the changes that would be seen with an arterial stent made from the shap memory alloy Nitinol.
The SMAs are considered to be passive response type
devices, as they do not require separate sensors to operate.
They react directly in response to the external stimulus,
as opposed to the ER and MR fluids and piezoelectric
materials that can be considered active response devices.
In this capacity of passive response, the pseudo-elastic
materials have found use as eyeglass frames and antennas
for mobile phones.
Shape memory alloys have been used in products such
as electrical connectors, safety taps to prevent hot water
scalding children, vascular stents to repair blocked
arteries, airflow controls, orthodontic arch wires and
components of eyeglass frames.
The excellent biocompatibility of Nitinol, combined
with its non-magnetic nature and excellent mechanical
properties, has resulted in its extensive use in biomedical
applications such as coronary stents.
Some use of SMAs has been made in passive dampening
systems to mitigate earthquake damage. The roof of the St
Francis Basilica in Assisi has been joined to the wall with
Nitinol anchors consisting of wire bundles that stretch in
response to vibrations, dissipating the seismic loads.
PHoTOCHROMICITY
Photochromic materials undergo a reversible
photochemical reaction that results in darkening
proportional to the level of exposure to UltraViolet
(UV) light. These materials are most commonly seen in
photochromatic (also known as transition) lenses and may
be made from glass or polymer, such as polycarbonate. An
example of this effect is shown in Figure A3.1.8i,in which
one lens was initially covered and the other exposed to
sunlight for 30 seconds.
Glass lenses have molecules of silver chloride (AgCl)
within the silica crystal structure of the glass. The presence
or otherwise of UV light determines the direction in which
a reversible oxidation-reduction reaction, shown in Figure.
A3.1.8j and representing a Le Chatelier type chemical
reaction of the type indicated below, will proceed.
When the level of UV radiation exposure is low, light
passes through largely unimpeded. When exposed to
UV light, however, the reaction proceeds to the right,
forming colloidal silver that acts to absorb up to 80% of
the incident light and leading to the observed darkening
of the lens. On removal of the lens from exposure to the
UV light, the reaction reverses direction and the silver
recombines with the chloride ions to form AgCl and
the glass clears. The photochromatic lenses made from
polycarbonate, by contrast, have a coating of an organic
molecule, which changes shape on exposure to UV light,
leading to darkening of the lens and which reverses when
exposure to UV light decreases.
ELECTRO-RHEOSTATIC AND MAGNETO-
RHEOSTATIC MATERIALS
Electro-rheostatic (ER) and magneto-rheostatic (MR)
materials are fluids that can change their viscosity,
becoming semi-solid, when exposed to an electric or
magnetic field, respectivelp. When the applied field is
removed, the materials return to their original fluid state.
These materials are most commonly colloidal suspensions®
within oil. Because of this, one of many considerations
in their use is the length of time the particles remain in
suspension. As a general rule, as the particle size decreases,
time in suspension increases.
When an electric field (in the case of ER materials) or
magnetic field (in the case of MR materials) is applied, the
suspended particles align themselves with the field lines.
The shear yield strength of the fluids is then determined
by the strength of the applied field, such that varying the
field strength can control the viscosity.
Because of this reversible control of viscosity, ER and MR
materials can act as switches, allowing the control of fluids
in real-time.
The ER and MR fluid must, however, satisfy many
criteria:
low toxicity
» non-abrasive
« non-corrosive
« long storage life
« long working life
« high boiling point
« low freezing point
« particles must remain as a colloidal suspension and
not settle out.
The control over viscosity has allowed the ER and MR
fluids to find uses in clutches, hydraulic valves and shock
absorbers. The 2002 Cadillac Seville STS and second-
generation Audi TT automobiles have used MR fluids in
their suspension systems, while the National Museum of
Emerging Science in Tokyo, Japan, has installed MR fluid
devices throughout the building to reduce shock loading
from high winds and seismic activity.
Potential uses include suggestions for medical devices
to prevent sleep apnea, in which the windpipe closes
during sleep, and devices to deliver cardiac massage.
Haptic applications, such as a Braille Tablet and gloves,
provide tactile stimuli to accompany computer-generated
scenarios.
THERMOELECTRICITY
Thermoelectric materials exhibit the feature that, when
exposed to a temperature differential, an electric potential
is created with the phenomenon known as the Seebeck
Effect. Conversely, when exposed to a differential electric
potential, a temperature change occurs and is known as
the Peltier effect.
Thermoelectric devices offer the prospect of developing
power generators, cooling systems and waste heat recovery
systems with no moving parts or polluting waste.

A3.1.9
iomaterials, especially bioplastics, can offer improved
circularity in a product’s life cycle and a reduced carbon
footprint, because they are derived from renewable
sources like plants and microorganisms. These features
give biomaterials a significant advantage over traditional
fossil fuel-based materials in a circular economy.
Biodegradationisaprocess during whichliving organisms,
primarily microorganisms like bacteria and fungi, break
down materials into simpler non-toxic substances. While
not all biomaterials will biodegrade, many will. This
provides a valuable end-of-life option, preventing waste
from being sent to landfill or incineration. See Figure
A3.19a

A3.2.1
In the natural world, structural design is inextricably
linked to survival. Phenomena such as the tensile geometry
of a spider’s web, the hexagonal cells within a beehive,
and the spiralling configuration of the DNA double helix
demonstrate nature’s ability to fuse functionality with
aesthetic precision.
Similarly, in the built environment, architectural and
engineering structures influence the human experience.
Bridges that appear to defy gravitational forces, residential
structures that offer refuge from environmental hazards,
and urban parks designed to promote well-being and
social interaction all underscore the interplay between
form, function and cultural intent.
Solid Structures
These are formed from a single mass of dense and strong
material, designed to resist forces by sheer volume.
In Nature, bones and teeth are solid structures built
from calcium phosphate and collagen. Tree trunks and
coral reefs are natural solids designed for support and
anchoring.
In the built environment, dams and retaining walls
are solid structures that rely on their bulk for strength.
Ancient monuments like the pyramids of Giza are iconic
examples, being both massive and enduring.
Frame Structures
Composed of interconnected elements (like beams or
rods), these structures form a skeleton that supports
loads.
In Nature, the skeletal system of vertebrates serves as
a framework, providing both movement and support.
Leaf veins create a structural framework that transports
nutrients while giving shape.
In the built environment, skyscrapers and bridges use steel
or reinforced concrete frames to support immense loads.
Furniture, bicycles and scaffolding all rely on frame design
for efficiency and flexibility.
Shell Structures
These are thin, curved surfaces that enclose a space,
distributing forces across the entire shape.
In Nature. eggshells, seashells and exoskeletons protect
and support using minimal material but optimal shapes.
The dome of a tortoise’s shell or the curved carapaces of
beetles are examples of natural engineering.
In built environments, domes, vaults, and even modern
stadium roofs exemplify shell structures. They are light
yet strong. Both vehicles and aircraft often employ shell
construction to strike a balance between aerodynamics
and strength.
Structures in their totality often exist as a combination of
structural subgroups: for example, a bird’s bone (frame)
covered by muscle and feathers (shell-like), all optimised
for flight.
Human builders do the same - blending solid foundations,
skeletal frames and lightweight shells for architecture
that's both durable and aesthetically inspiring.

A3.2.2
Frame Structures
Frame structures consist of a skeleton-like framework that
supports various loads. Examples include luggage trolleys,
greenhouse frames, warehouse racking or cranes. The
structural elements are often arranged in triangles or other
shapes that provide strength with minimal material.
In product design, frame structures are:
« favoured for their lightweight strength
« used in furniture, tents and aircraft seats
« chosen when maximum rigidity with minimal weight
and material cost is required
Shell Structures
Shells are hollow and curved structures that distribute
loads across their surfaces. Examples include egg shells,
car body panels or motorcycle helmets. Even with little
internal structure, their shapes still provides them with
remarkable strength.
In product design, shells are:
« ideal for enclosing and protecting components, such as
in containers, appliances or cars
« designed to provide an aesthetic that hides and protects
internal components and, in some contexts, provides
aerodynamic benefits or degrees of watertightness
« employed to efficiently distribute loads across a surface
without internal support.
Solid Structures
Solid structures are made from one piece of material or
multiple parts fused into a single mass. They are common
in both product design and architecture, where durability,
stability and resistance to external forces are critical.
Examples include hammer heads, injection moulds or
stone statues. They're extremely strong but tend to be
heavy and use a lot of material.
In product design, solid structures are:
« found in tools, machine parts or moulds
« ideal where durability and impact resistance matter
more than weight or material cost
« resistant to heavy forces without deforming easily.
Combining structures
Designers often combine these structures to get the best
combination of properties. Examples include:
Motor vehicles
« Frame: Steel chassis supports mechanical components
and absorbs impacts.
« Shell: Outer body panels (e.g. doors, roof) made from
stamped metal or composites for aerodynamics and
aesthetics.
« Solid: Engine block, transmission housing and other
dense components machined from solid metal,
3D Printers
Frame: Rigid aluminium or steel structure ensures
precision.
Shell: Enclosure panels for safety and dust protection.
Solid: Print head, motor housings and build plate
components.
Modern Buildings
Frame: Steel or reinforced concrete skeleton supports
floors and roof.
Shell: Glass curtain walls or cladding panels for weather
protection, insulation and aesthetics.
Solid: Foundation, elevator shafts and stairwells made
from concrete.
Figure A 3.2.2 shows an Airbus A380 that has a frame
structure (fuselage skeleton), solid components (landing
gear) and a shell skin for aerodynamics.

A3.2.3
Simply Supported Beams
These are beams supported at both ends, typically resting
freely on supports, such as a bridge over two piers. They
carry loads applied in the middle or along the length
and transfer that force to the supports. They are good for
evenly distributing loads.
veryday examples include a bookshelf resting on brackets
at each end, benches with beam-like seats supported at
each end by legs or frames and floor joists using timber or
steel beams that span between walls or girders.
Fixed Beams
Fixed beams are rigidly attached at both ends, so they can’t
rotate or shift. Designed to resist bending and movement,
they distribute loads more efficiently along their length.
The result is less sagging compared to simply supported
beams. Applications include contexts that require high
rigidity and minimal deflection.
Everyday examples include structural components
embedded in concrete or welded into place. Solar panel
mounts use fixed beams in ground-mounted arrays to
resist wind loads and maintain alignment. Tunnel linings
employ fixed beam segments to help maintain structural
integrity under the pressure of large overburdens.
Cantilever Beams
A cantilever extends out from a single fixed point,
unsupported at the other end. Perfect for overhanging
structures, they carry load over free space without needing
a support on the outer end and provide opportunities for
bold, gravity-defying designs.
Everyday examples include residential building balconies,
diving boards and aeroplane wings cantilevered from
the fuselage. Early aircraft had light structures that were
braced with wires and struts. However, these introduced
aerodynamic drag, which limited performance. While
heavier, the cantilever avoids this issue and allows the
plane to fly faster.
In architectural applications, cantilevered staircases with
steps anchored into a wall with ne visible support beneath
them create sleek and space-saving designs.
Columns
Unlike horizontal beams, columns are vertical structural
members, They transfer compressive loads from above,
such as the weight of a roof or upper floors, and channels
that force down to the foundations and add to the stability
of the structure.
Examples include structures such as classical Greek
temples, using Doric, Ionic and Corinthian columns to
define the aesthetic and structural rhythm. Furniture
applications include table and chair legs. In residential
homes, timber or steel columns often replace load-bearing
walls to create open-plan layouts, Figure A 3.2.4c shows the engaged columns embedded in
the side walls of the cella (small chamber) of the Maison
Carrée in Nimes, France. Dating from the 1st century AD,
it is the only fully preserved temple of the ancient world.

A3.2.4
Note: As introduced in Section A 3.2.1, all structures,
such as bridges, are designed and built considering
the same fundamental forces, e.g. tensile, compressive,
shear, bending and torsional. These structural forces are
categorised as either static or dynamic.
Static vs. Dynamic Forces
Static forces are constant and unchanging over time. Also
known as deadloads, they include forces derived from the
mass of a structure. They maybe long or short term. They
may change slowly but involve no impact or vibration.
Examples include the force of a building’s roof pressing
down on the walls, furniture such as desks, bookshelves
and beds exerting a constant force on the floor or a parked
car resting on a bridge or a garage floor, etc.
Dynamic or live forces are changing or moving forces,
including shock, impact, or vibration (e.g. periodic,
random, transient. Examples include loads from vehicular
and pedestrian traffic, vehicle braking, expansion and
contraction, wind, water from stream flow and ground
movements, etc.
Compression
Forces applied to an object that try to squash or reduce
the object in size are identified as compressive forces.
Examples include forces on columns, arches and load-
bearing walls. The legs of a chair in use are pushed down
by your weight, placing the chair legs into a state of
compression.
Tension
Tensile forces are those applied to a body that attempt
to stretch or make the body longer. Therefore, tension
is a pulling force that stretches or elongates a material.
Examples include: suspension cables, ropes, tie rods or a
rope in a tug-of-war battle that is being pulled from both
ends.
Torsion
A twisting force that rotates a structure or object around
its axis. Acts on: Drive shafts, door handles, and structural
beams under torque. Examples include twisting a
screwdriver or wringing out a wet towel.
Bending
Bending forces cause a material to curve, i.e. a combination
of tension and compression on opposite sides. A wooden
ruler, when pushed down at one end, demonstrates the
principle of bending. Examples include bridges, beams
and diving boards,
Shear
A force that slides layers of material against each other in
opposite directions is known as a shear force. Shear stresses
are generated in a material by the action of these forces.
Shear stresses may be single, double or punching stress.
Examples of shear forces include lateral forces on bolts
and the act of trimming paper or fabric with scissors.
Tensile, compressive and shear forces are most often
measured in megapascals.

A3.2.5
Note: Stress and strain were introduced in Chapter B 3.2.2,
and a fully labelled stress-strain diagram can be seen in
Figure B 3.2.2a.
Stress (o) is the relationship between an applied load and
a material’s cross-sectional area, measured in pascals (Pa).
Pascals are a unit of pressure and reflect a force of one
Newton per square metre or N/; 'm?. In some calculations,
cross-sectional area is determined in mm’, giving stress in
terms of N/mm? (N mm™) or megapascals.
Strain (g) is an engineering quantity that measures the
change in length of a body relative to its original length
when subjected to a load. Strain is a dimensionless
quantity.
Young’s Modulus is the ratio of stress to strain within a
material’s elastic region.
It quantifies a material’s stiffness or how resistant it is to
elastic deformation. Young’s Modulus is shown graphically
as the slope of the straight-line portion at the beginning
of a stress-strain graph. As an example, steel has a high
Young’s Modulus (very stiff) while rubber has a very low
one.
Yield Strength is defined as the stress at which a material
begins to deform plastically. It indicates the transition from
reversible (elastic) to irreversible (plastic) deformation.
On a stress-strain graph, it is the point where the curve
starts to bend away from the straight line. After this
point, the material won't return to its original shape when
unloaded.
Ultimate Strength, or tensile strength, is the maximum
stress the material can withstand. It is also the peak load-
bearing capacity before necking begins. On a stress-strain
graph, it is the highest point on the stress-strain curve.
Beyond this point, the material starts to weaken and
deform rapidly.
'The fracture point indicates total failure of the material. It
is the last reading on the stress-strain graph.

A3.2.6
In simple terms, Young’s Modulus, also known as the
modulus of elasticity, is a measure of how stiff a material is.
It describes how much a material will stretch or compress
under an applied stress. The higher the Young’s Modulus,
the stiffer the material. When materials are placed under
stress, their Young’s Modulus plays a significant role in
predicting how they behave.
Materials with a high Young’s Modulus are:
« stiff and resistant to deformation: these materials, like
steel or carbon fibre, barely stretch or compress when
a force is applied
« efficient at supporting loads: they transfer forces
effectively without much shape change
« used in load-bearing structures: bridges, skyscraper
frames, golf club shafts, sailing masts and aircraft
wings often rely on them.
Materials with a low Young’s Modulus are:
« flexible and elastic, e.g. rubber or soft plastics, they
deform noticeably under stress
« better at absorbing energy, making them ideal for
damping vibrations or cushioning impacts
« used where flexibility is needed. Joints, seals and shock
absorbers rely on this property.
The following table shows a range of structural materials
organised from low to high modulus of elasticity, along
with typical applications for each:
Note: Ceramics and advanced materials are extremely
stiff but often brittle, limiting their structural usefulness,
unless carefully designed.
Composites can be engineered to achieve specific modulus
values depending on fibre orientation and matrix.
Designers and engineers choose materials with the
appropriate modulus based on how much rigidity or
flexibility a structure needs. Specifying materials with
an inappropriate Young's Modulus can lead to premature
failure or inefficient performance. For example, in
buildings in earthquake zones, a balance of both rigidity
and flexibility ensures strength and some flexibility to
absorb shock loads.

A3.2.7
 structure is said to be in equilibrium when all the
forces and moments (torques) acting on it are balanced.
This means the structure is stable and not accelerating or
rotating unexpectedly. There are two key conditions for
equilibrium:
1 Translational Equilibrium: The sum of all forces
acting on the structure in every direction must be
zero, This means that the sum of all vertical forces
equals zero, and the sum of all horizontal forces
equals zero.
In equation form, it reads: ~ ZF, =0 and ZF =0
This ensures the structure doesn’t move linearly.
2 Rotational Equilibrium: The sum of all moments
(torques) about any point must also be zero.
In equation form, this reads: M =0
This prevents any unwanted spinning or tipping.
Therefore, for a body to be in equilibrium then, as we have
already observed:
F,=0 and IF,= 0
A structure will fail when these conditions of equilibrium
are not met. Simply put, this means there is an imbalance.
Common causes for this include:
« Overloading: Ifthe forces applied exceed the structure’s
strength, it can collapse or deform.
« Uneven force distribution: If loads aren't spread
evenly, parts of the structure may experience excessive
stress or torque.
« Foundation instability: If the supporting base
structure or foundations shift or sink, a disruption in
the force balance will occur.
» Dynamic impacts: Sudden forces like earthquakes
or wind gusts can throw a system out of equilibrium
faster than it can respond.

A3.2.8
Strengthening structures involves clever use of geometry,
material science and engineering principles. The
following paragraphs demonstrate how structures can
be strengthened through the application of a range of
techniques, including the use of struts, careful shape
selection, lamination and the use of composite materials,
contributing to stronger and more resilient structures.
Struts: Strength through support
Struts are structural components designed to resist
compression and provide support. They do this by:
« Distributing loads: Struts help spread forces across a
wider area, reducing stress concentration.
« Preventing buckling: In frameworks like trusses,
struts stabilise long members that might otherwise
bend or buckle.
« Adding rigidity: They increase stiffness in lightweight
structures (e.g., bicycle frames, bridges).
Examples of applications include truss bridges, aircraft
landing gear and roof supports in buildings.
Case study: Struts strengthening structures
In many buildings, such as schools, houses and small
airports, the roof structure must span large open spaces
without internal columns. One common solution is the
use of a triangular roof truss, which relies heavily on struts
to maintain strength and stability.
A rectangular frame forming the base of the roof, if
left unsupported, will easily deform under the weight
of roofing materials or wind loads. The introduction
of diagonal struts, in this case, compression members,
connecting opposite corners of the frame, transforms the
rectangle into a series of triangles, which are geometrically
stable and resist deformation.
TThe diagonal struts carry compressive forces, pushing
back against loads that would otherwise cause the frame
to collapse. By forming triangles, the structure distributes
forces more evenly and reduces stress on individual joints.
The struts allow the roof to span wider areas without
sagging or requiring vertical supports in the middle of the
space.
This principle is visible in many lightweight steel or timber
truss systems. For example, in the roof of a school hall,
you'll often see a series of triangular frames with diagonal
struts running between the top and bottom chords. These
struts are critical for maintaining the rigidity of the roof
under live loads like snow, wind or maintenance workers.
Shape:
Geometry as a reinforcement tool
The shape of a structure can dramatically affect its strength
and stability through the use of design elements such as:
« Triangles: Used in trusses because they are inherently
stable and resist deformation.
» Arches and domes: Efficiently transfer loads into
compression, ideal for bridges and roofs.
« Corrugation: Wavy or ribbed surfaces (like corrugated
metal) increase stiffness without adding weight.
« I-beams: Concentrate material where stress is highest
(top and bottom flanges), minimising weight.
Examples of applications include geodesic domes, vaulted
ceilings and structural beams in buildings.
Case study:
I-beams in the construction of high-rise towers
Few projects exemplify the strategic use of structural steel
elements as effectively as the Burj Khalifa in Dubai.
Standing at 828 meters, the tower posed unprecedented
