engineering challenges, particularly in terms of vertical
load management, lateral stability under wind and seismic
forces and the need for efficient material usage to mitigate
foundation stress.
A critical component of the Burj
Khalifa’s structural system was the
integration of steel I-beams within
its bundled tube design. These
beams were employed extensively
in the floor framing systems and as
reinforcement within the central
core walls. The choice of I-beams was not incidental; their
geometric configuration, characterised by wide flanges
and a central web, offered the optimal balance between
bending resistance and shear strength. This made them
particularly suitable for spanning long distances between
columns, thereby enabling open-plan layouts.
The high strength-to-weight ratio of steel I-beams
contributed significantly to the overall efficiency of the
structure. By reducing the need for excessive vertical
supports, the design team was able to minimise material
consumption while maintaining structural integrity. The
modular nature of I-beams also facilitated prefabrication
and expedited on-site assembly, assisting with the project’s
timeline.
Importantly, the incorporation of I-beams into the hybrid
steel-concrete framework allowed the Burj Khalifa to
achieve a level of dynamic performance that is rare
in buildings of this type. The structure is capable of
controlled sway under high wind conditions, absorbing
and redistributing forces without compromising occupant
safety or structural soundness.
Lamination: Layering for toughness
Lamination involves bonding multiple layers of material
together.
How lamination strengthens structures:
« Improves toughness: Layers can absorb and dissipate
energy, reducing crack propagation
« Enhances flexibility: Allows bending without
breaking, useful in curved or dynamic structures
« Combines properties: Different layers can serve
different functions (e.g, waterproofing, strength,
insulation).
Examples of applications include plywood (layers of
wood veneer), laminated glass (used in car windshields),
skateboards and surfboards
Case study: Laminating timber
Glued laminated timber (glulam) is a stress-rated
engineered wood product composed of wood laminations,
or ‘lams; that are bonded together with strong, waterproof
adhesives, Glulam components can be a variety of species,
and individual ‘lams’ are typically 50 mm or less in
thickness.
Glulam has greater strength and stiffness than comparable
dimensional lumber. Kilogram for kilogram, it is stronger
than steel. This means glulam beams can span long
distances with minimal need for intermediate supports.
Glulam eliminates the natural performance variations that
characterise solid sawn lumber. This is because glulam
beams are engineered wood products manufactured to
meet specific performance characteristics.
Laminates also include advanced composites, which are
generally defined as a family of lightweight structural
materials with reinforcing fibres such as carbon, aramid
or high-strength fibreglass that are embedded in a
polymer matrix. It is these continuous high-stiffness
strength fibres that distinguish advanced composites from
other reinforced materials. Composite bridge decks are
lightweight and highly resistant to corrosion, yet have
the strength and ability to carry high loads. They weigh
less than one-fifth the weight of a comparable concrete
slab and have greater durability with lower fatigue and
maintenance.
Cross-laminated timber (CLT) is an engineered timber
product consisting of dense solid panels of kiln-dried
wood, engineered for strength through laminations of
different layers. This innovative and versatile product
gains its increased rigidity and strength from the multiple
layers of boards placed crosswise to adjacent layers. CLT
is claimed, on a strength-to-weight basis, to have the same
structural integrity as reinforced concrete. Producing significantly less greenhouse gases than steel or
concrete during its manufacture, CLT is a cost-effective,
stable and durable alternative to traditional structural
building materials. CLT can be used for long spans and
assemblies, such as those found in floors, walls or roofs.
Cross-laminated timber (CLT) has emerged as a transformative material in contemporary archltecture, offering both structural integrity and environmental benefits. A pioneering example of it's applications is the Forte building in Victoria Harbour, Melbourne, completed in 2012. At the time of its construction, Forté was recognised
asthe tallest residential timber building globally, marking a
significant milestone in sustainable urban development.
Internationally, several landmark projects have further
demonstrated the versatility and scalability of CLT. In
London, Dalston Works stands as one of the largest CLT
structures ever constructed. This mixed-use development
exemplifies the material’s capacity to reduce construction
waste and carbon emissions while maintaining
architectural sophistication. Also in London, The Smile,
an installationby Alison Brooks Architects for the London
Design Festival, showcased CLT’s structural capabilities
through its dramatic cantilevered form, achieved without
supplementary support.
In Norway, Mjestdrnet in Brumunddal
redefined the limits of timber
construction. Rising to 85.4 meters, it
is currently the tallest timber building
in the world, constructed primarily
from CLT and glulam. The project
serves as a benchmark for sustainable
high-rise design and demonstrates the feasibility of timber
in large-scale vertical structures.
Australia continues to push the boundaries of timber
innovation with the forthcoming Atlassian Sydney
Headquarters. Upon completion, this hybrid timber tower
is projected to reach 180 meters, positioning it as the
tallest of its kind globally. CLT will play a central role in its
structural framework, reinforcing the material’s relevance
in next-generation architectural solutions. 
Composite Materials:
Tailored Strength
Composites combine two or more materials to create a
product with superior properties.
How composites strengthen structures:
» High strength-to-weight ratio: Ideal for aerospace
and automotive applications.
« Directional strength: Fibres can be oriented to resist
forces in specific directions.
« Corrosion resistance: Many composites are more
durable than metals in harsh environments.
Examples of applications include carbon fibre in aircraft
and racing cars, glass reinforced polymers (GRP) in boats,
bridges and sports equipment and reinforced concrete in
buildings and bridges.
Case study: Concrete as a composite
Concrete is a composite material made from sand, gravel,
cement and water. The cement is a mixture of various
minerals which, when mixed with water, hydrates and
rapidly becomes hard, binding the sand and gravel into
a solid mass (Figure A 3.2.8g). Concrete is a very flexible
building material: it can be poured into forms as a stiff
but workable mix, and it can be given any shape. Concrete
attains high compressive strength and resistance against
most natural attacks, though not against CO, or SO, in
polluted air, and is rather weak in tension. Concrete alone
is limited to structures that are only subject to compressive
stresses. Therefore, steel bars are introduced to improve
the tensile strength of concrete beams. Concrete structures
of this sort are said to be made of reinforced concrete.
A second method of resisting tensile forces in concrete
structures is by prestressing. High-strength steel wires or
cables are positioned in a mould and placed in tension.
Concrete is then poured into the mould, completely
covering the cables. After the concrete has hardened, the
tensile stress on the cables is released and, as the cable
attempts to return to its prestressed condition, compressive
stresses are transferred to the concrete. Figure A 3.2.8h
shows the steps involved in casting a beam of prestressed
concrete. In service, tensile forces must first reduce these
compressive stresses before actual tensile stresses are
generated.
As an alternative, post-stressing involves introducing
forces into the concrete after it has achieved a specified
strength. Stressing jacks are used to pull the strand
tendons (steel rods) to the required load, and anchorages
hold the tendons in tension. Post-tensioned concrete has
tendons that are tensioned usually on-site, allowing for
longer spans and thinner slabs in structures like bridges
and high-rise buildings.
Many ‘high-performance’ concrete structures require the
added strength gained from pre- and post-tensioning.
Bridges, skyscrapers, parking garages and many other
types of structures all employ this technique.
The table below provides a summary of methods of
improving structural strength, the mechanism by which it
operates, structural benefits and practical applications.

A3.2.9
Note: As identified in chapter B 3.2.5, the factor of safety
or safety factor is a complex concept having several
methods of calculation. In its simplest form, it could be
defined as the extent to which the design strength exceeds
the expected service loads. The approach most often used
in designing engineering structures is to consider the
relationship between the maximum UTS of the structure
or material and the design or working stress o. The factor
of safety of a structure is defined as:
FOS=UTS +a.
From this relationship, it can be seen that the factor of
safety required should always be greater than one.
Note: Chapter B 3.2.5 identified that the size of the safety
factor employed is determined by several considerations,
including:
The table below provides a summary of methods of
improving structural strength, the mechanism by which it
operates, structural benefits and practical applications.
« certainty of loads
« design life of structure
« quality of manufacture
« consequences of failure
« environmental influences
« criticality of the component
« ease of repair or maintenance
« certainty of material properties
«statutory and code requirements.
Safety factors(SF)
varywidelydependingon
the application, risk level, material behaviour and consequences of failure.
A breakdown of different safety factor requirements across industries, with specific examples and explanations, is documented following.
A Safety Factor of 1 means that the structure is designed to
withstand exactly the maximum expected load, no more,
no less.
In practical terms, this means that if the factor of
safety is one, then the strength value of the structure is
equivalent to the maximum allowed load. As such, there
is no margin for error, unexpected stress, material flaws or
environmental changes.
Example:
If a beam is expected to carry 1500 N, and it's designed to
fail at 1500 N, then:
« SF=1500N+1000N=1
Designing with a factor of safety of one is risky because it
assumes:
« Perfect material quality
« No unexpected loads (e.g, wind, earthquakes,
misuse)
« No degradation over time (e.g., corrosion, fatigue)
This is rarely realistic in the real world.
Reasons why engineers build in extra strength to account
for uncertainties include:
« Material variability: real-world materials may have
flaws or inconsistencies.
« Load unpredictability: actual loads may exceed
estimates due to accidents or environmental factors,
« Wear and ageing: structures degrade over time due to
fatigue, corrosion or weather.
« Human safety: lives may depend on the structure’s
integrity—failure is unacceptable.
Civil Engineering
Examples:
Bridges: SF ~2.0-2.5 Must support dynamic loads
(vehicles, wind, earthquakes) and endure decades of wear.
Failure could be catastrophic for public safety.
Buildings: SF ~1.5-2.0 Designed for static and live loads
(people, furniture), with added margin for material
degradation and environmental factors.
Aerospace Engineering
Examples:
Aircraft wings: SF ~1.5: Weight is critical for fuel efficiency
and performance. Materials are high-grade and precisely
tested, allowing lower factors of safety.
Spacecraft components: SF ~1.25: Extremely controlled
environments and precise load predictions. But failure is
high-risk, so redundancy is built in elsewhere.
Mechanical & Lifting Equipment
Examples:
Cranes and hoists: SF ~5.0 Sudden overloads, human
error and high risk of injury or death if failure occurs.
Elevator cables: SF ~11.0 (often even higher): Passenger
safety is paramount. Cables are designed to hold many
times the expected load.
Pressure Vessels & Boilers
Examples:
Industrial boilers: SF ~4.0: Internal pressure can cause
explosive failure. Safety margins account for corrosion,
fatigue and thermal stress.
Gas cylinders: SF ~3.5: Must withstand high internal
pressure and rough handling.
Consumer Products
Examples:
Ladders: SF ~2.0: Must support dynamic human loads
with margin for misuse.
Furniture: SF ~1.5: Lower risk, but still needs to account
for variability in use.
he table below addresses the contributing factors as to
why safety factors are so variable.

Calculate the minimum diameter of a steel rod carrying a

of 590 MPa for the steel rod.
FOS =UTS+ocor

=590+4
= 147.5 MPa
o =load + area
area =load + ¢
=(30x 10%) + 147.5
=203 mm’
But A=nd+4
& =(203%x4)+11
d =16 mm
Therefore, the minimum safe rod diameter is 16 mm.

A3.3.1
Machinery in all its numerous forms makes possible
much of what we take for granted in our modern world,
from the clothes we wear and the food we eat to modern
communication systems. Mechanisms and machinery
have been integral to the process chain.
Since before recorded history, machinery has played
a crucial role in human progress through its ability to
reduce the physical burden on both humans and animals
by producing four basic types of mechanical motion.
Linear motion describes movement in a straight line with
a constant direction.
Rotary motion - describes movement about a fixed axis,
e.g. wheels and gears, a spinning top, etc.
Oscillating motion - involves repetitive back-and-forth
or up-and-down movement around a central equilibrium
point and is characterised by a specific frequency and
amplitude, e.g. pendulum, spring, tuning fork.
A3.3
Reciprocating motion - represents a repetitive back-and-
forth linear motion over a fixed distance, e.g., the lever,
specifically when the fulcrum is in the middle, and cams,
which are rotating or sliding pieces used to transform
rotary motion into linear, reciprocating motion.
While the efficiency, capacity and capability of modern
machinery has increased dramatically from that of
antiquity, the same four forms of mechanical motion
outlined above are provided by six machines we call the
‘simple machines.
All of today’s complicated machines are based on the six
‘simple machines’ listed below:
« inclined plane
« wedge
« lever
« wheel
« pulley
. screw
In plain terms, simple machines help us to do work
with less effort, which is expressed as their Mechanical
Advantage (MA).
Inclined plane: The inclined plane is a simple machine
that uses a sloping surface to move heavy objects to a
higher elevation with less force than lifting them vertically.
This trade-off allows for a lower input force over a longer
distance. The mechanical advantage (MA) of an inclined
plane, which quantifies the force reduction, is the ratio of
its length to its height.
A more gradual slope (less steep) requires less input force,
resulting in a greater mechanical advantage. However,
it increases the distance the object needs to travel, as
illustrated in Figure A3.3.1a. Examples include ramps,
slides and doorstops.
Figure A 3.3.1a Forces acting on an inclined plane
If an object is lifted straight up a vertical height (h), the
work done (Work-out) is equal to the force required to lift
it (output force) multiplied by the vertical height (k). The
Work out is:
Work-out = Output force x Vertical height (h)
‘When using an inclined plane of length L, the work done
(Work-in) is equal to the force required to move the object
up the incline (Input force) multiplied by the length of the
slope (L), where the force is applied to achieve the same
vertical height.
Work-in = Input force x Slope length (L)
In the ideal frictionless situation Work-in equals Work-
out, therefore:
Work-in = Work-out
Input force x Slope length (L) = Output force x Vertical
height (h)

Wedge: A wedge can be thought of as a portable, simple
machine, triangular and resembling two inclined planes
back to back with a thick end and a thin end. Similar to
an inclined plane, it can also be used to hold an object
in place, as in a door stop. However, here the effort is not
applied to move the load up an incline; instead, it is applied
in a direction to reach a maximum depth of penetration.
Effort (E) is applied perpendicular to the broad edge, and
the separating force (Load) is distributed in a direction
normal to the sides to separate objects, as illustrated in
Figure A3.3.1b to split wood. Examples include a wood
splitting wedge, axes, chisels, knives and ploughs in
addition to use as a doorstop.
The mechanical advantage (MA) gained with a wedge
depends on the ratio of the length of the slope to its width,
such that the longer and narrower the wedge, the greater
the mechanical advantage.
Length of wedge side
Width of wedge
L=5
MA
1
Lever: the lever consists of a beam supported at a
stationary pesition and about which it can rotate. This
stationary pesition is known as the fulcrum (or pivot
point).
There are three classes of lever: 1st, 2nd and 3rd, with
each class having either the fulcrum, load or effort in the
central position.
Using the first class lever shownin Figure A 3.3.1c in which
the fulcrum is positioned between the load and effort, the
dotted lines in each instance represent the new position of
the beam, following the application of sufficient effort to
move the load.
Depending on the class of lever considered, and the
location of the Load and Effort relative to the Fulcrum,
the mechanical advantage, distance and rate at which the
load and effort move can vary considerably, as illustrated
in Figure A3.3.1c.
The calculation of alever’s effect relies on the component of
the force perpendicular to the lever arm, which produces
the torque (rotational force) that moves the lever. It is this
torque that is used in the law of the lever to determine
how much force is needed to balance the load. When
calculating the effects of the lever, the Forces of Effort and
Load always need to be considered to be acting normal to
the lever plane.
Although levers have been in use since before recorded
history, the name of one person is most associated with
the lever—the Greek engineer and mathematician
Archimedes. It was Archimedes who gave a mathematical
description of the lever and is recorded as saying in 230
BCE “Give me a lever long enough and a place to stand
and I will move the World".
Archimedes showed that the distance the effort and
load were situated from the fulcrum was important and
affected the effort required to move or balance a load.
The distance from the fulcrum for any force on a lever is
called the lever arm. More specifically, when considering
the distance of the effort from the fulcrum, the distance is
known as the effort arm, and similarly, the distance of the
load from the fulcrum is known as the load arm. The load
and effort each represent forces moving the lever around
the fulcrum in different directions; this turning action is
known as a moment or the torque (t). The lever arm is
therefore sometimes described as the moment arm or
torque arm. When in balance, the sum of these moments
will be zero:
 Moments = ()
EffortxLength of Effort Arm-LoadxLength of Load Arm =0
This is also known as Archimedes’ Law of the Lever,
which states that ‘for a system to be in equilibrium, the
product of the force and its distance from the fulcrum (the
pivot point) must be equal on both sides of the lever’
The mechanical advantage (MA) gained with a lever
depends on the ratio of the effort and load, indicating how
much the machine multiplies the input force.
Load
Effort
Wheel and Axle: a wheel is a circular object that rotates
about its axis and facilitates movement by rolling,
particularly when used in conjunction with an axle that
is fixed in place such that both the axle and wheel turn
together as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.1d. Therefore, if a
turning force is applied to either the wheel or the axle, the
other component of the pair will also turn.
The mechanical advantage obtained under ideal,
frictionless conditions (where efficiency is 100%) arises
from the difference in the diameter of the wheel and axle,
or more accurately, from the difference in their radii, and
can be thought of as a first-order lever system (see section
A 3.3.9) with the fulcrum at the centre of the axle’s point
of rotation.
_ Radius of Wheel
~ Radius of Axle
R
r
MA
Examples include bicycles, gears.
Pulley: Pulleys are an adaptation of the wheel and consist
of a grooved wheel (known as a sheave) over which
a belt or cable is passed (or reeved). The pulley is used
to transmit power, change the direction of a force and
provide assistance through mechanical advantage by
using multiple wheels.
There are three basic classifications of pulley systems:
fixed, movable and compound. Fixed pulleys remain
stationary, while movable pulleys move with the load, and
compound pulleys combine fixed and movable pulleys to
increase mechanical advantage.
‘The mechanical advantage (MA) of a pulley system is
calculated by counting the number of ropes supporting
the load.
MA = number of cable segments supporting a load.
ixed Pulley System
A fixed pulley system has the pulley anchored in a frame
known as a block, which is in turn fixed to an elevated
position. These pulleys are attached to a stationary point
and only redirect the force of the rope, not increasing it.
Single pulleys alone offer no mechanical advantage but do
change the direction of the effort relative to the load, i.e.a
downward force translated through a single pulley causes
upward movement. In pulley systems, the MA and the rate
at which this is achieved, known as the velocity ratio (VR),
are dependent on the number of cables present within the
system and supporting the load.
A simple, single fixed pulley system is shown in Figure
A3.3.1e. In this arrangement, the effort is equivalent to the
load force (assuming ideal frictionless conditions of 100%
efficiency). The pulley simply changes the direction of the
force, but there is no mechanical advantage (MA); that is,
MA = 1. Similarly, the distances moved by the load and
effort are the same, so VR=1.
he hazards of operating such eqipment are described in
this speech by Gerard Hoffnung to the Oxford University
Debating Union. The mechanics described are correct. The
following is audio, so please consider your neighbours.
Movable pulley systems
In a movable pulley system, the pulley is not anchored to
the ceiling, but rather one end of the cable is fixed, and
the other end is attached to the load via a pulley that is
free to move. These pulleys, therefore, move with the load,
requiring less effort to lift a heavy object but increasing the
distance the cable must be pulled. In the movable pulley
system shown in Figure A3.3.1f, even though there is only
one pulley, the mechanical advantage gained is 2 due to
the fact that two cable segments are supporting the load.
In order to obtain a mechanical advantage higher than
two, a compound system is required, which combines
both fixed and movable pulleys.
Compound Pulley Systems
When a fixed pulley is combined with a movable pulley,
it is known as a compound pulley system. A simple
compound two-pulley system is shown in Figure A
33.1g
In the arrangement shown, one end of the cable around
the movable pulley (carrying the load) is attached to
the ceiling, with the cable passing over a fixed pulley to
the effort. Again, because only two cable segments are
supporting the load, the MA is 2.
In a slightly different arrangement shown in Figure
A3.3.1h, three pulleys are used in which an upper
supporting pulley is fixed and two connected movable
pulleys are attached to the load. In this example, four
cable segments are supporting the load, so the mechanical
advantage is 4.
In a compound pulley system, the mechanical advantage
(MA) can be determined using two methods:
Method 1
MA = m, where m represents the number of cable
segments supporting the load, or
MA = 2n, where n represents the number of movable
pulleys used
In the Compound pulley system shown in Figure A 3.3.1h
two movable pulleys are used, so n = 2. Therefore, using
Method 1, MA2x2=4
Alternatively, the number of cable segments supporting
the load m = 4. Therefore, using Method 2, MA m = 4.
Block and Tackle Pulley System
A special category of the compound pulley system is the
Block and Tackle system, in which all of the fixed and
movable pulleys are arranged on a common single frame,
placing one above the other as shown in Figure A 3.3.1i.
In this system, the cable is attached to the fixed pulley and
then threaded around the movable pulley(s) (carrying the
load).
Because of the arrangement in a single frame, the MA of
the Block and Tackle system, such as that shown in Figure
A 3.3.1i, cannot use Method 1 (in which the number of
movable pulleys is counted), but must use Method 2, in
which the number of cable segments supporting the load
is used.
In a slightly different two-pulley Block and Tackle
arrangement shown in Figure A3.3.1j, all three ropes are
supporting the load, so the mechanical advantage is 3,
even though only one movable pulley is being used.
Screw: when discussing mechanical advantage, ‘screw’ is
used as a general term for any device that incorporates a
helical thread and acts as a simple machine transforming
a rotational force (torque) to a larger linear force. This
definition encompasses a wide range of applications
beyond typical fasteners, including bottle caps, pipe
fittings and other threaded components that convert
rotational motion into linear motion or vice versa.
The screw is essentially an inclined plane in which
the incline has been cut into a helical path around the
circumference of a rod, producing a threaded surface, as
illustrated in Figure A3.3.1k. As the screw is rotated about
its axis, the screw progresses linearly along the threads.
In this way, a screw converts rotational motion into linear
motion.
The pitch diameter shown in Figure A 3.3.1k is the
diameter of an imaginary cylinder whose surface would
pass through the threads of a mating thread at the point
where the thread width and groove width are equal. While
the pitch diameter is an important dimension in any
threaded device, influencing its fit and functionality, to
calculate mechanical advantage the outside (or nominal)
diameter is used, not the smaller pitch diameter.
For each complete turn, the thread advances the distance
represented by the distance between adjacent threads,
as measured at the thread crown. This distance is the
thread pitch (p). In moving any load the distance of one
thread pitch, the Effort must move over a circular distance
calculated using the external (nominal) diameter.
The ideal mechanical advantage (MA) gained with a screw
is calculated by dividing the circumference of the screw by
the thread pitch.

A3.3.2
In mechanical systems, inputs are forces or motions
that initiate action, processes are the mechanisms that
transform these inputs into a desired output, and outputs
are the resulting forces or motions. Think of it like this: a
car’s engine (process) uses fuel (input) to move (output).
A fundamental aspect of mechanical systems represented
by simple machines is that they are designed to take
an input, which could be force, motion, energy, or a
combination thereof, and transform it into a desired
output, which could be another form of force, motion,
energy, or a combination. This transformation is achieved
through processes within the system.
These processes are the mechanisms or actions within the
system that transform the inputs into outputs. This can
involve:
Mechanical linkages: Like gears, levers, and cams that
convert motion or force.
Energy conversion: Devices that convert one form
of energy into another, like a generator converting
mechanical energy into electrical energy.
Control mechanisms: Systems that regulate the flow of
energy or motion within the system.
The value of mechanical systems achieved through the
use of the simple machines comes in the Mechanical
Advantage (MA) they provide. In simple terms, this means
simple machines help us to do work with less effort.

A3.3.3
Mechanical Advantage (MA)
The value of simple machines comes in the Mechanical
advantage (MA) they provide. In simple terms, this means
simple machines help us to do work with less effort. The
mechanical advantage of a machine is directly calculated
using the relationship between the output force (load,
resistance) and the input effort, ie. it represents the
amount to which the machine amplifies effort.
MA =
For example, if, by using a simple machine, a 500N object
(load) can be moved by applying an effort of only 10N, a
mechanical advantage of 50 was obtained. Since both the
load and effort are measured in Newtons, they cancel each
other out so that MA is a dimensionless number.
Function
The function of simple machines is to make work easier
by altering the force, direction, or distance required to
complete a task. This is measured by what is known as
the velocity ratio (VR), which is the relationship between
the displacement distance the effort moves relative to the
distance the load moves in the same time. It represents
the speed of the input versus the speed of the output.
Mathematically, the formula may be shown as:
Distance moved by Effort
Distance moved by LoadVR=
Values of VR greater than 1 indicate there is a magnification
of effort operating, while values less than 1 indicate that
a diminution of effort exists. Again, since both figures
in this equation have common units, they cancel out to
produce a dimensionless number.
In an ideal frictionless world, the Work Input (Effort)
would equal the Work Output (Load).
‘Work Input (Effort) = Work Output (Load)
Input Force x Distance moved equals Output Force x
Distance moved:
Effort x Distance Moved by effort
= Load x Distance Moved by load

system:
Input Power (P,) = Output Power (P,)

That is, in a frictionless world where the velocity ratio
(VR) equals the mechanical advantage (MA), the input
force needed is inversely proportional to the speed of the
effort.
In real-world machines, however, the velocity ratio is often
greater than the mechanical advantage due to factors like
friction, which reduces the efficiency of the machine.
Efficiency (n)
The efficiency of simple machines represents the ratio
of output work to input work, essentially measuring
how much of the work put inte the machine is used to
do useful work. It's a way to quantify how effectively a
machine transforms input energy into output energy. In
real-world situations, efficiency is always less than 100%
due to energy losses like friction.
Machine efficiency is expressed as the relationship
between mechanical advantage and velocity ratio and may
be expressed as a decimal or percentage.
Work Output

For an ideal machine, where no energy losses occur due
to friction or gravity, the efficiency is 100%. In an ideal,
frictionless machine, n) = 1, and the mechanical advantage
equals the velocity ratio. However, in real-world machines
in which gravitational and frictional forces must be
considered, the efficiency factor is n < 1, resulting in a
reduction in the output force compared to what would be
achieved in an ideal machine.
