A3.3.4
A fundamental aspect of mechanical systems is their
ability to modify motion by changing its speed, direction
or power of motion. This is achieved through various
mechanisms like gears, belt drives, cams, pulleys, levers
and linkage systems.
Gears can increase or decrease rotational speed. A larger
gear driving a smaller gear increases the output speed but
decreases the torque (or rotational force). Conversely, a
smaller gear driving a larger gear reduces the output speed
but increases the torque.
Belt Drives are mechanical systems that transmit power
and motion between rotating shafts using a flexible belt,
often over pulleys.
Cams are mechanical components primarily used to
transform rotary motion into linear motion, or to create
a specific pattern of motion in a contacting element
called a follower. They are essential in various machines
and mechanisms, including engines, machine tools and
sewing machines.
Pulleys, particularly in combination, can increase or
decrease speed by changing the circumference of the
pulleys used to transmit motion.
Levers can amplify the force applied to an object and also
redirect forces, making it easier to lift or move things.
INTRODUCTION.TO. MECHANICAL SYSTEMS
Linkage Systems are used to connect rigid bodies at pivots,
enabling the transmission of power, information and the
modification of motion in various mechanical devices.
They are particularly useful for converting rotational
motion to linear motion, and vice versa.

A3.3.5
The six simple machines (lever, inclined plane, wheel
and axle, wedge, pulley and screw) are the fundamental
building blocks of all mechanical systems, both simple
and complex. Simple machines, when combined, create
complex machines that perform more intricate and varied
movements.
Simple Mechanical Motion: refers to the basic types of
motion that can be created by simple machines, such as
linear, rotational, reciprocating and oscillating motion.
These four forms of mechanical motion can be combined
to create both simple and complex mechanical systems.
These combinations allow for a wide range of movement
and functionality in devices and machines.
Simple mechanical systems might use only one of these
forms of motion:
Linear motion: a sliding drawer, rack and pinion gears, a
train moving along a track
Rotary motion: a spinning top, gears, pulleys and belt
drives
Reciprocating motion: an ocean buoy,
COmMPpressors
pumps,
Oscillating motion: a swinging pendulum, cams
‘The Pizza cutter shown in Figure A 3.3.5a represents a
simple mechanical device consisting of a simple lever
combined with a wheel and axle to deliver force at the
perimeter of the cutting disc.
Complex systems use two or more forms of simple
mechanical motion working together to achieve more
complex forms of mechanical motion, and can include
combinations of gears, levers, pulleys, linkages and cams
to achieve the desired outcome.
One of the earliest and most comprehensive records of
complex mechanical devices is the 1556 treatise De Re
Metallica by the German metallurgist Georgius Agricola’s
which specifically detailed mining and metallurgical
techniques and machines from 16th-century Europe. The
book provides detailed descriptions and illustrations of
machinery, including various pumps for water drainage
and hoists for ore extraction, and it remained a standard
reference for over 180 years.
One of the images from this work, showing a waterwheel-
powered ore crushing machine, is presented in Figure
A 3.3.5b. The machines described by Agricola worked
by having water fall into the buckets of an overshot or
breastshot wheel, using gravity to turn the wheel’s axle.
A large ‘pit wheel’ fixed to the waterwheel’s axle meshed
with a horizontal bevel gear known as a ‘wallower, which
then meshed with gears to convert the horizontal rotation
of the ‘wallower’ into a vertical motion. This rotation was
then transferred via gears to the crushing mechanism,
representing linkages, which in Agricolas work were
typically trip hammers. An offset cam was used for each
trip hammer, mounted on a rotating shaft that would lift
the heavy, iron-shod hammers, which would then fall
under their own weight to crush ore in a mortar below,
with repeated blows from the hammer crushing the ore
into smaller pieces.
A can opener and car jack, shown in Figures A 3.3.5¢
and Figures A 3.3.5d, would be considered complex
mechanical devices because, although they appear to
be of simple construction, they combine several simple
machines to perform their function.
Devices relying on complex mechanical systems include
various machines and systems, such as washing machines,
vehicles (cars, trains, aeroplanes), industrial machinery
and even building systems like elevators and Heating,
Ventilation and Air Conditioning. These systems often
involve multiple moving parts, mechanisms and control
components to achieve specific tasks. A bicycle, for
instance, is an intricate machine that uses a combination
of levers, wheels and axles, and gears to create complex
rotational and linear motion. Similarly, wheels and axles,
gears, levers and cams are required to create the combined
rotational motion of a clock.

A3.3.6
Gears are rotating machine parts, often with teeth,
that transmit rotational motion and/or torque between
neighbours in addition to being used to change speed,
direction and/or the alignment of shafts. They do this by
meshing with other gears, during which two or more teeth
of adjacent gears are engaged, allowing for the transfer of
power and rotation. As illustrated by the meshing gears
of Figure A 3.3.6a, the direction of rotation changes with
each addition to the gear train, while changes in the pitch
diameter (represented by the continuous circles in Figure
A 3.3.6a) measured at the tooth pitch line lead to changes
in speed.
ost gears (except for the worm gear) transmit power
from one gear to another by rolling contact between
mating teeth, although pure rolling occurs mainly at the
tooth pitch line; on either side of that position, sliding
contact occurs as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.6b.
Gears are found in a wide range of designs and are used to
fulfil applications, from bicycles, cars and heavy machinery
to wind turbines and clocks. Several common gear designs
(although by no means exhaustive) are described below.
Spur Gear
Spur gears are the simplest gear type, consisting of a
cylindrical gear with straight teeth that run parallel to the
shaft’s axis, as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.6d. They are used
to transmit motion and power by meshing with a similar
gear on another parallel shaft, and can be used to change
speed, torque, or direction.
The tooth form is perpendicular to the gear face, meaning
that all forces are in the plane of the gear, and no axial
thrust should be present. The teeth are machined with
what is known as an involute profile that allows smooth
meshing that reduces sliding contact between teeth.
dvantages of Spur Gears:
« Simple and compact design
« Cost-effective to manufacture
« High efficiency in power transmission
» Wide range of sizes and ratios available
« Easy to maintain and replace.
Disadvantages of Spur Gears:
« Generates high noise levels
« Limited to parallel shaft arrangements
 Prone to axial thrust and backlash
« Requires proper lubrication for smooth operation
« May experience increased wear and tear.
Spur Gear Applications:
« Automobile transmissions and engine mechanisms
« Robotic arms, joints and drive mechanisms
« Conveyor systems, printing machines
« Clocks and watches
« Washing machines, mixers and blenders.
Helical Gear
Helical gearing differs from spur gearing in that teeth are
cut across the gear face at an angle rather than straight, as
illustrated in Figure A 3.3.6e. This results in the contact
line between meshing teeth progressing across the face
from the tip at one end to the root of the other, leading to
several teeth being in contact at any one time. This results
in reduced noise and vibration characteristics compared
to the spur gears, while the more gradual tooth loading
substantially reduces wear.
dvantages of Helical Gears (compared with Spur
Gears):
« Smooth and quiet operation
« Increased load-carrying capacity
« Higher efficiency due to greater contact area
« Improved torque transmission
« Better resistance to shock and impact loads.
Disadvantages of Helical Gears:
« Higher axial thrust compared to spur gears
» More complex design and manufacturing process
« Require proper alignment for optimal performance
« Slightly lower mechanical efficiency compared to spur
gears
« Increased cost.
Helical Gear Applications:
« Automotive transmissions
« Industrial mixers, extruders and crushers
« Robotic arms, CNC machines and automated
systems
« Aircraft engines, gearboxes, and landing systems.
» Wind turbines and power plant equipment, from
turbines to generators.
Bevel Gears
These are used to connect two shaft axes that intersect and
are coplanar. The teeth of bevel gears are similar to those
of spur gears in that they make a line contact across the
face of teeth, although the teeth are smaller at the front
than at the back.
Unlike spur and helical gears, in which the teeth are cut
from a cylindrical blank, bevel gears have teeth cut on an
angular or conical surface so that the shaft axes intersect at
90 degrees, although other angles may be used.
There are two types of bevel gear: straight-tooth and spiral,
although both straight and spiral designs have intersecting
axes.
Straight-tooth bevels — These gears, also known as plain
or simple bevels, have teeth cut straight across the face of
the gear, as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.6f. They are subject
to much of the same operating conditions as spur gears
in that straight tooth bevels are efficient but somewhat
noisy. They produce thrust loads in a direction that tends
to separate the gears.
Spiral-bevels — These gears have curved teeth that
provide an action similar to that of a helical gear, see Figure
A3.3.6g. This produces a smoother, quieter operation
than straight-tooth bevels. Thrust loading depends on the
direction of rotation and whether the spiral angle at which
the teeth are cut is positive or negative.
Advantages of Bevel Gears:
Efficient power transmission at varying angles
« Ability to transmit motion between intersecting
shafts
« Versatility in handling different speed ratios
« Smooth and quiet operation
Widely available in various sizes and configurations.
Disadvantages of Bevel Gears
« Complex design and manufacturing process
« Higher cost compared to spur gears
« Requires proper alignment for optimal performance
« Limited load-carrying capacity
Increased complexity for maintenance and repair.
Bevel Gear Applications:
Automotive differential systems distribute power
between the rear wheels.
» Marine propulsion systems, such as boat sterndrives,
transmit power from the engine to the propeller.
« Power tools such as saws, drills and milling machines.
« Heavy construction and mining equipment.
Hypoid Gears
Hypoid gears resemble spiral bevels, but the shaft axes of
the pinion and driven gear do not intersect, as illustrated
in Figure A 3.3.6h. This geometry allows the driving and
driven shafts to continue past each other so that they can
be supported at both ends. This provides greater rigidity
than the support provided by the cantilever mounting
used in some bevel gearing.
In a hypoid gear set, the smaller gear has a greater helix
angle than the larger gear. As a result, exceptionally high
gear ratios can be achieved, making it especially effective
for high-speed reduction using a single set of hypoid
gears.
lthough hypoid gears are stronger and more rigid than
most other gear types, they are one of the most difficult
to lubricate because of high tooth-contact pressures.
High levels of sliding between tooth surfaces also reduce
efficiency.
Despite these demands for special lubrication, hypoid
gears are used extensively in rear axles of automobiles with
rear-wheel drives.
Advantages of Hypoid Gears:
« The larger pinion size and increased tooth contact area
of hypoid gears allow them to handle high torque and
heavy loads
« Design minimises sudden tooth contact, reducing
noise and vibration compared with straight gear
designs
« Compact Design is valuable for space-constrained
applications like automotive differentials
« Efficient power transmission between non-parallel
shafts.
Disadvantages of Hypoid Gears:
Complex design and manufacturing process
» Requires precise alignment during assembly
« The sliding motion between teeth generates more
friction, resulting in lower efficiency compared to
bevel gears
» Sliding action requires high-performance lubricants to
prevent excessive wear.
Hypoid Gear Applications:
« Wind turbines
« Robotic arms, joints, and industrial automation
systems
« ommonly used in rear-wheel drive vehicles, especially
in the differential systems
« Power tools such as power saws, drills and other heavy-
duty machinery.
« Construction and mining equipment, such as
excavators and bulldozers.
Ring Gear
A ring gear (also known as a Girth Gear), as the name
implies, is a gear in which the teeth are cut in either a
spur or helical pattern on a ring-shaped rim. The teeth
may be cut into either the interior or exterior of the ring,
depending on the application, as illustrated in Figure
A3.3.6i.
These gears are often used to transmit power and torque
in mechanical systems as part of a planetary gearbox. In
vehicles, it's commonly found in the rear axle where it
meshes with the pinion gear to transfer power from the transmission to the wheels. Ring gears are also used in
starter systems to engage the crankshaft.
Ring gears are often fixed in position, providing a
stationary outer ring for internal gearing such as planetary
gears, where a small, centrally fixed sun gear rotates, while
other (planetary) gears orbit (as illustrated in Figure
A3.3.6j), while in other applications they may rotate
dvantages of Ring Gears:
« Allow high efficiency, high torque of power
transmission in a compact form.
Disadvantages of Ring Gears:
« Higher cost
« Specialised manufacturing required
Ring Gear Applications:
‘Wind Turbine blade tilt bearings
« Internal combustion engine starter
« Haul Truck Wheel Hub Planetary Gearboxes
« Aecrospace applications such as helicopter rotor
drives
« Heavy machinery superstructure rotation (e.g. Slew
bearings)
« Robotic systems in rotary actuators control the
movement of arm and leg joints
« As part of automatic and hybrid transmissions, the
ring gear helps to achieve multiple gear ratios.
Rack and Pinion
A rack and pinion gear system converts rotary motion
into linear motion (or vice versa). This system consists of
a cylindrical gear (the pinion) whose teeth mesh with a
linear bar-shaped gear (the rack), across which teeth have
been cut, as illustrated in Figure A3.3.6k. Rotating the
pinion causes the rack to move linearly, and conversely,
moving the rack linearly will cause the pinion to rotate.
While the teeth in a rack and pinion system are often of
the spur gear type, helical gears can be used, the main
criterion being that the tooth pitches are the same.
The rack-and-pinion is used extensively in machine tools,
lift trucks, power shovels and other heavy machinery
where rotary motion of the pinion drives the straight-
line action of a reciprocating part, as shown in Figure
A 3.3.6l. Generally, the rack is operated without a sealed
enclosure in these applications, but some type of cover
may be provided to keep dirt and other contaminants
from accumulating on the working surfaces.
Advantages of Rack and Pinion
« Can offer high efficiency
« Well-suited for heavy-duty applications
« Designed to withstand high stresses and prolonged
use
« Offer precise directional control and consistent linear
movement
« Compact design makes them suitable for applications
where space is limited
« Straightforward design with few components
contributes to ease of maintenance
« Highly customisable, allowing for various
configurations and adaptations to suit different
applications.
Disadvantages of Rack and Pinion:
« Repair and replacement can be expensive
«+ The constant contact and stress between the rack and
pinion teeth can lead to wear and deformation over
time, affecting the precision of motion.
Rack and pinion applications
« Lift trucks
« Power shovels
» Railway switches
« Cog railway systems
» Automobile steering mechanisms
Machine tools such as drill press spindles.
Worm Gears
Worm gears, also known as screw gears, consist of a
cylindrical screw-like ‘worm’ in which the teeth are cut
in a pattern similar to those of a helical gear. The worm
meshes with a larger gear, and is most often the driving
member, acting as a screw such that one full turn advances
the meshing gear by one tooth. The meshing (driven)
gear is typically in the shape of a wheel with the shafis
perpendicular and non-intersecting, as shown in Figure
A33.6m.
A worm gear can also be paired with a rack, although
movement back and forth is limited by the length of the
rack, a constraint not associated with a wheel, as shown in
Figure A3.3.6n.
While most meshing gear teeth roll as they advance, the
primary motion in a worm-gear set is one of sliding,
leading to a consequential reduction in efficiency of worm
gearing compared with other major gear types. Friction
between the two gears is typically reduced by pairing a
wheel made of bronze with a worm of hardened steel.
Advantages of Screw Gears:
« Compact design
« High torque output
« High gear reduction
« Self-locking capability
Precise motion control.
Disadvantages of Screw Gears:
« Complex manufacturing process
« Limited ability to handle high-speed rotation
« Lower efficiency compared with other gear types
lubrication for« Requires proper optimal
performance.
Screw Gear Applications:
Robotics arms and joints
= Automotive steering systems
« Conveyor systems
» Gearboxes of machinery and equipment
« Lifting equipment such as screw jacks and hoists.
Ratchet and Pawl
A ratchet and pawl mechanism is a simple gear system
that allows rotation in one direction while preventing
movement in the opposite direction. It consists of a
toothed wheel (ratchet) and a spring-loaded finger (pawl)
that engages with the teeth, as shown in Figure A 3.3.60.
When the ratchet wheel is rotated in one direction, the
pawl can easily slide over the angled teeth. However, when
the ratchet attempts to rotate in the opposite direction,
the pawl’s teeth engage with the ratchet’s teeth, preventing
movement.
Advantages of Ratchet and Pawl
« Prevents reverse motion
» Straightforward design and reliable performance.
Disadvantages of Ratchet and Pawl
« Impacting action of the pawl can generate noise.
Ratchet and Pawl Applications
« Turnstiles
« Tie-down straps
« Socket wrenches
« Winch mechanisms
« Clocks and watches (see Figure A3.3.6p
Idler and Compound Gears
Idler Gear: In gear systems, an idler gear acts as a
connecting or intermediate link between the drive gear
(input) and driven (output) gear, and therefore allows the
transmission of motion between shafts that are too far
apart to use direct meshing.
These gears are therefore often referred to as intermediate
gears. The intermediate gears can be part of a simple gear
train in which only one gear is mounted on each shaft
or be compound gears in which two or more gears are
mounted on a common shaft.
‘When an odd number of intermediate gears is present, the
input and output gears rotate in the same direction, while
an even number results in the input and output gears
FigureA 3.3.6q Effect of odd and even numbers of
intermediate idler gears on direction of rotation of the
input (drive) gear and output (driven) gear. (Top) Odd -
same direction, (Bottom) Even - opposite direction..
Advantages of Idler Gears:
» Allow changes in the direction of the output shaft
« No speed ratio changes in a simple gear train
« Allows transmission of power over greater distances.
Disadvantages of Idler Gears:
« The overall complexity of the gear train increases
« Addition of an idler gear increases friction and wear
over time.
Idler Gear Applications:
« Timing belts
« Conveyors
Compound Gears
A compound gear consists of two or more gears on a single
shaft (shown in Figure A 3.3.6r). Because all the gears
are on the same shaft, they all rotate at the same speed;
however, if the gears are of different pitch diameters,
each gear will have its own gear ratio (the ratio of input
to output speed), allowing for greater speed and torque
changes compared with simple gear trains.
A gear train containing several compound gears is
presented in Figure A3.3.6s
Figure A3.3.6S Diagram of a Compound Gear Train. Note
in this diagram that the compound gears are acting as idler
gears because they are between the Input (Drive) gear and
the Output (Driven) gear.
Advantages of Idler Gears:
« Flexible configurations
« Efficient torque transfer
« Large speed reductions are possible
» Compact layout optimises space.
Disadvantages of Idler Gears:
« Increased weight
» Higher manufacturing complexity
Compound Gear Applications:
« Clock mechanisms
« Industrial machinery
« Automotive transmissions.
Spline Shafts
Although splines are not typically considered gears, they
share some similarities in terms of their use to transmit
torque and rotation. Splines are characteristically a series
of equally spaced deep grooves, or splines, cut along the
surface of a shaft and designed to interlock with a mating
component that has corresponding grooves or slots, see
Figure A 3.3.6t.
While both splines and gears are used to transmit torque and rotation, splines primarily connect components on the same axis, acting as a form of keyed connection, providing a robust and precise way to connect rotating components, such as gears, wheels, and couplings.
Advantages of spline shafts:
« Can provide a strong and durable connection
« Interlocking design ensures accurate alignment between the mating components.
» Load is shared across multiple teeth, reducing stress on any single component.
Disadvantages of spline shafts:
« Greater potential for fretting wear
« Higher cost compared to keyed shafts
Internal splines can be more difficult to manufacture than
external splines due to accessibility issues.
Spline Shaft Applications:
« Connecting axles, wheels and other drivetrain components
« Power transmission in pumps, compressors and conveyors.

A3.3.7
A belt drive is a type of pulley system that uses pulleys
and a belt to transmit power between two or more rotating
shafts. The belt acts as a flexible link, wrapped around the
pulleys to transfer rotational motion and torque as shown
in Figure A 3.3.7a. Belt systems rely on maintaining
sufficient frictional contact between the wheel and the belt
so that slippage does not occur and rotational motion of
one wheel is transmitted with minimal loss to the other
wheel. The cross-sectional shape of the belt can also
vary from flat, square, round and vee-shaped to match a
complementary shape in the wheel periphery to provide
adequate surface area for friction.
efore the availability of electrical drives, most factories
used steam power to drive a single long shaft known as
a lineshaft from which belt and pulley assemblies could
distribute power to individual drive machinery within a
workshop, as shown in Figure A 3.3.7b.
Belt drives are still used extensively today in conveyor
systems, vehicle timing belts, treadmills, compressors, belt
sanders, etc., as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.7c.
In contrast to belt drives, chain drive systems use wheels
with teeth around the periphery known as sprocket wheels
or sprocket gears, which mesh with the spaces in a chain
in order to transport the chain around the sprockets.
Because friction is not required to maintain contact, chain
drives can generally accommodate greater loads; in fact,
lubrication is required to reduce energy loss and wear
through the frictional contact between the sprocket teeth
and chain.
The sprocket and chain in concept lies between the pulley
and gear systems and consists of two sprocket gears joined
by a continuous chain, as shown in Figure A 3.3.7d.
Similar to gear systems in which power is transmitted by the
meshing of the gear teeth, in a sprocket and chain system,
power is transmitted by the meshing of the chain with the
sprocket teeth, as illustrated in Figure A3.3.7e. Effectively,
the chain can be thought of as an interconnecting gear.
The chain typically consists of repeated, interlocking units
comprised of two side plates joined at either end by a pin
and separated by a bush, as shown in Figure A 3.3.7f. The
pin and bush connection between the chain links allows
them to articulate freely, producing a strong, flexible
unit.

A3.3.8
Cams represent a class of rotating components that are used to transform rotary motion into linear motion. This is accomplished in collaboration with a second component known as a cam follower that is in contact with the cam
surface and which, as the name implies, follows the
motion of the cam. The end of the follower in contact with
the cam can be of a variety of forms: flat, pointed, roller
or spherical.
The follower converts the rotary motion ofa cam into linear
motion. As the cam rotates, the cam follower interacts with
shaped protrusions on the cam known as ‘lobes’, resulting
in the follower being driven up and down, translating
circular motion into linear reciprocating motion.
The nature of the reciprocating motion is therefore
determined by the shape or profile of the cam. Seven
commonly encountered Cam profiles are illustrated in
Figure A3.3.8a.
Each of these Cam profiles provides a distinct motion:
Circular - represents a circular disc with an axial centre
of rotation and free of lobe protrusions, Cams of this sort
provide a constant, smooth motion and a consistent force.
This can be found in applications like rotary switches,
vaOtfffflves and various mechanical devices where a
consistent pressure or engagement is needed.
Eccentric/Off-centre - represents a circular disc with the
centre of rotation off-centre. An eccentric cam provides
a constant, smooth motion without periods of rapid
acceleration or deceleration, while producing an up-and-
down motion in the follower. It is used to convert rotary
motion into linear reciprocating motion.
Pear/egg - this cam design features a single lobe and
produces a rapid rise and fall in only a portion of the cam’s
rotation, followed by a dwell period. Pear cams find use
in internal combustion engines, where they are used to
accurately regulate the opening and closing of the valve,
air-fuel intake and exhaust.
Triangular - also known as a Reuleaux triangle provides
a reciprocating motion that found use in film projectors,
where film advancement needed to be stepwise, such that
each frame stops for a fraction of a second before being
advanced quickly.
Oval/ellipse - these cams effectively have two opposed
lobes (or excentricities) with the centre of the ellipse offset
from the centre of rotation. This produces variations in
speed and acceleration during rotation, dependent on the
ellipse’s shape. Oval cams are found in internal combustion
engines (specifically oval track racing camshafts), door
locking mechanisms, where they help the cylinder rotate
and engage with the lock body, and on compound archery
bows, where they reduce the draw weight the archer must
overcome.
Snail - These cams have the appearance of a snail shell,
which results in the follower rising slowly, followed by a
sudden drop. The design means that they can only rotate
in one direction. The snail cam finds use in stamping and
punching applications where regular cuts or holes are
required, and for timekeeping in mechanical clocks and
watches to trigger events like the day advance.
Heart - The heart shape of these cams means that the
follower rises and falls steadily with uniform velocity
and no stationary period. They were originally used
in Post Office master clocks and to wind the bobbin of
sewing machines. Heart-shaped cams find use today
in applications such as electromechanical controllers
for appliances like dishwashers and washing machines,
and in some specialised machinery to provide a specific
mechanical action or pressure. These cams allow a follower
to have a uniform rise and fall with a predetermined
speed, valuable for controlling mechanical switches and
other components in a predictable way.
The translation of rotational motion of a Pear Cam into
reciprocating up and down motion of the follower is
illustrated in Figure A3.8.8b, showing the Dwell, Rise,
Return, Dwell (DRRD) motion produced over 360°
rotation of the cam.

A3.3.9
As indicated in section A 3.3.1, levers are classified into
three classes based on the relative positions of the fulcrum,
the point where the lever pivots, and the load (the force
being applied) and effort (the force being applied to lift
the load). The mechanical advantage obtained by a lever
depends on the relative positions of these three elements.
A lever is therefore variously described as a first, second
or third order (or class) lever. An example of the general
arrangement of each class of levers is shown in Figure
A3.39a.
E L
A
‘|Second
Class A
E
Third
L
E
Class A
Figure A 3.3.9a Schematic representation of the three
categories of a lever
First
Class
The easiest way of remembering the designation of levers
into the three classes is to remember the element in the
centre by the anagram FLE.
EFL IstClass eg See Saw, Scissors, Pliers, Claw
Hammer
FLE 2ndClass eg Wheelbarrow, Bottle opener,
Nut cracker
FEL 3rdClass eg. Shovel, Baseball and Cricket
bats, Broom, Tweezers
The human body alse includes levers. In these instances,
the fulcrum is represented by a joint, the lever arm by the
bones, and the effort by the muscles:
1st Class: Depending on the location of the fulcrum
relative to the load and effort, first class levers can either
magnify effort or distance moved, as illustrated earlier in
Figure A 3.3.1c.
There are several first class levers in the human body, with
the head being perhaps the most obvious example. The
head (load) pivots at the junction of the occipital bone of
the skull with the atlas bone at the top of the spine, forming
the Atlanto-occipital joint (the fulcrum). Movement of
the head is controlled by the Sternocleidomastoid and
trapezius muscles attached to the sides and rear of the
skull (Effort). When the head is held upright and facing
forward, the centre of gravity of the head (the load)
lies over the atlanto-occipital joint. In this position, the
muscles of the neck can be considered to be at rest, and
there will be a zero turning moment about the fulcrum.
As the head is lowered, the centre of gravity moves away
from the fulerum, increasing the load arm relative to the
effort arm that is providing the opposing force applied by
the neck muscles to hold the head in position.
In the example shown in Figure A3.3.9b, all distances are
assumed to be effective lever arms for simplicity, in which
the Load and Effort have been resolved into their vertical
components. An adult human head weighs between 4.5
kg and 5 kg, representing a load force of between 45 N
and 50 N.
Z Moments =0
(effortxeffort arm) — (loadxload arm) =0
(E % 0.05)— (45 x 0.065) =0
E =2.925+0.05
=58.5N
Therefore, in this example, the muscles would need to
exert a force of 58.5 N to hold the head in equilibrium,
and the mechanical advantage is <1.
Alternatively, when the neck muscles contract to tilt the
head back in neck extension, the centre of gravity of the
head moves back closer to the fulecrum while the effort
arm is increased relative to the load arm producing a small
mechanical advantage >1.
‘The ankle joint can also act as a lst class lever when
plantarflexing (when the foot is pointed downward), as
occurs when pushing the gas pedal down. In this action,
the ankle joint acts as the fulcrum, the tibia and fibula
form the load, and the gastrocnemius muscle applies the
effort.
The elbow acts as a first class lever during elbow extension
as the triceps (attached to the ulna bone) contract and a
third class lever during flexure as the biceps (attached to
the radius) contract.
2nd Class: Second-class levers magnify the applied effort,
resulting in a mechanical advantage greater than one.
Metatarsophalangeal joints located in the ball of the foot
at the base of the toes act as a fulcrum when standing on
tiptoe with the body as the load and the effort provided
by the calf muscles (gastrocnemius and soleus) via the
Achilles tendon, see Figure A3.3.9c. Again, the Load and
Effort have been resolved into their vertical components
in the lever diagram
The load arm is the distance from the head of the tibia
at the ankle joint to the metatarsal heads, while the effort
arm is the distance between the attachment point of the
calf muscle to the heel bone and the metatarsal heads.
3rd Class: While first-class levers can provide mechanical
advantage (amplify effort) or increase range of motion,
and second-class levers amplify effort by allowing a
smaller effort to move a larger load, third-class levers
amplify distance and speed at the expense of increased
effort. The defining characteristic of third class levers is
that the effort arm is always shorter than the load arm,
meaning that mechanical advantage is sacrificed in favour
of speed and distance, resulting in an MA<1.
The elbow and knee joint both act as the fulcrum for the
effort. In the case of the knee, the effort in extending the
lower leg (the load) is provided by the contraction of the
quadriceps muscle via connection to the large bone of the
lower leg, the tibia, as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.9d. Again,
the Load and Effort have been resolved into their vertical
components in the lever diagram.
In the case of the elbow during a biceps curl, effort is
provided by the biceps muscle connecting with the radius
bone of the forearm (load).
‘The Metacarpophalangeal joints of the hand also represent
a 3rd class lever system.

A3.3.10
A mechanical linkage system is a collection of
interconnected rigid bodies (links) connected by
joints that allow for controlled movement and force
transmission. These linkages can transform motion (e.g.
from rotational to linear or vice versa), change direction,
alter speed or apply a mechanical advantage. Examples
include crankshafts, linkages in cars and robotic arms.
Parallel, reverse and bell-crank linkages are types of
mechanisms that change or transfer movement.
Parallel linkages represent a mechanism that uses two sets
of parallel bars connected by joints, which allow these bars
to move in parallel, creating an expansion and contraction
effect by transmitting movement in the same direction,
as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.10a. This type of linkage is
found in many commonly encountered situations, such
as train pantographs, windshield wipers, scissor-type car
jacks, step stools and linkages used to maintain toolbox
drawers horizontal when the box is opened.
One of the most commonly encountered forms of the
parallel linkage is the criss-cross "X’ pattern shown in
Figure A 3.1.10b. This mechanism is typically driven
by a pneumatic, hydraulic or mechanical actuator that
allows vertical movement via moving pivot points, so that
movement of one joint produces an identical movement
by a second joint. These mechanisms use a pantograph-
type parallel linkage system that allows extension and
retraction while maintaining a stable concertina-like
operation. Examples include Mobile Elevated Work
Platforms (Scissor lift), Safety barriers and computer
workstations as shown in Figure A3.3.10b.
Reverse linkages are mechanical systems that use a fixed
pivot point (fulcrum) to change the direction of motion.
This means the input movement will be reversed, causing
the output movement to go in the opposite direction, as
illustrated in Figure A 3.3.10c.
Note that even though the criss-cross ‘X’ pattern examples
of parallel linkages shown in Figure A 3.3.10b have central
fixed pivots, they are not reverse linkages because the
direction of movement is the same as the input; it is not
reversed. In the case of the scissor lift, for example, the
platform movement (up or down) is in the same direction
as the actuator’s input.
Bell-crank linkages are composed of an ‘L shaped
connector with a fixed pivot position at the bend and
connectors attached at the end of both legs by a movable
pivot, as illustrated in Figure A 3.3.10e. This arrangement
results in a change in direction of objects or force by
90°, and derives its name from its original employment
to operate doorbells. If the fixed pivot point is at the
midpoint of the bell crank, link movement will be equal,
and no mechanical advantage (MA) is gained. However, if
the fixed point is not at the midpoint, those distances vary,
and mechanical advantage can be gained.
Examples of the bell-crank linkage are bicycle brakes
connecting the handlebar lever to apply brake pads to
either the wheel rim, as illustrated in Figure A3.1.10f, or against a disc (rotor) located at the wheel hub.
All of the linkages described above contain what are
known as Revolute Joints, also called hinge or pin joints.
While a single revolute joint is not considered a linkage
itself, revolute joints are a basic building block for linkages
that allow for rotation and relative movement between the
connected links, and are crucial for the functionality of
these types of linkages.

A3.4
Guiding question
How are electronics present in everyday products?
Electronics are integral to countless everyday products,
powering everything from mobile devices and home
appliances to industrial automation and medical
equipment. They are used to process information, control
functions and convert energy, enhancing our lives in
numerous ways:
« Communication & Entertainment - Televisions,
Smartphones, Computers, Game consoles, MMS &
SMS services, etc.
« Home Appliances - Microwave ovens, Air conditioners,
Security systems, etc.
« Wearables - Smart watches, Fitness trackers, Bluetooth,
GPS map assistance, Contactless payment, etc.
« Transport - Traffic flow monitoring and signalling
systems, GPS, Electronic toll collection (ETC), Speed
monitoring technologies, Smart card fare payment,
etc.
« Industry - Robotic systems, Inventory management,
Scheduling and Process optimisation, Modelling
and design analysis, Cloud computing and Artificial
intelligence (AI), etc
« Defence - End-to-end encryption (E2EE), Command
and control, Sensors & relays, Cyber defence and
disruption, etc.
« Medicine - Telehealth, Monitors & Diagnostic tools,
Implantable devices, etc.
« Energy Management - Smart grids, Power conversion
systems, Battery Storage, Renewable energy
technologies, etc

A3.4.1
 simple electronic system generally operates with input,
process, output and a feedback loop. The input provides
the system with the data or information it needs to operate.
The process component manipulates the input to produce
the desired output, which is the system’s result.
21
In a closed-loop system, part of the output is fed back to
the input and used to adjust the process or input, thereby
allowing for better control and stability (see also Section
A3.4.11).
An example of a simple electronic system incorporating
input, process, output and feedback is a thermostat-
controlled room heater, as described below.
Input: The current room temperature is measured by a
sensor.
Process: The system compares the current temperature
with the desired temperature set by the user.
Output: If the room is too cold, the heater turns on; if it's
warm enough, the heater stays off or turns off.
Feedback: The system continuously monitors the room
temperature and adjusts the heating accordingly to
maintain the desired level.
Thiskind of cycle—gatheringdata, processingit, producing
a response and adapting based on results—is common in
many real-world systems, from coffee machines to cruise
control in cars.

A3.4.2
‘The late 19th century, marked by the invention of the
telephone (1876), phonograph (1877), and incandescent
light bulb (1879), is considered the beginning of
the electronic revolution because these inventions
demonstrated the practical application of electricity for
communication, entertainment and illumination.
‘While only considering electrical devices, rather than
electronic, because they did not incorporate active
components that could manipulate electrical signals such
as vacuum tubes (1906), transistors (1947) or integrated
circuits (1958), the earlier inventions were fundamental
in establishing the core principles and technologies that
would later underpin the electronic age.
Electronic devices are now ubiquitous in today’s world.
They include smartphones, sophisticated sound systems,
computers and some smart light bulbs and are increasingly
integrated into daily life. Designers can play a significant
role in influencing consumer purchasing behaviour of
such devices by promoting sustainable, safe and energy-
efficient products through:
« Use of modular designs
« Age-appropriate safety labelling
« Design for ease of part replacement
« Design emphasising energy efficiency
« Encouraging longer product life-cycles
« Reduce/eliminate sensitive
materials
environmentally
« Choose materials that are readily recyclable or
biodegradable.
ELECTRONIC PRODUCT ECO-LABELLING
The EPEAT™ (Electronic Product Environmental
Assessment Tool) label is a globally recognised ecolabel
that identifies environmentally preferable electronic
products, see Figure A3.4.2a. It assesses products based
on their environmental performance throughout their
lifecycle, including factors like material selection, energy
conservation, and end-of-life management. Products
meeting specific environmental criteria can be registered
in the EPEAT system, receiving ratings of Bronze, Silver,
or Gold based on the number of criteria met.
« Bronze: Meets all required criteria.
« Silver: Meets all required criteria plus a percentage of
optional criteria.
« Gold: Meets all required criteria plus a higher
percentage of optional criteria.
‘While EPEAT™ is a comprehensive and rigorous eco-
labelling system that can help purchasers identify more
sustainable technology products, it is not mandated
by any government or regulatory body. Manufacturers
voluntarily register their products.
Electronic product safety
labelling
Regulatory approvals, governments and agencies often
require products to pass safety assessments before they
can be sold. Certification marks and labels are placed
on products to show they've been tested and meet safety
standards. Examples include:
« CE Marking (Europe): Indicates compliance with EU
safety, health and environmental requirements.
« UL Mark (United States): Shows that the product was
tested for safety by Underwriters Laboratories.
« GS Mark (Germany): Stands for Gepriifte Sicherheit or
“Tested Safety.
« CCC Mark (China): China Compulsory Certificate for
products meeting Chinese standards.
Quality management systems (QMS) incorporate systems
like ISO 9001 or [SO 13485 to ensure quality and safety in
design and manufacturing.
isk assessment and hazard testing include systematic
checks like stress testing, flammability, toxicity evaluations
and failure mode analysis to ensure safety under various
conditions.
Consumer feedback and recall systems monitor consumer
reviews and complaints and can identify issues quickly.
If something goes wrong, product recall systems ensure
dangerous items are pulled from the market quickly.
Electronic product energy
labelling
Many consumer items for domestic use, particularly
white goods, carry an energy rating designed to provide
consumers with information about the energy efficiency
of the appliance, helping them make informed purchasing
decisions. Such information is important, as running
costs over the appliance’s lifetime can exceed the initial
purchase price.
Canada became the first country to introduce a
mandatory energy rating label for appliances in 1978,
called ‘EnerGuide’ (see Figure A3.4.2a), and was quickly
followed in 1979 by the USA with ‘EnergyGuide. Both
labels indicated the energy consumption in kilowatt-
hours (kWh) and included a linear scale to indicate where
the appliance being considered is positioned with respect
to the most and least efficient models.
The USA additionally introduced the Energy Star label
in 1992, which is an optional endorsement for products
that meet specific energy efficiency standards, see Figure
A3.4.2b. This label complements the EnergyGuide label by
indicating that the appliance is among the most efficient in
its category.
In 1986, Australia became the third country to introduce
an energy rating system, although it did not become
mandatory until 1992. In a departure from the linear scale
used in Canada and the USA, a six star rating scale was
used, where the more stars indicated greater efficiency,
see Figure A3.4.2c. Multiple colours were also used to
highlight important information.
One star represents a base energy consumption (BEC).
Each additional star is awarded for a defined energy
reduction factor (ERF), which may be anywhere from 15%
to 30%, depending on the product group.
As the general efficiency increases, the BEC value is
re-evaluated and lowered appropriately, resulting in
a recalibration of the label. This reduces the need to
add additional stars to the label as efficiency improves,
However, some appliances, such as televisions, improve
faster than others, and these products use a ten star label.
Between 1992 and 1995, the EU also introduced a
labelling system, an example of the most recent version of
which is shown in Figure A3.4.2d for a refrigerator. The
label includes a scale with several efficiency classes from
‘A’ (Excellent), coloured green, progressing by increasing
length to ‘G’ (Poor), coloured red. The category to which
the appliance under consideration belongs is arrowed.
Efficiency is again recorded as kWh/annum along with
noise generated (quoted in decibels - dB).
This label has been updated several times, with the most
recent being in 2021 to rescale the energy efficiency
classes to allow for more efficient appliances to become
available.
Similar mandatory labelling systems are now used in over
80 countries to indicate the energy efficiency of electric
and electronic products, with most opting for either a star
or class system of efficiency classification. A selection of
these labels is presented in Figure A 3.4.2e. The labelling
system has seen a substantial improvement in energy
efficiency of whitegoods driven by consumer knowledge
and preference for energy-efficient products.

A3.4.3
Digital signals are made up of two basic conditions of
being ‘off” or ‘on’ These conditions can be represented
numerically as the numbers zero (0) and one (1) for of”
and ‘on’ respectively.
This is the key difference between analogue and digital
systems, as analogue systems use continuous signals that
are infinitely variable between an upper and lower limit,
and can include both positive and negative values, while
digital systems use discrete signals (like binary code).

A3.4.4
nalogue systems utilise signals that continuously
change over time, and as such, sine waves are a common
example of such a signal, representing a fundamental type
of analogue signal. They represent a periodic, smooth
oscillation with a constant frequency and amplitude.
Because these signals can varyin amplitude and frequency
over time, they can take on any value within a defined
range, unlike discrete digital signals, allowing for a wide
range of information to be conveyed.
Voltage
The term voltage is the common name for the electric
potential between two points, or the Potential Difference,
and is a measure of the work (W) that must be done
to move an electric charge from one point to another.
Potential difference is measured in volts and is symbolised
by the capital letter 'V" The relationship can be represented
as indicated in Eqn. A3.4.4a below.
w
V= E Equation A3.4.4a
Where W is the work expressed in joules, required to
move the charge Q.
The Potential difference (Voltage) can therefore be
thought of as a ‘pressure’ that drives electric charge around
an electric circuit.
A battery is a source of potential difference providing
a steady, continuous voltage to an electric circuit and is
represented within a circuit diagram as two uneven lines
normal to the circuit, as indicated in Figure A3.4.4a.
The battery in driving a steady electric charge around
an electric circuit is therefore a source of direct current
(DC), with voltage and current related by Ohm’s law.
Current
Electrical current is a measure of the flow of electrons
past a point in a unit of time and can be represented by
Eqn. A3.4.4b below.
= 8 Equation A3.4.4b
]
Where [ is the electrical current measured in amperes and
expressed in the units amps, symbolised by the capital
letter ‘A’ while the electric charge Q on an electron is
measured to be 1.6 x 10" Coulombs
Direct current (DC)
Direct current systems provide a constant flow of electrons
in a single direction from negative to positive; this is
known as the ‘true’ direction of current flow compared
with the ‘conventional’ direction of current flow.
Note: The circuit diagram in Figure A3.4.4b shows current
flow to be from the positive terminal to the negative
terminal. This is often designated as the conventional
current direction. This direction of flow was proposed
in a theory developed by Benjamin Franklin before the existence of electrons was known. The conventional current direction described above (from
positive to negative) is still used today, even though electron flow around a circuit proceeds from negative to positive.
Direct current can be produced from a generator or
batteries, where it can be stored chemically for use as an
unchanging supply, as illustrated in Figure A3.4.4c. In a
DC circuit, both voltage and current are unchanging with
respect to time unless there is a change in resistance
Unfortunately, transmission losses through a conductor
mean that DC cannot be sent efficiently over long
distances.
Within a DC circuit, voltage and current can be considered
as unchanging with time, such that Ohm’s law (V =IR)
represents the relationship.
Resistance
Resistance is related to voltage and current by Ohm’s law,
stated in Equation. A3.4.4c, and is so named because it
describes the property of a material that opposes (or
resists) the flow of electrical current.
V=IxR Equation A3.4.4c
The Ohm's law relationship demonstrates that if the
resistance increases and the current remains unchanged,
the voltage will increase.
Alternatively, if the voltage is to remain constant as the
resistance increases, the current must decrease. This is
because resistance acts as an opposition to the flow of
current. A higher resistance means it’s harder for the
current to flow. Resistance causes the conversion of
electrical energy into heat within a circuit. This heat-
producing effect creates a “voltage drop’ across the resistor,
meaning the overall potential difference (voltage) across
the circuit is less than the source voltage.
The relationship between voltage, resistance and current
can be memorised using the Ohm’s Law triangle shown in
Figure A3.4.4d
Resistors are components within an electrical circuit
that retard or resist the free flow of electrons. Resistance
is measured in ohms and is symbolised by the Greek
letter omega (2). When drawing circuits, resistors are
represented by a saw tooth line or rectangle (Figure
A3.44e).
A simple electric circuit is presented in Figure A3.4.4f in
which three resistors are present, arranged in series
The total resistance of the circuit (R,) shown in Figure
A3.4.4g is obtained by adding the resistors as shown in
Equation. A 3.4.4d
Equation A3.44d.R =R + R, + R,
Therefore, for the circuit shown in Figure A 3.4.4g, the
total resistance is:
R.=3+5+6=140Q
When resistors are placed in parallel within an electrical
circuit, as shown in Figure A 3.4.4h, the total resistance
of the circuit (RT) is obtained by adding the reciprocal of
each resistor to obtain the reciprocal of the total resistance
(Equation A3.4.4e). Taking the reciprocal of this result
gives R
Frequency
Frequency is the number of cycles of an Alternating
Current (AC) wave per second, measured in Hertz
(Hz). In AC circuits, the frequency of the voltage source
determines the frequency of the current.
Nicola Tesla is generally credited with the establishment
of alternating current as an efficient means of electrical
transmission in 1888. While AC cannot be stored in a
battery for later use, it can be readily transformed to higher
or lower voltages. Because of this, very high voltages can
be transmitted from power stations and later stepped
down by local transformers for domestic use. Similarly,
small individual transformers can be used to operate
portable low-voltage appliances.
Alternating current gets its name from the way that the
direction of electron flow changes or alternates. In this
process, the positive and negative charges at either end of
the conductor switch positions, resulting in reversals of
electron direction. As shown previously, by Ohm's law, for
a given resistance, the voltage and current have a constant
ratio which is maintained i.e. they vary in unison. Each
reversal of current is termed a cycle.
Alternating current is generated using rotating-coil
generators in which a cycle is completed for every complete
rotation of the generator shaft. At various times during a
cycle, the value of current or voltage can be determined
by sin @, where @ represents the angle of rotation of the
generator shaft.
The variation of current and voltage in an AC circuit
can therefore be represented by a sine wave moving with
respect to time as shown in Figure A3.4.4j. AC is therefore
described in terms of cycles per second or a frequency,
with the units Hertz (Hz).
The oscilloscope allows visualisation of an alternating
waveform by essentially drawing a graph of voltage
versus time, in which voltage is displayed on the vertical
or Y-axis, and time on the horizontal or X-axis. Figure
A 3.4.4k shows the typical sine waveform of voltage versus
time, showing both the peak amplitude and peak-to-
peak amplitude, Figure A3.4.4] shows the typical square
waveform of a voltage versus time plot showing both the
peak amplitude and peak-to-peak amplitude.
The frequency (f) of an alternating waveform is related to
a fundamental characteristic of the waveform, the wave
period (7). As illustrated in Figure A3.4.4m and Figure
A3.4.4n, the wave period represents the time for one
complete cycle or wavelength (1) to pass and is measured
in seconds.
Because frequency is defined as the number of cycles
per unit time, the expression shown in Equation A 3.4.4f
can be used to relate frequency and period. As can be
appreciated from the equation, the units of frequency are
s~ or Hertz (Hz).
Similarly, the wavelength and frequency of a waveform are
related to the wave velocity (v) by the equation A3.4.4g
shown below.
The two primary AC frequencies used around the world
are 50 Hz and 60 Hz. Therefore, one complete cycle is
completed every 0.02 seconds or 20 milliseconds (ms).
A source of alternating voltage is represented within an
electric circuit as indicated in Figure A3.4.40 and is also a
source of alternating current (AC).
Power
Power, expressed in the units of a Watt (W), is the rate at
which energy is used and can be expressed as shown in
Equation A3.4.4h

Capacitance
A capacitor is an electrical component composed of
two parallel charged metal plates separated by a layer
of dielectric insulating material. Capacitance (C) is the
measure of a capacitor’s ability to store electrical energy
and is the amount of charge that a capacitor can store per
unit of voltage. It is the ratio of the charge stored on the
capacitor to the voltage across the capacitor and is inversely
proportional to the distance between the capacitor plates.
The capacitor allows the temporary storage of charge (Q) in
a circuit, which is, in turn, dependent on the permittivity
(¢) of the material separating the plates and the surface
area of the plates (A).
Axe
Where C = % = Equation A3.4.4
The basic unit of capacitance is the farad (F); however,
because the farad is a large unit, capacitance is usually
expressed in terms of micro-farads (WF).
The farad is named for Michael Faraday (1791 - 1867)
who, in a remarkable series of experiments, discovered
The connection between electricity and magnetism. This
achievement paved the way for the electric motor and
dynamo and, hence, our modern electricity grids.
Two circuit symbols commonly used to represent a
capacitor are illustrated below in Figure A3.4.4q.

the effective plate spacing is the sum of the individual

Because a resistor acts to slow the flow of charge,
the charging and discharging of a capacitor is not

as the time constant of the circuit represented by the Greek

the resistance. The time constant is defined as the period

A3.4.40.

Because it is an exponential relationship, it theoretically
takes an infinite time to complete, In practical terms,
however, completion is generally considered to be achieved
after five time constant intervals (5t). To comply with
Ohm’s Law, as a capacitor is charging, the voltage rises and
the current across the capacitor falls. On discharging, the
voltage falls and the current increases.
