In AC circuits, the voltage continuously oscillates,
causing the capacitor to charge and discharge repeatedly
with each cycle of the voltage. This results in a 'swing' in
voltage across the capacitor as it follows the AC waveform.
The capacitor’s ability to respond to these changes is
characterised by its capacitive reactance (XC), which is
inversely proportional to the frequency and capacitance
as shown in equation A 3.4.4r.
Digital multipliers
SI multipliers, also known as metric prefixes, are used
to express units of measure at different scales—from
the tiniest nanoseconds to massive gigabytes. These
prefixes help simplify numbers by providing a shorthand
for powers of ten, making it easier to work with very
large or very small quantities. These prefixes are handy
across scientific, engineering and everyday applications,
including measuring electrical resistance.
The table below shows the most commonly used SI
multipliers:
Examples include:
« A millivolt (mV) is one-thousandth of a volt.
« A gigabyte (GB) contains a billion bytes.
« A micrometer (um) is a millionth of a meter.
These prefixes are handy across scientific, engineering and
everyday applications.

A3.4.5
Digital systems are built around the concept of using
discrete values to represent and process information, as
opposed to analogue systems, which rely on continuously
varying signals. The digital system is based on binary
numbers and only uses two digits: 0 and 1. These are
known as binary digits or bits. Each bit represents a state,
represented as:
0 > OFF (no signal or low voltage)
1> ON (presence of signal or higher voltage)
The following points describe how digital systems make
use of discrete values.
« All data, i.e. numbers, text, audio or video, is converted
into binary. In this case, the letter ‘A’ becomes 01000001
in binary (in ASCII).
« Logic circuits interpret and manipulate these on/
off signals using logic gates (AND, OR, NOT, etc.),
following specific rules.
« Memory devices like RAM and flash storage hold
data as bits. Each bit is stored by either charging
or discharging a capacitor or flipping a switch-like
transistor state.
« Digital communication systems transmit bits using
pulses.
Logic gates act as digital switches, in which an output of
0’ or ‘1’ is produced. Depending on the combinations
of logic gates used, various operations can be performed
within a circuit.
The commeon logic gates encountered are shown in Figure
A3.4.5a. As may be seen from Figure A3.4.5a, there are
three (3) basic gate symbols representing a ‘NOT" gate,
an ‘AND’ gate, and an ‘OR’ gate. The other gate functions
are meodifications of these symbols to represent some
modification to the output that takes place.
Note that for every operation, there is a logic gate that
performs an inverse or ‘NOT” version of that operation.
The symbols for both are similar except for the inclusion
of a small circle on the output side of the NOT versions,
known as an 'inversion bubble’
A NOT ’ i _— . A QJlo 0 0
gate, also called an inverter, is a digital logic gate OR B
that inverts the input signal. If the input is high (1), the 0 1 1
output is low (0), and if the input is low (0), the output is 1 o |1
high (1). NOT gates are fundamental building blocks in 1 1 1
digital logic circuits. They are used to create more complex
logic functions, like NAND and NOR gates, and for signal
inversion in various applications, including memory and
control circuits.
The AND gate can be thought of as analogous to a simple electric circuit in which switches are present in series. If even one of the switches is open (input 0), no current flows. However, if both switches are closed (both inputs
1), current flows.
Similarly, the OR gate can be thought of as analogous to a simple electric circuit in which switches are present in parallel. If even one of the switches is closed (input 1), current flows even if all of the other switches are open. Only if all switches are open (all inputs 0) will there be no current.
A ‘truth’ table for logic gates is shown in Figure A 3.4.5b. The truth table is so called because it is a visual representation of the logical output, true (1) or false (0), of a circuit under all possible combinations of input and output conditions.
For ‘'n’ inputs, there will be 2n rows, each representing the output for a unique combination of input values. Each input value will be either a ‘1’ o ‘0" as shown below. By convention, the output is labelled ‘Q’

A3.4.6
Passive electronic components, like resistors, capacitors,
switches and relays, manage energy within a circuit
without requiring an external power source, acting as the
foundation for electronic systems by providing stability
and control. They regulate current, store charge and filter
signals.
Active electronic components, like diodes and
transistors, require external power and are responsible
for amplification, switching and signal modulation. While
switches are usually classified as passive components,
mechanically switching from one position to another,
electronic switches consisting of resistors and diodes can
be active electronic components. These active electronic
switches require an external power source to control or
modify the electrical signal.

A3.4.7
nalogue and digital electronic components serve distinct
but crucial roles in inputting information into electronic
systems. Analogue components, such as sensors, provide
continuously varying signals, while digital components,
including switches, use discrete, on/off (binary) states.
Understanding their purposes is key to designing and
implementing electronic systems.
ANALOGUE COMPONENTS
Sensors
These devices translate physical quantities into electrical
signals, allowing electronic systems to 'sense’ their
environment, gathering real-world data of the sort listed
below, for various applications like home automation,
industrial monitoring and scientific research.
« LightSensors: Convertlight intensity into a measurable
electrical voltage or current
« Temperature Sensors: Detect temperature changes,
typically outputting a voltage or resistance value
proportional to the temperature.
« Humidity Sensors: Measure the amount of moisture in
the air, often producing a voltage or resistance change
with humidity levels.
« Sound Sensors (Microphones): Convert sound waves
into electrical signals.
Analogue Input Modules
These modules act as interfaces between analogue sensors
and digital systems, often converting the analogue signals
into digital values for processing by a microcontroller or
PLC.
Digital Components
Switches
These are basic binary devices that create a closed or open
circuit, representing on/off states. They are often used to
control the flow of power or signal in a circuit.
Digital Input Modules
These modules read binary signals from switches or other
digital sensors (e.g., presence/absence of an object) and
provide them as inputs to a digital system

A3.4.8
Analogue
An analogue signal is one in which energy is continuously varying, such as a sine wave. The value of the energy in the analogue wave can be at any particular value, within limits, at any instant.
The process of modifying an analogue signal to ensure
that the signal is strong, clean and within the acceptable range for the next stage of processing or measurement is known as signal conditioning and is performed with the aid of Op-amps.
A number of processes are undertaken during signal
conditioning, including amplification, attenuation,
filtering, isolation, AC coupling, DC offset correction and
event detection to prepare the signal for accurate analysis
or digital conversion.
« Amplification: Increasing the signal's amplitude to make it stronger.
« Attenuation: decreases the signal's amplitude to make it weaker.
« Filtering: Removing unwanted noise or interference by
selectively passing or blocking certain frequencies
« Isolation: Protecting the signal from electrical noise or ground loops.
« AC Coupling: Blocking DC components while allowing AC signals to pass through. This is often achieved by placing a capacitor in the circuit, which acts as a high-pass filter for AC signals and a low-pass filter for DC signals
« DC Offset: Shifting the signal’s DC level to a desired value, This ensures that the output signal has the correct DC level, even if there are DC errors in the input signal
« Event Detection: Identifying specific events or changes in the signal.
Digital
Rather than being a continuously varying wave, a digital
signal is comprised of a sequence of discrete pulses in
which energy is either ‘on’ or ‘off” and can therefore be
represented by the digits 1 and 0. Each of these is termed a
‘bit’. This is described as a binary signal in which the signal
has only one of two possible values.
Digital program control systems use a series of instructions
(a program) to control the flow of data and operations
within a digital circuit. This allows for complex logic and
decision-making.
These systems rely on several key electronic components
such as logic gates, microcontrollers/microprocessors,
digital sensors, analogue-to-digital converters (ADCs),
digital-to-analogue converters (DACs) and digital signal
processors (DSPs).
Digital program control involves several key processes:
« digitisation, often using concepts like sampling and quantisation,
« data management, including data validation, security and storage,
« process automation to reduce manual repetitive tasks,
« system integration, involving interconnectedness of digital systems,
« continuous monitoring and review to optimise performance.
These processes are implemented to improve efficiency,
enhance decision-making and ensure the -effective
operation of digital systems.
Analogue-to-Digital Converters (ADCs)
In order to transmit an analogue signal digitally, the signal
must first be processed by an analogue-to-digital converter
(ADC). The ADC performs this task by ‘sampling’ the
analogue signal at regular intervals of time to build up a
digital representation of the wave in the form of discrete
numerical values. The greater the sampling frequency, that
is, the more times per second that the analogue signal is
sampled, the lower the conversion error.
The Nyquist-Shannon Theorem (shown below) states
that the sampling rate must be greater than or equal to
twice the bandwidth to accurately recreate the sampled
waveform:
S 2B
where § is the sample rate and B is the bandwidth.
Following the sampling operation, the signals are
allocated to an amplitude or energy level and are said to
be quantised. The level of quantisation is determined by
the number of bits used to code the sampled data. For
example, if a 3-bit codeword was used, there are only eight
discrete levels among which the data could be allocated.
If an 8-bit codeword had been used, 256 levels would
have been available, while a 16-bit codeword would have
provided 65,536 levels, Compact disc recordings typically
use 16-bit.
The number of discrete quantum levels in an ADC's output
is therefore related to the number of bits as below.
Quantum levels = 2b
‘Where b is the number of bits in the codeword.
Using the example of an 8-bit codeword, 256 levels would
be obtained, ranging from 0 to 255 or -128 to +127. The
quantisation step size (q) is calculated as the full-scale
voltage (VFS) divided by the number of quantisation
levels, as shown below:
q=VFS+2b
Using the example of an 8-bit codeword and a VFS of
0-1V:
256
—0.00390625
~3.91 mV
That is for the example shown, of a full-scale voltage (VFS)
of 1 Volt and an 8-bit codeword, the quantisation step size
(g) representing the analogue voltage increment between consecutive digital code levels is 3.91 millivolts. Smaller quantisation step size (i.e., more bits) results in finer analogue voltage resolution and better accuracy, but also increases the complexity of the DAC.

A3.4.9
A programmable interface controller (PIC), alternatively known as a Peripheral Interface Controller or Programmable Intelligent Computer was developed by General Instrument (now Microchip Technology) in 1977, to replace traditional, hard-wired relay logic systems in industrial and other applications that were
considered to be inflexible and difficult to modify or update when process requirements changed. This shift offered numerous benefits, including increased flexibility,
easier maintenance and enhanced control capabilities.
PIC components essentially represent a small computer
complete with input, output and memory that can be
programmed to perform simple tasks, see Figure A 3.4.9a.
A PIC is not, however, a CPUj; it is a microcontroller,
meaning it is a self-contained system on a chip or an
Integrated Circuit (IC), with memory and a processing
unit, but smaller and less powerful than a CPU.
Software, often in the form of .hex files, is loaded into
a PIC microcontroller using an external programmer
connected to the computer via a USB or other interface.
‘The programmer then utilises specific programming pins
called I/O (input/output) pins on the microcontroller
to transfer the code. This process is often facilitated by
software, such as Microchip’s MPLABX IDE, that compiles
the code and then uploads it to the microcontroller.
Input pins on a PIC microcontroller are designed to
recognise different voltage levels. Generally, they will treat
voltages above a certain threshold as a digital '1' (or logic
high), and voltages below that threshold as a digital '0'
(or logic low). Input pins are often connected to external
devices like switches, sensors, or other logic circuits. The
voltage level at the input pin will then reflect the state of that external device. Similarly, output pins can be configured to output a specific voltage level. By writing a '1' or '0' to a particular output port, the microcontroller can set that pin’s voltage to its high or low state, respectively.
Some PIC microcontrollers can also read analogue voltage levels. This is typically done using an Analogue-to-Digital Converter (ADC). The ADC will convert the analogue voltage into a digital representation that the microcontroller can process.
Because the PIC is capable of storing and running a simple program, many modern electronic devices incorporate PICs to provide various programmable options. The program options available in modern washing machines, refrigerators, microwave ovens, smartphones and televisions are provided through PICs.
The flexibility of the PIC comes from the wide range of functions for which it can be utilised. While the capacity is largely unnecessary in any one application where a PIC is suitable, the use ofa purpose-built controller for every application, to ensure that full use of capability is made, would be time consuming and expensive to build and maintain compared with the convenience of a single component that can satisfy a large number of different requirements albeit with redundant capabilities.
The inclusion of PICs in a product can therefore allow the reduction of other circuitry, and because PICs can replace many circuit components, stock levels can be reduced and assembly simplified. This reduction of components and simplification of assembly can lead to lower cost and greater product reliability.
The flexibility of the PICs, therefore, provides benefits like high performance, low cost, greater reliability, simplified assembly and maintenance and reduced inventory needs due to interchangeability and programmability. The ability to program PICs also gives them an extended life. PICs are particularly applicable for portable electronic products because they are:
« Flexible
« Compact
« Low cost
« Widely available
« Low consumers of power
« Generally purpose versatile
« able to include access to free development tools

A3.4.10
Electronic systems, including those with embedded microcontrollers, act as a bridge between the input and the output, translating the initial stimulus into a desired action or outcome. Output devices are used to perform a specific function based on an input or stimulus. This is a fundamental concept in control systems and automation.
Analogue output devices are essentially transducers (i.e. they convert energy from one form to another) that take digital information (usually from a computer or control system) and convert it from a digital form into continuous, real-world signals like voltage or current. They are used to control devices and systems by providing a variable output that can be adjusted smoothly, unlike digital outputs, which are discrete on/off signals.
Analogue devices include the following:
« Microphone: Converts sound waves (acoustic energy) into electrical signals.
« Loudspeaker: Converts electrical signals into sound waves (acoustic energy).
« Thermocouple: Converts temperature differences into a voltage.
« Photodiode: Converts light into continuous electrical signals with the strength proportional to the intensity of light received.
« Sonar transducer: Converts electrical signals into sound waves
« Haptic devices: Many haptic devices convert analogue electrical signals to control force feedback
« Headphones: Convert electrical signals into sound waves
« Speakers: Use an analogue electrical signal to move the speaker cone and create sound waves
« Motors: Variable Frequency Drives (VFDs) operate in response to a continuous range of electrical inputs.
Digital outputs frequently utilise logic gates and microcontrollers to process and transmit information as discrete signals, although both digital and analogue circuits employ fundamental components like resistors, capacitors and transistors their configurations and applications differ significantly.
Digital devices include the following:
« Printers: Converts digital data (a digital signal) and converts them into a physical, tangible output
« Monitors: Modern computer monitors use digital signals to represent and display images
« Projectors: Display images or videos on a larger screen or surface.
« Braille readers: Convert digital signals into a tactile output for visually impaired users to read text.
« Digital signage: displaying information in public spaces
« Haptic Devices: Many haptic devices convert digital signals into mechanical vibrations
« Light Emitting Diodes (LEDs): receive their input of electrical current in a digital format, ‘'on/off' to produce a visible output (light) that is also either 'on’ or 'off".
« Liquid Crystal Displays (LCDs): receive their input in a digital format, which can be displayed digitally without conversion
« Motors: some motors can be controlled by a digital on/off control function
« Solenoids: typically operate in an on/off, or binary, manner, meaning they are either fully energised or completely de-energised
« Relays: act as electrically controlled switches, having only two states: 'on' and 'off', although the signal controlling the relay could be analogue.

A3.4.11
Open- and closed-loop electronic circuits are used to control the dynamic behaviour or amplification factor of a system, known as the ‘gain’ In an open-loop system, an input is accepted and operated on before an output is created, as illustrated in Figure A 3.4.11a. An open loop does not monitor the output to modify the input signal, meaning there is no feedback loop. The gain is therefore solely dependent on the internal characteristics of the amplifier.
Because of this, any changes in the processing of the input
that might affect the output are not taken into account. An example of an open loop would be the operation of a simple light switch in which the light is turned on or off without any feedback to adjust the brightness.
In a closed-loop system, the output is fed back and compared with the desired result. This information is then used to adjust the input to modify the output, as illustrated by the block diagram for a closed-loop system in Figure A34.11b.
Examples of closed-loop systems would be the operation of a thermostat to maintain a desired temperature, automobile cruise control and automatic brightness control of PCs and phones.
There are two basic forms of feedback encountered in electronic systems, known as positive and negative feedbacks. Both forms of feedback return part of the output signal to a comparator circuit, which compares the output produced with the desired output. If the signals differ, an error signal is produced that is used to adjust the input signal. Then the cycle continues until the desired
output requirements are achieved.
Positive feedback systems do this by adding the return signal to the input signal, and can result in a rapid cycle of amplification (as illustrated in Figure A3.4.11c), leading to system overload or saturation, meaning that the output voltage is as positive as it is capable of being. The annoying squeal sometimes encountered with a microphone is an example of positive feedback. Positive feedback is used in systems where rapid transition between positive and negative saturation is desired. When the input voltage is higher or lower than the reference voltage, the output voltage moves to either the positive or negative saturation positions, such that a continuously variable input is transformed into a square wave that undergoes abrupt step changes of positive to negative, shown in Figure A3.4.11d. This is sometimes referred to as clipping.
By contrast, in negative feedback systems, the return signal is subtracted from the input, resulting in a reduction of the input and a tendency to form a self-correcting loop that will drive the system to stabilisation at the aim. Such systems can be prone to ‘hunting, however, (in which the output overshoots the aim and oscillates towards its goal), if an excessive delay in the feedback signal is present, as illustrated in Figure A3.4.11d
Negative feedback loops can also be prone to saturation if the system reaches a physical or operational limit, such as a maximum capacity or a point where further input doesn’t produce a change in output. In these cases, the negative feedback mechanism is still operating, but its effect is limited by the constraints of the system. While both systems have their advantages, most use negative rather than positive feedback.

A3.4.12
An operational amplifier (op amp) is a high-gain, DC-
controlled, analogue amplifier, meaning that a large
amplification of the input signal is obtained and fed as the
output. The Op-amp received its name from its original
purpose of performing mathematical operations in early
computers.
To achieve this, the op-amp contains a number of
components, including transistors and resistors, by which
the two input voltages are compared and the difference
between them amplified. Because the op-amp has
become an indispensable component in itself, it is usually
represented by a simple block diagram symbol as shown
in Figure A3.4.12a. All of the voltages are measured with
respect to the ground, although for convenience the ground
terminals are not shown in the standard representation.
As indicated by the labels in Figure A3.4.12a, one input
(V) has a negative sign and is known as the inverting
input, and the other (V) has a positive sign and is known
as the non-inverting input. The power supplies +V__and
-V, are often omitted from the diagram for the simpler
form shown in Figure A3.4.12b.
Op-amps are typically controlled by negative feedback, by
which the output is fed back to the negative input in an
attempt to make the difference between the inputs zero.
InInternet of Things (IoT) devices, the signals from sensors
need to be amplified to make them strong enough to be
processed by microcontrollers or other digital devices. Op-
amps, particularly analogue op-amps, are commonly used
to amplify signals from sensors in IoT home appliances
and other applications. They can amplify analogue signals
from sensors, acting as a pre-amplifier before the signal
is processed further, often by a microcontroller or digital
system. Op-amps also find use in various other analogue
circuits like filters, integrators and voltage comparators.
Op-amps can be used as part of the circuitry in Analogue-
to-Digital Converters (ADCs) to amplify and condition
the analogue signal before it is converted to a digital
representation.

A3.4.13
Embedded systems are essentially specialised computer
systems designed to perform a dedicated function within
a larger device or system. They enable devices to sense,
process information and communicate with other devices
or systems, often without direct human interaction. This
communication canbe wired or wireless, and it uses various
protocols and interfaces. These include serial protocols
like UART, SPI, and 12C, which are commonly used
for communication within a system or between nearby
devices. For communication over longer distances or with
networks, protocols like Ethernet and USB are employed,
often involving additional hardware and software layers.
Wireless protocols such as Wi-Fi, Bluetooth and Zigbee
are also used for communication in embedded systems.
Embedded systems enhance the functionality, efficiency,
and automation capabilities of everyday products by
enabling devices to perform specific tasks, often in real-
time, leading to increased automation, automotive control
and use in medical devices. All of which lead to improved
performance and enhanced user experiences. In addition,
their lower power requirement compared with general
purpose computers makes them suitable for battery-
powered or energy-constrained applications.
By the use of error detection and correction mechanisms
and fault-tolerant designs, embedded systems reduce
errors and assist in fault finding and correction operations.
These strategies minimise the likelihood of errors and
ensure that even if they occur, the system can recover or
continue to operate safely.
Digital systems often communicate with each other using
embedded systems to perform specific tasks This is a key
aspect of Internet of Things (IoT) devices, such as smart
thermostats, wearable health trackers and industrial
sensors, that use embedded systems to collect data, process
it and communicate with the internet or other devices.

A3.4.14
‘There are hundreds of symbols used in a circuit diagram
to identify components. A selection of the more common
symbols is provided in Figure A3.4.14a. The International
Electrotechnical Commission (IEC) standards, such as
IEC 60617 (also known as BS 3939) and IEC 61131-3,
provide guidelines for these symbols.

B3.1
Guiding question
Why is material selection crucial to designers of
products?
Material selection is crucial in product design because it
directly impacts a product’s functionality, aesthetics, cost,
manufacturability and environmental impact. Choosing
the right materials is essential for ensuring a product
performs as intended, meets design requirements and is
commercially viable.

B3.1.1
The initial step in selecting materials for a specific
application and design involves clearly defining the
necessary properties the product must have, through
an understanding of the products intended use and
the specific conditions it will encounter. This includes
considering factors like strength, stiffness, resistance to
environmental degradation, extremes of heat and cold,
impact resistance, abrasion resistance, etc.
Based on the performance requirements, a selection of
potential materials that best meet the properties and
performance criteria is developed and often involves the
use of material selection charts (Ashby Charts) to evaluate
different materials.
The key material properties that will ensure the product
functions as intended need to be identified. These
properties might include tensile strength, toughness,
flexibility, thermal conductivity, corrosion resistance, UV
tolerance, etc., strength, durability, cost and environmental
impact, as well as how the material will be processed which
often requires compromises.
The choice of material will also be influenced by the
manufacturing process, as some materials are easier
to machine, mould or weld than others, in addition
to consideration of the cost and feasibility of different
manufacturing methods.
The environmental impact of the material throughout its
lifecycle (from production to disposal) is also becoming
increasingly important and needs to be factored into the
material selection process.
Material selection charts
(Ashby Charts)
Material selection charts, also known as Ashby plots, are
graphical tools used in materials science and engineering
to visualise and compare the properties of different
materials. Developed in the 1990s by Professor Michael
Ashby of Cambridge University, Ashby plots are an
invaluable tool for material selection in various design
applications.
These charts typically plot material properties on a
logarithmic scale, allowing for comparison of different
materials against selected performance indices such as
strength versus density. By plotting performance indices
for a variety of materials on the same graph, those materials
best suited for specific applications are highlighted.
Ashby plots also reveal trade-offs between different
properties. For instance, a material might have high
strength but also high density, which can be easily seen
on the plot. By drawing lines of constant performance
(often called ‘design lines’), engineers can quickly screen
materials that satisfy the desired performance criteria and then further refine their selection based on other factors.
The plot of strength versus maximum service temperature
narrows down candidate materials for an application requiring high-temperature strength, while revealing the limitations of other material classes such as polymers, under such conditions.
With the aid of such charts, an initial screening 
and selection can be undertaken by identifying materials
that meet specific performance requirements, such as
mechanical properties, environmental impact, corrosion
resistance, manufacturability, density or cost.
Ashby plots allow:
« the retrieval of the typical properties of a particular
material,
« a comparison of the properties of different materials,
revealing their comparative efficiencies,
« the selection of the materials/manufacturing processes
during the product design stage, and
«+ substitution studies exploring the potential of one
material to replace another.
Performance Indices
Beyond basic property comparisons, Ashby plots can
be used to visualise performance indices, which are
combinations of material properties that are relevant to a
specific application. These indices are typically commonly
applied mathematical relationships such as specific
strength (o, / p), specific stiffness (E / p) and fracture
toughness (MPa.m) but can include any relevant measure
such as the ratio of any property with respect to cost e.g.
the ratio of Thermal Conductivity to cost (A / §) or energy
per cubic metre as illustrated in Figure B 3.1.1c.
By combining material properties into a single value,
performance indices enable users to quickly rank
materials and identify the most suitable options for a
given design or function. Using an Ashby plot of strength
versus specific stiffness, for example, identifies materials
that are strong and stiff for their weight. Such a plot would
also reveal trade-offs, such as materials that might be
very strong but also heavy, while others might be stiff but
relatively weak. The Ashby plot shown in Figure B 3.1.1c
of strength against energy per cubic metre shows the
relationship between a material’s ability to withstand force
and its energy content per unit volume. It helps identify
materials that offer the best strength for a given energy
input, guiding material selection for applications where
energy efficiency is crucial.
Materials with higher strength-to-energy ratios will be
located towards the upper left corner of the plot. This
indicates that they can withstand greater forces for a given
amount of energy input. This information is valuable
for selecting materials for lightweight structures, energy
storage devices or other applications where minimising
energy consumption is important.
Computer-aided material selection tools are available
that incorporate databases of material properties and
algorithms for calculating performance indices. These tools
incorporate features like Ashby charts and performance
indices to facilitate the selection process. Examples include
GitHub, Ansys Granta Edupack, Cambridge Engineering
Selector (CES) and Material Property Databases such as
‘MatWeb, ‘Matmatch;, and ‘MakeltFrom’

B3.1.2
aterials are often selected for specific applications based
on their aesthetic characteristics, which can include
appearance, texture, and how they interact with light.
These characteristics play a crucial role in the overall
design and emotional appeal of a product, influencing
user perception and experience as illustrated by the
requirements pyramid shown in Figure B3.1.2a. It is this
that designers often strive to develop to differentiate their
product from that of competitors’ products that may have
the same functionality and usability. To create desirability
and satisfaction over and above functionality, follow the
four pleasures discussed in Section CL.3.
Wood offers a natural grain texture and can be stained
or varnished for colour and protection. Metals can
be polished, brushed, etched, sandblasted, painted or
powder-coated for different textures and colours. Plastics
and glass can have a variety of textures created by using
a textured mould, while textiles can be chosen for their
inherent textures and dyed or printed for colour.
The choice of finishing technique depends on factors like
the material type, desired aesthetic, required durability
and cost considerations and will determine the look and
feel of the finished product. They create what can be
considered part of the product’s personality or character
through which a product can be differentiated from its
competitors. The product personality includes not only
the aesthetics but the associations and perceptions these
aesthetics engender, as illustrated in Figure B3.1.2b,
influencing how users perceive and interact with the final
product,
Celebrity endorsements, for instance, create associations
and perceptions rather than directly impacting a product’s
aesthetic qualities. They create a connection in the
consumer’s mind between the celebrity’s positive attributes
(like trustworthiness, expertise or attractiveness) and the
endorsed product, influencing the product’s perceived
personality.
Research suggests that designs that create a product
personality that aligns with (or is congruent with) a
consumer’s perception of themselves will create a greater
sense of attachment. Marketing campaigns often utilise
the strategy of linking product personality to a consumer’s
self-image or aspirations by associating the product
with positive lifestyles, particularly those involving
sophistication, sport, travel, self-sufficiency and leisure.

B3.1.3
A number of factors, such as life cycle costs, environmental
impact and availability, including considering local
sourcing and recyclability, affect material selection.
Balancing these factors requires thorough research,
comparison of different options and potentially consulting
with experts,
« The cost of materials directly impacts the overall
project budget. Readily available and easily processed
materials tend to be less expensive than specialised or
high-performance materials. It is important, however,
to not only consider initial purchase costs but total
costs over a materials entire lifespan, including
maintenance and disposal.
he reliability of supply chains for the chosen materials
and potential disruptions that could impact availability
must also be considered.

B3.1.4
Secondary research provides background information,
identifies potential materials, and helps understand the
broader context of material selection. Materials property
data is readily available that allows easy comparison and
identification of materials in the marketplace place such as
shown in Figure B3.1.4a. This form of research is often the
first avenue taken to identify potential candidate materials
and to identify potential problems encountered under
similar situations and environments.
Primary research provides first-hand, original data, based
on tests performed on selected materials to compare and
contrast properties, assess performance under defined
conditions or gather user feedback from prototypes.
Combining both approaches allows for a comprehensive
understanding and a robust justification for material selection, manufacturing processes, and cost considerations.
Example:
Development of a new lightweight bicycle for on-road use.
Desired Properties: Good stiffness-to-weight (Specific
stiffness), comfortable ride, durability, moderate cost.
Secondary research would identify existing research on
materials like bamboo, steel, carbon fibre, aluminium
alloys and titanium alloys for bicycle frames similar to that
outlined in Figure B 3.1.4b.
rimary research would involve the collection of feedback
from cyclists about their needs and preferences regarding
frame weight, stiffness, comfort, durability and cost.
Based on the secondary research and cyclist feedback,
material selection might then proceed with the
development of a prototype for primary research testing,
possibly using focus groups of cyclists interacting with the
bicycle to elicit comments regarding improvements and
performance relative to currently available bicycles.

B3.2.1
In engineering and architecture, a structure is a complex
system or arrangement of interconnected parts designed
to support weight and resist forces. Such structures are
visible everywhere around us, in cars, machinery, bridges,
dams and buildings. as well as in everyday domestic
articles such as chairs and tables. Each of these structures
has been designed to perform a specific function and to be
stable while withstanding forces or loads.
In engineering terms, a structural design is assessed to
ensure it can effectively perform its intended function and
is in compliance with relevant standards and codes. At the
initial design stage, this often includes modelling of the
structure using computer-based methods such as Finite
Element Analysis (FEA) to confirm resistance to expected
loads and to ensure that appropriate safety factors have
been included.
During construction and throughout the life of a
structure, visual inspections, testing and analysis often
continue to identify any potential issues or deficiencies.
Such assessments are crucial for ensuring the safety
of those who interact with the structure, maintaining
regulatory compliance and informing decisions related to
renovations, insurance or property transactions.

B3.2.2
There are five basic types of stress, outlined below and in
Figure B 3.2.1a, that a structure may be subject to:
« Tension - stresses produced by forces trying to pull
apart or lengthen a material, and are distributed
uniformly over the cross-section.
« Compression - stresses produced by forces trying to
compress or reduce the length of a material, and are
distributed uniformly over the cross-section.
« Shear stresses are produced by forces trying to slide
one part of the material over the other and may be
single, double or punching. Shear stress distribution
is not uniform across a cross-section, unlike tensile
and compressive stresses. They vary depending on the
shape of the cross-section and are typically maximum
at the neutral axis and zero at the outermost surfaces.
« Torsion - stress induced by a twisting force, also
known as torque, Torsional stress developed in a
material is highest at the surface and tends to zero at
the centre. This is why hollow drive shafts are often
used; they offer the same torsional strength as a solid
shaft of the same outer diameter, but with less material
and therefore less weight.
« Bending - also known as flexural stress results in
tensile and compressive stress on opposite sides of a
neutral axis, with maximums at the outer surface.
In addition to the types of stress illustrated in Figure
B 3.2.1a, there are several ways in which loads are imposed
on a structure that may give rise to these stresses. These
loads need to be provided for in the design in order to
maintain structural integrity. These loads are outlined
below and illustrated in Figure B 3.2.1b for a small
building:
« Dead Loads - represent permanent, unchanging
static loads of the structure (e.g. Walls, Floor, etc)
and are primarily vertical, inducing compressive
and bending moments. These loads are crucial in
structural engineering as they form part of the design
assumptions, especially when considering the capacity
of the foundation to withstand the overall weight.
Determining dead loads is often an iterative process, as
member sizes and weights may need adjustments during
the design to ensure they can carry their weight and any
superimposed loads.
« Live loads (Imposed loads) - these are temporary
loads and include all the variable loads that are not
part of the permanent structure. These loads are also
primarily vertical and compressive, unless unevenly
distributed, which can lead to bending stresses.
Examples include the weight of people, furniture and
vehicles - all of which are assessed during the design
phase and included in the structural calculations.
Unforeseen loads, like locks placed on guardrails, are
typically accounted for in structural design through the
application of safety factors and consideration of potential
misuse. These factors ensure the structure can withstand
loads beyond the expected operational ones, providing
a margin for unexpected events, misuse or material
degradation. However, they can pose a risk to the structure
if the magnitude and distribution on the structure are not
managed appropriately.
« Environmental Loads (Wind, Rain, Snow, Seismic,
Flood, Thermal) - These loads are often horizontal and
are generally bending loads, but they can also induce
compressive, tensile and shear stresses depending on
the structure and the load distribution.
For example, wind pressure pushing against a building’s
wall will create bending in the wall, inducing compressive
stresses on the windward side and tensile stresses on the
leeward side. Thermal expansion/contraction can involve
compressive and tensile stresses and be both horizontal
and vertical in nature, depending on the constraints.
Seismic loads, by comparison, are a combination of tensile,
compressive, and shear forces with their magnitude and
direction changing rapidly throughout a seismic event.
Environmental loads are accounted for in building
codes that take into account local circumstances, such
as the frequency and intensity of the environmental
factors. Building codes dictate how to design structures
to withstand these forces, ensuring safety and stability,
especially in regions prone to specific hazards.
s Other (Settlement, Vibrational, Impact) -
Settlement refers to the downward vertical movement of
soil or ground due to applied loads. This movement is
typically a result of the soil compressing or consolidating
under the weight of a structure or other load. The direction
of settlement is primarily vertical and downwards.
Hydrostatic loads on a structure are associated with the
presence of groundwater and act horizontally, normal to
subsurface structures such as foundations, increasing in
magnitude with depth. This pressure can be significant,
especially in areas with high groundwater tables,
potentially causing damage to foundations if not properly
accounted for in the structural design.
Vibrational stresses can arise from machinery, wind and
seismic activity and can be a complex combination of
compression, tension, shear, torsion or bending, acting
in multiple directions. The specific type and direction
depend on the nature of the vibration and the structure’s
response. Similarly, loading imposed through impact
will also often involve a complex interplay of tensile, compressive, and shear stresses, which can vary significantly across
different parts of the structure.
‘There are four basic means by which a structure can be
strengthened.
« Reinforcement with additional materials to increase its
strength and load-bearing capacity.
» Modifying the design by adding bracing to increase
resistance to lateral forces like wind or earthquakes.
« Increasing the thickness or density of existing materials
to improve their strength.
« Improving connections by using stronger bolts or
welding.
A typical tensile test stress-strain diagram, also known as a
stress-strain curve, is shown in Figure B 3.2.2a, annotated
to show the important features.
‘The stress-strain diagram shows the relationship between
tensile loading of a material and its reaction to that load in
terms of extension.
Stress (o) is the relationship between an applied load and
a material’s cross-sectional area, measured in pascals (Pa).
While the stress-strain curve for tensile testing is the most
commonly encountered, similar curves are available for
compression, shear and torsional tests, each describing
how materials behave under different types of loading.
Pascals are a unit of pressure and reflect a force of one
Newton per square metre or N/m’.
In some calculations, cross-sectional area is determined
in mm’, giving stress in terms of N/mm” or megapascals
(MPa).
Strain (e) is an engineering quantity that measures the
change in length of a body relative to its original length
when subjected to a load. Strain is a dimensionless
quantity.
Tensile, compressive and shear forces are most often
measured in megapascals.
Figure B 3.2.1a displayed the three fundamental types of
stress: tensile, compression and shear.
As can be seen from Figure B 3.2.2a, a number of
interesting things happen during the test. Firstly, the
graph shows a straight line relationship between stress and
strain. This represents a zone of elastic extension in which
the material under test will return to its original length
once the load is removed. This will be treated later in this
section. Following the elastic region, if loading continues,
plastic deformation will occur, meaning that, if the load is
removed, the original length will not return. This position
is known as the yield point.
YIELD POINT / PROOF STRESS
The yield point occurs on the stress-strain diagram where
the test specimen continues to stretch (strain) without
an increase in load (stress). Some materials, notably low-
carbon steels, exhibit two such points that are identified
as the upper and lower yield points. For structural
applications, the yield stress is usually a more important
property than the tensile strength, since once the yield
point is passed, the structure has deformed beyond
acceptable limits.
Many materials do not show a clearly defined yield point
and, for these, a transition position to yielding is called
the proof stress (or off-set yield stress) and represents the
stress level at which a material will experience a specific
amount of permanent deformation (typically 0.2% or
0.1% of the original gauge length), see Figure B 3.2.2b.
These materials transition smoothly from elastic to plastic
deformation, making it difficult to pinpoint the exact
moment yielding begins.
Medium and high-carbon steels, along with aluminium
alloys, copper, titanium, magnesium and some polymers
are examples of materials that display no obvious yield
point and require the application of the proof stress
process to create a pseudo yield point known as proof
stress. These materials don’t show a defined transition to
plastic deformation like low-carbon steel; instead, they
gradually deform, so engineers use the offset method
to define a practical yield-like point. This ensures safe
design limits and helps avoid permanent deformation in
structural applications.
Ultimate tensile stress
‘When stress continues in the plastic zone, the stress-strain
curve passes through a maximum, called the ultimate
tensile strength (UTS). Ductile materials may begin
to ‘neck’ while continuing to elongate before fracture,
illustrated in Figure B 3.2.2c. Necking refers to the localised
reduction in cross-sectional area. Both elongation and
reduction of area are measures of ductility.
Calculations involving stress and
strain
Example 1: Determine the ultimate tensile stress (UTS)
of a 15 mm diameter rod if the maximum load it can hold
is 70 kN.
Load =70x 10°N
Diameter(d) = 15 mm
Area =mxd’ +4
=176.7 mm’
UTS =load + area
=70%10° + 176.7
=396 Nmm™ or 396 MPa
Example 2: A concrete column (160 mm in diameter
and 1.5 m high) is placed under a compressive load until
failure occurs. If the column fails at a load of 450 kN,
determine the ultimate compressive stress (UCS) of the
column. Superscript again for kilo newtons twice
Load =450 x 10° N
Diameter(d) = 160 mm
Area =nxd +4
=20 % 10° mm’
UCS =load + area
=450 x 10* + 20 x 10°
=2238 Nmm™ or22.38 MPa
Example 3: A mild steel rod of 120 mm in length is
stretched to a length of 120.2 mm. Find the strain (g)
present.
Strain = change in length + original length
=0.2+120
e =0.00167
Note: As long as the units used are consistent, there is no
need to convert millimetres into metres.
Single shear stress
In this situation (Figure B 3.2.2d), a load of 17 kN is
attempting to shear a pin holding the two plates together.
The area undergoing shear is the cross-sectional area of
the pin.
Pin diam = 25 mm load
=17x10°N
Shear area = I1x d*/4
=490.87 mm’
o =load + area
=17 x 10 + 490.87
=346 Nmm™ or 34.6 MPa
Double shear stress
In double shear, the area undergoing shear occurs in
two separate planes (Figure B 3.2.2e), so both must be
considered.
The effect of double shear is to reduce the overall stress in
the system by increasing the area of shear.
Determine the shear stress in the 12 mm pin if the
maximum force applied is 15 kN.
Load =15x10°N
Diameter = 12 mm load
Area=nx 122 +4
=113 mm’,
Because the system is in double shear, the total shear area
will be:
A =113x2
=226 mm?
o =load + area
=15x 10’ + 226
=66.37 Nmm™ or 66.37 MPa
Punching shear stress
In this situation (Figure B 3.2.2f), the shear area is not the
cross-sectional area but the area defined by the perimeter
(in this case, the circumference) of the punch, n x 4,
where the shearing action takes place, multiplied by the
thickness of the plate.
Ifa 40 mm hole is punched in a 5 mm thick plate using a
force of 27 kN, calculate the stress generated.
Load =27 x 10°N
Diameter = 40 mm
Thickness = 5 mm
Shear area =I1x40 x 5
= 6283 mm’
o =load +area
=27 % 10" + 628.3
=42.97 MPa
Young’s Modulus
In Section A 3.1.6, Young’s Modulus (or the modulus
of elasticity) was identified as a measure of stiffness or
resistance to deflection when a body is subjected to axial
tensile or compressive stresses, and is visible on the stress-
strain diagram as a straight line, as illustrated in Figure
B 3.2.2a. As it is with tensile and compressive strength, the
Young’s modulus of a material is temperature dependent
and will generally decrease with rising temperature. This is
because increased thermal energy causes atoms to vibrate
more, weakening the interatomic bonds and making the
material more flexible and less resistant to deformation
under stress.
This relationship between stress and strain within the
elastic region was discovered by the English scientist
Robert Hooke (1635-1705) and is known as Hooke’s Law.
The point where this linear relationship ends, marking the
beginning of non-linear behaviour, is called the elastic
limit (or proportional limit). Beyond the elastic limit, a
material may exhibit plastic deformation, where it does
not fully return to its original shape when the load is
removed.
Although Hooke was involved in a wide range of scientific
pursuits, including major contributions to astronomy,
meteorology, geology, microscopy and optics. He was also
one of the first to investigate biological samples, leading
to the naming of the cell. He is, however, principally
remembered for his work on the elastic nature of
materials.
Hooke's Law, describing the relationship between stressand
strain within the elastic limit of a material, states, ‘that the
force needed to deform a material is directly proportional
to the amount of deformation. This relationship was
discovered during his work with springs and resulted
in Hooke's general constant of proportionality (k) for
a material under compression or tension in the elastic
zone, which can vary based on an object’s dimensions and
geometry. Despite Hooke's discovery of this relationship,
the modulus of elasticity is called Young’s modulus rather
than Hooke's modulus.
A closely related concept, Young’s modulus, was developed
by the English scientist Thomas Young (1773-1829),
and quantifies this ratio as a constant (E) representing
a material’s stiffness and its ability to deform elastically,
and is therefore considered to be a mechanical property
of a material. It is represented by the slope of the stress-
strain curve within the elastic region. A high Young's
modulus (steep slope) indicates a stiff material, while a
lower modulus (shallower slope) indicates a more flexible
material, as shown previously in Figure A 3.1.6b. Young's
modulus (E) is therefore a specific application of Hooke's
Law and has the units of GPa (10* MPa)
From this, the mathematical relationship can be expressed
as:
Stress = k x Strain or
E = Stress/Strain
Example 1: Calculate the Youngs modulus (E) for an
18 mm square section of steel 3 m long, given it is stressed
to 21.6 MPa and exhibits an extension of 0.2 mm.
Stress (o) =21.6 MPa
Strain (g) = extension + original length
=02+3x10’
=6.66x10°
(Note that Strain is a dimensionless number since the
numerator and denominator are in a common unit, in this
case, millimetres)
E = Stress/Strain
=21.6+666x 107
= 324,324 MPa
=324 GPa
Example 2: Using the force-extension plot obtained for
Ductile iron in Figure B 3.2.2g, calculate the Young’s
Modulus, assuming a diameter of 12 mm.
Data provided:
Force F =45 x 10°N
Original diameter (d)) = 12mm
Cross-sectional area = 113.1mm*
% Elongation (AL/Ly)) x 100 = 0.15%
.-, Strain (AL/L) = 0.0015mm
Calculate Stress
Stress (o) = Force + Area
= 45000N = 113.1mm*
=397.9 MPa
Young’s Modulus (E) = Stress/Strain
=397.9/0.0015
=1265,267 N mm> (MPa)
=265 GPa
Stress-strain diagrams
Stress-strain diagrams can be generated for axial tension
and compression, and shear loading conditions. Almost all
of the mechanical properties of a material can be obtained
from its stress-strain diagram curve (Figure B 3.2.2a).
The information we can obtain from the stress-strain
curve includes;
» Young’s modulus
« Elastic limit
» Yield stress
« Ultimate stress
» Breaking stress
» Resilience
« Ductility
« Toughness
« Elongation
» Brittleness
Engineering and true stress
If the original cross-sectional area is used to calculate the
stress for every value of applied force, then the outcome
is known as engineering stress. However, if the applied
force is divided by the actual (constantly changing) value
of the cross-sectional area, then the outcome is known as
the true stress, see Figure B 3.2.2h.
True stress-strain curves are used when analysing material
behaviour under significant plastic deformation, especially
in scenarios like metal forming or crash simulations.
They provide a more accurate representation of material
behaviour during deformation than engineering stress-
strain curves because they account for the changing cross-
sectional area and length of the material as it deforms.
Working stress, also known as allowable stress, is a design
concept that uses a factor of safety to ensure a material or
structure doesn’t fail under load. This value is determined
by dividing the material's yield strength (or ultimate
tensile strength) by a factor of safety. There will be more
on Factors of Safety in Section B 3.2.5.

B3.2.3
ll structures, from a garden shed to a house, office tower
or bridge, need to be designed and constructed with due
recognition of the forces that will act on that structure.
Failure to do so risks delays, added costs and the potential
for collapse and injury.
The ‘Bent Pyramid’ built in 2600 BCE for the pharach
Sneferu is an early example of a structure that showed
early signs of instability due to the excessive weight being
carried at the original build angle, requiring a change to
avert collapse, see Figure B 3.2.3a.
In more recent history, a number of structural failures
have occurred due to an inadequate assessment of forces
acting on the structures. Two famous historical examples
are the Quebec Bridge collapse of 1907 and the Tacoma
Narrows Bridge failure of 1940. These are outlined below
due to their dramatic nature, although many others could
equally have been cited.
The Quebec Bridge collapse in
1907
In 1907, the central span of the Quebec Bridge suddenly
collapsed during construction, falling into the St Lawrence
River, with the death of 75 of the 86 men working on the
bridge at the time, see Figure B 3.2.3b. The cause of this
failure was found to be primarily an inadequate design of
the lower chords in the anchor arm near the main pier,
specifically the latticing on the compression chords. The
weight calculations for the bridge were found to have been
significantly underestimated, leading to an inaccurate
assessment of the required strength of the structural
members,
Although early signs of buckling of the bridge were
detected, it took almost a month before the seriousness
was acknowledged, by which time buckling of the lower
compression chords was noticed. Two hours after the
initial buckling of the lower cords was reported, the bridge’s
central span collapsed into the river. The St. Lawrence
River was blocked by the wreckage for approximately two
years.
Buckling is a phenomenon uniquely associated with long,
thin structural elements subjected to compressive loads.
If a critical compressive load is exceeded, the structura
The collapse of the Quebec Bridge had a profound impact
on engineering practices, leading to significant changes
in design, supervision and professional standards. The
disaster highlighted the dangers of inadequate supervision,
the importance of rigorous design checks, and the need
for clear lines of responsibility in engineering projects.
Tacoma Narrows Bridge collapse
1940
In 1940, a suspension bridge was designed to span the
Tacoma Narrows Strait in the US state of Washington.
With a total length of ~1,810 m and a main span between
the two towers of 840 m, the bridge was revolutionary for
its time due to its unprecedented slenderness. Its main
span was designed with a depth-to-span ratio of 1:350,
meaning the depth of the stiffening plate girder was 350
times smaller than the span length, compared to a typical
ratio of 1:84 at the time. In addition, the bridge’s width-to-
span ratio of 1:72 was also the narrowest among bridges of
similar length.
The bridge’s narrow roadway and shallow plate girders
made it highly susceptible to wind-induced oscillations.
This resulted in a phenomenon called aeroelastic flutter,
where wind forces caused the bridge to twist.
Even during its construction, the bridge earned the
epithet ‘Galloping Gerti€' due to the vertical oscillations
of the bridge deck in the wind. After the bridge opened to
the public on July 1, 1940, these wind driven movements
continued to the extent that four months later, on 7th
November and subjected to a gale force wind speed of
around 42 mph (~67 kph) a twisting motion (torsional
vibration) was induced in the bridge deck leading to its
collapse only 4 months after it had opened to the public,
see Figure B 3.2.3c.
Originally ascribed to wind-driven torsional resonance of
the structure, it was later believed to be a result of wind-
driven amplification of torsional oscillation that, unlike
a resonance that peaks at a specific frequency, increases
with increasing wind speed.
The problem of wind-borne oscillations was not foreseen
because of a limited understanding of aeroelastic effects
and the limited availability of wind-tunnel testing at the
time. Engineers then were primarily focused on vertical
and lateral movement caused by traffic and temperature,
not on the vertical movement caused by wind.
The collapse of the Tacoma Narrows Bridge significantly
changed engineering practices, particularly in suspension
bridge design and wind load analysis. The failure,
caused by aeroelastic flutter, highlighted the importance
of considering aerodynamic stability and led to the
development of more robust designs.
‘The disaster spurred the development and adoption of
more sophisticated analytical techniques, such as finite
element analysis (FEA), to better predict how bridges
would behave under various loads and conditions.
Sampoong Department Store
collapse 1995
The five-story Sampoong Department Store in Seoul,
South Korea, collapsed in 1995 due to a combination of
design flaws, construction errors, and negligence, rather
than natural causes, see Figure B 3.2.3d.
Originally designed to have four above-ground and four
basement levels, the plans were changed mid-project
to five above-ground floors when the owner decided to
change the building to a department store. This conversion
required the removal of some columns to accommodate
escalators and the use of thinner columns at increased
separation compared to the original plans, along with the
installation of a heavy rooftop air conditioning unit. All of
these changes increased the dead weight of the building
while reducing its structural capacity and were undertaken
by the owner without engineering evaluation.
In addition, there was no centralised mechanism to
manage, audit or track the work of contractors and
subcontractors involved in the alterations.
The department store opened in 1990, attracting an
estimated daily customer number of 40,000 people.
Evidence of cracking in the ceiling began to be noticed
five years after opening in April 1995, but was ignored or
dismissed until the building collapsed in June 1995, killing
502 people and injuring 937. The collapse highlights the
consequences of negligence and non-compliance with
building codes and a lack of oversight.
Following the Sampoong Department Store collapse,
South Korea implemented significant reforms, including
enhanced building codes, stricter regulatory oversight,
and a push for greater transparency and accountability in
the construction industry.
Finite element analysis (FEA) began its development in
the 1940s and 1950s, gaining significant momentum in
the 1960s and 1970s with the emergence of commercial
software. While the method has roots in earlier
mathematical concepts, its practical application and
widespread use are attributed to the work of engineers and
researchers in the mid-20th century.
Most often used for stress calculations, Finite Element
Analysis is a computerised method used to test virtual
models under a variety of load conditions. It may be used
to test the design of new products or analyse existing
products in new situations and has greatly increased the
confidence of structural analysis.
FEA results are often shown using colourful diagrams
called contour plots or colour maps, such as those shown
in Figures B 3.2.3e and B 3.2.3f. On these visualisations,
red zones identify high stress or danger zones, i.e. areas
of high stress, while blue zones highlight low stress or
safer zones. The colours blend across the object to locate
areas where stress builds up and areas where things are
calmer.
Other common graphs include displacement plots, which
indicate movement under loading, and safety factor
simulations, which compare how strong the material is vs.
how much stress it is facing.
A number of FEA software packages are available. Some
include components to measure thermal, electromagnetic,
fluid and structural dynamics. Using the structural
assessment component, FEA can simulate how an object
would react under service loads or conditions. Under the
FEA process, a virtual model shows how loads would
be distributed within the object. These reactive forces
may be in response to loads generated by expansion due
to temperature variations, external loads, pressures or
vibration, see Figure B 3.2.3e.
Through the action of dividing the model up into a series
of simple but discrete (finite) elements joined through
nodes, the program can analyse a complex object by
considering it as a series of resolvable units, as illustrated
in Figure B 3.2.3f.
Observations are then made as to how the object may
perform under a variety of conditions. When assessing a
structure, FEA may be employed to generate stiffness and
strength visualisations. This data may, in turn, assist with
weight minimisation, materials selection, manufacturing
process choices and costing.
Complex steel connections can be analysed with Finite
Element Analysis — both to determine the flow of forces
through the connecting members and to determine the
peak stresses in and around welds to determine the fatigue
resistance, see Figure B 3.2.3f. In many cases, FEA is used
to verify simplified design methods.
FEA can be used to study the interactions of assembled
components on-screen, before incurring the costs of
physical prototypes. Static or dynamic loads may be
simulated to evaluate a design’s performance under
extension, bending and twisting to evaluate stress, strain
and displacement. FEA thus allows design simulations to
evolve before manufacturing.
FEA often finds applications in the car industry, where
simulated collisions using components, assemblies or
whole-of-vehicle designs can show the result of various
crash scenarios. The data would show distributed forces,
twisting, buckling, movement and even failure of various
elements of the design that can inform decisions such as
the need for strengthening, see Figure B 3.2.3g.
FEA is very cost and time-saving. Manufacturing and
physical testing costs far outweigh those of producing and
testing a computer simulation. It substantially decreases
the time from design concept to product reality. FEA
analysis, however, can only inform designs to a point
where physical testing may be required to confirm the
specifications of the final product.
Finite Element Analysis can also be employed to assess the
continued viability of a structure or the acceptability of
a product following quality assurance testing. Analysis of
the structure with the defect included allows engineers to
assess the suitability of continued service and the efficacy
of proposed repairs.
The presence of internal flaws in a casting, for example,
can be modelled as either voids or cracks, depending on
the specific application and the nature of the flaw. Cracks
are often modelled as a sharp discontinuity (a very thin
void), especially when simulating fracture mechanics,
while voids can represent larger, rounded defects. The
choice between modelling a flaw as a void or a crack
depends on the specific application. If the focus is on
fracture mechanics and crack propagation, a sharp crack
representation is more appropriate. If the focus is on the
overall mechanical behaviour of a component with a
manufactured defect, then a void representation might be
‘more suitable.
While FEA is today used extensively in the design
of products and structures, providing confidence in
many areas of design, it can produce inaccurate results
if incorrect assumptions, inappropriate modelling
techniques and flawed input data are used. These errors
can lead to misinterpretations of stress, strain, and other
critical parameters, potentially causing costly design flaws
or even structural failures.

B3.2.4
When analysing an engineering structure, Force diagrams,
also known as free body diagrams (FBDs), are used to
generate a visual representation of the forces acting on an
object and to determine the net force resulting in either
motion or equilibrium (stability).
A free body diagram is called ‘free’ because it isolates a
single object from its environment, representing only the
external forces acting on it. These forces are depicted as
arrows, and the object itself can be removed, leaving only
the force vectors acting through its centre of gravity. This
isolation allows for a clear, simplified analysis of the forces
involved, as illustrated in Figure B 3.2.4a.
If all of the forces shown in Figure B 3.2.4a are equal, the
object remains at rest. If, however, F, is greater than the
opposing force F,, the resultant force (F,) will move the
object to the right, and its magnitude will be F, - F,. This
is fairly clear. If, however, the forces to which the object
is exposed are at a variety of angles, the resultant vector
direction and magnitude may not be clear. In this instance,
we use a technique in which the arrows are rearranged
head-to-tail (also known as tip-to-tail).
The magnitude and angle of the resultant force can be
measured directly from the diagram using a rule and
a protractor. A force of 63 N acting at an angle of 28" is
indicated.
Because of inaccuracies in drawing and measurement,
the answer obtained by the graphical method described
will not be exact, but rather a good approximation. A
more accurate, although time-consuming, method of
determining the resultant vector, would be to solve
analytically by resolving each vector into its x and y co-
ordinates.

BEAM AND TRUSS BRIDGES
In its simplest form, a beam bridge is essentially a rigid
horizontal structure resting on two (or more) piers (Figure
B 3.2.4e). The beam itself must be strong enough so that it
does not bend under its weight and the added weight of
crossing traffic. The piers directly support the mass of the
beam and any traffic. While beam bridges originally
consisted of solid wooden beams, the two most common
beam designs today are box and I-beams (Figure
B 3.2.4f).
Manufactured from steel, the I-beam
has the cross-sectional shape of a
capital letter ‘I' and is made up of a
central vertical web and two horizontal
flanges.
For beam designs of similar cross-sectional area, the
I-beam has the greatest strength because it places the
largest amount of material as far away from the neutral
axis of the beam as possible. I-beams are also easily
manufactured by hot rolling processes at steel mills.
Notice that the simple beam shown in Figure B 3.2.4¢ has
two different types of supports, fixed and roller. These will
be explained in more detail later in this chapter.
A box beam typically has two webs and two flanges. The
webs in this design give the box beam greater resistance
to twisting or torsional forces, and it is most often used
in preference to an I-beam in curved structures, where
torsional forces play a greater part.
Any flexing of a beam due to loading causes one edge to
be pushed together or shortened (compression), while the
other edge is pulled apart or stretched (tension), as shown
in Figure B 3.2.4e.
Trusses
Many beam bridges use concrete or steel beams. The size
of the beam, and in particular its height, controls the
distance that the beam can span. Increases in the height
of the beam generate more material to distribute the load,
enabling longer spans. To create very tall beams, engineers
add extra members to create frameworks or trusses.
Individual truss members improve rigidity and greatly
increase the beam’s ability to distribute any compression
and tension throughout the structure. Truss frames are
most often based on triangles. The triangular design
allows the transfer of the load from a single point to a
considerably wider base area. Despite the ability of a truss
system to dissipate loads, beam style bridges can still only
span limited distances. Greater span distances require an
increase in truss size until it reaches a point where the
bridge’s mass becomes so large that it cannot support
itself.
Trusses are often used in architecture and engineering.
They are supporting structures made of beams, girders
or rods, usually made of steel or wood. A truss usually
takes the form of a triangle or a combination of triangles.
The triangular configuration is capable of carrying large
amounts of weight based on the principle that a triangle
cannot collapse or vary its shape unless the length of one
of the sides is changed. The top and bottom chords control
how the compression and tension are distributed.
Truss bridges come in a variety of different styles. The
design, location and composition of the truss determine
the type built. Early in the Industrial Revolution, engineers
developed a range of truss designs and materials. The two most commonly used truss designs were the Howe and
Warren trusses, but a large range exists, including, but
not limited to, Briinel, Fink, K, Pegram, Pratt, etc. Four
examples of truss forms are illustrated in Figure B3.2.4g.
n advantage of truss bridge construction is that a
roadway may pass over or through the structure.
‘Trusses effectively increase the height over which loads are
distributed and allow a greater span length than beams;
they nevertheless have a limit to the practical distance they
can span. To span even larger distances, individual spans
may be joined together and supported by intermediate
piers to resist deflection.
Trusses may also incorporate the features of arch design,
in which the natural shape of the arch adds strength. Two
examples are presented in Figure B 3.2.4h and B 3.2.4i.
ENGINEERING MECHANICS
‘The mechanics of structures involves the calculation of
deformations, deflections, and internal forces or stresses
within structures caused by loading. These calculations
may be performed at the design, evaluation or maintenance
stages of a structure’s life.
Truss analysis
Trusses provide a practical and economic solution to the
design and construction of many structures, including
bridges and buildings. They consist of straight members
connected at joints. All applied forces act at the joints and
the members carry only tensile or compressive forces, In
the real world, structures are composed of many trusses
joined together to make a space frame. The trusses
examined here are only pin-jointed, two-dimensional
constructions, and as such, all forces are coplanar.
Actions (loads)
A concentrated load is one applied over such a small
length that it is considered to act at a point. A uniformly
distributed load (UDL) is distributed evenly over a
specified length and is generally expressed in kN/m.
Figure B 3.2.4j shows examples of both point and
uniformly distributed loads acting on a simply supported
beam.
Reactions at supports
Bridge trusses and frameworks require support. It is usual
for one end of the framework to have a fixed support
(hinge or pin) and the other end a roller or rocker to
allow for movement caused by temperature variations.
Examples of this support are shown in Figure B 3.2.4k
below for the North and South ends of the Hexham bridge
over the Hunter River in NSW. The Northern end is fixed,
while the Southern end has a rocker support. Rocker
supports are similar to rollers in resisting vertical force
while allowing horizontal movement.
The reaction at roller supports is always calculated as
perpendicular to the line joining the centres of the rollers
(Figure B 3.2.41).
The reaction at a pinned support can be in any direction.
This, yet to be determined direction, is often shown by
a wriggly line (Figure B 3.2.4m) and is governed by the
need to maintain equilibrium.
Remember:
For a body, such as a truss, to be stable, it must be in
equilibrium. In simple terms conditions of equilibrium are
met when all of the forces acting on a body are balanced.
These forces include vertical, horizontal and rotational.
Calculating reactions at supports
Equilateral triangles form the basis of the ‘Warren’ truss
shown in Figure B 3.2.4n; therefore, each member is of
identical length. For the purpose of calculations, assume
each member to be of two units in length.
Conversely, R, = 35 kN in an upward direction by
equating the sum of the vertical forces up with the sum
of the vertical forces down (there are no horizontal forces
here to be considered).

Solving truss problems
Forces in individual members and reactions at supports
for bridge structures may be determined by using one
of two methods: the method of joints or the method of
sections.
Method of joints
As we will see in this section, it is here that the free body
diagrams introduced earlier are useful in analysing the
forces acting on each joint. By isolating each joint of
the truss, the forces within each member required for
equilibrium (where the sum of the x and y directions is
zero) can be calculated.
The ‘method of joints’ technique is the simplest way of
resolving forces by isolating truss joints. Using the truss
system from the earlier example (Figure B 3.2.4n), we
have already calculated the reactions at supports. If we
are asked to calculate the force in members 4, 5 and 3, 5,
we first need to isolate the joint in question as shown in
Figure B 3.2.4q.
The forces in these members may be found by using one of
two methods: analytical or graphical.
To solve the problem graphically, we use the knowledge
that if the whole truss is in equilibrium, then any part
of the truss must also be in equilibrium. This being the
case, we can form a force triangle using the members
available.
Note that the triangle must close to create equilibrium, as
in Figure B 3.2.4r.

measuring the unknown members to determine their

This method is not precise, and measurements must be
made to within 0.5 mm to reduce inaccuracies. The larger
the scale employed, the greater the accuracy.
The direction of the force in the member will also indicate
whether the member is in tension or compression.
In the case of member 3,5, the force in the member is
acting away from the joint. To balance this force in the
member, an equal and opposite force must be acting at the
other end of the member as shown in Figure B 3.2.4s. The
internal forces are resisting a stretching or tensile force
imposed from outside; therefore, the member is said to be
in tension (7).

The internal forces are resisting a reducing or compressive

as shown in Figure B3.2.4r.

Member 4, 5 = 20 + sin 60° =23.09 kN
Method of sections
Instead of isolating a single joint, the ‘method of sections’
cuts the truss with a section plane and removes a whole
section to be dealt with separately. This technique has
the advantage of going straight to the member in need
of resolution without working through individual joints
along the way. When establishing the cutting plane, it is
important to remember to cut two or three members, one
of which is the member whose force is to be found.
Example: Find the force in member Y, as seen in the
Warren truss in Figure B 3.2.4u. The method of sections
approach will allow us to go directly to member Y rather
than work through a series of joint analyses.
The first step in solving the problem is finding the reaction
at support R, or R;..
