A line representing a cutting plane is now inserted, taking
care to cut the member in question and three in total, as
shown in Figure B 3.2.4v.
Once the cutting plane is established and the reactions at
supports calculated, one side of the truss may be separated
out for analysis. Usually, the side with the least number
of external forces is chosen; however, the left-hand side
has been selected in this instance (Figure B 3.2.4w).
Remember, if the truss is in equilibrium, then any one
part of that truss will be in equilibrium as long as all forces
acting (internal and external) are considered.
The truss must now be placed in equilibrium by using
the unknown forces to balance the system. A point is
now chosen about which to rotate the truss in space. This
point may be on or off the truss. This point is used to
eliminate all the unknown forces with the exception of the
member to be determined. Multiple forces in members are
removed by finding their point of intersection. This point
is then used as the point of truss rotation. The force in
the member yet to be determined is used to balance the
remaining forces in the truss.
In this case, the unknown members X and Z (Figure
B 3.2.4x) are parallel and do not intersect; therefore,
another intermediate step must be first employed.
X may be found first by rotating the truss around the joint
where the 20 kN force and Y and Z intersect. Because this
will be the fulcrum, the 20 kN force and Y and Z will have
no turning effect on the truss. The only forces affecting the
system will be the 49,17 kN force, the 35 kN force and the
force in member X. By observation, the 49.17 kN force will
cause the truss to turn in a clockwise direction, while the
35 kN force will cause the truss to turn in an anticlockwise
direction. Because the clockwise moment will be larger,
the force in member X must balance the system by causing
an anticlockwise moment as shown in Figure B3.2.4y.
In this situation, we again use 2 as the base length of each
triangle; therefore, V3 will be the perpendicular distance
between the force in member X and the fulcrum. Thus, the
equation for balancing this section of the truss is:

Using the force in X and turning the truss around the left-

Again, by observation, all the external forces are now
acting together to turn the truss in a clockwise direction.
The only balancing force comes from X and is insufficient
to hold the system in equilibrium, so the force in member
Y must be acting to turn the system anticlockwise, placing
Y in tension as shown in Figure B 3.2.4z.
The equation balancing the rotation of this truss is now:

Bending stress, shear force and
bending moments
Internal forces within a beam vary along its length.
Shear force and bending moment diagrams
are graphical representations illustrating the
magnitude of these varying forces at any given
oint along a beam.
Example 1
Find the internal forces acting in the beam shown in
Figure B 3.2.4ab.
To find the internal forces acting in the beam, a series of
points is examined along its length and calculations are
made.
To determine the shear force at point A, only consider the
forces acting on the beam up to this point. Recall Figure
B3.2.1a (detail) which illustrated the meaning of ‘shear”:
The shear force acting on the beam = 3 kNT ; therefore, for
equilibrium, 3 kN is acting internally within the beam.
To determine the bending moment at point A, the length
of the beam up to this point must be considered. To
maintain equilibrium, the bending moment at A must be
0 kNm because the force on the beam acts through the
fulcrum as shown in Figure B3.2 4ac.

A simple correlation between the force diagram and
the production of the shear force and bending moment
diagrams can be shown graphically.
A shear force diagram can be constructed by treating the
forces on the free body diagram as either up or down. Each
force is then plotted on an axis, after which the points are
joined.
Analytically, bending moments can be calculated as the
area under the shear force diagram and the relevant value
plotted on the bending moment diagram.
These values may also be plotted graphically to show
the changing values over the beam as shown in Figure
B 3.2.4af.
Note: To achieve equilibrium, the shear force diagram
starts and finishes at 0. Also, the maximum bending
moment always occurs where the shear force diagram
equals zero.
Example 2
Given the simply loaded beam shown in Figure B 3.2.4ag,

Note: To achieve equilibrium, the shear force diagram
starts and finishes at 0. Also, the maximum bending
moment always occurs where the shear force diagram
equals zero.
Uniformly distributed loads
In many engineering situations, loads do not appear as
point forces but as loads distributed over a length or an
area. Forces such as these are recorded in force per unit
length, ie. kN/m (kN m™). Figure B 3.2.4ah shows a
distributed load created by the mass of a beam measuring
3 kN/m.
If the beam shown in Figure B 3.2.4ai is 6 m long, then
a force of 18 kN is distributed over the entire beam.
Uniformly distributed loads and the accompanying shearIf the beam shown in Figure B 3.2.4ai is 6 m long, then
a force of 18 kN is distributed over the entire beam.
Uniformly distributed loads and the accompanying shear
force and bending moment diagrams are often illustrated
as shown in Figure B 3.2 4ai.
Cantilever loading
A cantilevered beam is anchored at one end only, allowing
overhanging structures to bear loads with no additional
support. Cantilever beams find use in a variety of
structures, including bridges, balconies, stadium roofs,
and even fixed-wing aircraft designs. The Narendra Modi
stadium in Motera, Ahmedabad, Gujarat, India employs
the use of cantilevers that extend 30 meters from a
perimeter steel tension ring to provide an unobstructed
view of the pitch to spectators, see Figure B 3.2.4aj.
BENDING STRESS
Under load, beams will bend slightly. When bending
(as shown in Figure B 3.2.4al), the lower surface has a
tendency to increase in length while the upper surface
wants to contract. This creates a situation where, while the
upper layers are in compression and the lower layers in
tension, there lies a region in the centre (the neutral axis)
where the forces are balanced. The stress across the beam
varies with the maximum values occurring at the outside
surfaces.
he location of the neutral axis is important in calculating
bending stress, but so too is the cross-sectional shape of
the beam and how the mass is distributed around the
neutral axis. Beams of equal mass per unit length and
cross-sectional area, such as shown in Figure B3.2.4am,
will generate different bending stresses.
The second moment of area provides a means for
calculating the relationship between the neutral axis, the
cross-sectional shape of the beam and the distribution of
the mass. The moment of inertia is not related to the length
or material of the beam. The second moment of area is
calculated using the formulae shown in Figure B 3.2.4an.
Notice that the cross-sectional area for each of the beams
is identical.
Note: In IB current (2025 on) exams, the second moment
of area will always be provided.
As can be seen in Figure B 3.2.4an, where a series of
beams with identical cross-sectional areas (3217 mm?)
but varying shapes have their second moment of areas
calculated and later loaded to produce a stress of 100 MPa
in each section, the I-beam is the strongest of the three
sections. It displays the strengthening effect of placing
large amounts of the beam’s mass away from the neutral
axis,

B3.2.5
Factor of safety or safety factor
Factor of safety is a complex concept having a number
of means of calculation, but in its simplest form could be
defined as the extent to which the design strength exceeds
the expected service loads.
The approach most often used in designing engineering
structures is to consider the relationship between the
capacity UTS of the structure or material and the design
or working stress o. Working stress, also known as
allowable stress, is a design concept that uses a factor of
safety to ensure a material or structure doesn't fail under
load. Determined by dividing the material’s yield strength
(or ultimate tensile strength) by a factor of safety.
‘Working stress (o) = UTS + FOS
‘The Factor of Safety of the structure is therefore defined
as:
FOS = UTS + Working stress (o)
From this relationship, it can be seen that the Factor of
Safety should always be greater than one.
In a factor of safety relationship, Ultimate Tensile
Strength (UTS) is primarily used for brittle materials
where yielding is not a significant concern, or when the
design requires the component to withstand failure rather
than just preventing permanent deformation. For ductile
materials, yield strength is generally used in factor of
safety calculations, as permanent deformation (yielding)
is often considered a failure criterion.

‘The size of the safety factor employed is determined by
several considerations;
« Certainty of loads
« Design life of the structure
+ Quality of manufacture
« Consequences of failure
+ Environmental influences
« Criticality of the component
« Ease of repair or maintenance
« Certainty of material properties
« Statutory and code requirements
While safety factors are initially applied during the design
phase, monitoring and reassessment are crucial for
maintaining structural integrity throughout the structure’s
lifespan.
This is important because:
« materials can degrade over time due to exposure
to environmental factors, wear and tear, or other
processes. This can reduce the material’s strength and
its ability to withstand loads.
« a structure’s intended use might change, leading to
different load patterns or increased demands on the
structure, e.g. an increased vehicle weight and number
of vehicles passing over a bridge compared to original
design assumptions.
The Genoa Morandi Bridge
collapse in 1981
The collapse of the Genoa Morandi Bridge in 2018 was
primarily caused by the corrosion of the bridges steel
cables that supported the roadbed and the deterioration
of the concrete, leading to a structural failure. Engineering
experts identified that corrosion, particularly of the
steel cables, significantly reduced the bridge’s strength.
Additionally, the use of prestressed concrete, which is
known to deteriorate more quickly than other materials,
contributed to the weakening of the bridge.
Some concerns were also raised about the adequacy of
past maintenance practices and the overall state of the
structure.
The collapse of the Genoa Morandi Bridge (shown in
Figure B3.2.5a) resulted in 43 deaths and is a tragic example
of how safety factors can change over time, leading to
catastrophic consequences and significant financial
repercussions, The incident highlights the impact of aging
infrastructure, inadequate maintenance and the potential
for severe economic disruption.
In addition to the loss of life, consequential costs of
approximately €422 million in damages were incurred
for companies in the city arising from property damage,
increased costs and lost profits, along with a total (re)
insurance industry loss of between €400 million and €600
million.
Aredesigned bridge (named the Genoa-San George Bridge)
built in 2020 was estimated to have cost between €150 and
€200 million and includes special features integrated into
the design, such as monitoring robots and solar panels to
enhance safety, efficiency and sustainability. The robots,
weighing approximately 2 tonnes, travel along rails on
the underside of the bridge, inspecting the structure and
sending data to a control centre, alerting operators to
maintenance issues, while the solar panels provide a
renewable energy source for the bridge’s operations.
Postscript
Following the collapse of the first rail bridge over the Firth
of Forth in Scotland, the designers of its replacement
(1880s) came up with this memorable way of explaining
how the new bridge would ‘work’

B3.3.1
Mechanical Advantage: Gears
Gears have existed since 2600 BCE, and the invention
of rotating machinery provided a means of controlling
the speed of rotation. Early gears were made from wood
with eylindrical pegs for cogs, lubricated with animal
fat. Today, gears are rarely made from wood but are
manufactured from a wide variety of alternative materials,
including steel, aluminium, brass, plastic and composites,
depending on the application. The motive force to drive
gears has also varied from human and animal to wind,
water, steam, diesel and electricity.
In essence, gears consist of cantilevered protrusions
(teeth) arranged in equal spacing around the edge of a
disc connected to a central shaft. Through their positive
engagement by the meshing of teeth on the surface of
one gear with the teeth on another, they permit the
transmission of power from one location to another by
transferring rotary motion from one shaft to another
(Figure B 3.3.1a). In doing this, they act as a mechanical
advantage (MA), allowing for the efficient transfer of
power and control over rotational motion in various
machines and mechanisms.
or this transfer to be accomplished efficiently, lubrication
is necessary between the meshing surfaces of the gear to
reduce frictional forces that would oppose motion. This is
because most gears (with the exception of the worm gear)
transmit power from one gear to another by a combination
of rolling and sliding contact between mating teeth. Pure
rolling contact occurs in spur gears as the contact passes
through the pitch line position, which represents the
point where the pitch circles of the two gears intersect,
with varying degrees of sliding occurring on either side of
this position. By contrast, worm gears are designed with
a screw-like worm that meshes with a gear (the worm
wheel). Instead of rolling, the worm’s threads slide against
the teeth of the worm wheel as they rotate. This sliding
contact is less efficient than rolling contact and can lead to
increased friction and heat generation.
Gear systems typically contain both a gear that transmits
power (the drive gear) and a gear that receives the
transmitted power (the driven gear or idler gear).
As discussed previously in A 3.3.6, depending on the
combination and type of gears used, the speed, torque
and direction of rotation can be affected. When two gears
mesh together, the smaller of the two is called the gear
pinion, while the larger is called the gear wheel or simply
the gear.
Rather than drawing complex gears (including individual
teeth), gears are often represented by circles or concentric
circles ofvarious sizes (Figure B3.3.1b).
Gears are not only able to increase speed and change the
direction of motion, but may also be configured to increase
torque or turning forces. When two gears are meshed, as
in Figure B 3.3.1c they act in the same way as levers. Each
gear tooth acts like a lever with the fulcrum at the centre of
the gear. The longer the lever is, the greater the force that is
applied to the shaft of the follower.
If, for example, a gear with more teeth (larger radius)
rotates once, it will move a greater distance along its
circumference compared with a gear with fewer teeth
(smaller radius) rotating once.
‘The simple gear train shown in Figure B 3.3.1c shows
two meshed gears of differing sizes. The result of gear ‘A’
making one revolution (clockwise) will be gear ‘B" making
4 revolutions (anticlockwise). This occurs because it takes
the ten-toothed output gear ‘B’ four revolutions to match
the travel of the 40 teeth moved through one revolution
of the input gear ‘A’ In this arrangement, an increase in
output speed is therefore obtained, although a decrease in
torque occurs, Such a relationship represents a high gear
ratio.
When a smaller gear drives a larger gear, as represented
in Figure B3.3.1d, the result of the smaller gear ‘A’ making
one revolution (clockwise) will be gear ‘B’ making 0.25
revolutions (anticlockwise). This occurs because it takes
the 40 teeth of the output gear ‘B’ only a quarter of a
revolution to match the travel of the 10 teeth moved
through one revolution of the input gear ‘Al Therefore, a
decrease in output speed occurs, although an increase in
torque is produced. Such a relationship represents a low
gear ratio.
A gear ratio describes the relationship between two
meshing gears, indicating how many times the drive gear
(input) must rotate for the driven gear (output) to rotate
once. It’s calculated by dividing the number of teeth on the
driven gear by the number of teeth on the driving gear.
number of teeth on driven (output) gear
number of teeth on drive (input) gearGear ratio =
A higher gear ratio (indicating the input gear has more
teeth than the output gear) means the output gear rotates
more slowly but with more torque, while a lower gear ratio
results in faster output speed but less torque.
Gear ratios are often expressed as a ratio, like 3:1, or as
a single number, like 3, which indicates the number
of rotations of the driving (input) gear needed for one
rotation of the driven (output) gear. For example, if the
driven (output) gear has 80 teeth and the driving (input)
gear has 32 teeth, the gear ratio is 80+32, or 2.5. The input
gear must complete 2.5 rotations for every single rotation
of the output gear. The example in Figure B 3.3.1c would
therefore have a gear ratio of 1:4 or 0.25, while Figure
3.3.1d represents a gear ratio of 4:1 or 4.
The mechanical advantage (MA) of a gear system is a
measure of how much the gear ratio multiplies the force
(torque) applied to the output gear. In a 4:1 ratio, the
torque is multiplied by four, although the speed is reduced
by a factor of four. The MA of this arrangement is therefore
4.
Remember, as with all machine systems, this relationship
remains true only for the theoretical condition of 100%
efficiency. In real-life systems, when inefficiencies such
as friction are applied, the mechanical advantage is
reduced.
Since the gear ratio is a relationship between the
number of teeth on the input and output gears, so is the
mechanical advantage. This leads to the terms often being
used interchangeably, with a higher gear ratio indicating a
greater mechanical advantage.
Example 1:
Calculate the mechanical advantage of a gear system in
which the drive wheel has 100 teeth and the driven wheel
has 20 teeth.

Mechanical Advantage: Pulley
The mechanical advantage (MA) of a pulley drive system,
whether simple or complex, is determined by counting the
number of ropes supporting the movable pulley(s) and/
or the load. Each rope supporting the load or a movable
pulley contributes to the mechanical advantage. You
can also calculate it by dividing the output force by the
input force, or by dividing the effort distance by the load
distance.
There are, therefore, three methods of determining the
mechanical advantage of a pulley system:
As outlined in section A 3.3.1 in a movable pulley system,
the mechanical advantage (MA) can be determined by
counting the number of ropes supporting the movable
pulley, such that :

Count of the number of ropes supporting the load
If a more precise method of determining MA is required,
or the counting of the number of ropes supporting the
movable pulley(s) is not practical (such as when compound
pulley systems are involved), two alternate methods are
available to determine the ideal mechanical advantage:

not occur and are therefore sometimes indicated as the
IMA as opposed to the real-world or actual mechanical
advantage (AMA).
Mechanical Advantage: Belt
While belt drives have a long history, especially in the
textile industry in applications such as the spinning wheel
illustrated in Figure B 3.3.1e, their widespread use became
a defining characteristic of the 18th and 19th-century Industrial Revolution and the introduction of steam
power.
Figure B 3.3.1e Spinning Wheel with the Drive Band or
Belt (B), transferring power from the Drive Wheel (A) to
the ‘Flyer’ assembly (C), where fibres are twisted into yarn
and wound onto a bobbin. The Drive wheel is turned
by a treadle connection (H) to a foot pedal (G) via a
crankshaft.
The Industrial Revolution saw a significant shift from
manual labour to machine-driven production, with
belt drives playing a crucial role in transmitting power
from steam engines to various machines via lineshafts,
especially in factories, as illustrated in Figure B 3.3.1f.
Before the introduction of electric motors, a lineshaft was
the method used to distribute power throughout a factory.
Using a common shaft with multiple pulley wheels along
its length, power could be distributed to individual
machines via belts. By adjusting the pulley sizes or by
using a system of shifting belts between fixed and loose
pulleys, power from the lineshaft could be used to control
individual machines. This allowed for greater efficiency
and control in manufacturing processes.
While the use of lineshafts is today rare, belt drives find
extensive use in conveyor systems in applications such
as bulk material transport, airport baggage returns,
travelators (moving walkways) and escalators. In addition,
the use of endless belt drives is found in numerous
everyday applications, such as:
« Automobiles
« Window blinds
« Vacuum cleaners
« Sewing machines
+ Washing machines
« Garage door openers
» Pumps and compressors
« 3D printer X, Y axes control
« Bandsaws, pedestal drills and lathes
A number of different belt shapes are available, including
Vee, Flat, Round and Toothed, with the shape used
determined by the type of drive (friction or positive
engagement), the amount of power to be transmitted, the
speed ratio, and the specific application requirements.
‘Fan belts’ on automobiles were originally employed
to transmit power from the crankshaft to a fan used to
cool the radiator on vehicles. Over time, the purpose of
this belt extended to powering secondary systems such
as those required for the water pump, air-conditioning
compressors, alternators, power steering and brakes, etc.
With this change the path taken developed from a simple
direct circuit to a ‘serpentine’ path to service the power
demands of these secondary systems, illustrated by Figure
B33.1g.
Figure B3.3.1g View of a positive engagement (toothed)
‘Serpentine’ automotive timing belt. [ISO 7967-3:2022
Reciprocating internal combustion engine]
Factors like the centre distance between pulleys, space
available and the working environment also play a
significant role in selecting the appropriate belt shape.
Belt drives are known for their cost-effectiveness, quiet
smooth operation, and ability to span larger distances
than gear drives, but can slip under heavy loads, and
have a lower power transmission capacity compared with
gear or chain drives, offering only moderate mechanical
advantage.
The method for the calculation of the mechanical
advantage (MA) of a belt drive in an ideal world in which
losses due to friction are absent is similar to that for pulleys
and is determined by the ratio of the diameters (or radii)
of the driven and driving pulleys or by the inverse of the
ratio of their rotational speeds.

Mechanical Advantage: Lever
As outlined in section A 3.3.1 the mechanical advantage
(MA) of a lever system indicates how much the machine
multiplies the input force (Effort).
Load

As seen previously, there are three classes of levers: first,
second and third, with the class determining the relative
positions of the effort, fulcrum and load as represented by
the anagram FLE.
E F L Ist Class
F L E 2nd Class
FEL 3rd Class

in Figure B 3.3.1h

Figure B3.3.1h Levers with forces normal to the lever

However, this is often not the case. Giovanni Batista
Benedetti (1500s) recognised that the forces on a lever
did not always act normal to the lever but could act at an
oblique angle, Under such conditions, the calculation of
equilibrium moments about the fulcrum could not use
the simple lever arm distance used when the force acted
normal to the lever. He found, however, that the forces
could be resolved graphically such that a new position
on the lever existed at which the force could be said to
be acting normal to the lever. The lever arm at which the
force was acting obliquely could then be rearranged to be
acting normal to a new position from the fulcrum, known
as the effective lever arm.
L E
Figure B3.3.1i Graphical determination of the effective
lever arm.
In the simple example shown in Figure B 3.3.1i-a, both the
effort and load are shown to be operating obliquely to the
lever. The effective lever arm is obtained by projecting the
lines of force for both the Effort and Load until a position
is reached where a line normal to those force lines can be
drawn that will intersect with the fulcrum point, as shown
in B3.3.1i-b. The length of this line is the length of the
effective lever arm. By taking this length as the radius of a
circle centred on the fulcrum, the original diagram can be
redrawn with the forces shown to be acting normal to the
lever, as indicated in Figure B3.3.1i-c.

ote: When calculating mechanical advantage with an

the force acting along the direction of movement is what
directly contributes to lifting the load

In many implements that utilise levers, the angle the load
and effort act relative to the fulcrum continuously changes
during operation, as is the case with the Cable Shovel
shown in Figure B 3.3.1s. With this change, the position
at which forces act relative to the lever can also vary. Two
simple examples of this are a pair of scissors (see Figure
B 3.3.1u), which uses two first class levers in opposition,
and Shears (see Figure B 3.3.1v), representing a second
class lever. As both of these tools close, the position of
cutting (Load) moves away from the fulcrum, increasing
the effort required to continue cutting.

B3.3.2
Velocity Ratio: Gears
The velocity ratio (VR) for a gear system is a measure of
the amount by which a gear train increases or decreases
the speed of the driven gear compared to the drive gear
and is related to the gear ratio, although it is conceptually
different.
284
While the gear ratio focuses on the tooth count relationship
within a single gear pair, the velocity ratio describes the
overall rotational speed relationship between the input
and output gears of a gear system, encompassing both
simple and complex gear trains. In essence, the velocity
ratio considers the cumulative effect of multiple gear pairs
in a system, whereas the gear ratio is specific to a single
meshing pair.
If, for example, the drive gear has 60 teeth and the driven
gear has only 15 teeth, the velocity ratio of gears can be
calculated by applying the same formula used for the gear
ratio, irrespective of how many intermediate gears there
may have been. In other words, this ratio reflects the
relative distances travelled by the input and output gears.
Number of teeth on driven (output) gear
Numberof teeth on drive (input) gearVR=
The velocity ratio can also be calculated based on the
ratio of speeds (also known as the speed ratio) between
the driven and drive gears, although in this case, the
numerator and denominator switch positions as shown
below. The key is to understand the context and whether
the velocity ratio is being calculated based on speeds or
distances moved by effort and load.
Speed ofdrive (input) gear
¥R= Speed of driven (output) gear
1 revolution
= Jrevolutions
=0.25
Note that when calculating the velocity ratio of a gear
system, the ratio of teeth is the inverse of the ratio of
speeds. Specifically, the velocity ratio (when based on
teeth) is calculated by dividing the number of teeth on the
driven gear (the output gear) by the number of teeth on
the drive gear (the input gear). However, the speed ratio
is the inverse of this; it's the speed of the drive (input)
gear divided by the speed of the driven (output) gear. One
is based on the number of gear teeth, while the other is
based on the ratio of gear wheel speeds as measured in
revolutions.
In simpler terms, the velocity ratio is about how fast the
gears are spinning relative to each other, and the speed
ratio is about how fast the input and output shafts are
turning, although they both arrive at the same resultant
value. While they describe different aspects, both
ultimately reflect how the gear system changes speed.
In the situation above, the gearing configuration is
designed to increase the speed of the driven (output) gear.
In this instance, the gearbox is known as an ‘overdrive’
or speed increaser. If the system were reconfigured with
the 15-tooth gear as the driver, the effect would be one of
reducing the output speed but providing a velocity ratio
of 4 (and a mechanical advantage up to 4, depending on
the efficiency of the system). This reconfigured system is
known as a ‘reduction’ gearbox for obvious reasons; while
output gear speed is reduced compared to the input gear,
torque is increased.
Velocity Ratio: Pulley and Belt Drive System
The velocity ratio calculation is the same for both pulleys
and belt drives. In essence, the velocity ratio is the ratio of
the speed of the driven component (N,) to the speed of the
driving component (N"). It indicates how many times the
driven pulley rotates for every one rotation of the driver
pulley
Rotational speed of drive pulley (N,)
R= Rotational speed of driven pulley (N.)
The velocity ratio can also be calculated by considering
the ratio of the diameter of the driven component (d,) to
the diameter of the driving component (d,).
Note that there is an inverse relationship of the ratio when
calculations are based on speed compared to diameter.
Diameter of driven pulley (d:)
= “Diameter of drive pulley (d\)
N, _ds
N =ds
This ratio indicates how much faster or slower the driven
pulley will rotate compared to the drive pulley. A velocity
ratio greater than 1 means the driven pulley rotates faster,
while a ratio less than 1 means it rotates slower.
Note that for an ideal machine in which no energy is
lost due to factors like friction, air resistance or other
inefficiencies, MA=VR, meaning that the machine’s output
force equals the input foree, resulting in 100% efficiency.
However, in a real-world scenario, where machines
experience efficiency losses, the mechanical advantage
(MA) is equal to the efficiency (n) multiplied by the velocity
ratio (VR). This can be expressed as the equation:
MA =nx VR
here efficiency (n) represents the ratio of output work to
input work, and in practical machines, it’s always less than
1 due to factors like friction.

Efficiency (n) of a gear system
As previously discussed in section A 3.3 the efficiency (n)
of a system is determined by dividing the output power by
the input power and multiplying by 100%. In gear systems
where Power is a function of torque and rotational speed
in RPM or radians per second, this percentage represents
the portion of input power that is effectively converted into
useful output power, with the remainder lost primarily
due to friction and churning.

Efficiency (n) of a belt drive system
‘While both gear and belt systems share the same basic
formula for efficiency (n) shown below, the specific
factors influencing power transmission differ significantly.
This leads to different sources of power loss and varying
characteristics in terms of efficiency, speed and other
factors.

mefi

100
In a belt drive system, power and speed are transferred
between pulleys through a belt that connects them. The
torque (or power) and speed of the driving pulley, along
with its diameter, influence the speed and torque delivered
to the driven pulley. In other words, the power transmitted
through the belt drive is proportional to the speed and
torque of the driving pulley.
Belt drive efficiency is impacted by the flexibility of the
belt and the reliance on friction for power transmission,
which introduces factors like slippage and material
properties that affect performance. These factors include
the materials of both the belt and pulley, the amount of
slip between them, the speed of the belt and belt tension,
which can vary from one side to the other.
The torque itself provided by the drive pulley is related to
the drive pulley radius (r) and the tangential force of the
belt, known as the Effective force (F;) or circumferential
force (F.).
Torque(t) = Effective driving force(F;)xPulley radius (r)
The effective driving force (F;), is the portion of the input
force that is used to overcome the resistance or load.
The input force is therefore equal to the Effective driving
force (F,) plus any losses, such as those associated with
friction.
Input Force = Effective Driving Force (F,) + Losses
The effective diving force itself is directly influenced by
the tension in the belt, which is typically different on the
two sides when it’s being used to transmit power. This is
because one side of the belt is being pulled onto the pulley,
creating greater tension, and is called the ‘tight side’ (T+).
The other side of the pulley is coming off the pulley and
consequently is under lower tension and is known as the
‘slack side’ (T-), as illustrated in Figure B 3.3.3a.
This difference is essential for power transmission, as the
tight side carries the majority of the driving force while
the slack side maintains tension to prevent slipping.
The effective force (F,) is the difference between the
tension on the tight side (T') and the slack side (T_) of
the belt:
Since F=T -T
Torque (T) = (T, -~ T ) xr
Since Power is the rate at which Work (in this case, Torque)
is applied over time, and Work is force times distance, in a
rotating system like a pulley, the equivalent of distance is
angular displacement, and the force is the torque.
Previously, concerning gears, we found that:
Power = Torque (t) x rotational displacement (w)
However, since the belt itself is moving linearly, not
rotationally, the relevant distance for calculating work is
the linear distance the belt travels.
Therefore, the power of a belt system is calculated as the
force applied by the belt (tangential force on the pulley)
multiplied by the belt’s linear velocity rather than the
angular velocity of the pulley. The power transmitted (P)
is calculated as:
P=F xv
=(T,-T)xv
Where v is the linear velocity of the belt, usually measured
in meters per second (m s
The design of the belt drive system, including pulley sizes,
belt tension, and the coefficient of friction between the
belt and pulleys, all affect the amount of torque and power
that can be transmitted.
If you have a belt drive with a certain speed and you
increase the tension in the belt (thereby increasing the
torque), you will increase the power being transmitted.
Conversely, if you increase the speed of the belt while
maintaining the same torque, you will also increase the
power transmitted.

=52 x 20 = 1040W

Output power (P) is the effective force (F,) multiplied
by the linear belt speed (v):

Efficiency (n) of a chain drive system
In most mechanical systems, chain drives offer a balance
of efficiency and flexibility, generally outperforming belt
drives but falling short of gear drives in terms of overall
efficiency. Chain drives achieve this by providing positive
engagement and reduced slippage compared to belts,
while also offering more flexibility than gear drives. Gear
drives, when well-designed and lubricated, can achieve
the highest levels of efficiency due to minimal slippage
and friction.

B3.3.4
Gear and belt-driven systems are used to modify rotary
motion, altering direction, speed and power transmission.
They achieve this by transferring rotational force and
motion between shafs.
Gear systems are characterised by their ability to change
the direction and speed of rotation, as well as the amount
of torque transmitted. By varying the size and number of
teeth on mesh_ing gears, engineers can create a gear ratio
that either increases speed while decreasing torque, or vice
versa. Gears are commonly used in applications requiring
high power transfer over short distances and where a non-
slip connection is crucial
Belt-and-pulley systems, also called belt drives, offer
flexibility in the arrangement of shafts and can be used to
change the direction of rotation by using a crossed belt.
They can also be used to adjust speed by utilising pulleys
of different diameters. Belt drives are often preferred
when the shafts are not in close proximity or when some
flexibility is needed in the system.
Gear Drive Systems
Calculation of the rotational speed of gears
As discussed in B 3.3.2, the velocity ratio is directly
determined by the number of teeth on the driving and
driven gears. This remains true for more complex drive
trains where there are multiple intermediate gears between
the drive and the final driven gear. The intermediate gears,
while affecting the direction of rotation and potentially
the overall size of the system, do not influence the ratio of
speeds between the input and output shafts.
Figure B 3.3.4a shows a gear box for a hand drill in which
the gear arrangement shown transfers motion from a
vertical plane to a horizontal plane; however, while the
style and number of intermediate gears will determine
operations in terms of direction and plane of movement,
they do not influence the gear ratio of the drill overall. The
velocity ratio for the drill is only determined by the ratio of
the number of teeth on the drive gear to the output gear.
‘The handle turns a helical drive wheel (the input gear) that
has 37 teeth. This gear meshes with another, smaller helical
gear of 16 teeth, which in turn turns a 34-tooth crown gear
on the same shaft. The crown gear is meshed with another,
smaller bevel (output) gear of 15 teeth.
With this information, we can calculate the velocity ratio
of the system overall as well as the velocity ratio of each
gear pair within the gearbox.
The calculation for the Velocity Ratio of this system (a:d)
would be as follows:
Number of teeth on driven (output) gear
Number of teeth on drive (input) gear

=04
This means that for every 15 turns of the handle, the drill
bit will turn 37 times. This gear arrangement is therefore a
multiplier of speed.
These formulae hold for each of the different types of gear
systems, except a worm drive gear, which is considered to
consist of only one tooth.
Having determined the velocity ratio of a system, we can
quickly determine the output speed of a gear train. Taking
the previous example, if it takes one (1) second to complete
a turn of the handle, which represents one revolution of
the drive (input) wheel, the drive wheel speed is one (1)
revolution per second or as more commonly expressed in
terms of revolutions per minute, 60 rpm.

Belt or Chain Drive Systems
Beltand chain drive systems, at their simplest, are modified
pulley systems consisting of two wheels connected by a
flexible continuous intermediary, as illustrated in Figure
B 3.3.4c. As one pulley rotates, it drives the belt, which
in turn drives the other pulley(s), the driven or follower
pulley.
This mechanism allows for the transfer of rotary motion
and torque from one shaft to another, often with a change
in speed or direction. This intermediary may be made
from any number of materials, such as leather, plastic,
rubber, steel, etc., depending on the capacity requirements
of the system.
A change in direction of the driven pulley is obtained by
using a crossed belt arrangement as illustrated in Figure
B 3.3.4d
Belt systems rely on maintaining sufficient frictional
contact between the wheel and the belt so that slippage
does not occur and rotational motion of one wheel is
transmitted with minimal loss to the other wheel. The
cross-sectional shape of the belt can also vary from flat,
square, round and vee shaped to match a complementary
shape in the wheel periphery in order to provide adequate
surface area for friction.
In contrast, chain drive systems use wheels with teeth
around the periphery known as sprocket wheels or
sprocket gears, which mesh with the spaces in a chain
in order to transport the chain around the sprockets.
Because friction is not required to maintain contact, chain
drives can generally accommodate greater loads; in fact,
lubrication is required to reduce energy loss and wear
through the frictional contact between the sprocket teeth
and chain.
In the open belt system the belt runs directly between two
pulleys on parallel shafts such that the driven pulley rotates
in the same direction as the driving pulley. Typically, the
two pulleys are of differing diameters allowing changes in
speed. The stepped cone pulley system (such as the one
shown in Figure B3.3.4¢) is often used to control spindle
speeds in a drill press and bandsaw speed.
Belt drives are able to transmit power over large distances
while absorbing a certain amount of shock and vibration
along with some degree of misalignment between the
drive and driven (follower) ends. For these reasons, they
find extensive use in bulk goods conveyor systems (Figure
B 3.3.4f).
The mechanical advantage (MA) of belt and chain
drives is determined by the ratio of the driven (follower)
component’s size (pulley diameter or sprocket teeth) to
the driving component’s size or the driven pulley diameter
divided by the driving pulley diameter.
For chain systems, ranging from a simple bicycle chain
to the system shown in Figure B 3.3.4g, the mechanical
advantage (MA) is similar to gearing and is the number
of teeth on the driven sprocket divided by the number of
teeth on the driving sprocket.
Calculation of the rotational speed of belt and chain
drives
The velocity ratio of a belt drive is the ratio of the driver
to the follower and essentially indicates how many times
the driven (follower) pulley rotates for each rotation of the
driver pulley.
As previously shown in section B 3.3.2 velocity ratio (VR)
is calculated as:
Diameter of driven pulley (d.)

represents the diameter of the wheel and N is its rotational

Again, there is an inverse relationship between the velocity ratio calculated using distance or speed, seen previously with gears. This is because the belt maintains a constant speed, and a larger pulley requires more distance to travel the same length of belt as a smaller pulley in the same amount of time.
The Velocity ratio of a belt drive based on drive pulley diameter (d,) and driven pulley diameter (d,) is: 
Therefore, by increasing the drive wheel diameter from
350mm to 400 mm, the speed of the driven pulley was
increased from 50 rpm to 62.5 rpm.
Unlike a gear train, where only the drive and final driven
gear determine the velocity ratio of the system, each pulley
in the belt drive system contributes te the overall velocity
ratio. The belt wraps around each pulley, transferring the
rotational motion. The ratio of the diameters of each pair
of pulleys (driver and driven) within the system affects the
speed change as the belt travels between them.

B3.3.5
he invention of the cam in the mechanical sense is
attributed to the ancient Chinese during the Han Dynasty
(206 BCE to 220 CE) for trip hammers, while the camshaft
itself was first described by the 12th-century Muslim
inventor and polymath Ismail al-Jazari living in what is
now Tiirkiye.
The camshaft was employed to automate various
mechanical devices, such as water clocks, water-raising
machines and automata. One such automaton, described
in The Book of Knowledge of Ingenious Mechanical Devices,
was the Mechanical Servant, shown in Figure B3.3.5a.
Housed in a cupboard, the automaton would offer a
glass of wine using cams located on a hidden camshaft
and driven by a water wheel and a reservoir system. The
movement of these cams, programmed by moving levers
and pegs on the camshaft, caused the figure to extend a jug
of wine into the room.
As outlined previously in section A 3.3.8, cams and
followers are elegant mechanical devices that include a
vast variety of Cam shapes, with designs ranging from
simple flat plates to complex grooves on cylindrical or
spherical surfaces to geometric shapes. These devices
allow the rotary motion of the cam to be transformed into
reciprocating motion, or a complex pattern of rises, falls
and dwells through interaction with a follower.
Two of the most iconic uses of cams and followers are in the
Sewing machine shown in Figure B 3.3.5b, and automotive
engines, specifically in the valve timing mechanism of the
Internal Combustion Engine shown in Figure B 3.3.5¢.
Before computerisation, cams were used in sewing
machines to allow the automatic creation of various
decorative and utility stitches, such as zigzag, blind
hem and other patterned designs. As the cam rotated, a
follower translated the rotary motion of the cam into a
specific reciprocating pattern for the needle bar to create
the desired stitch.
Figure B 3.3.5¢ shows a Camshaft shaft in which three
Cam groups (each comprising two cams of the pear type)
can be seen located along the shaft with the lobes of each
group angled differently on the shaft to create a specific
timing sequence for the valves
« The camshaft rotates, driven by the crankshaft.
« Each cam lobe on the shaft in turn pushes against a
follower (often a tappet or rocker arm), causing the
valve to open at precise intervals for fuel intake or
exhaust release as illustrated in Figure B 3.3.5d.
« Once the lobe rotates past, the valve closes — often
aided by a spring.
This precise timing is critical for engine performance, fuel
efficiency, and emissions control.
Figure B3.3.5d CAM shaft and valve assembly for an
internal Combustion Engine [Adapted from ISO 7967-
3:2022 Reciprocating internal combustion engine]
Other applications include:
« Textile machinery (controlling needle motion)
« Automated packaging systems (timed filling or
sealing)
« Mechanical toys (like a duck’s beak opening and
closing)
« Printing presses (controlling paper feed or ink
rollers)

B3.3.6
One of the most significant developments in the study
of levers was the analysis of their operation within the
human body by Italian scientist, Giovanni Alfonso Borelli
(1608- 1679), who is credited with the application of the
mechanics of the lever to the study of human movement.
Movement is derived by the action of muscles on the
bones of the skeleton, particularly the long bones, which
act as the levers, while the joints serve as the fulcrum.
Although this observation was made by both Aristotle and
later in the second century by the Roman physician Galen
of Pergamon (131-201AD), the true nature of the lever
systems within the human body were not appreciated.
Galen assumed that human movement was the result of
long lever arms allowing weak muscles to overcome large
resistive loads.
This assertion went largely unquestioned until Borelli’s
analysis, which showed that because the muscle
attachments, which convey the effort, are located close to
the joints, the levers of the human body were primarily
magnifiers of motion rather than force. This means that
a greater effort must be exerted than the resistive load
the lever is trying to overcome. The treatise detailing
his work was published in 1680 following his death,
and was appropriately titled De Motu Animalium, ‘On
the movement of animals, as had Aristotle’s study of the
mechanics of animal movement over 1900 years earlier.
Because of his work on human movement, Borrelli is
considered by many to be the father of biomechanics.
First order levers
A first order lever is the type most often thought of when
asked for an example of alever. There are several first class
levers in the human body, with the head being perhaps
the most obvious example, previously illustrated in Figure
A 339b.
Another example of a first class lever is the action of the
triceps on the bones of the forearm. By contracting, the
triceps act as extensors, allowing the forearm to straighten
relative to the upper arm bone, the humerus. As may be
seen in Figure B 3.3.6a the triceps are attached to the
ulna bone at a position close to the fulcrum represented
by the elbow joint. The effort arm is therefore very short
compared to the load arm, represented by the length of
much ofthe ulna and the bones of the wrist (carpals) and
hand (metacarpals).
In the example of a bell pull shown in Figure B3.3.6a,
through contraction the triceps must supply a
comparatively large effort to overcome a relatively small
resistive load due to the attachment position of the
muscle. The real benefit of this arrangement is not in
magnifying the force of the muscles but in magnifying the
speed and distance moved by the arms and legs. Because
of the difference in the lever arms, the muscles of the arms
and legs need to contract over a relatively short distance
to produce a large movement at the other end, as shown
in Figure B3.3.6b.
This is also the principle of first order levers such as the
catapult or trebuchet, shown in Figure B3.3.6c.
Second order levers
Similarly, the speed and distance produced in lifting the
heel of the foot off the ground, representing one of the few
second order lever actions in the human body, illustrated
in Figure B3.3.6d (and previously in Figure A3.3.9¢) is
obtained through a relatively large effort from the calf
muscle.
Third order levers
The most easily recognised third order lever in the human
body is probably the action of the biceps muscle on the
radius bone of the forearm. The biceps act as flexors,
allowing the forearm to bend relative to the upper arm
bone, the humerus. As may be seen in Figure B3.3.6e the
biceps are attached to the radius bone at a position close to
the fulcrum represented by the elbow joint.
The effort arm is therefore very short compared to the
load arm, represented by the length of the radius and the
bones of the wrist (carpals) and hand (metacarpals).
The maximum moment about the elbow created by a load
held in the hand is when the forearm is at 90" to the body
as illustrated in Figure B 3.3.6e. At this angle the entire
weight of the load is vertical, that is normal to the lever
arm. However during bicep curls this angle changes as
illustrated in Figure B 3.3.6f meaning that the percentage
of the load that is normal to the lever arm reduces, as
illustrated in Figure B 3.3.6g.

B3.4.1
Electronics are deeply integrated (embedded) into
everyday products across numerous categories, including
home appliances (refrigerators, microwaves, washing
machines, fire alarms), entertainment devices (TVs,
game consoles), communication tools (smartphones,
computers), automobiles (engine control units, EV
battery performance, GPS systems, autonomous control)
and even personal accessories like digital watches and
wearable fitness trackers.
Embedded systems are key components in modern
electronics powered by advancements in miniaturisation,
while connectivity has allowed the sharing of information,
driving innovation and expansion into consumer
electronics, industrial automation, cybersecurity and
healthcare.
Electronic systems are incorporated into product design
through several key steps:
+ defining requirements,
« selecting electronic components,
« designing the circuit and firmware,
« creating hardware and enclosure prototypes and
« performing rigorous testing and validation.
To create user-focused, functional and reliable products,
industrial designers use CAD and electronic design
automation (EDA) simulation software to integrate
mechanical and electronic components, balancing user
needs with technical feasibility. Since industrial designers
often lack deep technical knowledge of electronics,
they must collaborate with electronics experts, using
visualisations of user interactions to convey design goals.
This shared visual language ensures electronics experts
understand the user-centric objectives and can derive
appropriate technical criteria, fostering co-thinking
and a unified understanding for effective product
development.
The core challenge is balancing user needs with what is technically possible and economically viable.
Today, even simple products use a combination of electrical and electronic components and circuits that typically rely on a set of core elements working together to perform specific functions like amplification, switching, signal processing or power conversion. A breakdown of commonly encountered electrical and electronic components is presented in Figure B 3.4.1a.
he distinction between electrical and electronic componentry is important because it clarifies the different functions of the components. Electrical devices primarily use power to perform a function, such as transforming electrical energy into other forms of energy (like heat or light), whereas electronic devices manipulate the flow of electrons to process information and add meaning to signals. While both electrical and electronic devices deal with electricity, ‘electronic’ refers specifically to the use of components like transistors to control electron flow an process information, whereas ‘electrical’ is a broader term for anything related to electricity. Essentially, all electronic devices are electrical, but not all electrical devices are electronic.
The transition from non-electronic to electronic devices
in consumer products began with the invention of the electromagnetic relay independently in the USA by Joseph Henry and in Britain by Edward Davy in the 1830s. The relay, an electrically operated switch, enabled long-distance control of electrical devices, making technologies like the telegraph possible. The transition to electronic
devices was subsequently accelerated with the invention
of the solid state transistor in 1947 by Bell Labs scientists
John Bardeen, Walter Brattain and William Shockley.
An achievement that saw them win the 1956 Nobel prize
in Physics.
Early consumer electronics used vacuum tubes which
were large, fragile and created heat that needed cooling.
The transistor’s smaller size, lower cost and increased
reliability revolutionized electronics and subsequently led
to the invention of the Integrated Circuit (IC), which then
paved the way for more compact and advanced electronic
devices to enter the consumer market during the 1960s and
beyond. The independent development of the analogue
integrated circuit by Jack Kilby (Texas Instruments) and
Robert Noyce (Fairchild Semiconductor) in the late 1950s
and early 1960s further propelled electronics into the
wider consumer product market. Jack Kilby was awarded
the 2000 Nobel Prize in Physics for demonstrating the
first working IC in 1958 using germanium (see Figure
B 3.4.1b). Kilby used Germanium because, at the time,
germanium technology was more established and reliable,
despite silicon being available and offering advantages for
higher temperatures and better mass production. Kilby’s
germanium IC prototype demonstrated the concept of
integrating components onto a single chip although it
relied on gold wires for external connections, which
made large-scale production impractical. By contrast
Robert Noyce's silicon-based, monolithic integrated
circuit (meaning all of the components were on a single
piece of semiconductor material) was mass-producible
and is the foundation of modern microchips. Since Kilby
and Noyce developed the core concept and methods for
creating a ‘chip’ that could hold multiple components,
their inventions were the foundation for both types of
integrated circuits that exist today.
Analogue integrated circuits (ICs), or chips contain
fundamental components like resistors, capacitors,
diodes and transistors and began appearing in consumer
devices like TVs and refrigerators in the mid-1960s to
process continuous, nen-binary signals such as sound or
temperature.
Unlike digital ICs, which use logic gates to handle discrete
0s and 1s, analogue ICs are designed to manipulate
varying signals, performing functions like amplification
and signal processing. The transistor-based linear
(analogue) ICs were closely followed by digital logic ICs,
which began appearing in consumer products in the
1970s and 1980s, with their use expanding as production
became more widespread and costs decreased. From the
1980s onward the digitization of consumer products like
refrigerators and stereos became more common, largely
driven by rapid advancements in IC technology such as
Very-Large-Scale-Integration (VLSI). This technology
enabled unprecedented miniaturization and complexity,
integrating millions of transistors onto a single silicon
semiconductor microchip to create smaller, more
powerful, energy-efficient and feature-rich devices that
reduced both manufacturing costs and consumer prices.
This in turn led to advanced functions, such as precise
temperature control, energy management algorithms,
digital signal processing and the development of modern
smart appliances.
Today digital ICs and mixed-signal ICs (MSICs) have
largely displaced analog ICs in consumer appliances, as
digital and mixed-signal chips offer greater precision,
programmability and the ability to control multiple
functions, leading to more efficient, ‘smarter’ appliances.
Pure analog ICs are still used for specific functions
like signal amplification and conversion. Modern
appliances integrate digital processors and analogue-
to-digital converters (ADCs) to manage complex tasks
such as temperature regulation, energy usage and
communication.
The domestic refrigerator
The domestic refrigerator is a commonly encountered use
of electrical circuits and electronics in a modern consumer
product. Prior to the 1970s, domestic refrigerators relied
on electromechanical components to regulate temperature
and other functions, with sensors like bulb-thermostats
controlling the operation of components such as the
compressor's pump motor and solenoids. These systems
were based solely on electric circuits, using physical
switches and bimetallic elements to detect temperature
changes and activate or deactivate the cooling cycle, rather
than the integrated electronic boards and thermistor
sensors found in modern refrigerators.
Electronic printed circuit boards (PCBs) began
appearing in domestic appliances, including refrigerators,
in the mid-to-late 1970s, with their adoption accelerating
in the 1980s as they offered benefits in cost, size and
functionality reduction. However, it was not until the
mid-1980s and 1990s that PCBs became commonplace,
with many complex domestic appliances using integrated
circuits on PCBs by the 1990s.
Modern appliances use mixed-signal integrated circuits
(ICs) to bridge the gap between the analog physical
world and the digital control systems of the appliance.
Analog ICs process continuous, real-world signals from
sensors (like temperature or voltage), while digital ICs
handle these signals using binary code for precise logic
and control. Their combination within mixed-signal ICs
enables complex functions such as accurately converting
sensor data into digital information for the appliance’s
management system, leading to increased efficiency and
functionality.
Today many refrigerators entering the market contain a
control hub represented by an embedded PCB that receives
data from a variety of sensors (motor pump, temperature,
humidity, cameras, load cells and gas sensors to detect
spoilage etc), allowing advanced features like smart
home connectivity, energy efficiency management, food
inventory tracking with expiration alerts, personalised
meal planning and Integrated Displays acting as digital
communication hubs for calendars, notes and schedules
via touchscreen interfaces.
An example of modern refrigerators of this type
entering the market is the Samsung Bespoke Al range
(shown in Figure B 3.4.1c) which includes a large WiFi
connected touchscreen that turns the refrigerator into
a communication hub offering calendars and notice
board functionality, while internet connectivity provides
access to social media apps such as Spotify, You Tube and
Facebook, along with search engines and access to local
television channels. An internal camera with Al image
recognition capable of identifying over 30 types of fresh
food also allows image-to-recipe integration.
Key steps in incorporating electronic systems are:
« Early Integration: Start the design process with
a holistic view, integrating electronics into the
product’s core concept from the beginning, not as an
afterthought.
» Cross-Disciplinary Collaboration: Foster close
communication and teamwork between industrial
designers, who focus on aesthetics and user experience
and electrical engineers, who handle the technical
aspects of the electronics.
« System Architecture Definition: Define the
overall architecture, components, interfaces and
functionalities of the electronic system to ensure it
meets the product’s specific requirements.
+ Leverage Electronic Modules: Utilise electronic
design automation (EDA) tools and pre-built modules
for complex functions like Wi-Fi, Bluetooth or GPS
to reduce development costs, save time and simplify
certification.
« Printed Circuit Board (PCB) Design: Design the
custom circuits and embed them with modules onto
a PCB to form the electronic brain and secondary
functions of the product.
« Prototyping and Iteration: Create working prototypes
to test the integration of electronic and mechanical
components, allowing for early feedback and design
adjustments through an iterative process.
o Verification and Validation: Thoroughly test the
electronic system to ensure it functions as specified,
are reliable, safe for the end-user and compliant with
all necessary regulations.
« Finalization and Production: Prepare the design for
mass production, ensuring all elements are seamlessly
integrated and optimised for efficiency and cost.

B3.4.2
Specialised testing
An electrical circuit consists of a path around which
electrical current can travel. The efficiency with which
the current flows around the circuit is determined by a
number of factors related to the ease with which current
can flow through various components within the circuit.
Specialised instrumentation for the testing of electrical
circuits includes:
+ Voltmeter: Measures the electrical potential difference
(voltage) between two points in a circuit and is
connected in parallel with the component being
tested.
« Ammeter: Measures the current flowing through
a circuit. It is connected in series with the circuit to
intercept the current.
+ Ohmmeter: Measures the electrical resistance of a
component or circuit,
+ Megometer: Measures insulation resistance, often
called continuity testing.
« ‘Watt Meter: Measures electrical power.
« Oscilloscope: Visualises electrical signals over time,
displaying voltage changes on a screen. This allows for
the analysis of waveform characteristics.
« Clamp Meter: A convenient tool that measures current
by clamping around a wire without needing to cut it or
open the circuit.
« Energy Meter: Measures the total consumption of
electrical energy, often used for billing purposes.
« Circuit Breaker Identifier: A specialised tool that
helps identify which circuit breaker controls a specific
outlet or light fixture by sending and receiving signals
through the wiring.
Multimeters (see Figure B 3.4.2a) either Digital or
Analogue can replace traditional single-function meters,
combining the capabilities of a voltmeter, ammeter and
ohmmeter into a single, versatile device. Some more
advanced digital multimeters can also measure continuity
and capacitance
Circuit testers such as multimeters can measure various
electric values such as the measurement of;
« Resistance,
« Voltage,
» Current
« LED polarity,
» Insulation continuity.
Multi-meters either Digital or Analogue are fundamental
tools for measuring basic electric circuit parameters such
as voltage, current and resistance and converting the input
signal into a readable format.
The term multimeter arises from the combination
of the functions of a number of (multiple) meters in
one instrument, such as a voltmeter, ammeter and an
ohmmeter,
Measurement results on an analogue instrument are
confirmed by the deflection of the needle relative to
one of several scales. The more modern digital meters
display a value on a LCD screen. The digital instruments
generally provide a more accurate response and are less
susceptible to interpretation errors. Some circuit testers
can also measure the value of a capacitor, a coil, or the
characteristics of a transistor etc. Selection of the test type
is made by adjusting a knob on the front of the instrument
and positioning probes into the relevant sockets. They are
particularly useful when diagnosing faults. A multimeter
uses a range selector and rotary dial to measure voltage,
current, or resistance.
Voltage:
The testing of voltage is possibly the easiest test performed.
The voltmeter is placed in parallel across the position to be
checked and the value read, No disruption of the circuit is
involved, see Figure B 3.4.2b.
Before measurement begins an appropriate range needs
to be selected. If the multimeter shows a ‘1, the voltage
is outside the selected range and needs to be adjusted.
The black probe always goes into the COM (commeon)
terminal while the red probe goes into the V terminal for
voltage.
Current:
Testing of current involves turning the power to the
circuit off in order to create a break in the circuit to
position the multimeter in series, making it part of the
circuit, see Figure B 3.4.2b. The multimeter dial is set
to the appropriate Amperes (A) or Milliamperes (mA)
setting. The black probe is plugged into the ‘COM’ port
and the red probe into the designated ‘A’ or ‘mA’ port. The
multimeter’s probes are then connected in series with the
circuit such that when power is restored the current flows
through the meter to give a reading.
Resistance:
To measure resistance with a multimeter the dial is turned
to the Q (ohms) setting and the black probe positioned in
the COM port and the red probe in the Q) port. The probes
are then placed in parallel with the resistor you want to
test, following removal of the resistor from the circuit, see
Figure B 3.4.2b. For accurate readings, the probes should
be placed on the opposite ends of the single resistor being
tested. During the test the multimeter sends its own
current through the component using an internal battery
and measuring the voltage drop and displays the resulting
resistance value.
Insulation testing
Some of the more common causes of insulation breakdown
or failure include; excessive heat or cold, moisture, dirt,
corrosive environments, vibration, UV light deterioration,
aging and physical damage e.g. nicks or cuts.
One method of insulation testing involves the use of a
megohmmeter or megger (shown in Figure B 3.4.2¢).
Connected across the insulation material to be tested the
megohmmeter applies a high voltage DC current for a
period of time (usually 60 seconds). Insulation resistance
is measured in this way and is often called continuity
testing. Over time, a chart can be produced to show
the part’s history of insulation resistance. A downward
curving trend would indicate deterioration of the
insulative properties of the material over time and point
to its renewal or replacement.
Insulation testing is required to:
« Prevent electrical shocks,
« Assure safety of personnel,
« Reduce downtime of equipment,
« Evaluate quality of repair work.
Such testing is used on the insulation of various electrical
components, including wires, cables, motors, generators,
transformers and appliances, to test its ability to withstand
electrical current. It's particularly useful for detecting
degradation in thin, easily damaged insulation, such as that
found in motor windings and for performing preventive
maintenance on long cable runs and in electrical panels.
Power measurement
Watt Meter
A watt meter as shown in Figure B 3.4.2d is designed to
measure power directly in Watts and is used to measure
real-time electrical power being consumed or produced
by a circuit. Such measurements help to monitor appliance
power usage, check if a device is getting sufficient power,
compare the energy efficiency of different devices or
diagnose issues with an electrical circuit’s health and
power balance, Watt meters can be used for both everyday
household energy management and for more complex
analyses in electrical engineering.
The Watt meter combines the functions of an ammeter
and voltmeter within a single device allowing it to sample
the voltage across and the current through a load. The
meter then multiplies these values at a series of specific
instants and averages the results over a cycle to calculate
the real power in Watts.
P=V]
The current coil connects in series with the load, while the
voltage coil connects in parallel across the load, allowing
the meter to accurately determine power consumption.
For more complex situations, especially in AC circuits, a
power analyser or Digital Storage Oscilloscope (DSO)
can be used. These instruments use voltage probes to
measure voltage at various points and a current probe to
measure current.
Power Analyser
A power analyser measures voltage and current over time
in AC and DC circuits to calculate electrical parameters
like real and reactive power, power factor and harmonics.
This is unlike an oscilloscope, which only displays the
voltage-time graph. Power analysers add specialised
functions for calculating power consumption, switching
losses and signal distortion, providing comprehensive
data for energy efficiency, compliance testing and system
analysis.
While a watt meter provides a basic power reading, a
power analyser offers detailed data for in-depth analysis
of a system’s performance and is preferred for measuring
power consumption in household appliances and other
applications requiring high accuracy, steady-state
measurements and compliance with power standards.
Digital Storage Oscilloscope (DSO)
Oscilloscopes are used to visualise and analyse signal
waveforms over time. A digital storage oscilloscope
(DSO) (shown in Figure B 3.4.2¢) converts an analog
electronic signal into digital data, stores it in memory and
then displays it on a screen, enabling analysis, processing
and archiving of the waveform for later reference. It
differs from an analog oscilloscope (ASO) by using
digital memory instead of a Cathode Ray Tube (CRT) for
waveform display and storage, allowing DSOs to show
single-shot events, provide advanced processing, store
waveforms indefinitely and transfer data to computers.
hey are essential for visualising how electrical signals
change over time, showing waveforms and helping to
analyse signal integrity, frequency and timing issues.
Visually displaying voltage, amplitude, time/frequency
and signal shape helps diagnose faults like noise, signal
integrity issues, or component failures, by revealing
abnormal patterns, voltage levels or timing distortions that
a multimeter can miss. For example, abnormal ringing
indicates impedance problems, distorted rising edges
suggest a slow component and an incorrect amplitude
may signal a faulty power supply.
Benefits of the DSO include:
« Allows easy transfer of data to a computer,
« Easily produces a hard copy of the display,
« Allows long-term display of a transient quantity,
+ Allows computation and signal processing within the
DSO,
+ Allows visualisation and analysis of signal waveforms
over time, essential for understanding how electrical
signals change over time and helping to analyse signal
integrity, frequency and timing issues.
Function/Signal Generator
A function/signal generator is a versatile electronic device
that creates and outputs controlled electrical test signals,
such as sine, square and triangle waves, with adjustable
frequency and amplitude. It serves as a critical tool in
electronics, allowing engineers and technicians to test,
evaluate and troubleshoot the performance and behaviour
of electronic circuits and devices by providing known,
repeatable inputs.
A function/signal generator and a digital storage
oscilloscope (DSO) are frequently used together in
electronics to test and analyse circuits by providing a
known input signal and displaying the circuit’s output.
The function generator creates waveforms like sine or
square waves, while the DSO captures and visualises how
the circuit responds to these signals over time, allowing
for measurements of frequency, voltage and other
characteristics.
Clamp Meter
A clamp meter is a handheld electrical tester with hinged
iron jaws that can clamp around a conductor to measure
current without breaking the circuit, making it safer
for live circuit testing, see Figure B 3.4.2f. It is used by
electricians and technicians to measure high currents,
troubleshoot electrical problems and monitor industrial
and residential systems, as well as automotive and HVAC
applications and to check the power supply for welding
cables.
The clamp meter detects the magnetic field created in a
conductor when current flows. By selecting either AC
or DC functions, the meter’s internal sensor (Hall Effect
sensor for DC or a current transformer for AC) converts
this field into a current reading displayed on the screen.
LCR Meters
LCR meters (see Figure B 3.4.2g) measure the inductance
(L), capacitance (C) and resistance (R) of electronic
components by applying an AC signal and measuring
the resulting voltage and current. They are used for
characterising passive components like resistors, capacitors
and inductors in various applications, including circuit
design, quality control and troubleshooting. Beyond basic
L, C and R, advanced meters can also measure complex
parameters like dissipation factor and Equivalent Series
Resistance (ESR) at different operating frequencies.
While some advanced digital multimeters can measure
capacitance and resistance, their capabilities are typically
limited compared to a dedicated LCR meter, which uses
AC signals and advanced techniques to characterise
components under real-world conditions.
Logic Analysers (see Figure B3.4.2h) are electronic
test instruments that capture and display multiple
digital signals from a system to help debug and analyse
its performance, focusing on the timing relationships
between signals and decoding complex communication
protocols. Unlike an oscilloscope that shows analog
waveforms, a logic analyser interprets high and low voltage
states (1s and 0s) to present data in timing diagrams, state
traces or protocol-decoded formats, making it essential
for embedded systems development and digital circuit
debugging.
Spectrum analyser
Spectrum analysers are electronic instruments that
visualise the spectral content of signals by displaying
their power or magnitude against frequency (see Figure
B 3.4.2i) . They allow engineers to see how a signal’s
energy is distributed across different frequencies,
which is crucial for tasks like analysing radio waves,
troubleshooting electrical and audio issues and
optimising system performance by identifying unwanted
signals or frequency components in various applications
like telecommunications, broadcasting, aerospace and
research. The process involves sweeping a frequency range
to reveal signal strength at each frequency.
A spectrum analyser displays a signal’s frequency-domain
characteristics (amplitude vs. frequency), while a Digital
Storage Oscilloscope (DSO) shows the signal’s time-
domain waveform (amplitude vs. time). DSOs are ideal
for analysing signal timing, transitions and transient
events, whereas spectrum analysers excel at identifying
interference, noise and harmonic content by focusing on
the signal’s spectral components.
Circuit breaker identifier
A circuit breaker identifier, or breaker finder, is 2 tool
that uses a two-part system to locate the specific circuit
breaker controlling an outlet or light fixture. It works by a
transmitter, plugged into the outlet, sending an electronic
signal through the circuit. A handheld receiver then scans
the electrical panel, detecting the signal and alerting the
user, often with a beep or light, when it’s at the correct
breaker, see Figure B 3.4.1j.

B3.4.3
As shown previously in Section A 3.4.4, electrical current
is a measure of the flow of electrons past a point in a unit of
time and can be represented by the relationship below:

Conductors and resistors
The above terms are descriptive or qualitative designations
in that they describe or give an indication of the quality
of the relative ability of something or someone to allow
transfer or passage. In the usual context of engineering,
this is the passage of electricity and/or heat.
An electrical conductor, for example, readily allows the
passage of an electric current, while an electrical resistor
restricts or impedes the passage of current, usually
generating heat in the process. They are, in essence, mirror
images of each other, in that a good conductor could also
be described as a poor resistor and vice versa,
Conductance and Resistance
As with the previous discussion of conductors and
resistors, conductance and resistance relate to the ease
with which an electric current (I) moves through a
material. Conductance (G) and resistance (R), however,
are quantitative terms, since a number or quantity can be
assigned to them. As might be expected, conductance is
inversely related to resistance as indicated below;
G=%
Ohm'’s equation (Law), V = IR, could therefore be rewritten
in the form
V=i
The standard SI system unit of conductance is the siemens,
symbolised by the capital letter S. The previous unit of
conductance was the mho. Note that the mho is a reverse
spelling of ochm and was meant to emphasise the inverse
relationship with resistance, which uses the ohm (€2).
Therefore: G = -;lz— = Efi =ohm'=mho=§
As discussed in Section A 3.4.4, resistance is the opposition
to current flow within a specific object, such as a wire or
resistor. It is an extrinsic property that depends on the
material’s resistivity and its geometry (ie. length and
cross-sectional area).
ote the difference in terminology: resistance (£2) units
the ohm and resistivity (p) units the ohm™.
While resistance is affected by a material’s resistivity and
its geometry, resistivity is an ‘intrinsic’ property, meaning
it is a fundamental quality of the material dependent on
the material itself, its temperature and potentially other
factors, but not on its dimensions. The resistance measured
through a thick copper wire will therefore be different to
that measured on a thin copper wire, while the resistivity
of both wires will be the same.
If we look at resistance and its relationship with resistivity,
we find:

I m 1 mho S= RA = ohmm® =~ ohmm = m m
If an object’s dimensions are known, by measuring its
resistance, its resistivity and hence its conductivity can
also be determined.
Power is related to resistivity and conductance through
Ohm’s Law and the definition of power dissipation in a
circuit, making power proportional to resistivity for a
given conductor,
P:J-’xR:I-‘x%—lL
Similarly, conductance (G) is the reciprocal of resistance
(G = 1/R), so power can also be expressed as:
pP= < or P=VG
Conductance (G) is used in power calculations for specific
electrical components or circuits when emphasising the
ease of current flow, while conductivity (o) is used for the
material’s inherent ability to conduct electricity.
Similarly, resistivity (p) could be used to calculate power
when you don’t know the component’s resistance (R)
but have its material, length (L) and cross-sectional area
(A), as you must first find the resistance using R = pLA,
then use the power equation P = I°R or P = V¥R. Figure
B 3.4.3a shows the values of resistivity and conductivity
for a variety of common materials.
‘The different values for the resistance (R) of resistors
used in the construction of a circuit are obtained by
using wires of different resistivity (p) and dimensions
(length and cross-sectional area). Therefore, to achieve
different resistor values, manufacturers can select different
materials (changing resistivity) or change the length and
thickness (dimensions) of the conductor.
