Commonly available resistors use materials like carbon
film, metal film (nichrome, tantalum nitride), metal-
oxide film, carbon composition (a mixture of carbon
and ceramic) and specialised alloy wires (Nichrome for
wirewound resistors) to achieve their specific resistive
properties. These materials are chosen for their cost-
effectiveness, precision, stability and ability to handle
specific power levels, influencing the resistor’s overall
performance. A selection of commonly available resistors
is shown in Figure B3.4.3b, illustrating the changing size
and shape.
Note that resistors have a Watt rating to specify the
maximum amount of power they can safely dissipate as
heat without overheating, changing resistance or burning
out. This wattage rating is crucial for circuit design to
prevent damage to the resistor and ensure the reliability of
the entire circuit, as exceeding the power rating can lead
to component failure and safety hazards.
Most commercial resistors are not internally uniform but
rather have a more complex construction, designed to
have a specific resistance rather than an intrinsic material
property. The resistance of the smaller cylindrically shaped
resistors is indicated by colour bands around their bodies,
where the first two or three bands are significant digits,
the next is a multiplier and the last indicates tolerance
(accuracy) as indicated in Figure B 3.4.3c. In a resistor’s
colour band system, a greater gap, often a space or a
wider spacing, is intentionally left before the final colour
band to indicate the tolerance value, making it easier to
distinguish the last band and improving clarity for reading
the resistor’s value.
While national colour standards existed, they were largely
superseded by the international IEC standard, making the
colour code a universally accepted system for identifying
resistors.

B3.4.4
For the calculation of resistance and capacitance in series
and parallel in a circuit, reference is made to section
A 3.4.4. Combining resistors and capacitors creates what
are known as RC circuits, which are fundamental to
controlling timing, filtering signals and improving power
management by stabilising voltage and smoothing out
noise. For example, these circuits are used to create timed
delays, filter out unwanted frequencies in audio or radio
signals and stabilise power supplies in electronic devices.
An RC circuit, therefore, acts as a fundamental electronic
component with special properties, serving as a basic
building block for more complex circuits rather than being
just a simple combination of a resistor and a capacitor. It
exhibits time-dependent behaviour, due to the capacitor’s
ability to store and discharge energy at a predictable
rate, controlled by the RC time constant (t = RC) which
dictates the speed of charging and discharging in an RC
circuit. This unique characteristic allows RC circuits to perform functions such as filtering, signal delay, timing
and waveform shaping, making them essential in various
electronic applications.
RC circuit in series
A series RC circuit has the resistor and capacitor connected
in a single current path and acts to alter the impedance of
an AC circuit, where impedance (Z) is the total opposition
to current flow in an alternating current (AC) circuit
due to capacitors, inductors and resistors. Such a circuit
can be used to block certain frequencies or slow down
rapid changes in voltage, acting as a timer or a damping
mechanism in electronic applications. Series RC circuits
find application in signal processing, performing functions
such as signal filtering, signal integration and time-delay
generation by charging the capacitor sequentially, creating
time-dependent responses.
In an RC low-pass filter (shown in Figure B 3.4.4a), the
resistor is in series with the input, while the capacitor
is in parallel with the output. In this arrangement, high
frequencies are blocked from reaching the output load.
At low frequencies, the capacitor’s impedance is high,
allowing most of the voltage to reach the load, while at
high frequencies, the capacitor’s impedance is low, short-
circuiting the signal to ground and diverting it away from
the load.
In an RC high-pass filter (shown in Figure B 3.4.4b),
this order is reversed relative to that of the low-pass
circuit, with the capacitor in series with the input and the
resistor in parallel with the output. In this arrangement,
low frequencies are blocked from reaching the output
load. Because the capacitor’s impedance is high at low
frequencies, it acts like an open circuit, while higher
frequencies encounter a lower capacitive impedance,
allowing them to pass through to the output across the
resistor.
RC circuit in parallel
By comparison, a parallel RC circuit has the resistor and
capacitor connected side-by-side across the same two
connection points. In parallel RC circuits, the components
provide multiple paths for current, with the capacitor
bypassing high-frequency signals and acting as a filter or
decoupling compenent to smooth voltage fluctuations.
Each switching component within a circuit has inherent
in its operation an associated delay arising from the time
taken to establish terminal contact firmly. If different
paths are taken through a circuit, the time taken for the
inputs to arrive at a logic gate can differ. This difference
or delay in arrival times can cause fluctuations or spikes
in the output signal from the logic gate, particularly if the
input switching intervals are short. That is, if the inputs
are arriving as rapidly changing pulses of 1s and 0s. Such
a situation is known as ‘contact bounce’ or ‘chatter’. As
a consequence of contact bounce, the leading edge of the
signal can exhibit fluctuations or spikes before a clean
signal is established.
Due to the contact bounce creating rapidly changing
inputs and the different propagation delays of the paths,
the inputs to the logic gate do not arrive simultaneously.
This can cause the logic gate’s output to briefly flicker to an
incorrect state before settling to its correct stable value.
In order to reduce the effects of contact bounce and the
signal delay associated with it, an RC circuit is often used in
order to adjust the timing of the inputs so that they arrive
at the same time at the logic gate. This circuit consists
of a resistor in series with the voltage and a capacitor in
parallel. The half-life charge decay associated with an RC
circuit can be used to compensate for such circuit delaysby
utilising it to filter the leading edge signal. As the capacitor
charges, the output voltage increases steadily until the
switching voltage is reached and discharged, providing a
cleaner signal.
The circuit shown in Figure B 3.4.4c represents a circuit in
which one of two inputs is delayed in arriving at an AND
logic gate due to contact bounce.
By the inclusion of a parallel RC circuit and using the
half-life charge decay associated with an RC circuit
(representing the time it takes for the capacitor’s charge
to decay to half its initial value), delays in the arrival of
signals due to contact bounce can be compensated for
by filtering out spurious input signals. In this way, signal
inputs arrive simultaneously at the logic gate, producing
a cleaner signal, as indicated in Figure B 3.4.4d. This
effectively removes rapid fluctuations and ensures that
slower, more meaningful signals arrive at a logic gate
simultaneously, resulting in a cleaner output.
RC series circuits are often used when a predictable
charge/discharge rate is needed, such as in timing circuits
or filters, while parallel circuits are used when multiple
components need to operate from the same voltage source
or when redundancy is important.

B3.4.5
Flow Diagrams
‘These are known by a variety of names, including, but
not limited to: flow diagrams, process charts, process
models, process flow diagrams or workflow diagrams;
in every case, they provide a graphical representation of
a process or algorithm. Actions, sequencing and links
are easily visible. Each step is marked by a standardised
symbol representing an action or process. The four most
commonly used symbols are graphics representing the
terminator, input/output, process and decision. Symbols
are linked by arrows showing the direction or flow of the
chart. Figure B 3.4.5a shows a simple process constructed
as a flowchart. Notice the use of the four principal symbol
types.
No
Output
o)
Figure B3.4.5a Simple flow chart
Flowcharts assist in communication by arranging tasks
into logical sequences and actions. This allows the viewer
to see a concise set of operations and the interrelationships
between the component stages.
Block diagrams
A block diagram (also known as a functional diagram)
can be considered a type of high-level flowchart, but it’s
important to distinguish its primary purposes: while
flowcharts emphasise the sequential steps and decision-
making process of a workflow or algorithm, block
diagrams focus on the components and relationships
within a system. A block diagram shows the main parts
of a system and how they interconnect to achieve a
desired output, while a flowchart illustrates the order of
operations.
Block diagrams provide a visual representation of circuit
connections and major functional operations, avoiding
details of the circuit arrangement in favour of a clear
representation of how the components are related. They
provide a high-level overview of the entire circuit allowing
variations in circuit designs to be more quickly assessed.
These diagrams are used for system-level development,
showing major components and their interconnection
without detailing individual circuits,
ey features of block diagrams are:
« Provides a broad overview of the system design and
inter-relationships.
« Focuses on function with lines connecting each block,
indicating data flow.
+ Used in the initial stages of circuit design, acting as a
starting point for detailed design.
« Are solution-neutral descriptions of ‘what’ the system
does rather than ‘how’ it is physically implemented.
A functional block diagram for a simple electrical circuit
with two independent current paths, one of which has
three incandescent lamps in parallel and the other two
additional lamps in series, is presented in Figure B 3.4.5¢
nother functional block diagram is presented in Figure
B 3.4.5d, this time for a Direct Online (DOL) 3 Phase
starter, typically used to connect 3 Phase induction motors
directly to the power supply, for applications such as
water pumps, fans, conveyors, compressors and industrial
machines.

B3.4.6
Circuit diagrams
Circuit and system diagrams are flow charts that show
the logical connections of circuit components and their
functions using symbols in an organised, abstract way,
using standard line types and symbols to represent wires
and components. They prioritise clarity of circuit design
over physical layout and are best for understanding
how a circuit works. While real-world circuit diagrams
rely on colour-coding for safety and identification,
textbooks often omit colours in simplified diagrams to
focus on the fundamental principles of operation, as the
colours themselves don't affect the theoretical concept
being illustrated. Textbooks prioritise clarity of the
electrical schematic over the physical details, using line
drawings without colour to represent components and
connections.
Similarly, logic diagrams are a specific type of circuit
flowchart used in digital electronics to map the operation
of logic gates, which are the building blocks of complex
digital functions.
Figure B 3.4.6a provides the circuit diagram for the simple
block diagram shown in Figure B 3.4.5¢ using either an
AC or DC source. The diagram also includes a possible
substitution of the series of incandescent lamps in an AC-
powered circuit with LEDs. Incandescent lamps are high-
voltage AC devices that operate by passing current through
a high-resistance filament, heating it to the point where
it produces light. When replacing incandescent lamps
with LEDs, you are replacing a simple device with a more
complex, low-voltage DC device. This change necessitates
the addition of an LED driver (transformer) to provide the
necessary power conversion and regulation.
igure B 3.4.6b provides the circuit diagram for the simple
block diagram shown in Figure B3.4.5d. In addition, a
possible substitution of the series path with LEDs in an
AC-powered circuit is indicated. The US electrical system
uses different phase wire colour codes depending on the
voltage: black, red and blue are common for 120/208V
3-phase circuits, while brown, orange and yellow are used
for 277/480V 3-phase systems. White is for the neutral
in 120/208V circuits and grey is the neutral in 277/480V
circuits, with green serving as the ground in all cases.
Wiring Diagrams
‘Wiring diagrams, also known as connection diagrams,
illustrate the physical reality of the circuit, showing the
actual physical location of components and the routes
and colours of wires as they are installed in equipment. 
Colour coding in a wiring diagram is used to indicate the function of each wire, such as active, neutral and earth (or ground), to avoid incorrect connections and maintain safety. DC circuits typically use Red for positive and Black for negative.
The wiring diagram for the DC circuit based on the circuit diagram shown in FigureB 3.4.6a is shown belowin Figure B 3.4.6¢. Note that the incandescent lamps in series would need to be of a lower voltage to maintain brightness. This illustrates the situation that when resistors are present in series within a circuit, there is a voltage drop associated with each resistor such that the total voltage drop can be divided among the resistors in the circuit in proportion to the value of each resistor. The larger the resistor, the larger the voltage drop.
he wiring diagram for the single-phase AC-powered
circuit shown in Figure B 3.4.6a is presented in Figure
B3.4.6d.
If circuit diagrams are best for understanding how a
circuit works, wiring diagrams provide a visual map of an electrical system that shows the physical layout and precise connections between wires, cables and components like light fixtures, switches and outlets, rather than just the functional relationships. They are used for installing, repairing and troubleshooting electrical systems in homes, vehicles and complex machinery by
providing a physical blueprint for wiring. This allows for efficient work, prevents safety hazards and ensures that installations comply with regulations.
Wiring standards differ significantly by region, mainly due to the historical development of electrical systems with the primary differences being voltage, frequency and plug/socket type.
A wire colour regime is also used in wiring diagrams to
identify the function of each wire, such as power, neutral,
or ground. This prevents miswiring, reduces confusion
during installation and repairs and helps prevent electrical
shocks or equipment damage. Adhering to these colour codes ensures compliance with safety standards and
simplifies the work of electricians. Historical differences
have, however, also resulted in differences in the colours
assigned to each function between countries, as indicated
in Figure B3.4.6e.
Wiring colour codes can vary significantly from country
to country. Figure B3.4.6e shows the most recent colour
codes for three-phase AC wiring in a number of countries.
These codes are regularly reviewed by each country and
have, in many cases, changed appreciably over the years,
meaning that wire colour codes in older buildings can be
significantly different to those currently specified.
Electrical socket design also varies, as anyone who has
travelled internationally will attest. There are about 15
different types in use (see Figure B 3.4.6f), including
types such as A, B, C and G, commonly found in regions
like North America, Europe and the UK, respectively.
This international variation necessitates travel adapters
to ensure that electrical plugs from one country can be
safely used in the sockets of another, but it is crucial to
also verify the voltage and frequency compatibility of your
devices with the local power supply.
Australia, Britain and China use the 230-240V range and
50 Hz frequency, but utilise distinct plug types (Type I for
Australia, Type G for Britain and Type A/I for China).
Europe is a mixed region with some countries using 230-
240V and 50Hz with various plug types (like the CEE 7
standard, or Type F), though some countries outside the
continental network use Type G or other types. The USA
generally uses 110-120V and 60 Hz, with Type A and B
plugs.
As can be seen in Figure B 3.4.6g, the number of pins
present varies from two to three. Two-prong (ungrounded)
plugs do not include an earth pin, having only an active
and a neutral pin and assume use is with devices with low
shock risk, such as plastic-encased devices that are double-insulated or otherwise deemed to have a low risk of shock
and therefore do not require an earth connection.
The three-pin sockets include an earth to provide a safe
path for electricity to flow to the ground in case of a fault,
preventing electric shock from appliances with metal
casings, which is crucial for user safety.
Most homes and small businesses use what is called
single-phase power for standard appliances and lower
power needs, while three-phase power is typically used
for industrial, commercial and high-demand settings like
factories and data centres to run large machinery, motors
and heavy loads.
The main difference between three-phase and single-phase
power is the number of live wires employed, A single-phase
supply uses one alternating current (AC) wave active wire
to deliver power, while a three-phase supply uses three
separate, staggered AC wave active wires, as illustrated in
Figure B 3.4.6h. Both single-phase and three-phase power
circuits use a neutral wire to complete the circuit, but
the three-phase system’s three staggered waveforms offer
greater stability, constant power flow and higher capacity,
while the earth wire provides safety by providing a path
for fault currents to flow.
Figure B 3.4.6h Normalised waveforms of the
instantaneous voltages in a three-phase system. The
graph maps voltages over time for one whole cycle of the
system. Time starts from the left and increases towards
the right side. Each of the three phases starts and ends the
cycle in the same value, as each cycle is (ideally) identical.
[User:J Messerly modification of original SVG by User:
SiriusA, Public domain, via Wikimedia Commons]
The plugs used with single-phase and three-phase power
are not the same, as three-phase power requires a more
complex plug and socket to accommodate the extra live
wires. Three-phase plugs are therefore distinct from
single-phase plugs because of the need to include two
extra active connections. They are also typically larger and
are rated for higher amperages compared with standard
single-phase plugs.
Three-phase power plugs almost always include a ground
(earth) pin for safety, but they may or may not have a
neutral pin depending on the equipment’s requirements.
For example, equipment with a balanced three-phase
load, like many motors, uses only the three active phases
and the earth pin. However, if the equipment requires a
lower voltage for controls or to handle unbalanced loads, a
neutral pin is included, resulting in a five-pin plug.
IEC 60309 specifies the design, construction and
performance of plugs, sockets and couplers for industrial
and other high-power applications. It ensures the
safety and compatibility of these connectors, which are
identified by colour coding and a unique ‘clock position’
keying system to prevent accidental insertion into
incorrect sockets. The standard defines various electrical
characteristics like voltage and current ratings, as well
as mechanical properties such as water and dust ingress
protection (IP ratings) and durability. Yellow indicates
a 100-130V range, Blue signifies a 200-250V range and
Red denotes a 380-480V range. While the Red colour
code (shown in Figure B 3.4.6i) is used for voltages within
the 380-480V range, its use can be extended to voltage levels up to 1000V, depending on the specific industrial or
commercial application.
Figure B3.4.6i Five-pin 400V, 32A industrial three-phase
power plug for heavy commercial and industrial use.
Voltage rating and other characteristics are represented
by a colour code. [Stephan N, CC BY-5A 3.0, via
Wikimedia Commons]
The Perilex brand five-pin domestic three-phase power
plug (shown in Figure B3.4.6j) is approved for use in
Germany, Austria and the Netherlands for high-power
applications in both commercial and residential settings.
These plugs are typically rated for 400V and have versions
for 16A or 25A and are used for appliances such as large
stoves and heaters, induction cooktops, large machinery
and heavy-duty power tools and are considereda precursor
to the IEC 60309 system. They are typically smaller in size
compared with plugs specified in IEC 60309

B3.4.7
The Input-Process—Output (IPO) model shown in
Figure B 3.4.7a is a functional framework that explains
how a system takes in information or resources (inputs),
transforms them through a series of operations (processes)
and produces a result (outputs). It breaks down complex
systems into their three fundamental components,
allowing for a structured way to analyse how a system
functions.
Input »| Process ——> Output
Figure B3.4.7a Representation of the Input-Process—
Output (IPO) model
The first stage is the Input stage, where the system receives
external elements like materials, information or energy,
which are then used to sense environmental changes and
initiate the transformation into a new form.
Examples of Inputs:
« User commands, button clicks, as inputs to a computer
program or calculator.
« In an organisational context, task characteristics,
team composition and available resources govern the
operation of a team.
+ Raw materials, labour and infrastructure costs in an
industrial system.
« Video and audio information from a webcam allow a
computer to sense and react to the visual and auditory
environment in a video call.
« Sensors (suchas Lightand Temperature sensors provide
data (input) related to changes in the environment, for
passage to the second process stage.
When related to electronic systems, the first stage of the
IPO model involves the receipt of sensor inputs from the
environment or users and converts them into a voltage
or digital signal that the microcontroller can understand
and use for subsequent analysis or decision-making in the
second (process) stage.
A selective list of the range sensors available is provided in
Figure B3.4.7b

B3.4.8
The second stage of the Input-Process-Output (IPO)
model is the process stage and is the dynamic phase where
information (input(s)) is converted into a desired result
(output). In electronic systems, a microcontroller like
a PIC performs this stage by processing sensor data to
control actuators, such as motors or displays. This stage
functions as the mediating link between the system’s data
or resources and its final results.
As outlined in A 3.4.6, a microcontroller or programmable
interface controller (PIC) is a single-chip computer
system that integrates a CPU, memory (RAM and
program storage) and programmable input/output
(I/O) peripherals to control specific, automated tasks in
embedded systems like appliances or automotive systems.
Unlike more versatile microprocessors found in personal
computers, microcontrollers are designed for specialised
functions, making them a compact, self-contained
solution for controlling device operations rather than
general-purpose Gomputmg
Different components within the microcontroller
communicate with each other and external devices via
signal paths known as buses that enable data transfer
and interaction between the CPU, memory and I/O
peripherals on the chip and with other components, as
indicated in Figure B 3.4.8a.
Microcontroller software and programming
The specific functions of a microcontroller are determined
by its programmed software, which is stored in its memory.
‘While the CPU executes low-level binary machine code,
programming is facilitated by higher-level languages.
Programming languages for microcontrollers:
» Low-level languages: Assembly language is an
example, closely resembling machine code. These are
typically hardware-specific and not easily transferable
between different microcontroller types.
« High-level languages: Languages like BASIC, C, or
C++ are more generic and can be adapted for various
microcontrollers with minimal changes. They are
dominant in modern microcontroller programming
due to their ease of learning, interpretation,
implementation and debugging.
« Manufacturer determination: The specific
programming languages supported for a
microcontroller are set by its manufacturer.

B3.4.9
Digital systems use logic gates, such as the XNOR gate
for equality, to compare binary input data by performing
bit-by-bit logical operations. For multi-bit inputs,
digital comparators are built from combinational logic
gate circuits. These comparators, including magnitude
comparators, determine if one binary number is greater
than, less than or equal to another by comparing bits
sequentially from the most significant bit (MSB) to the least significant bit (LSB). An example of a comparator
circuit is shown in Figure B 3.4.9a
These gates form the foundation of:
+ Decision-making in microcontrollers
+ Arithmetic operations in CPUs
« Signal filtering in digital communication
« Control systems like alarms, sensors and automation
Logic circuits are broadly classified into two fundamental
groups: combinational logic circuits and sequential logic
circuits. The key distinction is that combinational circuits’
outputs depend solely on present inputs, while sequential
circuits’ outputs are influenced by both present inputs
and the circuit’s previous states, thanks to the inclusion
of memory elements like flip-flops. These elements store
single-bit output data until triggered by an input signal,
often a clock pulse, allowing them to store the ‘state’ of a
system and form the basis of digital memory.
Sequential logic circuits
Sequential circuits use memory elements to store previous
input information, meaning that a sequential circuit’s
output is influenced by its history. While the overall circuit
has memory to store past states, a portion of it is dedicated
to combinational logic that produces both the external
output and the signals that define the circuit’s next state.
Sequential circuits can be found in two forms: synchronous,
as shown in Figure B 3.49c and asynchronous. The
differentiating feature of these circuits is that synchronous
circuits use a clock/timer that coordinates the changing
internal output and internal input, while an asynchronous
circuit does not.
Because the logic gates, which perform the operations
on the data, require a finite amount of time to respond
to changes to their inputs, the absence of a clock in
asynchronous circuits can lead to problems if two signals
arrive at the same time.
External Input External Output
——> External ——>
utput
Internal Internal
Input Output
Memory
Clock
Figure B3.4.9¢ Synchronous Sequential Logic Circuit
Combinational logic circuits
Combinational logic circuits differ from sequential circuits
because their outputs depend only on the inputs at that
specific time, not past states, giving them no memory or
feedback. This means changes in a combinational circuit’s
inputs have an immediate effect on the output, while a
sequential circuit’s output also depends on its history, see
Figure B 3.4.9d.
Multiple [ Combinatorial
Inputs [—| Logic Circuit
Figure B 3.4.9d Combinational Logic Circuit
Because combinational circuits lack a memory and
have outputs determined by current inputs, they can be
described by Boolean Algebra in Truth Tables (introduced
previously in A 3.4.5), where all input possibilities and
resultant output is shown or logic diagrams using graphical
representations of standard logic gates, forming the basis
for circuit implementation.
Intermediate results in a combinational logic diagram
are shown by labelling the output of each logic gate or
intermediate point with a unique, arbitrary symbol, not
just ‘Q’ You then define the Boolean function for that
symbol, which is a function of the gate’s inputs and use
repeated substitution to find the final output Boolean
function as illustrated in Figures B 3.4.9¢, B 3.4.9f and
B3.4.9g

B3.4.10
In digital systems, the third stage of the Input-Process-
Qutput (IPO) model is Output, which represents the
final results, products or information that is produced
by the system after processing the inputs. This output
can be displayed to a user, sent to another system, or
used to control the environment, fulfilling the role of
communication or command.
Output devices are essential for digital systems to
interact with the physical world—whether it's displaying
information, making noise or moving something. A range
of appropriate output devices, based on their function, is
shown in Figure B 3.4.10a.

B3.4.11
In simple terms, a communication protocol is a set of rules
that define how serial data is transmitted and interpreted
between devices, ensuring that information passes
accurately and reliably.
Wi-Fi Protocol
Wi-Fi is a wireless networking technology that uses radio
waves to provide wireless high-speed Internet access.
Commonly seen symbols for Wi-Fi are shown in Figure
B 3.4.11a, with the name itself being a trademarked phrase
owned by the non-profit organisation Wi-Fi Alliance that
refers to the governing IEEE 802.11x standards.
The Wi-Fi Alliance was formed in 1999 and is a global,
non-profit organisation of companies that promote Wi-
Fi technology based on the IEEE 802.11 standards. The
Alliance acts to ensure interoperability, reliability and
backward compatibility and to promote wireless local-
area-network technology for devices from different
manufacturers, by offering a certification program and
brand usage.
The simplified Generational naming of new IEEE
standards “Wi-Fi X’ was introduced in 2018 to simplify
naming for consumers, who found the older IEEE 802.11
alphanumeric codes confusing and difficult to understand.
‘The new naming convention begins with Wi-Fi 4 because
it was the first to offer significant improvements, such as
dual-band (2.4 and 5 GHz) support and the use of Multiple
Input, Multiple Output (MIMO) technology, making it a
clear, recognisable benchmark for future improvements.
Before this, versions were named 802.11b, 802.11g and
802.11ac, but these names didn't indicate whether a new
version was an improvement over the last. The new system
provided a clear, generational numbering scheme (Wi-
Fi 4, Wi-Fi 5, Wi-Fi 6, etc.) to make it easier for users to
identify the capabilities and performance of different Wi-
Fi generations, see Figure B 3.4.11b.
While some industry sources informally refer to these
as ‘Wi-Fi 1, “Wi-Fi 2, and “Wi-Fi 3, these labels are not
officially recognised by the Wi-Fi Alliance. An embedded
system’s Wi-Fi protocol is a set of rules that defines how a
specialised computing device (the embedded system) can
communicate wirelessly over a Wi-Fi network to exchange
data with other devices, like the internet or other smart
devices.
The protocol used for Wi-Fi is IEEE 802.11, a family of
standards that define the technical basis for wireless local
area networks (WLANs). These standards dictate how
devices communicate using radio frequencies and they
are continually updated to improve speed, range and
efficiency. This protocol specifies the methods for sending
and receiving data, ensuring that the embedded system
can connect to a Wi-Fi network using the established
IEEE 802.11 standards and that data is formatted and
interpreted correctly by all participating devices.
Wi-Fi provides the wireless medium and protocol for
devices to connect to a router or access point, which acts
as a gateway to the network. This connection happens at
the lowest layers of the network model.
The rules and procedures for how data is transmitted over
the internet and other computer networks are defined
by the TCP/IP protocol stack model shown in Figure
B 3.4.11c. This model breaks down the complex task of
communication into four distinct layers and is a practical
model of network communication that forms the basis of
the Internet:
« Application,
« Transport,
« Internetand
« Link Layer
Each of these layers performs a specific function. Protocols
like HTTP, TCP, IP and Ethernet operate within these
layers to ensure that data is correctly formatted, addressed,
routed and delivered between devices across a network.
Application layer
Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) - serves as a
foundational application layer protocol within the Internet
protocol suite, enabling the exchange of hypermedia
documents, primarily HTML, over the World Wide Web.
It operates on a client-server model, where web browsers
act as clients initiating requests and web servers respond
with the requested resources.
While central to web browsing, HT'TP’s utility extends to
various other applications requiring data transfer over the
internet, including file downloads and API (Application
Programming Interface) communications between
different software systems.
Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3) - the function is
to download emails from a mail server to a local device.
Because emails are downloaded, users can read them even
if their device isn't connected to the internet.
Developed by the Internet Engineering Task Force
(IETE), this is the third and most widely used version of
the Post Office Protocol, a standard Internet protocol for
downloading email from a mail server to an email client.
Transport layer
Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) - provides
reliable, ordered delivery by using sequence numbers
to track and reassemble data bytes in the correct order,
acknowledgments (ACK’) to confirm receipt of data
and retransmission mechanisms to resend any lost or
corrupted segments detected by a time-out or a missing
ACK. TCP is used where data integrity is crucial, including
web browsing, file transfers and email, as it ensures every
piece of data arrives correctly.
User Datagram Protocol (UDP) - used for faster, less
reliable transmission. The lower reliability stems from
the fact that it is a send-and-forget protocol that sends
data without establishing a connection, performing
acknowledgments, or guaranteeing packet delivery or
order. UDP finds use where speed is prioritised over
perfect delivery, such as live video and audio streaming,
online gaming and VoIP calls.
Network layer
Internet Protocol (IP) is responsible for logical addressing
and routing data packets or ‘Datagrams’ across networks.
It assigns unique IP addresses to devices, determines the
best path for data to travel and forwards packets from
their source to their destination using a process of routing
and addressing. If a data packet is too large to be sent
over a particular network, the IP layer can fragment it
into smaller datagram packets for transmission and then
reassemble them at the destination.
The IP layer is designed to be independent of the protocols
above and below it, enabling it to carry data from a variety
of applications and ensure communication across diverse
network types.
Data link layer
Ethernet protocol - is responsible for moving data across a
specific local network segment. It does this by formatting
data into basic units known as ‘Frames, addressing them
with MAC (Media Access Control) addressesand handling
the physical transmission and reception of these frames on
the network medium. Ethernet specifically governs how
data is transmitted over wired networks, ensuring devices
on the same local network can communicate reliably.
Operating under the IEEE 802.3 standard that defines the
physical link layer and MAC for wired Ethernet networks,
the standard governs the physical and media access aspects
of data transmission on a local network segment.
Physical layer
802.11 Wi-Fi Protocol - handles the actual transmission
of raw data bits across a physical medium, defining the
hardware specifications like cables, connectors and
electrical/optical signal characteristics. It translates data
into signals that can travel over the medium and back into
bits for the network interface to receive.
Bluetooth protocol
The symbol for Bluetooth is shown in Figure B 3.4.11d
and combines the ancient Danish runes for the initials ‘H’
and ‘B’ (% and E ) of Harald Bluetooth, a reference to the
10th-century Danish King Harald Gormsson (also known
as Harald Bluetooth) who united Denmark and Norway.
The name was suggested as a temporary codename for
a short-range wireless program, but it caught on and
became synonymous with the technology, ultimately
being kept as the official name. The trademark is owned by
Bluetooth SIG, Inc., a global organisation that maintains
and protects the Bluetooth brand.
Bluetooth technology operates in the 2.4GHz ISM band
and enables data exchange over short distances of typically
less than 10 m. Its structure consists of three-layered
stacks, consisting of an Application, Host and Controller
layer, as illustrated in Figure B 3.4.11e.
The commonly adopted protocols used in Bluetooth are
Point-to-Point Protocol (PPP), Internet Protocol (IP),
User Datagram Protocol (UDP), Transmission Control
Protocol (TCP) and Wireless Application Protocol (WAP).
Telephony Control Protocol — TCS-BIN is the protocol
used here, which is a bit-oriented one.
Point-to-Point Protocol (PPP) - establishes a direct, one-
to-one communication link between two points.
Bluetooth also has a stack or layer arrangement, with
each layer containing different protocols required for
operation.
Application layer protocols
Object Exchange Protocol (OBEX) is a communications
protocol that enables devices to exchange binary data
objects, such as files, contacts and images. Initially
designed for Infrared (IrDA) connections, it was later
adopted by the Bluetooth standard and works as a
lightweight client-server system for spontaneous, ad-hoc
data transfers between wireless devices.
Point-to-Point Protocol (PPP): provides a standard way
to transmit Internet Protocol (IP) datagrams over a serial
link, allowing Bluetooth devices to provide IP-based
services like the Internet to higher-level applications.
PPP converts the packet-oriented data stream into a
serial data stream suitable for the underlying RECOMM
connection.
Service Discovery Protocol (SPD): allows Bluetooth-
enabled devices to ‘discover’ and exchange information
about available services on other devices. Services are
identified by Universally Unique Identifiers (UUIDs),
which are unique identifiers that represent the service
type, enabling clients to precisely target the services they
need.
Message Access Profile (MAP): defines how different
Bluetooth-enabled devices interact to exchange messages,
such as SMS and relies on services provided by Host-level
layers like GATT and L2CAP.
Advanced Audio Distribution Profile (A2DP): a
Bluetooth profile that enables the wireless transmission of
stereo audio, such as music, from one device to another.
The A2DP profile requires that audio data be compressed
using a codec before it can be transmitted efficiently over
a Bluetooth connection.
Radio Frequency Communications (RFCOMM) is a
Bluetooth transport protocol that creates virtual RS-232
serial ports over the L2CAP layer to provide a reliable,
full-duplex data stream for applications designed for
physical serial ports. It enables seamless integration of
legacy software with Bluetooth by acting as a bridge and
serves as the foundation for essential Bluetooth profiles
like the Serial Port Profile (SPP) and Dialup Networking
(DUN).
Host layer protocols
Generic Access Profile (GAP): defines the basic rules and
procedures for Bluetooth device discovery, connection
and data exchange, ensuring interoperability between
devices by establishing device roles and defining how they
broadcast their presence and initiate connections. When
devices are not connected, a device will ‘advertise’ its
presence, allowing other devices to ‘scan’ for it and display
its name, which enables a user to see what devices are
available before pairing. GAP defines these fundamental
behaviours, ensuring that all Bluetooth devices can
interact with each other at a foundational level.
Generic Attribute Profile (GATT): defines a standardised
framework for how two Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE)
devices discover and exchange data. It works on top of
the Attribute Protocol (ATT), organising data into a
hierarchy of services and characteristics within a lookup
table. This structure allows devices to read, write and even
subscribe to notifications for specific data values, enabling
communication for various applications like wearables
and IoT devices.
Attribute Protocol (ATT): defines the fundamental rules
for how data is structured, stored and accessed in a client-
server model for Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) devices. It
specifies the concept of ‘attributes, which are the smallest
units of data (like a heart rate value or battery level)
and provides the messages and procedures for a client
device to discover, read, write and receive notifications or
indications for these attributes from a server device.
Security Manager Protocol (SMP): manages the
establishment of secure connections between Bluetooth
Low Energy (BLE) devices by handling pairing,
authentication and key distribution. It defines how devices
exchange information to secure their connection through
generating and storing various keys like Long-Term Keys
(LTK) and Identity Resolving Keys (IRK), ensuring data
integrity, confidentiality and privacy.
Logical Link Control and Application Protocol (L2ZCAP):
acts as a multiplexer, allowing multiple higher-level
protocols and applications to share a single logical link
between devices. It handles segmentation and reassembly
of large data packets into smaller ones for transmission
and reassembling them on the receiving end. L2CAP also
provides connection-oriented and connectionless data
services, per-channel flow control and error control for
reliable communication over Bluetooth links
Host Controller Interface (HCL): is a standardised
specification that defines the interface and protocol for
communication between a Bluetooth Host (the software
managing the Bluetooth connection, application logic and
higher-level Bluetooth protocols) and a Controller (the
dedicated hardware managing the radio and lower-level
Bluetooth functions). It ensures interoperability between
different vendors’ hardware by standardising the format
of commands sent by the Host to the Controller, as well as
events and data returned by the Controller to the Host.
Controller
Voice Coder-Decoder (Voice CODEC): acts as a ‘coder-
decoder’ by first compressing and converting an analog
voice signal into a digital format suitable for internet
transmission (encoding) and then converting it back to
an analog signal by decompressing it at the receiving end
for playback (decoding). This compression is crucial for
efficient transmission over networks by reducing the data
size, which allows for real-time communication over the
internet.
Link Manager (LM): handles connection setup,
authentication, security and power control by exchanging
LMP messages with another Link Manager across a
physical link.
Baseband Link Management Protocol (LMP): is a set
of messages used by the Link Manager to communicate
with another Link Manager. LMP messages are carried
over logical links and are interpreted only by the receiving
Link Manager. They are not passed to higher layers of the
Bluetooth stack.
Bluetooth Radio Frequencies (RF): performs the physical
transmission of data by converting digital bits into
radio signals, modulating them onto a carrier wave and
transmitting them over the airwaves at 2.4 GHz. It is often
called the PHY layer because it deals with the physical
layer of the network, handling the hardware-specific
details of how data is converted into and from signals in
the radio spectrum. It implements fast frequency hopping
(e.g., 1600 hops per second) by rapidly switching channels
to enhance security and reduce interference.
5G, Bluetooth and wireless technologies
Innovations like the Internet of Things (IoT), 5G
and distributed computing frameworks such as edge
computing that move data processing and storage closer
to the physical location of the data’s source or user are
enabling seamless device and system integration by
providing the necessary infrastructure for massive data
exchange and localised processing.
IoT connects a vast number of devices, 5G provides a
high-speed, lowflattem:y1 network for these devices to
communicate and edge computing processes data near its
source, reducing latency and improving reliability for real-
time applications.
Wi-Fi offers high-speed, longer-range connectivity for
internet access and data transfer within a local network,
while Bluetooth provides low-power, short-range device-
to-device communication. 5G delivers extremely high
bandwidth, ultra-low latency and broad coverage for
real-time, mission-critical applications, but requires
cellular infrastructure. The choice depends on the
embedded system's needs, such as data rate, range, power
consumption and the requirement for internet access
versus direct device communication.
5G communication offers significant improvements over
3G and 4G, providing significantly faster speeds, lower
latency (near real-time responsiveness) and greater
bandwidth to connect more devices simultaneously.
While the introduction of each new generation has offered
advances in capabilities over the last, the changes have not
always gone smoothly. In October 2024, Australia shut
down its 3G mobile network to free up capacity and despite
this being preceded by a public awareness campaign,
many consumers and businesses were caught off guard,
resulting in devices suddenly being blocked from all
mobile services. Even phones advertised as 4G/5G could
be blocked from all services, including data, SMS and
emergency (Triple Zero), if they were not VoLTE (Voice
over Long-Term Evolution) compatible. This was because
many phones with 4G capabilities were still designed to
fall back to the more established 3G infrastructure for
robustness.
The problem arose largely because of a regulatory decision
made by the Australian Communications and Media
Authority (ACMA) to block service to 4G phones that
did not have VoLTE technology. The result was that
devices that were 3G-only or 4G non-VoLTE phones
were suddenly blocked from making any mobile calls,
sending data or sending SMS messages. Many older
devices, including essential medical alarms, also stopped
working when the 3G network was shut down because
they couldn’t connect to the newer network, forcing users
into sometimes expensive upgrades.
Because of this experience, many countries, such as
the UK and France, delayed their 3G shutdowns until
the unexpected compatibility and safety issues were
resolved.
As countries move away from 3G networks to 4G and 5G,
VoLTE technology, which offers clearer High Definition
(HD) voice, faster call connections and the ability to use
data simultaneously with voice calls, is becoming essential,
especially for connection to emergency numbers.
Comparison of 5G with 3G and 4G
3G (Third Generation): Introduced in 2001, 3G was the
first generation to offer widespread mobile internet access,
enabling features like video calls and basic mobile web
browsing.
4G (Fourth Generation): Introduced faster internet
speeds, higher-quality video calls and the ability to stream
high-definition video on mobile devices. It increased data
capacity and reliability for data-intensive tasks.
5G (Fifth Generation): This is the current (2025)
generation, offering ultra-high speeds (potentially reaching
10 gigabits per second or more) and substantially lower
latency, allowing for near-instantaneous communication.
Its increased bandwidth and massive network capacity
enable more devices to connect simultaneously, making
it ideal for applications like virtual reality, autonomous
vehicles and advanced industrial automation.
5G vs. Wi-Fi
Cellular Technology: 5G (and 4G/3G) is a form of cellular
technology, where a network of mobile towers provides
wireless internet connections over wide areas.
Local Wireless Technology: Wi-Fiisa different technology
that uses local wireless access points, like a home router, to
connect devices to a local network.
Complementary, Not Dependent: While both 5G and Wi-Fi provide internet access, 5G does not rely on Wi-Fi communication. Instead, they are complementary technologies, with Wi-Fi being more accessible for home or office use and 5G providing broader, mobile connectivity.
Bluetooth does not rely on WiFi or cellular communication;
it is an independent, short-range radio-frequency technology that connects devices directly to each other without needing an internet or cellular connection. It uses
low-power radio waves on the 2.4 GHz band to transmit
data between devices within a limited range, making it ideal for portable use and devices like headphones
or speakers where a constant internet connection isn't
necessary.
Bluetooth uses a different communication method called
frequency hopping to avoid interference and does not
require an internet connection to function.
The choice of Bluetooth, 5G, or Wi-Fi protocols
dramatically impacts loT embedded system scalability and
interoperability by defining their data rates, range, power
consumption and network capacity. Bluetooth offers
low-power, short-range and scalable device-to-device
communication with a unified management entity but is
limited in data throughput. Wi-Fi provides high-speed,
medium-range connectivity suitable for bandwidth-inntensive applications but consumes more power. 5G
enables massive device connectivity, low latency, high
data rates and long-range communication, ideal for large-
scale and real-time IoT deployments but requiring more
complex infrastructure.

C3.1.1
The primary objective of product analysis and evaluation
is to generate a comprehensive understanding of the
various components and features that collectively
contribute to a product’s functionality, consumer appeal
and competitiveness within the marketplace, This critical
examination seeks not only to identify the product’s
inherent strengths but also to expose potential weaknesses
and areas of underperformance. In doing so, it provides
actionable insights that inform iterative improvements
and strategic innovations for future development.
A variety of approaches can be used to conduct a product
analysis exercise:
« Understanding user needs provides critical insight
into a product’s effectiveness and usability from the
consumer’s perspective, ensuring that it aligns with
real-world expectations and behaviours.
Evaluating the product’s form and function assesses
the appropriateness of materials, manufacturing
techniques and aesthetic considerations to identify
any deficiencies that may compromise performance or
user satisfaction.
+ Analysing a product’s competitive positioning enables
designers to benchmark its market performance against
rival offerings, informing strategies for innovation,
differentiation and enhanced market relevance.
« Strategic analysis examines key business decisions,
particularly concerning pricing models and marketing
approaches. The result provides insights into market
penetration, profitability and brand perception.
« Sustainability audits examine opportunities to improve
the products environmental responsibility without
incurring prohibitive costs, thereby aligning with eco-
conscious consumer values and emerging regulatory
standards.

C3.1.2
A diverse range of stakeholders is critical to the success
of the product analysis process through the provision
of a variety of views, shaped by their occupations, life
experiences and expertise. Diversity helps ensure a product
serves a wider audience. What seems intuitive to one group
may be a barrier for another. When a diverse group of
individuals collaborate, a greater range of creative ideas is
generated. When stakeholders are involved in the process,
they feel heard and valued. This leads to stronger product
support and ‘buy-in’ across the organisation. Stakeholders
are typically drawn from the following groups.
Consumers: Offer contextual, user-based feedback
concerning ease of use, functional efficacy and perceived
value, thereby ensuring the product aligns with user
expectations and behaviours.
Product Development Managers: aggregate all of the
information and incoming data to provide a holistic
response that guides further product development.
Product Designers: Prioritise aesthetic appeal, functional
integration and user experience, while ensuring the design
aligns with principles of sustainability and innovation.
Engineers: Evaluate the technical feasibility of design
concepts, offering expertise in materials science,
structural integrity, system constraints and opportunities
for performance enhancement.
Marketing Teams: Analyse market trends and competitor
positioning to inform the products unique selling
proposition, pricing strategy, promotional channels and
distribution potential.
Customer Support Staff: Serve as a vital conduit for
feedback by identifying recurring pain points, functional
shortcomings and areas for improvement based on direct
consumer interaction.
Business Leaders: Ensure that product development
initiatives reflect organisational values, operate within
budgetary parameters and generate a return on investment
aligned with the company’s strategic goals.
This framework reinforces the value of inclusive
collaboration, enabling the creation of a solution that
is not only technically and aesthetically refined but also
commercially viable and broadly accessible.
This diverse, inclusive collaboration should ensure an
outcome that is not just technically sound and aesthetically
pleasing but also viable in the real world across a range of
consumers.

C3.1.3
 SWOT analysis provides the structure to assist with
finding problem areas and features that may benefit
from development or further innovation. The framework
consists of 4 quadrants to allow for better focusing specific
tasks, i.e. strengths, weaknesses, opportunities and threats,
hence the acronym SWOT.
« Strengths: What valued features does the product
or concept possess? Identified features may include
aesthetics, functionality, technological superiority and
brand value/reputation.
+ Weaknesses: What limitations hinder the product,
eg resource constraints, gaps in expertise, pricing,
intuitiveness, market placement, flaws in the initial
concept.
« Opportunities: Consider such things as market trends,
colours, new technologies and size.
+ Threats: What forces potentially pose risks? Consider
competitors, changing regulations, economic
downturns, supply chain issues or evolving customer
preferences

Porter’s Five Forces
A complementary tool to SWOT analysis is Porter’s Five
Forces Model, developed by Michael Porter in 1979, to
analyse the competitive intensity and attractiveness of an
industry. While SWOT analyses the internal strengths
and weaknesses and external opportunities and threats
of a specific organisation or project, the Five Forces
model helps businesses determine the attractiveness and
profitability of an industry by examining five competitive
forces.
The five forces are:
1. Competitive rivalry.
% Supplier power.
3. Buyer Power.
4. Threat of new entry.
5. Threat of substitution

C3.1.4
Function, performance and usability testing may each
generate quantifiable data that could play a critical role
in shaping product development. These tests combine to
produce hard evidence and a holistic response as to how
a product performs in context, rather than how it was
intended to perform..
Functional testing measures if the product performs its
intended tasks correctly, e.g. the buttons work, features
behave as anticipated etc. Data generated in this case
may be in the form of: pass/fail rates, error logs, defect
frequency and compliance with specifications. The aim
here is to determine that the product meets minimum
requirements before moving to the next stage of testing.
Performance testing measures how the product behaves in
context under various conditions, Data here may include
accuracy and intuitiveness and failure conditions.
Impact testing determines optimal system architecture,
ensuring the product can handle real-world demands
without degradation, e.g. loads, cycle frequency; etc.
Usability testing measures users’ interactions with the
product in a series of field tests, identifying key criteria
such as ease of use, efficiency, satisfaction, task completion
rates, time on task, error frequency, user satisfaction and
learnability.
Reverse engineering entails the systematic disassembly
and analysis of a competitor’s or one’s own product and
serves as a valuable approach for uncovering underlying
design rationales. This process reveals critical insights
into material selection, implementation of technological
advancements and cost-optimisation strategies. By
examining and comparing internal architectures and
component configurations across analogous products,
designers and engineers can identify opportunities for
innovation, alternative means of task execution and
improved performance efficiencies.
Combined, these tests and approaches provide a multi-
dimensional dataset that better informs future design
decisions by highlighting friction points and aligning
the product more closely with user expectations. Testing
teams may also validate their assumptions and predictions,
prioritise improvements and reduce risk.
Figure C 3.1.4 shows a D30 Lab apparatus testing impact
protection technology incorporated into protective cases
for consumer electronics, including phones

C3.1.5
Designers use a mix of analytical, observational and
creative strategies to compare competing products and
uncover opportunities for improvement.
Competitive product analysis allows designers to
systematically evaluate rival products to understand their
features, performance, pricing and user experience. By
creating side-by-side comparisons of product features,
rival products provide a series of benchmarks or standards
that need to be met or exceeded. This approach helps
designers to prioritise which features require addition,
deletion, enhancement or refinement.
Customer reviews are also a valuable source of real-world
feedback that reveals consumer satisfaction with specific
features or the product overall. In many cases, this data
indicates whether or not the consumer would recommend
this product to others.
In-house testing is conducted when design studios utilise
their equipment to evaluate products, including those of
competing brands. However, data obtained through such
methodsmay be subject to confirmation bias or conflicts of
interest. To enhance objectivity and credibility, designers
often commission independent external laboratories to
perform testing and supply unbiased data.
Value Proposition Mapping creates a response to consumer
fears, wants and needs and compares the benefits, features
and user experience. This helps clarify what truly resonates
with users and where differentiation is possible.
SWOT Analysis, as described earlier, represents a valuable
tool for comparison testing by providing a structured
framework to evaluate a product’s strengths, weaknesses,
opportunities and threats, enabling a comprehensive
comparison against competitors.

C3.1.6
Constructive discontent is a strategy that examines points
of frustration and annoyance arising from a product’s use
and uses that tension to drive improvement.
Designers ask the question, “What might annoy users
even slightly?” It is these points of friction that may
identify customer dissatisfaction. An example could
be a computer system that takes 90 seconds to start up.
Customers tolerate this waste of time, but a computer with
a faster startup time may give it a competitive edge.
Body language and non-verbal communication can also
provide insight into the user’s experience. Observation of users who develop their own ‘workarounds’ also points
to areas where a product’s performance falls short of user
expectations.
Designer empathy attempts to have designers experience
a product in an authentic context and view the product
from the user’s perspective. This empathetic lens helps
surface emotional disconnects like confusion, frustration
or boredom.
Constructive discontent fuels proactive iteration, driven
by the assumption that nothing is perfect and there is
always room for improvement.

C3.1.7
Product analysis serves as a critical approach through
which designers attain a comprehensive understanding
of a product’s operational functionality, market value,
competitive positioning and user-centric performance.
The systematic deconstruction of a product through a
variety of lenses, including materials, features, ergonomic
considerations and overall performance, allows designers
to identify strengths and limitations. These insights
underpin iterative refinement processes and strategic
product enhancements.
A pivotal component of product analysis involves expert-
led observational studies of real-world usage, enabling
the collection of authentic, contextually grounded user
data. This approach bridges the gap between anticipated
functionality and actual user experience, yielding
actionable feedback for design optimisation.
Benchmarking against similar market offerings within the
competitive landscape equips designers with an awareness
of prevailing industry standards, emerging aesthetic
trends and potential avenues for market differentiation.
The aggregation and interpretation of such data inform
critical design decisions across multiple domains,
including pricing strategy, manufacturing feasibility,
consumer preferences and brand positioning.
Through the investigation of both successes and
shortcomings, designers are motivated to reexamine
problems, explore new technologies and iterate the
product into a more appealing user-aligned product.

C3.2.1
Designers need to consider the environmental effects of
their products because a significant portion of a product’s
environmental impact is determined during the design
phase.
By incorporating a Life-cycle assessment (LCA) at
this early stage, environmental considerations can be
included that minimise negative impacts, reduce waste
and contribute to a more sustainable future. Additionally,
environmentally conscious design can enhance a product’s
appeal to eco-conscious consumers and improve brand
reputation.
Life-cycle assessment (LCA) is an internationally
standardised process to assess environmental impacts
on products and services. It is a tool to support decision-
making for manufacturers when assessing the impact a
product or process has on the environment. Specifically,
the process involves all stages of product development,
including:
« extraction and refining of raw materials,
« production,
+ packaging,
« use,
maintenance and
ultimately disposal.
LCA is a relatively young technique; it became popular in
the early 1990s. Initially, many thought that LCA would
be a suitable tool to support environmental claims that
could directly be used in marketing. Over the years, it has
become clear that this is not the best application for LCA,
although it is important to communicate LCA results in a
careful and well-balanced way.
In recent years, life-cycle thinking (LCT) has become a key
focus in environmental policy making. A clear example
is the concept of IPP (Integrated Product Policy) as
communicated by the EU. Also in Asia and the Americas,
many countries have developed strategies that promote
life-cycle thinking as a key concept.
Another development is the sustainability reporting
movement. “The majority of the Fortune 500 companies
now report on the sustainability aspects of their
operations.” (PRé Consultants, Introduction to LCA with
SimaPro, 2008).
Life-cycle analysis is just one way companies are becoming
more responsible for manufacturing-based emissions and
retrieving product waste. It also forces designers to think
about changing product design to favour the environment,
i.e. developing sustainable product designs that consider
energy, materials efficiencies, manufacturing processes,
in-service use and product disposal.
LCA stages
These are: pre-production, production, distribution,
including packaging, utilisation and disposal
The key stages in product life-cycle analysis include: raw
materials, manufacturing, distribution and end-of-life
disposal. The purpose of the life-cycle analysis process is to
highlight areas where opportunities may occur to reduce
energy or materials inputs, and examine environmental
consequences at every point in the product life-cycle.
The process may be broken down into four parts listed
below and arranged graphically in Figure C3.2.1b.
« Goal definition and scope - specifying context,
constraints and breadth of the review process,
« Inventory analysis — collecting and documenting data
on energy, materials inputs and emissions.
« Impact assessment — analysing environmental impacts
associated with the above inputs and emissions.
« Interpretation - preparing an interpretation of the
analysis for management.
Note that LCA approaches are assistive devices in the
decision-making process; the LCA itself makes no
decisions.
Environmental considerations
Every stage of product development has its associated
environmental impacts. These impacts can be broken
down into inputs and outputs. Inputs include energy and
raw materials, while outputs consist of energy, emissions
and waste.
The creation of these inputs and outputs can have a
variety of direct and indirect environmental effects. These
include, but are not limited to:
climate change
+ ozone depletion
» soil contamination and erosion
« loss of biodiversity and habitat
« reduction in water quality
« depletion of finite resources
« noise pollution.
A detailed life-cycle analysis incorporates every stage of a
product’s life; the so-called ‘cradle to grave’ span.
To be successful, the analysis must include an
environmental assessment of the impacts associated
with:
« pre-production, incorporating mining of raw
materials, processing and transportation
« manufacturing processes, including
requirements, waste, cooling and lighting
energy
« packaging and transportation; counting fuels,
emissions, inks, size and density
« in-service use, encompassing power requirements,
maintenance and in-use emissions
« end-of-life disposal, involving waste recovery,
recycling, reuse and landfilling.
A Life-cycle assessment (LCA) can also include an
assessment of the extraction of raw materials in the
product life-cycle by providing a comprehensive
analysis of the environmental impacts associated with
the production process used. Gathering accurate and
detailed data on energy consumption, emissions, resource
depletion, and site rehabilitation provides material for
further analysis/comparison. Evaluating the data collected
on the environmental consequences of raw material
extraction, such as greenhouse gas emissions, dust/noise/
water pollution, water usage and habitat disruption, helps
identify areas of concern. Setting achievable sustainability
goals based on the LCA findings, such as reducing carbon
footprints, minimising waste, promoting the use of
renewable resources and reviewing eco-friendly materials
with smaller environmental footprints, all contribute to
reducing environmental impacts.

C3.2.2
In the manufacturing phase of the product lifecycle,
a LCA may be employed to assess the effective use
of resources such as energy, water and raw materials
resources. Reduction of waste, fossil fuel-generated power,
and emissions can lower both manufacturing costs,
greenhouse gases and other pollutants.
In the transport and distribution phase, careful supply
chain management through the use of such strategies as
strategic factory location or buying local resources reduces
shipping times and costs.
A life-cycle analysis does not provide solutions but
data upon which to make better informed decisions. It
identifies the areas most in need of review based on their
environmental impacts.

C3.2.3
Product life-cycle stages
These encompass the role of the designer, manufacturer
and user.
Designers are responsible for bringing products from
the conceptual stage to the consumer. In this task, they
attempt to predict, target and capture market segments
from competitors through the continued development of
innovative designs. These designs all start with a clearly
defined brief. The role of a product designer is to find the
solution of best fit and encompasses many of the skills and
characteristics associated with marketing, manufacturing,
materials engineering, aesthetics and ergonomics.
Constantly in search of a competitive edge, designers
play a crucial part in differentiating their products from
others in the market. Companies also require designers
to trim costs by minimising packaging and generating
designs that use fewer materials, produce less waste and
contain more recycled and recyclable materials. Research
shows the overwhelming percentage (80% to 90%) of
committed costs (including manufacturing, distribution
and servicing) are locked in at the design stage. The
role of the designer is of paramount importance when
considering the financial aspect of every stage of product
development.
Manufacturers have an important role to play in
the selection of production processes. They have
responsibilities for both quality control and the speed
at which products are produced. They must specify
standards for components and outsourced sub-assemblies.
They are also responsible for product warranties and may
even provide after-sales service or technical training in
repair procedures. Manufacturers have a role to play
in minimising energy requirements, emissions, water
contamination, etc, through the adoption of waste
management programs designed to recycle or reuse waste
products. These measures reduce environmental impacts
but can also make sound economic sense. Users provide
the demand for products. They have the right to purchase
products that will perform as advertised. Their patterns of
spending determine whether a more durable product is
purchased over a disposable one.
Responsible use of products can also reduce power
requirements through such facilities as the use of low-
power or sleep modes. Thoughtful disposal of products
and packaging can increase recycling and reduce landfills.
Consumers have the power to encourage designers
and manufacturers to pursue both creative and green
developments of new and existing products through their
buying patterns. Product sales drive the process of design
and innovation. Consumers also have opportunities to
provide feedback to manufacturers and designers when
completing warranty or product surveys on the purchase of a new product.
Environmental impact assessment matrix
A matrix simply providesa way of assisting with organising
and presenting data for analysis. An LCA environmental
impact assessment matrix allows researchers to identify,
collect and value individual environmental impacts over
the entire product life-cycle. A streamlined life-cycle
analysis (SLCA) is quicker and cheaper than a full LCA.
Questions about each part of the life-cycle are examined
by the relevant teams throughout the life of the product,
and the environmental impacts are noted in a matrix.
A numerical scale (0-4) is used to record the degree of
environmental impact associated with each variable.
Relative values in each category are awarded within the
range of 0-4 (where the lower values signify a greater
impact on the environment. The final numerical result is
shown in the bottom right-hand corner of the table.
The results of the Streamlined Life-cycle Analysis show that
Biodiesel is the fuel of choice. However, the SLCA weighs
the importance of each life stage equally, which cannot
be the case in a real-world scenario. The environmentally
harmful emissions occur in each life stage. In this scoring
system, 4 is good and 0 is poor.
The simplified life-cycle analysis matrix is shown in
Figure C3.2.2b identifies product life-cycle stages for three
different fuels (diesel, bio-diesel and hydrogen) and their
various forms of environmental impact. Results differ
greatly, and so life stages should be proportionally levied
to account for this. If such were the case, and the use phase
of the product was weighed to be of higher significance,
hydrogen fuel would have been the clear winner.
The complex nature of individual product requirements,
including materials sourcing and processing, energy
inputs, emissions, production techniques, transportation
and in-service energy needs, means the nature and the
number of elements in the matrix will vary wildly. The
notion of a one-size-fits-all approach will not suffice.
o reduce the complexity of an LCA, some of the minor
components or indirect activities within a product’s
development may be removed from consideration when
establishing the scope of the analysis. These components
may, however, have a cumulative and significant impact
on the environment. If omitted from the parameters for
consideration when developing the scope of a LCA, then
the result can be misleading.
Many occasions will also require the weighting of the
separate matrix elements to produce results truly reflecting
the environmental impact of the product over its entire
life-cycle. Life-cycle analysis, using a balanced equivalency
for each of the matrix elements when analysing motor
vehicles, could produce an extraordinarily skewed result.
An overwhelming majority of reports document the
imbalance of environmental impact between production
and in-service use. Two examples of such reports are:
« EPA Automotive Trends Report - (U.S. Environmental
Protection Agency). Documenting comprehensive
data on vehicle greenhouse gas emissions and fuel
economies.
« Life Cycle Analysis Comparison - (Transportation
Energy Institute). Factors affecting the environmental
impact of vehicles, including electricity makeup and
fuel production chains are recorded here.
These reports highlight the significant environmental
impact during the operational phase of vehicles compared
to their production phase.
Theweighting process mustbe carefully considered. If some
elements of the life-cycle are inappropriately prioritised
and weighted, the final result can be even more distorted.
Results obtained in this fashion could potentially change
the emphasis for future product development from the in-
service phase to the manufacturing phase or erroneously
recommend one product over another.
In a document commissioned by the British Motor
Industry in 2006, showed a bias towards vehicle operation
compared to manufacturing of 9 to 1, i.e. 90% of all energy
consumed was in the operational phase. Although lower
for vehicles such as the Toyota Prius, a 2006 study delivered
a figure of 75% of energy use in the operational stage. The
remaining 25% was derived from a combination of fuel
production, manufacturing and end-of-life disposal. This
type of information is critical to the construction of an
accurate LCA matrix.
In contradiction to the previous example, when
considering electronic equipment for environmental
impacts, research identifies the in-use phase to be of
lesser importance due to reduced energy consumption
levels. Therefore, assessments in this category, based only
on energy use, could lead to wrong or grossly inaccurate
conclusions.
When assessing consumer electronics, it is argued
that consumers’ patterns of energy use have a greater
environmental impact than the replacement of older, less
efficient models. Extended use of an older product is a
more environmentally friendly choice than replacement
given the environmental costs associated with extracting,
processing and manufacturing.

A variety of LCA approaches may be applied to products
and services. Selection of the appropriate technique
is dependent on many factors. A selection of these
techniques includes:
« Cradle-to-grave or full life-cycle assessment
« Cradle-to-gate or partial life-cycle from resource
extraction (cradle) to the factory gate (before consumer
distribution).
« Cradle-to-cradle, where the end-of-life disposal results
in a recycling process.
« Gate-to-gate, another partial LCA examining only one
value-added process in the production segment.
« Well-to-wheel LCA used for distribution and transport
networks encompassing fuels and transportation.

A4.1.1
here are broadly two main categories of manufacturing
processes that encompass various techniques used to
produce different products:
Discrete manufacturing
Discrete manufacturing deals with the production of
distinet, individual items that can be counted and tracked
separately. it often involves assembly of parts, and includes
products such as appliances, cars, clothing and furniture.
The processes within this category can themselves be
organised into five categories:
. additive (3D printing) technologies
subtractive/wasting technologies
Forming techniques
joining techniques and
« finishing techniques.
Process manufacturing
Process manufacturing involves the production of
products that are not easily separated into individual
units. It focusses on the continuous or batch production
of materials that are transformed through chemical,
physical or biological processes, such as chemicals, food,
pharmaceuticals, etc.
Within this category would lie:
« casting
+ extrusion
« continuous rolling
+ blending technologies.
Different manufacturing techniques offer advantages based
on various factors like product complexity, production
volume, material properties and required precision. For
example, additive manufacturing (3D printing) excels
in producing complex geometries and customised parts,
while traditional methods like subtractive/wasting
manufacturing (machining) and casting are more efficient
for high-volume production or when specific material
properties are crucial.

A4.1.2
Additive manufacturing processes build physical models
by fusing, sintering or polymerising separate pieces of
material together. Modern additive manufacturing is
often referred to as rapid prototyping, solid free-form
fabrication, layered manufacturing or direct digital
manufacturing. It consists of a range of processes used to
produce solid components through the deposition and
fusing of consecutive layers of material.
Initially, designers create a CAD model which is then
converted into an STL (STereo Lithography) file. This
format was originally developed for stereo lithographic
manufacturing. The process converts surfaces into
polygons with their geometry recorded as mathematical
coordinates.
In some situations, IGES, (Initial Graphic Exchange
Specifications), formatting of the data may also be
appropriate. The advantage of rapid prototyping lies in its
ability to quickly and cost-effectively produce any shape
that can be modelled in a CAD program. Once started, the
build operation requires no operator input.
The production of physical models is done through the
slicing of the formatted 3D CAD models into multiple
layers ready to be sequentially rebuilt without the use
of any tooling or complex tool paths, thus significantly
reducing the amount of time to produce a prototype
or fully functioning model. While there are at least 20
different commercial additive manufacturing processes
