they all fall into just four major groups.
352
« Lamination
« Photopolymers
« Deposition (fibre and inkjet)
« Powders (layered and sprayed).
Use of these technologies significantly reduces the lead-
time required to bring a design to market.
Paper-based rapid prototyping, involves the use of
paper to create a prototype using 3D additive printing
techniques.
Because it uses paper as the raw material it is much quicker
and cheaper than other forms of 3D additive printing. In
this process, rather than distributing powder over the
surface and fusing the material with heat, sheets of paper
are bonded with an adhesive to progressively build up a
3D prototype.
A number of paper-based rapid prototyping techniques
are available, two of which are outlined below.
Selective deposition lamination (SDL) was invented in
2003 by Fintan and Conor MacCormack. Their company
Mcor Technologies marketsa 3D printer that uses standard
A4/legal sheets of paper to create a 3D model.
In the SDL process, the standard STL format 3D digital
data file is processed using a control software such as
SlicelT, which divides the computer model into slices
the thickness of a sheet of paper. An A4/legal sheet of
paper is initially attached to the build plate as the model
foundation, and a water based adhesive deposited over the
surface, such that a denser deposit is placed in those areas
that will become part of the model and a lower density
of adhesive supplied elsewhere. A tungsten carbide blade
then cuts the model outline into the top sheet before
the next application of adhesive and sheet of paper is
introduced. By repeating this process a laminated model
is progressively built.
On completion the surrounding waste portions of paper
are removed to expose the 3D model.
Laminated Object Manufacturing (LOM) is an older
form of paper-based rapid prototyping than SDL, and
involves the fusing or bonding of paper sheets using heat
to melt a layer of plastic on the bottom of the paper, which
is then cut with a blade or laser, as illustrated in Figure
Ad.1.2a
raft paper backed with a polyethylene-based, heat
sensitive adhesive is often used as the modelling material.
This product is both cost-effective and environmentally
friendly.
Once the layer has been cut the table moves down and the
paper supply is advanced ready for the next layer of the
model to be added.
Paper models are regularly sealed to prevent damage from
moisture and to improve their overall strength.
Stereolithography (SLA) was developed in 1984 by Chuck
Hall of 3D Systems Corp. SLA was the first commercial
3D printing process and uses a liquid photosensitive resin
that is cured by ultraviolet light. The prototype is formed
on a movable platform situated within a container of resin.
Starting at an elevated position with only a thin layer of
resin present, a laser beam of UV light is directed at the
surface. Once the desired area has cured, the platform
lowers sufficiently to allow resin to cover the cured resin
on the platform and the cycle repeats gradually adding
to the model layer by layer, as illustrated in Figure
A4.1.2b. Because a liquid is used, the developing model
would be unsupported if support structures were not
produced concurrently and which require removal after
completion
When the model is completed the platform is raised out of
the resin container and the model removed.
Fused Deposition Modelling (FDM) although a
common label, FDM is a trademarked term and patented
technology owned by the American-Israeli company
Stratasys founded in 1989. A more generic term is
Material Extrusion (ME) which encompasses processes
such as fused filament fabrication (FFF). FDM is a specific
form of material extrusion in which 3D printed objects
are created by heating a thermoplastic polymer to about
200°C and extruding a filament through a nozzle to build
the object layer-by-layer.
A wide variety of thermoplastic polymers can be used
with FDM printers such as PLA (Polylactic Acid), PET
(Polyethylene Terephthalate), PS (Polystyrene), ASA
(Acrylonitrile Styrene Acrylate), PVA (Polyvinyl Alcohol),
and the Polyamide Nylon.
Depending on their formulation, some thermoplastic
elastomers notably those with a low hardness of less than
60-70 Shore ‘A, such as Polyurethanes (PU) can be too
viscous at printing temperatures, making the avoidance
of printer nozzle clogging and smooth extrusion difficult,
although research continues to overcome this problem.
Selective laser sintering (SLS) is a 3D manufacturing
process in which a laser, (commonly C‘.O‘2 although others
can be used), is selectively focused onto a section of a
moveable table covered in a heat-fusible powder of either
metal or polymer. The power of the laser is set to bind the
powder without it melting (sintering). Repetitive scans of
the table with the laser build up the model in layers.
As each layer scan is completed, a new layer of powder is
deposited over the top and pressed flat with a roller. The
act of compressing the powder allows the model to be
constructed without supporting structures. Models may
even be manufactured with moving parts.
Once complete, the model is dusted off, with excess
powder recycled. SLS powders can be manufactured from:
thermoplastics, polystyrene, sand, wax, ceramics, steel
and even stainless steel as illustrated in Figure A4.1.2d

A4.1.3
Rapid prototyping using CAD modelling and 3D printing
technology to rapidly create a design and manufacture an
object allows initial testing and design validation to be
undertaken. The low cost and rapidity with which such
models can be created allows for multiple prototypes to
be printed as part of an iterative approach to product
refinement. The trade-off being that the surface finish and
fine detail of 3D printed parts may be wanting, requiring
some additional finishing operations.
By contrast, traditional prototyping techniques have
used methods such as turning, milling, drilling, carving
or moulding of materials such as metal, wood, clay,
foam, paper and plaster to create a prototype. Each of
these traditional prototyping techniques is more time-
consuming than 3D printing, often requiring extensive
planning and a longer development cycle, although
offering improved surface finish and finer detail compared
to 3D printing.
While rapid 3D prototyping is used to quickly create
functional prototypes for testing and design refinement,
production techniques typically prioritise precision,
material properties and cost-effectiveness for large-scale
manufacturing, potentially shifting to methods like
injection moulding or CNC machining.

A4.1.4
ISO/ASTM 52900:2021 Additive manufacturing - General
principles - Fundamentals and vocabulary outlines seven
categories of Additive Manufacturing (AM) processes:
« binder jetting
» material jetting
« sheet lamination
« material extrusion (ME)
« powder bed fusion (PBF)
« vat photopolymerisation
« directed energy deposition (DED)
Binder Jetting (BJ)
Binder Jetting is an additive manufacturing process
where a liquid binding agent is selectively deposited
onto a powder bed, fusing the powder particles together
to create a 3D object, see Figure A4.1.4a. It's a powder-
based technique that doesn’t require melting or sintering
during the initial build process, allowing for a wide range
of materials like metals, ceramics and sand to be used.
Material Jetting (M])
Material Jetting is an additive manufacturing process
that creates 3D objects by depositing droplets of liquid
photopolymer material onto a build platform and then
curing them with UV light, Figure A4.1.4b. It is similar
to inkjet printing, but instead of ink, it uses materials that
solidify under UV light to build 3D parts layer by layer.
Sheet Lamination in the context of additive manufacturing,
is a process where 3D objects are built by layering and
bonding thin sheets of material, see Figure A4.1.4c. These
sheets are then cut, shaped, and bonded together using
methods like adhesives, heat, or pressure. The final shape
is achieved by removing excess material through cutting,
often with a laser, or CNC machining.
Material Extrusion (ME)
Material Extrusion (ME) in the context of additive
manufacturing refers to a technique where a thermoplastic
material, typically in filament or pellet form, is heated and
then extruded through a nozzle moving along X and Y
axes to be selectively deposited on a build platform to
solidify and cool. The platform subsequently moves in
the Z direction in preparation for the next layer to be
deposited. In this manner a 3D object is gradually formed
layer-by-layer, see Figure A4.1.4d.
This process is also known as fused deposition modelling
(FDM) or fused filament fabrication (FFF) when not
specifically referring to the Stratasys-patented FDM
process. The materialis selectively deposited on a platform,
solidifying as it cools, and the process is repeated until the
desired shape is achieved.
Overhanging or complex geometries may require support
structures, which are also printed using the same or a
different material and can be either break-away or water-
soluble.
Material Extrusion has been used with medical grade
thermoplastics for the fabrication of tissue and bone
scaffolds.
Powder Bed Fusion (PBF)
There are four main types of Powder Bed Fusion (PBF)
processes:
Multi Jet Fusion (M]JF)
« Selective Laser Melting (SLM)
« Electron Beam Melting (EBM)
+ Selective Laser Sintering (SLS)
While they each represent a 3D printing process that uses
a heat source to fuse powdered material together, layer
by layer, to create a solid object they differ in the energy
source used and the materials to which they are applied,
see Figure A4.1.4e.
A CAD model is sliced into layers, and a machine then
spreads a thin layer of powder, followed by selectively
melting or sintering the powder using a laser or electron
beam according to the layer’s shape. This process repeats
until the entire object is formed.
Multi Jet Fusion (MJF) is a 3D printing process developed
by Hewlett-Packard which uses inkjet technology to
precisely deposit fusing and detailing agents onto a
powder bed of Nylon. The Nylon is then fused together
using heat from Infra-red lamps to create parts layer by
layer. MJF is known for rapidly producing strong, detailed,
and accurate parts, especially suitable for functional
prototypes and end-use parts.
Selective Laser Melting (SLM) is a metal additive
manufacturing process that uses a high-power laser to
fuse metallic powder in an inert atmosphere, layer by layer,
creating 3D objects. In this process the laser selectively
melts and fuses the powder based on a digital 3D model
resulting in fully dense, functional metal parts. SLM is
related to a similar 3D printing method known as Direct
Metal Laser Sintering (DMLS), although DMLS may only
result in partial powder melting.
Electron Beam Melting (EBM) isa 3D printing technology
that uses a high-energy electron beam to melt and fuse
metallic powders in a vacuum to prevent oxidation and
contamination of the metal powder. As for the other
Powder Bed Fusion processes, 3D objects are built layer by
layer. The EBM process is particularly useful for creating
high-strength, dense parts with complex geometries from
reactive metals like titanium. It also boasts faster build
speeds for larger parts compared with Selective Laser
Melting, and typically operates at a higher temperature
of up to 2000°C. This high temperature environment can
lead to reduced residual stresses and may eliminate the
need for post-build heat treatments.
Electron Beam Melting is typically more expensive
and less common than Selective Laser Melting due to
its specialised requirements of a vacuum chamber and
sophisticated electron beam systems, but it excels in
particular applications, notably with reactive metals.
Selective Laser Sintering (SLS): is usually associated with
the 3D printing of polymers using a laser to selectively fuse
or sinter powder particles together and is similar to Direct
Metal Laser Sintering (DMLS) of metals, although DMLS
may only result in partial melting of metal powder.
'Vat photopolymerisation (VPP)
Vat photopolymerisation is a 3D printing process where
a liquid photopolymer resin in a vat is selectively cured
by light, layer by layer, to build a 3D object, see Figure
A4.1.4f. This process, also known as stereolithography
apparatus (SLA) or resin 3D printing, involves using a
light source, such as a UV laser or a digital light projector
(DLP), to solidify the resin according to a digital design.
Vat temperatures can vary from 25” to 60°C depending on
the material.
This process is suitable for the 3D printing of high viscosity
thermoplastics difficult to process by FDM.
Directed energy deposition (DED) is a 3D printing
process that builds parts layer by layer by melting a
feedstock material (wire or powder) with a focused energy
source, such as a laser or electron beam, as it’s deposited
onto a substrate. This process can be used to create new
parts or to add material to existing components for repair
and modification. It is suitable for larger parts and repair
applications.

A4.1.5
irst introduced in 2013, 4D printing represents an
extension of 3D printing where the printed object
incorporates smart materials that can react to stimuli like
temperature, light or moisture to produce a shape change
over time, see Figure A4.1.5a.
Similar to the Shape Memory Alloys (SMAs), Shape
Memory Polymers (SMPs) exhibit a shape memory
effect (SME) by which they can return to their original
shape after being deformed, triggered by a change in
temperature, light or other stimuli.
SMPs are designed with a specific molecular structure that
includes both a ‘permanent’ structure and a ‘temporary’
structure, in which the permanent structure defines the
original printed shape, and the temporary structure,
(formed during the programming phase) is created by
deformation into a new shape.
During the programming phase, the SMP is heated above
its glass transition temperature (Tg), making the polymer
chains more flexible. The SMP is then deformed while
exposed to the stimulus into a ‘temporary’ shape and
cooled down, effectively ‘freezing’ the temporary shape as
the material solidifies.
When the deformed SMP is again heated above the Tg,
the temporary structure begins to relax and regain its
originally printed shape, as illustrated in Figure A4.1.5b.
Applicable to both SMP’s and SMASs, 4D printing « Smart textiles - wrinkle resistant clothing, security
can use most of the 3D printing technologies like tags
Stereolithography, fused deposition modelling (FDM)
or inkjet printing. The choice of technology depends on Some examples of SMPs include:
factors like the desired material, resolution and complexity
of the final structure. Ethylene-vinyl acetate (EVA)
The technology holds promise for a number of Polyurethanes
application:
Poly(e-caprolactone) (PCL)
« ‘soft’ robotics - gripping systems, mode-shifting
actuators Polylactic acid (PLA):
« Aeronautics - deployable structures such as antennas Thermoplastic polyurethane (TPU):
and solar arrays,
(Meth)acrylates
« Construction - smart sealants, vibration control, noise
reduction Polyethylene terephthalate (PET)
« Biomedical fields - catheters, stents, drug delivery Polyether-ether-ketone (PEEK)
Shape Memory Polymer Hybrids (SMHs) are a type of
shape memory material that combines conventional
materials with shape memory materials to create a material
with tailored thermomechanical properties. They are a
more accessible and flexible approach to shape memory
materials, offering a wider range of functionalities and
potential applications compared to traditional shape
memory polymers (SMPs) alone.
Shape memory polymer (SMP) hybrids are used in
biomedical applications like drug delivery and in
deployable structures such as sutures and vascular stents.
An example is the hybrid PU/PMAA composite which
combines the SMP properties of polyurethane (PU) and the
biodegradable and pH responsiveness of poly(methacrylic
acid) (PMAA). The shape memory effect of this hybrid is
activated by both thermal and pH/water stimuli.
PU/PMAA can be 3D printed, particularly using
techniques like fused deposition modelling (FDM).

A4.1.6
5D dimensional additive manufacturing, also known as
5D printing, isanadvanced form ofadditive manufacturing
that builds upon 3D printing by incorporating two
additional axes of movement, enabling the creation of
complex curved shapes and improved material properties.
It achieves this by utilising five degrees of freedom: three
linear (X, Y, Z) and two rotational (around the print head
and/or print bed).
Unlike 3D printing which typically uses flat layers, 5D
printing can produce objects with curved layers, resulting
in parts with enhanced strength and more intricate
designs.
A technology that incorporates smart materials into 5D
printing, combining the concepts of 4D and 5D printing,
has been called 6D printing. This emerging field aims to
create complex objects that can change shape, properties,
and behaviour over time in response to external stimuli
but is still largely in the research and development phase

A4.1.7
Subtractive prototyping is a relatively new term used to
cover operations using traditional manufacturing tools
such as; drills, lathes, routers and milling machines. The
term has been introduced as a contrast to the newer
additive technologies that have arisen.
Starting with a block or blank, subtractive manufacturing
continues to remove material until the part is finished.
These machines and processes may also be automated.
The major advantages of subtractive technologies are:
« high degrees of accuracy
« very smooth surface finishes
suitable for a wide range of materials
« smaller per part costs when mass produced
» larger dimensions and greater range of materials than
additive prototyping methods.
Subtractive or machining processes strike some difficulties
with varying thickness sections, deep slots, square
corners and features such as undercuts, As parts become
more complex so too do tool paths. The number of tool
changes also increases. Although issues such as these are
resolvable, they require great skill, creativity and problem-
solving abilities. From designing tool paths and machining
strategies to operating and monitoring the process,
machining is the work of experienced craftsmen.
Cutting encompasses a wide variety of operations
used to reduce a material usually in sheet form into
smaller or differently shaped products. The selection
of the appropriate method depends on a number of
considerations, such as material hardness and thickness,
accuracy required, production speed and material type
(wood, plastic, metal, ceramic, etc.).
Cutting processes include:
mechanical cutting: hacksaw, bandsaw, circular saw
abrasive cutting: cut-off wheel, water-jet
thermal processes: oxy-acetylene, plasma, laser, hot
wire
electrical discharge: wire cutting.
Oxy-acetylene cutting: is a
process in which acetylene
and oxygen supplied
from separate pressurised
containers are supplied via
rubber hoses to a cutting
torch were the gases
are mixed to produce
a flame that burns at
about 3,500°C (6,330°F).
Oxy-acetylene cutting
finds extensive use in the
cutting of steel due to its
portability and versatility.
Figure A4.1.7a shows the
equipment secured to a
trolley allowing movement
to wherever it is needed.
 flashback arrestor is usually installed on both lines at the
connection to the torch and the gas supply. The flashback
arrestor prevents shockwaves passing along the hoses to
the gas cylinder where an explosive decomposition might
otherwise occur. The flame of the cutting torch is used
to preheat the workpiece. Once the starting position for
cutting has been heated to a cherry red colour the oxygen
blast trigger on the top of the torch is depressed and an
increased flow of oxygen is preduced changing the flame
from neutral to oxidising. In this condition the steel
surface is oxidised, and since the melting temperature of
iron oxide is only about half that of the steel, liquid iron
oxide forms and is blown away by the flame allowing
continued oxidation and cutting. Figure A4.1.7b shows
manual oxy-acetylene cutting of a 25 mm steel plate.
Advantages associated with oxy-acetylene cutting include
portability of equipment, large pieces can be cut in place,
and the ability to cut curved and thick sections. It is also
faster than mechanical cutting methods with a lower initial
capital equipment cost when compared with mechanical
tools.
Disadvantages of oxy-acetylene cutting include the
creation of a heat affected zone and the limited number
of materials that may be cut. Metals such as aluminium
and stainless steel form tenacious oxides that prevent
further oxidation are thus unsuitable for cutting using this
method.
Plasma cutting: utilises an electric arc to ionise a high
velocity gas stream by stripping electrons from gas
atoms, creating a mixture of positively charged ions, free
electrons and neutral gas particles to form a plasma of high
temperature (~25000°C) and high kinetic energy. The gas
employed may be either an inert gas or, more commonly
today, compressed air. The material to be cut needs to be
electrically conductive, meaning it is restricted to metal
cutting applications. The temperature of the plasma melts
the metal, with the molten material being blown away by
the plasma stream, see Figure A4.1.7c.
Laser cutting: uses a high power laser directed towards
the flat sheet surfaces to be cut. A high quality surface
finish is generated as the laser vaporises the sheet material.
Laser cutters may also be used on piping or structural
shapes. Through the adjustment of power levels, laser
cutters may also be used for; 2D cutting, welding, boring
and engraving. A variety of laser cutters are employed
depending on their application.
Examples include:
» CO, - boring and engraving
« Nd - high energy, low repetitious cutting
« Nd-YAG -very high power engraving or boring.
When repetitive, complex or long production runs are
required, a computer may be employed to direct the
laser, as shown in Figure A4.1.7d. Laser cutting offers the
advantages of fine, accurate cutting with smooth surfaces
and lower energy requirements than plasma cutting. Kerf
widths as narrow as 0.10mm are possible resulting in a
lower heat affected zone. Metal thicknesses are, however,
limited in all but the newer higher energy units of 6,000
watts.
Electrical discharge (wire cutting): Electrical discharge
wire cutting, also known as wire electrical discharge
machining (WEDM), is a precise machining process that
uses high-frequency, rapidly pulsed electrical discharges
from a wire of brass or copper approximately 0.25 mm
thick to erode and cut conductive materials. The specific
current and voltage levels vary depending on the desired
cutting speed, precision and material being cut. Deionised
water is commonly used as a dielectric fluid to create and
maintain the spark gap, flush away eroded material and
prevent short circuits.
The wire never physically touches the workpiece, instead
sparks generated between the wire and the material erode
it away, allowing for intricate and accurate cuts. This
technique allows the precise cutting of hardened materials
of up to 300 mm thick which are difficult to cut with other
methods, see Figure A4.1.7e.
achining is a subtraction process in which a cutting
tool moves across a work piece to remove material from
the surface, as illustrated in Figure A 4.1.7f, The cutting
tool was at one time high speed steel ground to produce
a cutting edge. Today the cutting tool typically consists of
a tungsten carbide or ceramic insert that is mechanically
fixed to the tool holder. The cutting tip is shaped such that
the tip has a slicing action through the workpiece and the
material being removed (known as the swarf) is turned
back on itself and fractures. It is important that the swarf
is removed cleanly away from the cutting tip into a waiting
scrap pan in order to avoid damage to the workpiece and
cutting tool. This is particularly the case in CNC machining
operations, where stoppages to clean away entangled
swarf would significantly reduce efficiency. Machined
surfaces may be produced to very fine tolerances. Skilled
workers are required to operate machines or technicians
to monitor automated processes. In both cases expensive
capital equipment is required
‘The process of turning is a machining operation performed
on a Lathe in which material is clamped securely between
a headstock and tailstock and rotated (turned) at high
speed as a cutting tool, fixed in a movable tool post, is
moved along the surface, see Figure A4.1.7g.
Turning operations are ideally suited to the production
of symmetrical shapes and have traditionally been
performed under the manual control of a machinist,
although today repetitive operations are often automated
through Computer Numerical Control (CNC) systems.
While high speed steel cutting tips are still used, Ceramic-
metallic composites such as cemented carbide (Typically
tungsten carbide) along with cubic boron nitride (cNB),
Silicon nitride (Si,N,), alumina (ALO,) and diamond
tipped inserts are available in a number of different shapes
best suited to various operations such as rough turning,
finishing, chamfering, thread cutting and facing, see
Figure A 4.1.7h.
These materials provide high hardness and wear resistance
although accompanied by brittleness and relatively high cost.
he process of milling is a machining operation using a
rotating cutter head, around which a number of cutting
inserts are fixed. These remove material from a workpiece
secured to a bed. The cutting bed moves the workpiece
through the cutting area allowing the rotating cutter head
to remove material from the surface as shown in Figure
A417).
Using CNC (Computer Numerical Control) pre-
programmed with computer instructions often written
in G-code, precise movement of the cutting tool
and the workpiece enable the machine to perform
complex machining operations with high accuracy and
repeatability. The CNC system manages the movement of
the milling head along multiple axes (X, Y, and Z being the
most common) and controls the spindle speed and other
parameters like feed rate.
Such systems are now appearing outside of industrial
workshops. The CEREC (short for Chairside Economical
Restoration of Esthetic Ceramics or CEramic
REConstruction) is a benchtop milling machine used
in dental surgeries to create individualised ceramic
restorations (Figure A 4.1.7k).
Using an intraoral camera the dentist makes a digital
image of the region requiring an insert or crown and a
CAD/CAM image is created and sent to the CEREC miller,
in which a silicate ceramic block, colour matched to the
patient’s tooth, is waiting to be milled. The ceramic blocks
used in this process (Figure A 4.1.7k) are adhesively fixed
to a platform allowing them to be clamped within the
milling chamber.
Abrading involves the physical wearing away of surfaces
by means of rubbing, friction or erosion. The harder the material the more resistant is to abrasion. 
Processes employed depend on the material to be
abraded.
Abrasives may be:
« rigid tools such as files and abrasive wheels
+ suspended in a liquid e.g. water jet cutting
« carried by a jet of compressed air as in sand steel shot
or iron grit blasting.
Abrading may be used to remove material, shape an
article, smooth or add texture to a surface. Materials
suitable for abrasion include: ceramics, concrete, glass,
polymers, metals and composites. In Figure A4.1.71 a
process of abrasion of a steel workpiece using a grinder
can be seen.
brasive Water-jet cutting can be used to cut a wide
variety of materials and involves the direction of a thin
stream of water, onto the surface to be cut. Soft materials
such as paper, textiles, foam, rubber and leather can be
cut with only a focused stream of high pressure water,
however harder materials such as metal and stone require
the addition of fine abrasive particles such as garnet to the
water jet nozzle at ultra-high pressures. Pressures of 280
to 690 MPa and water velocities of between 400 m/s and
600 m/s are used depending on the material to be cut.
A schematic of a water jet cutting head is shown in Figure
A 4.1.7m, in which the high pressure water stream can be
seen to pass through a high hardness abrasion resistant
jewel orifice, where it is focused into a narrow stream,
before the garnet abrasive is introduced through a mixing
tube.
The operation of water-jet cutting is often undertaken
with the workpiece submerged in a water tank (Figure
A 4.1.7n), particularly when large items are involved, to
reduce noise and spray.

A4.1.8
Bending is a commonly employed metal forming
technique that uses force to deform a material past its
yield point, typically sheet metal or a plate, along a straight
axis, creating a desired angle or curve. It's a crucial process
in manufacturing, especially for creating parts with
angular or curved shapes. Depending on the material,
size and shape to be bent, the process may be performed
hot or cold and undertaken manually or with the aid of a
hydraulic press.
‘The manufacture of a horseshoe often involves bending
as a key step, especially in traditional forging methods.
Farriers shape horseshoes by heating the metal (usually
steel or aluminum) and then bending it into the desired
‘U’ shape using tools like hammers and anvils. This
bending process can be done hot (heated in a forge) or
cold (without heating) depending on the technique and
the desired outcome.
Bending is widely used in various industries, including
automotive, aerospace, and electronics, for creating
structural components, enclosures, brackets, and more.
Other materials such as timber can also be formed by a
process known as ‘steam’ bending where steam is used
to soften wood fiberes, allowing them to be shaped into
curves without cracking or breaking. The wood is then
secured in the desired form until it has cooled and dried.
PRESS-FORMING
Press forming (also known as die forming) is a
metalworking process where a flat sheet of material, like
metal, is formed into a desired three-dimensional shape
by applying pressure. This is typically achieved by using a
hydraulic press that forces the material between two dies,
one of which is a punch that shapes the material to match
the die's contours as illustrated in Figure A 4.1.8a. The
process is widely used across various industries, including
automotive, home appliances, and electronics to create a
range of parts and products.
Advantages of Press forming:
« Highly repeatable
+ Rapid production
« Cost effective
Disadvantages of Press forming
« High tooling cost limits process to high volumes
Deep-drawing: Is a specific form of die forming and
involves the creation of a three-dimensional shape where
the depth of the part exceeds its diameter or minimum
width, resulting in a deeper, often hollow, shape, see Figure
A 4.1.8b. Components produced by deep drawing may
require processing through multiple dies to reach their
final form because of limitations in how much a sheet of
metal can be deformed in a single step without causing
defects like wrinkling or tearing. By carefully controlling
material flow and stress distribution, using a combination
of techniques like blank holders, lubrication, controlling
punch and die radii, multistage deep drawing allows for
gradual deformation and stress management, preventing
localised thinning and instability.
Hydroforming: is a specialised type of press forming
that utilises high-pressure hydraulic fluid to shape metal,
whereas traditional press forming relies on mechanical
force. Both processes involve shaping material by pressing
it into a die, but the method of applying the force differs.
Hydroforming uses fluid pressure, while other press
forming methods use mechanical pressure.
It is particularly useful for creating complex shapes with
high strength and reduced weight. This process can
be applied to various metals like steel, aluminum and copper.
Casting involves the transformation of materials into a
liquid form before pouring (casting) them into a mould
or pre-prepared cavity for solidification or setting. It is
one of the oldest processing techniques known to man
and many variations exist depending on the material to be
cast, the number of items to be produced, the complexity
and surface finish required.
These considerations will determine the equipment
required to transform the material to be cast into a liquid
and the type of mould to be used. Metals, food (jelly,
chocolate), plastics (resins), composites (metal matrix
composites) and ceramics (glass and concrete) are all
suitable materials to cast. Neither timber nor textiles lend
themselves to this approach.
The advantages of casting include:
« Large or small products are possible
« Applicable to small or large product runs
+ Complex and hollow shapes with smooth section
changes possible
« Castings are produced on a batch basis and chemistry
can be generally made to order.
There are a number of commonly used casting methods
employed such as die casting, sand casting and precision
casting (lost wax casting).
Two forms of die-casting are performed and are named
after the method by which molten metal enters the die:
« gravity die-casting
« pressure die-casting.
Both forms of die-casting use a permanent mould
consisting of two platens, with the product shape and
runner system machined into the surface. The die-casting
mould is typically machined from tool steel for wear
resistance and strength. Metals suited for die-casting are
those with good fluidity and low melting points such as
lead, tin, magnesium, zinc, aluminium and their alloys.
In gravity die-casting (sometimes called permanent mould
casting), metal is poured from a ladle into the sprue and
the molten metal fills the mould under gravity. Because of
the relative simplicity of the process, even after the cost of
the dies has been taken into account, gravity die-casting
is relatively inexpensive. Automation can of course be
included when high volume production is required. The
casting of lead sinkers once performed by many anglers at
home is an example of gravity die-casting.
Figure A 4.1.8c shows ‘tin’ soldiers, approx. 65 mm high,
being cast in antique moulds from the early 20th century.
The two mould halves are clamped together, and the
molten metal alloy of tin and lead, (approx. 300°C ) is
poured by hand from a ladle into the mould. Once the
metal has solidified, the mould is opened. Sprues, (pouring
channels) and extraneous flash seen in the bottom image
are eventually removed during the fettling process.
Advantages:
« Improved casting tolerances
« Fewer defects than sand casting
« Low cost when a high number of castings are
required
« Improved surface finish compared to sand casting
« Higher production rate compared to sand casting
» Lowerspace requirements compared with sand casting.
Improved mechanical properties resulting from faster
cooling rate and smaller grain size compared to sand
casting.
Disadvantages:
+ Casting weight and size limited
« Mould Design changes are costly
« High initial die costs although cheaper than pressure
die casting
« Limited range of metals can be cast compared with
sand casting in order to avoid early deterioration of
the mould surface.
Articles produced by gravity die casting include exhaust
manifolds and train brake discs.
Notwithstanding the limitations listed above, from the
early 19th Century through to mid 20th Century the
conventional practice for the mass production of steel
was for the production of steel ingots. These ingots were
poured (teemed) from large ladles with a typical capacity
of 100 to 200 tonnes into a series of grey cast iron ingot
moulds of 6 to 13 tonnes capacity, see Figure A4.1.8d.
The high thermal conductivity of the iron mould allowed
rapid extraction of heat from the steel surface while
contraction of the steel ingot and the tapered mould wall
allowed later separation of the ingot and mould.
Once the steel has sufficiently cooled within the mould, the moulds were ‘stripped’ from the ingots and the ingots
placed into gas fired ‘soaking pits’ until hot enough for rolling. The mould is then allowed to cool before being reused.
The ingot casting process has today been replaced by
continuous casting techniques (shown in Figure A4.1.8e),
for the mass production of standard steel grades. This
shift was driven by the efficiency and cost-effectiveness
of continuous casting, which involves feeding molten
steel into a mould in a continuous process to create a long
strand that is then cut into slabs or ‘blooms’
Invented by the German engineer Siegfried Junghans in the early 1930s and later developed by the American Irving Rossi it became the dominant steel production method in the mid-1950s and today accounts for over 96% of global steel production.
In continuous casting, molten steel is poured from a ladle into an intermediate container known as a tundish. The tundish serves as the place where slag and impurities are removed from the melt and from there it enters an open ended water-cooled reciprocating copper mould.
As long as the tundish is supplied with sufficient metal to continue casting into the mould, casting can continue uninterrupted. Therefore as the main ladle is emptied a new ladle is usually waiting to take its place to continue a
sequence.
In modern steelmaking the continuous casting process may also be integrated with metal rolling. This secondary rolling operation is a forming process that changes the shape of the metal it processes.
Continuous casting is very economical in that the rolling
mill can be fed directly from the continuously cast metal casting strand with the rate of the rolling operation carefully synchronised with the speed of the continuous metal casting, effectively combining the two operations as one.
Continuous casting offers a number of advantages over the traditional ingot route:
« Faster production
« Improved surface finish
« Reduced space requirements
« Reduction in scrap metal losses
« Reduction in energy requirements.
Pressure die-casting (also known as injection die-casting):
involves the rapid injection of non-ferrous metals under
high pressure into a closed die cavity and is particularly
suited to high volume production.
Die-casting has an enormous range of applications, with
many manufacturing industries relying on one of the
many variations of pressure die-casting that include hot-
chamber and cold-chamber die-casting.
Hot chamber machines utilise a curved conduit known
as a ‘gooseneck’ that connects a molten metal holding pot
to the injection system, as shown in Figure A 4.1.8f. As
the casting process begins a piston raises a plunger at the
bottom of the gooseneck creating a vacuum and drawing
molten metal into the gooseneck, filling it. The piston
then rapidly lowers the plunger forcing the molten metal
under pressure from the gooseneck into the die where it
solidifies.
In order to avoid damage to the gooseneck and injection
system, this method is generally confined to use with
the lower melting point metals such as tin (232°C), lead
(328°C) and zinc (420°C) and some of the lower melting
point Mg-Al system alloys such as AZ91D and AM50.
Cold chamber machines are particularly useful for the
casting of non-ferrous metals with a melting point too
high for the hot chamber process, such as magnesium
(650°C), aluminium (660°C) and copper (1085°C). This
process utilises a separate furnace where the metal is
melted and held at the appropriate casting temperature, as
shown in Figure A 4.1.8g.
In the cold chamber die casting process, molten metal is
transferred from a furnace into a ‘shot’ chamber sleeve. A
plunger within this sleeve is raised to create a vacuum that
syphons a measured amount of molten metal from the
furnace into the chamber. Subsequently, the molten metal
is injected at high pressure into the die.
Pressure is maintained until the casting has solidified and
cooled sufficiently so that the mould can be opened and
the cast part ejected using injection pins. The die is then
closed ready for the next cycle.
In order to avoid problems of trapped air within the die
that might adversely affect the quality of the casting two
techniques for air removal are used. These involve either
the use of a vacuum to evacuate air prior to metal injection,
or the provision of several small channels leading from the
die cavity to the outside known as vents. The vents allow
the displacement of air from the die as the metal enters.
Unlike other casting processes die-casting does not
incorporate a metal reservoir such as a riser to feed
the cooling and contracting metal. Metal shrinkage is
controlled by the injection pressure and appropriate
design of the metal flow.
Pressure die casting is used extensively for products such
as automotive trim, handles, iron sole plates, disc drive
chassis, musical instruments, rotors for electrical motors,
kitchen faucets and steering wheels.
Sand casting: involves the production of a temporary
mould produced containing a cavity of the shape desired.
As the name implies, these moulds are manufactured from
sand and are typically produced in a facility dedicated to
producing metal castings called a foundry.
The traditional and still most common method of
producing a sand mould is to pour a mixture of sand and
various binding agents into boxes containing a half pattern
of the casting. Following the setting of the moulding sand,
the pattern is removed and the cavity surface is painted
with a refractory slurry that acts as a barrier to prevent
scouring as the molten metal enters.
The final mould is subsequently constructed by joining
the individual parts, see Figure A4.1.8h. The top half of
the mould is known as the cope while the bottom half is
the drag. Intricate and hollow shapes are created by the
incorporation of appropriately formed internal shapes, also
made of sand, known as cores. Provision is made during
the moulding process for metal entry into the mould and
the feeding’ of the casting during solidification.
Today, 3D printing is also being used to produce moulds
using additive manufacturing techniques like binder
jetting (BJ) or selective laser sintering (SLS). These
methods create moulds by selectively binding sand
particles together, layer by layer, according to a digital 3D
model. This process allows for complex geometries and
intricate details that are difficult or impossible to achieve
with traditional methods.
Figure A 4.1.8i shows molten metal being cast from a
small ladle into the sprue of a sand mould. In this figure
a number of weights can be seen on top of the mould,
positioned to ensure that hydrostatic pressure does not
result in the cope lifting.
Castings solidify first at those positions where heat
removal is greatest. These positions generally correspond
with areas where the molten metal comes into contact with
the mould wall. Solidification therefore usually proceeds
from the outer surface toward the casting’s interior.
As the casting solidifies, it contracts and the molten centre
‘feeds’ the growing front of solidifying metal. If extra
molten metal is not supplied, the last part of the cavity
to solidify will have insufficient metal to be completely
solid and a ‘shrinkage’ cavity will result. To aveid this
situation reservoirs of molten metal are often provided
by the incorporation of ‘risers’ into the mould. Risers are
designed to be the last part of the casting to solidify and
act to feed molten metal into the casting.
‘The heat from the casting gradually degrades the binding
agent and the mould crumbles allowing easy removal of
the casting called ‘shakeout’ or ‘knock-out’ ready for the
removal of risers and gating systems known as fettling,
see Figure A 4.1.8j.
If correctly designed, the casting is free of shrinkage (it
is ‘'sound’) and any shrinkage is contained completely
within the risers, which are removed during the fettling’
or clean-up stage.
Because these castings can be relatively large the grain-
size can also be large leading to lower strength. Grain-
size is controlled by adjusting casting temperature and
solidification rate and with the addition of grain-refining
elements along with later heat treatments.
Remember, as a general rule, slow cooling of molten metals
produces larger grains than rapid cooling which produces
grains of a smaller size. Manipulation of grain size may
also take place after cooling through such heat treatment
operations as annealing, quenching or normalising.
Precision casting: also known as investment casting (IC)
and lost wax casting refers to a metal casting process where
a wax pattern is created, coated with a refractory material
to form a mould, and then the wax is melted out, leaving a
cavity into which molten metal is poured.
Lost wax casting, represents a subset of sand casting
and based on archaeological evidence, has its origins in
Mesopotamia around 3000-4000 BCE. It appears that
the process was in use in most civilisations of the ancient
world, mainly for artistic purposes, from jewellery to
statuary both large and small. The bronze statue of Perseus
holding the head of Medusa produced by Benvenuto
Cellini in 1554, and the aluminium statue of Eros in
London'’s Piccadilly Circus cast by Alfred Gilbert in 1893
(Figure A4.1.8k), are famous examples of the process.
While the lost wax process continued to be used in artistic
circles because of the intricate detail, excellent surface
finish and near net shape characteristics it offers, the
mainstream engineering use of the process did not begin
until the Second World War. Atthattime, capacity restraints
in machining led to the use of the lost wax process because
of its ability to produce compenents that did not require
machining. Following the war, the development of the jet
engine created an increasing demand for the process in
the production of high precision castings of refractory
metals used for components such as gas turbine blades.
The lost wax casting process shown in Figure A 4.1.8],
has traditionally used a permanent mould or die often
made of metal to produce an expendable wax pattern.
The pattern may then be incorporated into an assembly
with similar patterns and attached to a wax feeding
system. The whole assembly is subsequently dipped into
a ceramic slurry to build up a coating. Fine sand is then
added to form a strong shell and the assembly allowed to
dry. The dry assembly is then placed in a mould flask and
surrounded by a coarser sand/slurry mixture and dried.
This coarser sand/slurry backing is technically known as
the investment. After setting, the mould is gently heated
to melt the wax pattern leaving a smooth mould cavity
behind. In the final stage of production, metal is cast into
the mould to form the part.
Controlled breakdown of the sand/binder mixture from
exposure to the temperature of the solidifying metal
allows the solidified casting to be easily removed from
the mould for fettling. During the fettling operation the
casting is separated from the metal feeder system of sprue,
runners and in-gates.
Advantages:
« Near net shape
« Freedom of design
« No joint lines present
« Allowance for pattern removal not required
« Production of thin sections and sharp detail
« High dimensional tolerances and surface finish
possible
« No limit to cast metal composition, subject to
restrictions of melting fluidity and soundness.
Disadvantages:
Labour intensive
» Casting size limited
« High initial die costs
« Thick sections not generally cast
» Die design changes may be costly.
Today, the lost wax process also finds use in the production
of hip and knee prosthetics, dental implants, valves and
auto parts along with traditional roles in the production of
items such as jewellery and instrument keys.
Increasingly 3D printing technologies such as
Stereolithography (SLA) and selective laser sintering
(SLS), are being used in the production of the sacrificial
wax patterns, offering to eliminate some of the previous
drawbacks of the precision casting process associated with
the high initial costs of the die and design changes.
The use of 3D printing technologies also allows the wax
‘tree’ or sprue system to be formed as an integrated unit,
eliminating the need to assemble each component onto a
central wax sprue to form the tree.
Advantages of 3D printing in precision casting include:
« Complex geometries: SLA can produce intricate
and complex geometries that would be difficult or
impossible to create through traditional assembly
methods.
« Reduced labour: Eliminating assembly steps saves
time and labor costs.
+ Improved consistency: An integrated SLA-printed
tree ensures consistent part placement and sprue
dimensions, leading to more predictable casting
results.
« Faster prototyping and production: SLA allows for
rapid prototyping and faster turn-around times for
casting.
The process of moulding relates primarily to the production
of polymer and polymer composite products, from simple
containers to complex composite structures, A wide range
of materials and processes are encompassed by the term
moulding from lay-up to Vacuum Thermoform Moulding,
each with its own characteristics and applications.
Lay-up: is an open moulding process that involves the
creation of a composite of textile encased in a resin
typically consisting of a styrenated unsaturated polyester.
This process can be undertaken as a fully manual process
known as ‘hand lay-up’ or semi-automated as ‘spray lay-up'.
‘The vacuum bagging process is a variant of the hand lay-
up process in which pressure is applied to the laminates
to ensure a bond. Vacuum bagging uses atmospheric
pressure to help compress composite layers that have been
laid-up in a mould or over a former. Once the laminate is
in position the whole assembly is enclosed in a vacuum
bag. Once sealed, the air is evacuated from the bag by a
vacuum pump to create a vacuum, resulting in external air
pressure forcing the laminate into the mould, see Figure
A4.1.8n.
Products made using this process include furniture,
boat hulls, wind turbine blades and structural panels or
decorative skins.
Hand lay-up involves the manual laying of woven mats
of a textile such as fibre glass, carbon fibre, Kevlar, etc.
into a mould that has been pre-coated with a cold curing
resin using a paint brush or roller. Once in place the mats
are coated with another layer of resin so that the textile
mat is both impregnated by and encased in the resin
as shown in Figure A4.1.8m. Because the hand lay-up
process can involve multiple layers of material combined
using adhesives, heat and or pressure to form a new
composite material with improved properties, it is a type
of laminating process.
Hand lay-up offers more control over fiber orientation
and thickness, potentially leading to higher quality and
performance, but it is also more labor-intensive and time-
consuming.
Advantages of vacuum bagging include:
+ Good surface finish
» Low moulding costs
« Large products are possible
« Produces a superior bond compared to other
processes.
« Since air pressure is evenly distributed around the
mould, the mould can be of simple lightweight
design.
«Allows the elimination of clamps and staples to
hold laminates in position and results in improved
distribution of pressure or a good uniformity of lay-
up.
In the spray-up process the textile fibres are cut up and
mixed with the resin before being sprayed together into
the mould by a semi-automated spray system, as illustrated
in Figure A 4.1.80. Spray lay-up is faster and more cost-
effective than hand lay-up, making it suitable for larger
parts and higher production volumes, but it can result in
a less uniform product with random fibre orientation.
Products such as boats, water tanks, shower units and
surfboards can be manufactured using both hand lay-up
and spray lay-up techniques.
Resin transfer moulding (RTM): involves the use of
fibre preforms that are positioned within a closed mould.
A liquid resin is then pumped (or transferred) into the
mould under pressure. Following curing of the resin,
the composite is removed and the mould prepared with
another preform. The bismaleimide (BMI) thermosetting
composites used in the F22 raptor and F35 Joint Strike
Fighter are manufactured using the RTM process.
Extrusion moulding: involves forcing a heated plastic
through a die to produce long continuous items like
pipes, tubes or rods and is distinct from extrusion blow
moulding.
Pultrusion: is similar to extrusion with the exception that
the material to be formed is pulled through a shaped dye
rather than being pushed. The name is a contraction of
pull and extrusion. Mats of glass, polymer or carbon fibre
stored on reels are pulled through a thermosetting resin
impregnation bath and then guided through preformer
dies to align the fibres and remove excess resin. In the
final stage of production the preform is pulled through
heated dies to cure the resin. The cured fibre reinforced
composite exiting the heated die in a continuous length is
then cut to requirements.
This process produces long continuous lengths of constant
cross-section with high stiffness and flexural strength. It is
often used in the production of supports for optical fibre
cable, see Figure A4.1.8p.
Compression Moulding: represents a generic term for
a number of processes that rely on the application of
pressure, often in concert with elevated temperature, to
distribute polymer over a mould to form a product. One
example of compression moulding is shown in Figure
A 4.1.8q and is known as Matched Die Compression Moulding. In this process, two metal platens one fixed and the other movable, are used to form the mould. These platens can generally be internally heated and/or cooled.
When the dieis closeda fixed gap exists between the platens in the shape of the product to be formed. A polymer, in sheet or granular form, is placed in the open mould and the movable platen or plug positioned on top. The mould is then heated while the top plug applies pressure. The polymer is heated to above its glass transition temperature
where it softens and flows to fill the mould cavity. Heat and pressure are maintained until the polymer has cured, at which time heating is stopped and the mould cooled before the pressure is released and the plug removed to allow removal of the product.
Matched-die compression moulding is primarily used for thermosetting polymers, though it can also be used for specific thermoplastic applications, particularly those involving fiber-reinforced composites.
Injection moulding is a manufacturing process where molten thermoplastic is injected under pressure into a closed mould, as illustrated in Figure A4.1.8r. The process involves three main parts: the injection unit, the mould and the clamping unit. Plastic pellets are fed into the injection unit via a hopper and melted by a heating unit. The molten plastic is then injected into the mould, where it cools and solidifies. Finally, the finished product is ejected from the mould after the clamps are released.
The injection unit consists of the polymer processing section where polymer granules are heated to a molten state ready for injection, while the clamping unit comprises a permanent mould consisting of two platens,
one of which is fixed and the other moveable.
Products are manufactured one after another in an automated cycle in which polymer granules loaded into the feed hopper are fed under gravity into the barrel containing a screw system. Heaters on the outer surface of the barrel melt the polymer as it is carried by the screw towards the end, where a non-return valve allows molten material to build-up increasing hydraulic back-pressure against the screw. Sensors monitor this pressure and when sufficient molten material has accumulated, rotation of the screw is stopped and the hydraulic cylinder and ram move the screw forward and the accumulated molten polymer is pushed through the nozzle and into the sprue, injecting the polymer into the die. The molten polymer passes along a runner system machined into the platen surface before passing into the product. The runner system is therefore located at the die parting line.
The mould remains closed until the polymer has cooled sufficiently, following which the movable platen is retracted, and the part formed is ejected. Although the initial capital cost of machinery and dies is high, surface finishes are excellent, intricate detail can be achieved and large production runs lower costs per unit.
The process is similar to injection die casting of metals, although in the case of injection moulding the material being injected under high pressure is molten plastic (both thermosets and thermoplastics).
Parts from small to moderate size (up to ~16 kg) including intricate parts with fine detail and thin walls can be produced by injection moulding, and include consumer electronics, automotive parts, hobby products, including toys like LEGO, model kits and parts for model trains and airplanes as illustrated by the model plane kit, shown in
Figure A4.1.8s.
Rotational moulding: also known as roto-moulding is
used to produce hollow plastic products and involves the
rotation or spinning of the mould in two axes to allow
centrifugal forces to force the molten plastic against the
inner surface of the mould as shown in Figure A 4.1.8t.
The process involves four basic stages.
« Fill mould chamber with the required amount of the
thermoplastic plastic powder.
« Heat mould chamber until the plastic is molten.
+ Spin the mould chamber to evenly distribute the
molten plastic within the mould.
» Cool the chamber to solidify the plastic.
The mould rotation is then stopped, the product removed
and the process repeated. Typically, this process is
employed in the manufacture of seamless water tanks
Blow moulding: is used where a large hollow object such as
a bottle is required. The process involves the confinement
of a semi-molten hollow plastic tube (parison) within a
mould such that one end of the tube is closed and the other
open. While the plastic tube is still at a temperature above
its glass transition temperature, (softening temperature),
compressed air is blown into the open end of the tube,
expanding it until it conforms to the shape of the mould.
The three variants of blow moulding used are known as:
« extrusion blow moulding
« injection blow moulding
= stretch blow moulding.
Extrusion blow moulding is illustrated in Figure A 4.1.8u
and involves the use of extruded plastic to create hollow,
three-dimensional objects like bottles. This is done by
inflating a parison (a hollow plastic tube) inside a mould.
The process begins with molten plastic being extruded
through a nozzle to form a hollow tube, called a parison.
This parison is then clamped between two halves of
a mould, which closes around it. Compressed air is
then injected into the parison, inflating it to fill the
mould cavity. Finally, the plastic cools and hardens
and the mould opens to release the finished part.
Advantages:
+ low capital costs,
« low tooling costs,
» high production rate and
« viability of small production runs possible.
Disadvantages:
« high scrap rate
« poor surface finish
« limited wall thickness control
+ scrap needs to be trimmed from container.
Injection blow moulding is a two stage process and
is illustrated in Figure A 4.1.8v. The first process stage
involves the initial injection of molten plastic into a mould
containing a parison core. When solidified, the mould
is opened and the core with the surrounding parison
attached is transferred to the blow mould while still hot,
ready for the second process stage. The blow mould is
closed around the core and parison and compressed air
blown into the core. The core then acts as a blowing rod,
forcing the plastic parison out against the mould wall.
When cooled, the blow mould opens and the product is
ejected ready for the next cycle.
The injection moulding process is also known as transfer
blow moulding and is the slowest of the blow moulding
processes but can be used to produce complex shapes and
is often used in the production of small plastic medicine
bottles.
Advantages:
high production rates
« little or no scrap to be trimmed
« superior transparency compared to extrusion blow
moulding
+ Injection moulding of the parison also allows more
control of shape and size.
Disadvantages:
« two moulds are needed
« High initial capital cost.
Stretch blow moulding involves the positioning of a
preform body consisting of a pre-shaped plastic tube (see
Figure A 4.1.8w) into a mould where the preform is heated
to the glass transition temperature.
Once the glass transition temperature is reached, a
blowing rod is inserted through the top and compressed
air is blown through the end of the rod stretching the
parison longitudinally while air is also ejected from the
surface of the blowing tube, pushing the parison radially
towards the sides of the watercooled mould where it cools,
as illustrated in Figure A 4.1.8x. The stretching operation
aligns the molecular chains, increasing the strength of
the walls while also improving solvent resistance and
transparency.
Widely used for PET, PE and PVC bottles. The high wall-
strength produced allows the containment of pressures
associated with carbonated drinks.
Advantages:
low scrap
« low labour costs
« good surface finish
« High production rates
« variety of parison blanks available ‘off-the-shelf”
Disadvantages:
« need to carefully control the process due to the thin walls.
Vacuum Thermoform moulding: involves the heating of a
thermoplastic sheet or film until it has softened sufficiently
that it can be made to conform to a shape under vacuum,
pressure or direct mechanical force as illustrated in Figure
A48z
There are a number of thermoforming techniques and
the process finds wide use in the production of products
varying in size from cups and trays to vehicle dash panels
and plastic pallets.
Advantages:
short lead times
low tooling costs
low forming pressures
Injection moulded quality
high production rates
availability of cheap wooden patterns.
Disadvantages:
« Poor dimensional accuracy
« inability to form holes and threads.

A4.1.9
PERMANENT JOINING TECHNIQUES
Permanent joining techniques are those that, once
formed, cannot easily be separated without damage.
Because of this, such items are not easily repaired or
modified, tending instead to be discarded in favour of a
new item exhibiting the functions and capacities desired.
Permanent joining techniques include the use of adhesives,
and a variety of welding techniques.
Adhesion: the joining of two surfaces by adhesion is
generally considered to be attained through the interaction
of van der Waals forces or polar forces. The process
typically involves the introduction of a thin layer of liquid
polymer (the adhesive) between the surfaces to be joined.
The adhesive undergoes polymerisation in-situ.
Adhesives, depending on their bonding action, may allow
for the separation of parts with little or no damage to the
original surfaces. They are commonly used to join thin
or dissimilar materials and don’t employ heat which may
cause distortion or damage. Some adhesives, composed
of metallic flakes, are even electrically conductive, cure at
room temperature and cause no damage to heat sensitive
componentry.
Common adhesives include the following: + Tensol cement - used for bonding a wide range of
thermoplastics such as acrylic.
« Polyvinyl Acetate (PVA) - a water-based adhesive
suitable for bonding porous materials such as wood, « Cyanoacrylate (superglue) - represents a special type
paper or cloth. of acrylic resin. Fast curing through a reaction with
moisture. Joints must be close fitting.
« Epoxy Resin - Can be used to join most materials and
is available in one or two-part systems consisting of a « Polyurethane glue - available in one or two-part
resin and a hardener. systems. Fast curing. Often used with primers. Forms
a flexible bond.
« Contact adhesive - applied to both surfaces to be
bonded to form a strong bond. Commonly consists of « Anaerobic acrylic - cures when air is excluded often
polychloroprene (Neoprene). used as thread locking compounds.
« Cascamite - a powdered adhesive based on urea- « Toughened acrylic - fast curing high strength adhesive
formaldehyde that is mixed with water before use. usually as a two-part system.
Fusion welding: « Electric-resistance welding
Fusion welding involves the heating of the surfaces to be « Electrode arc (includes MMAW, MIG and TIG)
joined until melting and intermixing at the joint occurs to
form a ‘metallurgical’ bond. This heat may be provided by « Laser and Electron beam welding.
a variety of methods including:
Additional metal may be introduced to the joint using
« Gas (also knownas oxy welding) either a consumable electrode, as in manual metal arc
welding (MMAW) or by a separate filler wire that is fed
« Aluminothermic welding
into the heat source as in metal inert gas (MIG) welding
or tungsten inert gas (TIG) welding.
In order to maintain a clean surface and reduce the chances
of oxides forming during fusion welding a protective slag
or inert gas shield is generally provided. The joint formed
is usually as strong as the parent material and is probably
the most widely used method of fabrication in use today.
Solid state welding: represents those processes where
joining is accomplished without melting. The bond is
created through atomic diffusion at the interface under
the influence of heat and pressure.
Forge welding
« Diffusion welding
« Ultrasonic welding
+ Explosive welding
« Rotational friction welding (RFW)
« Linear friction welding (LFW)
« Friction stir welding (FSW)
Solid-state welding techniques offer several advantages
over fusion welding, primarily due to the absence of
melting and solidification during the joining process. This
leads to reduced distortion, fewer metallurgical problems
and the ability to join dissimilar metals with greater ease.
Soldering and brazing: are similar operations and involve
the joining of two or more parts by melting a filler metal
with a heat source. The molten filler metal flows into the
joint and on solidifying, holds the parts together. The heat
source for soldering is usually a hot ‘iron’ while brazing is
accomplished with the aid of an oxyacetylene torch orasa
bulk process in a furnace.
The primary difference between the soldering and brazing
processes is the temperature at which the filler metal (often
an alloy) solidifies. The dividing line between the processes
is somewhat unclear however, as some definitions quote
a liquidus temperature (where the alloy is completely
liquid) of approximately 450°C, while others a solidus
(where the alloy is completely solid) of 500°C. For metal
alloys there is generally a mushy zone in which the metal
is in a partially liquid/solid state, while for pure metals the
solidus and liquidus are the same temperature.
Perhaps because of this, terminology is sometimes
confusing, as with the process of ‘silver soldering, which
usesasilver alloy filler metal with a liquidus of about 450°C
and which is considered to be a brazing operation.
Regardless of the exact division, soldering, shown in
Figure A 4.1.9¢, uses filler metals with a solidification
temperatures <500°C (e.g. Pb, Sn, Sb etc.) while brazing
generally uses alloys with solidification temperatures
500°C (e.g. Cu, Ni etc.) alloys
A section through a brazed joint between a copper
tube and copper expansion bellows performed by silver
soldering is shown in Figure A 4.1.9d.
The primary requirement of both processes is that the
surfaces to be joined are free of oxides, so that the filler
metal can ‘wet’ the surface. Fluxes designed to remove
oxides from the surface are used to provide a clean joint.
Both brazing and soldering rely on forming a metallurgical
bond through the dissolution of a small amount of the
base metal at the interface, resulting in the formation of
intermetallic compounds. This is achieved by heating the
joint to a temperature below the melting point of the base
metals but high enough to melt the filler metal. The filler
metal then flows into the joint via capillary action, creating
a strong bond through the metallurgical interaction with
the base metal.
Surface Mounting Technology (SMT): is a specialised
soldering technology and the dominant method for
assembling electronic components onto Printed Circuit
Boards (PCBs) used today. SMT replaces the older
technology where component leads were passed through
drilled holes in the board and soldered to pads on the
opposite side.
Using SMT, solder paste consisting of a mixture of solder
and flux, is precisely applied to designated pads on the PCB,
where the surface mount components will be positioned.
Components are placed directly onto the board’s surface
following which the PCB is passed through a reflow oven.
The heat in the oven melts the solder paste, creating a
strong electrical and mechanical connection between
the component leads and the PCB pads. The assembly
is then cooled, solidifying the solder and completing the
connection, see Figure A 4.1.9.
The use of surface mounting technology has provided
improvements over the older ‘through hole’ method by
allowing higher component density, smaller and lighter
components, faster automated assembly times, reduced
manufacturing costs and the mounting of components on
both sides of the PCB, increasing design flexibility.
Hot riveting: Now largely consigned to history, hot
riveting was the primary method of joining iron and steel
structural plates used in the construction of buildings,
ships, bridges and pressure vessels for over a century,
before being superseded in the 1950s by welding and
structural bolts.
The rivet consisted of a pin (shank) with a head at one
end and was originally made from wrought iron and later
from low carbon steel. These materials are relatively soft
with good ductility and did not require tempering after
cooling.
While riveting was an efficient operation it was labour
intensive, typically requiring a skilled team of four
workers. Operations required the rivets to be heated in
a small furnace close to the installation site, usually with
the pin shank down, in order to keep the head cooler
and retain some strength during installation. When an
appropriate temperature was reached the rivet would be
removed from the heat with tongs by one member of the
team, and thrown a short distance to be caught by another
team member with a funnel, who would position the rivet
in the hole prepared in the plates to be joined, before
getting ready to catch the next rivet, A third team member
would force the rivet head against the plate surface while
a partner on the other side deformed the end with a
pneumatic hammer. As the rivet cooled and contracted a
strong, permanent, watertight joint was formed.
A wide range of different factory head styles was used depending
on the application. In the example provided, rivets with a
countersunk factory head were used, while a tool with a
concave end was used to produce a rounded shop head.
The slag stringers of the wrought iron allow the flow lines
to be seen in the rivets. Note the tight joint formed by the
rivets between the two wrought iron plates.
The riveted joint is a physical connection with no melting
or metallurgical bond being formed between any of the
components. While providing some strength in tension,
the riveted joint was strongest in shear, arising from both
the rivets themselves and the frictional contact that was
achieved between the plates joined. Rivets were typically
installed in groups with each rivet separated from its
neighbour by a distance of 3-5 shank diameters. 
Advantages:
Variety of heads styles available
» Can be used to join dissimilar metals
« Can join metals of different thickness
« Strong, stable, watertight joint can be formed
« Does not produce adverse microstructural changes in
the materials joined.
Disadvantages:
« Access to both sides usually required
« Skilled team required compared to a single worker
needed to install bolts
« Must be drilled out to remove compared to bolts,
which are easily removed
« Not strong under tensile loading or cyclic stresses that
could lead to fatigue.
Metal Stitching: is not typically used as an initial
fabrication technique but was rather developed in the mid
1930s as a cold repair method to mend cracked or broken
cast iron, steel, bronze or other metal components without
welding. It involves inserting metal pins and locks into
carefully prepared holes along the fracture line, creating a
strong, pressure-tight repair, see Figure A4.1.h.
Metal stitching is particularly useful for parts where
welding is problematic or undesirable, such as cast
iron. Because it is a cold repair technique the original
metallurgy is maintained while distortion and stresses
associated with the heat of welding is avoided, while at the
same time offering restoration of the original components
strength.
Using a jig to guide placement, groups of holes are first
drilled across the fracture line up to 90% of a castings wall
thickness. These holes are then joined using sizing blades
to create a serrated aperture before a preformed nickel
alloy key is inserted using an interference fit. A second
group of holes is then drilled along the line of the fracture
and again joined and these holes filled with studs, each
stud biting into its predecessor, resulting in a pressure-
tight join and restoring rigidity to the casting. The studs
and keys are then ground down flush, completing the
repair.
Metal stitching has commonly found use in the
repair of engine blocks, bearing saddles, water jackets,
turbine casings and various other components in heavy
machinery.
Weaving: is the process of passing fibres or threads of
material under and over each other, (most often at right
angles, called warp and weft) in an interlacing fashion
typically undertaken on a loom. Weaving appears in
Egyptian writings and biblical references and there are
thoughts that forms of weaving existed in the Palaeolithic
era.
Similar to knitting, weaving has traditionally been
associated with the textile industry, however today a much
broader range of materials are woven.
These include:
« metals-steel, gold, silver, platinum, copper, titanium,
aluminium (woven wire jewellery, screens, fence
panels, sieves, catalyst gauze)
« ceramics (fibreglass mat, carbon fibre)
« polymer (outdoor furniture, mats, safety barriers,
body armour, sail cloth, vascular grafts)
« composites (woven fabrics within resin matrices)
« timber (furniture, privacy screens and shades).
The protective body armour chain-mail (also spelt maille)
was woven from small interlinked wire rings as early as
the 4th century BCE extending to the 16th century in
Europe, see Figure A 4.1.9i. It continued to be used in
parts of Asia, Africa and the Middle East as late as the 18th
century. The term ‘chain-mail’ itselfis a more recent term,
coming into common use in the 18th century; historically,
it was simply referred to as ‘mail’ or ‘maille’
Today woven wire mesh is used in a wide array of
applications across various industries. It is used for sieving,
filtration, separation, security (machine guards and
fencing), ventilation, animal flooring and architectural
design. An example of a fly screen woven from polymer-
coated steel wire is shown in Figure A 4.1.9j.
A more modern version of woven body armour is the
Kevlar vest, Patented by the DuPont Chemist Stephanie
Kwolek in 1966, Kevlar® entered commercial production in
1971 as a replacement for steel reinforcement tyre cord in
racing cars. The material’s unique properties, particularly
its high tensile strength-to-weight and ability to dissipate
energy from impacts meant that it quickly became famous
for its penetration resistance in body armour. Kevlar’s
heat resisting properties have seen it used for fire and
welding protective clothing and parts such as brake pads,
gaskets and hoses. The yarn is typically golden yellow
in appearance (as shown in Figure A 4.1.9k), although
treatment for UV protection produces a black yarn.
Although she did not benefit financially from the patent,
Kwolek’s groundbreaking work on Kevlar earned her
numerous accolades, including induction into the US
National Inventors Hall of Fame and solidified her place
as a pioneer in materials science.
Vascular grafts, crafted from materials like Dacron (PET)
or e-PTFE (Gore-Tex), are used to create a scaffold that
mimics the structure and function of blood vessels, see
Figure A 4.1.91 These grafts are often woven, braided, or
knitted and serve as a framework for tissue ingrowth and
regeneration, ultimately restoring blood flow in damaged
or diseased vessels.
TEMPORARY JOINING TECHNIQUES
Fastening or joining materials mechanically through the
use of screws, rivets, bolts, pins, clips, nails, press-studs
and snaps are considered temporary joining techniques.
Blind rivets: (also known as breakstem rivets and Pop
rivets) are typically used for the joining of sheet metal and
consist of a solid stem or mandrel terminating in a button
like head. A short tubular body or shaft with a flanged end
is positioned on the stem just below the head, as may be
seen in Figure A 4.1.9m.
This joining method is not intended for structural
applications,
Installation is achieved by inserting the shaft into a
prepared hole in the sheet metal being joined so that the
head of the mandrel protrudes from the other side. A
setting tool is then positioned over the mandrel stem such
that the flanged head of the shaft is held against the sheet
metal surface. With the aid of the setting tool, the mandrel
is pulled back through the tubular shaft. As this takes place,
the button head of the mandrel compresses and deforms
the end of the shaft between itself and the sheet metal
surface. Compression continues until the tensile strength
of the mandrel is exceeded and it fractures, leaving behind
a completed joint. Advantages:
Heat not involved
Single operator installation
« Can be installed from one side
Installation does not require high level of skill.
Disadvantages:
« Can only be used on relatively thin material
« Surfaces have to be perforated with a drill.
Bolts, Screws and nails: each of these rely on physical
interaction with the material being joined.
Nails remain in place by the pressure of the surrounding
fibres. Removal requires frictional contact forces to be
overcome.
Screws derive their fixation strength from a mechanical
bond in which pull-out requires the shearing of material
contained between the threads. As a screw is driven into
an assembly, the joint is placed in compression when the
head of the screw meets the surface.
Bolts act to hold two or more parts of an assembly together
by applying a clamping force between the bolt head and
nut. As the nut is tightened the bolt is placed in tension
and the joint assembly in compression. Bolts are available
in a variety of materials and strength levels and head
designs to match the situation. 

A4.1.10
Finishing techniques are often directed toward the
protection of surfaces by creating a barrier against
environmental factors that might degrade the visual
appearance and serviceability of a product. In addition,
finishing techniques can add to the visual aesthetic by
offering choice of texture and colour.
Anodising: anodising is an electrochemical process that
thickens the natural oxide layer on a metal surface, most
commonly aluminum, to produce a hard, durable and
corrosion-resistant finish. It is often used for decorative
purposes as the oxide layer is porous, which allows it to
absorb dyes and pigments, enabling a wide range of colors
and finishes. Unlike painting, anodising preserves the
metallic appearance of the metal.
