Electro-plating: is a general description of a process
that involves depositing a metal coating onto a substrate
using electrolysis that can offer a range of finishes, from
shiny chrome to matte silver, and can even be textured or
polished to achieve specific visual effects.
Common metals used in electroplating include gold,
silver, copper, nickel, chromium, zinc, tin and platinum.
Recently, advancements have made it possible to
electroplate certain plastics after they are prepared with a
conductive coating.
Electroplating enhances a product’s aesthetics by providing
a decorative metallic finish that can increase visual appeal
and add a sense of luxury or quality. Decorative coatings
are typically only 5 - 15 microns (um) (0.005 - 0.015mm)
thick, while coatings applied for corrosion resistance may
be up to 0.5mm.
Galvanising: is a process of applying a protective zinc
coating to iron or steel to prevent rusting. This coating
acts as a barrier, preventing corrosive substances from
reaching the underlying metal.
Hot-dip galvanising is the most common and recognisable
galvanising process, and involves immersing steel in a
bath of molten zinc, maintained at a temperature of 445-
465°C. The process creates a durable coating that can be
matte grey in colour or exhibit a crystalline pattern, called
‘spangles. The spangle pattern is a natural outcome of the
zinc solidifying and forming distinct crystals as it cools
following removal from the bath, see Figure A 4.1.10a.
Spangle size and appearance are influenced by the cooling
rate, zinc bath composition and steel type. Coating
thicknesses are typically between 50 and 150 microns
(um), (0.05 to 0.15 mm).
Other galvanising techniques include:
« Electro-galvanising is a specific type of an electro-
plating process in which an electrical current is used
to deposit zinc on the steel surface while submerged
in a zinc salt solution. This process produces a thin,
smooth, uniform and bright silvery-white coating
making it suitable for applications where aesthetics
are important, such as in appliances and automotive
parts. Coating thicknesses are typically between 5 to 20
microns (um), (0.005 to 0.020 mm),
388
« Sherardising is a thermal diffusion process in which
steel parts are heated in a rotating drum with zinc dust
and an inert filler. The zinc vaporises and diffuses into
the steel, creating a zine-iron alloy coating. Sherardised
surfaces generally have a matte gray appearance and
are generally thicker and more corrosion-resistant
than electroplated zinc, but thinner than hot-dip
galvanized coating.
Sherardising is often used to galvanise high-strength steel
parts where hydrogen embrittlement is a concern. Unlike
hot-dip galvanising and electro-galvanising, Sherardising
avoids the risk of hydrogen absorption during the coating
process, making it a safer option for such steels.
Powder coating: involves spraying a dry powder typically
made from thermosetting polymers like epoxy, polyester
or acrylic, combined with pigments onto the surface of
metal parts, creating a durable, impact-resistant layer,
often used on outdoor furniture, automotive and industrial
components, Powder coatings are typically cured by
baking in an oven at a specific temperature (typically
between 177°C and 204°C) for a set time (around 20
minutes). This heat causes the powder particles to melt,
flow together, and fuse into a continuous film, creating
a visually appealing, durable and permanent finish. By
carefully mixing pigments into the resin-based powder a
wide range of colours can be supplied.
Ceramic coating: is a liquid-based treatment, typically
composed of silica dioxide (8i0,) and other materials,
applied to vehicles forming a durable, glass-like layer
for paint protection, enhancing gloss and hydrophobic
properties. Ceramic coatings are often cured through air
drying at room temperature (around 21°C) and under
controlled humidity conditions allowing the solvents to
evaporate and triggering a chemical reaction that bonds
the coating to the substrate.
While the colour of the object remains the same before
and after the ceramic coating is applied, they can enhance
the appearance of the underlying color by creating a
glossy, hydrophobic surface that reflects light and makes
the color appear richer and more vibrant.
Sealants: such as oils, wax and silicone enhance materials
by providing a protective barrier against moisture, UV
damage and other environmental factors by filling pores
and creating a smooth, water-repellent surface preventing
damage to timber-based products such as swelling,
warping and rot.
Sealants can also add or deepen colour and add a glossy
or satin finish to surfaces, enhancing their natural beauty,
while improving maintainability by making surfaces
easier to clean.
Polishing: can remove imperfections, reduce friction and
improve resistance to wear, corrosion and environmental
damage resulting in a visually appealing product that lasts
longer and is easier to clean and maintain.
By creatingasmooth less porous surfaceon metal, polishing
reduces the areas where corrosive elements can attach and
react, while existing oxidation and contaminants that can
initiate or accelerate corrosion are removed.

A4.1.11
reating complex components and products often
requires a combination of different manufacturing
techniques to achieve the desired shape, functionality and
final appearance.
The production of complex products such as a hybrid
car engine for example requires the integration of a wide
combination of manufacturing techniques. It combines an
internal combustion engine (requiring casting, machining
and assembly) with an electric motor (requiring winding,
assembly, and potentially additive manufacturing
for some components) and a battery pack (requiring
electrochemical processes and assembly).
The manufacturing techniques for complex consumer
products such as mobile phones are chosen to balance
factors like cost, precision, efficiency, desired aesthetic
and functional qualities of the device. These techniques
include CNC machining or 3D printing of a precise
casing, injection moulding for complex plastic parts such
as buttons and housing, surface mounting (SMT) for
circuit board assembly, and various bonding and joining
methods for integrating components.
Electric lamps, like incandescent bulbs, LED lamps,
and fluorescent lamps, utilise various manufacturing
techniques including glassblowing, filament winding,
metal stamping and semiconductor fabrication with the
specific processes varying depending on the lamp type.
Depending on the style, the housings can be manufactured
using a variety of techniques, including CNC machining,
sheet metal fabrication and additive manufacturing (3D
printing).

B4.1.1
B4.1.2
CRAFT PRODUCTION
Before the Industrial Revolution, manufacturing, known
as craft production, was centred around local craftsmen
in medieval villages. Young apprentices, often serving
for as long as seven years, supported small, sometimes
home-based, workshops driven by skilled artisans. Most
manufacturing took place in cottages in rural villages.
Craft production refers to the process of creating goods by
hand or with minimal mechanisation, often emphasising
quality, uniqueness and traditional techniques. It involves
the creation of single, unique, and individual products,
generally requiring manual skills, where the skill of the
artisan determines the quality of one-off, unique pieces.
While slow, this method was often locally based and
allowed clients to converse directly with the manufacturer
to produce customised items. Because a craftsman’s
manufacturing involves a project from start to finish,
these skilled artisans had a variety of skills.
If machines were used, they were typically small and
generally operated by hand, animal power, wind or
running water. While windmills and water wheels had
been around for centuries, energy for craft production
was mostly provided by human muscle. In some instances,
earlier Roman-design water wheels were employed. Hand-
operated bellows forced air into the blacksmith’s forge (as
seen in Figure B 4.1.1a). Hand- or foot-operated spinning
wheels enabled the spinning of plant and animal fibres into
thread, while others wove the yarn into cloth. Timber was
fashioned with basic hand tools. Leather goods, clothing,
silverware and food products were all made similarly. Iron
and other metals were either hand forged or cast. Little
changed from one generation to the next as sons adopted
the role their fathers had previously occupied.
At this point in history, there was little need to generate
a surplus other than insurance against crop failure or
inclement weather. Neighbouring villages often produced
the same crops, so trade, while local, was still minimal.
The town’s craftsmen included bakers, blacksmiths
and carpenters. Villages, to a large degree, were self-
sufficient with products made from local materials,
manufactured on-site and repaired within the town. Much
of the machinery in use changed little until the Industrial
Revolution.
As transport routes were developed and improved, village
craftsmen such as weavers started to buy additional raw
materials to be spun into thread or woven into cloth and
sold to the emerging local merchants. Cottage labourers
also sold their work to merchants in an organised fashion
in a process that has been termed ‘proto-industrialisation’
Craft products soon became tradeable commodities, and
cottage workers who owned their equipment dealt directly
with the local market or used merchants to trade farther
afield. The opportunities of increased trade promised by
improved transport contributed to the need for changes
in the means of production. The main change involved
the mechanisation of processing and the centralisation of
production in large factories located in the cities. Many
smaller, more diversified operations (cottage industries)
were unable to compete and were closed. These changes
led to significant population shifts from rural areas to
cities.
Today, master craftsmen produce individually designed
pieces tailored to client requests. These pieces exhibit both
high quality and cost. Examples of this approach include:
haute couture (as seen in Figure B4.1.1b), jewellery and
customised furniture. One-off production may be used
for prototype development and testing before moving to
larger scales of production.
In developing economies, one-off production remains
viable due to the low cost of labour and the lack of
distribution infrastructure. In these situations, locally
based artisans meet the needs of local clients with
appropriate technologies and available materials,
Many countries, including Korea, Japan and China,
have recognised the importance of traditional crafts and
have designated highly skilled artisans as ‘living cultural
treasures’ to preserve these skills and technologies for
future generations. These artisans are often seen as living
embodiments of cultural heritage and play a crucial
role in passing down knowledge and skills through
apprenticeships.
These traditional skills are used for building maintenance,
repair and restoration work. An example of this can be
seen in the rebuilding of St George’s Hall roof at Windsor
Castle and, more recently, the reconstruction of Notre
Dame Cathedral after fire ravaged the structure.
raft production offers a range of distinctive advantages,
particularly in terms of product quality and individuality.
Each item is typically characterised by a high degree
of craftsmanship and uniqueness, often reflecting the
artisan’s personal skill and creative expression. This allows
for a high level of customisation, making it well-suited
to bespoke or specialised markets. Additionally, craft
production generally requires less energy than industrial
manufacturing processes and frequently incorporates
sustainable or locally sourced materials, aligning with
environmentally conscious practices.
Craft production is also associated with several inherent
limitations. The processesinvolvedare typicallylabour-and
time-intensive, which contribute to elevated production
costs and, consequently, higher market prices. The limited scale of output further constrains its competitiveness,
particularly when compared with the efficiency and cost-
effectiveness of mass production systems. As a result, craft
production tends to occupy a niche segment of the market,
appealing primarily to consumers who prioritise artisanal
value, exclusivity and sustainability over affordability and
scalability.
MECHANISED PRODUCTION
The centrepiece of the Industrial Revolution was the
availability of new sources of power and the accompanying
mechanisation. Before the Industrial Revolution, humans
or animals supplied power. James Watt's improved
Newcomen engine proved an effective and reliable power
source. Steam engines soon found applications in fields
other than mining, boosting productivity in the booming
textiles industry. While waterpower was still used as a
power source, steam engines provided more flexibility and
allowed industry to spread across the countryside, away
from sources of running water.
Mechanised production involves the introduction of
machinery into a process with the intent of removing
human and animal power sources. In its simplest form,
it replaces hand-crafting operations with machine-based
tooling. Machines replacing manual labour still require
human input to operate, control and often guide the
machines.
Mechanisation offers a range of significant advantages
within industrial and manufacturing contexts. It facilitates
the production of goods at reduced cost, with increased
speed, and with enhanced uniformity and quality. By
automating processes, mechanisation diminishes reliance
on manual labour, thereby enabling continuous operation
and significantly boosting productivity, particularly in
large-scale production environments. Moreover, the
mechanisation of hazardous tasks contributes to improved
occupational health and safety by mitigating workers’
exposure to dangerous conditions.
However, these benefits are accompanied by notable
drawbacks. The initial capital outlay required for
mechanised systems—encompassing plant infrastructure
and specialised equipment—is substantial. Additionally,
the transition to mechanised production often results
in workforce displacement, as traditional labour roles
are supplanted by a smaller cohort of technically skilled
personnel. Mechanisation may also constrain operational
flexibility, given that machines are typically designed for
specific, predefined tasks. Furthermore, the increased
energy demands associated with mechanised systems can
exacerbate environmental impacts, raising concerns about
sustainability and resource consumption.
PropucTion SYSTEMS
AUTOMATED PRODUCTION
First mechanisation, then automation, continued the
evolution of factories. Productivity, quality control and
costs per unit part all continued to improve.
Automation involves the replacement of human labour
with mechanical, electronic or computer-controlled
machinery processes and systems that operate
automatically or independently. Coincidental with this
came reductions in the workforce and human physical
labour. Employment patterns changed from large numbers
of unskilled workers to fewer numbers of machine
tenders. Machine, process and system control also evolved
from mechanical to electromechanical. Reliable electrical
power now became an imperative to the smooth operation
of equipment.
Such contrel has its origins in the work of Joseph Marie
Jacquard (1752-1834), a French inventor who developed
a system of punch cards (Figure B4.1.1¢) that controlled
the operation of a loom for the weaving of textiles with
complex patterns.
The emergence of CAD/CAM has had a major impact
on manufacturing by automating many processes and
procedures. Although capital investments may be high,
they have significantly reduced costs and improved both
productivity and quality.
CAM is an acronym for computer-aided (assisted)
manufacturing. Referring to a range of computer-
based technologies that assist designers, engineers and
manufacturers. These technologies are applied not only
to assist with product design, but with manufacturing,
stocktaking and life cycle assessment, among others.
CNC stands for computer numerical control. CNC
systems employ machine tools that read computer code
to control their operations. CNC machines often increase
productivity and flexibility.
Automation brings with it faster production times, reduced
costs, finer tolerances, automated mass customisation and
near-perfect repeatability. Faster production rates also give
manufacturers the opportunity to change production runs
more often, allowing for a diversity of product types.
Some typical applications where CAD/CAM may enhance
automation include:
scheduling
« quality control and inspection
« process planning, efficient work and effective
design of complex dies and moulds for casting, with
preprogrammed shrinkage allowances
« programming for NC, CNC, and industrial robots to
automate processes, including holding or transporting
components,
The advantages of automated production and assembly
include high efficiency and speed, resulting in a more
reliable product and consistently higher build quality.
Continuous, 24/7 operation of equipment with minimal
downtime greatly increases output. Reduction in human
error ensures uniformity in products, improving overall
quality control and reduced labour costs.
Machining is a process of subtraction. Pieces machined
may be produced to very fine tolerances. Skilled workers
are required to operate machines, or technicians to
monitor automated processes. In both cases, expensive
capital equipment is required. There is also a reduction in
the risk of workplace injuries because machines conduct
the dangerous tasks. Finally, the ease of scaleability of
production and the opportunity of automated systems
394
to track performance, detect issues early and optimise
processing is improved.
Automation in manufacturing and production systems
offers several notable advantages. Automated assembly
processes enhance consistency and reliability, resulting
in products of uniformly high quality. These systems
reduce variability inherent in manual operations,
thereby improving overall build precision. In the context
of machining—an inherently subtractive process—
automation enables the production of components with
exceptionally fine tolerances, supporting applications that
demand high dimensional accuracy.
Despite these benefits, automation also presents certain
challenges. The operation and oversight of automated
systems requires a workforce with specialised technical
expertise, including skilled machinists and process control
technicians. Moreover, the implementation of automation
necessitates significant capital investment in advanced
machinery, control systems and supporting infrastructure.
These financial and human resource demands can pose
barriers to entry, particularly for smaller enterprises or
those operating in low-margin sectors.
Assembly line production
Assembly-line production is an industrial process used
in mass production. It involves the movement of the
product to be manufactured from station to station, often
using conveyor belts or other transportation systems.
The product moves past workers, tooling and machinery
stations until the product is complete. Often referred to as
‘reductionism, assembly line workers are typically involved
in work reduced to simple, repetitive tasks that had little
association with the production of a final product. People
worked to a ‘rhythm’ but were involved in tasks devoid of
any interest, thought or problem solving.
With production methods and operations standardised,
workers were often deskilled compared to the craftsmen
of previous times. As mechanisation increased, many of
the tasks simply became machine-tending.
While Henry Ford was not the first to use the assembly line,
he is credited with refining the controlled movement of
standardised parts and revolutionising mass production.
Labelled as ‘Fordism, this allowed the mass production of
vehicles of a consistent quality. Consumers at this point,
however, had their purchase options limited as defined by
Ford'’s legendary quote, “My customers can also get any
colour, as long as it’s black.” Figure B4.1.1d shows workers
on a Ford assembly line in 1930.
Assembly line production offers a range of strategic
advantages that have made it a cornerstone of modern
manufacturing. One of its most significant benefits is
the high level of efficiency and throughput it enables.
By breaking down the production process into smaller,
repetitive tasks, assembly lines allow for faster production
rates and shorter cycle times. This streamlined approach
ensures that each worker or machine focuses on a specific
function, thereby reducing delays and increasing overall
productivity. Another key advantage is the reduction in
unit costs. Assembly line systems are designed to support
mass production, which facilitates economies of scale. As
the volume of output increases, the cost per unit decreases,
making products more affordable for consumers and more
profitable for manufacturers. This cost-effectiveness is a
major driver behind the widespread adoption of assembly
line techniques in industries ranging from automotive to
electronics.
Consistency and standardisation are also key features of
assembly line production. Because each product follows
the same sequence of operations, manufacturers can
ensure uniform quality and reliability across large batches.
This level of consistency is important in sectors where
precision and dependability are critical. In addition,
assembly lines reduce the need for highly skilled labour.
Workers are trained to perform specific, narrowly defined
tasks. The training process is also simplified and less
time-consuming. This not only lowers labour costs but
also makes it easier to scale up operations quickly when
demand increases.
Finally, the linear and organised structure of assembly
lines contributes to improved workflow and operational
oversight. The clear sequence of tasks facilitates better
coordination, easier supervision and more efficient use
of space and resources. This structured environment
supports continuous improvement and helps identify
bottlenecks or inefficiencies in the production process.
PropucTioN SYSTEMS
Despite its many advantages, assembly line production
also presents several notable disadvantages that can
impact both operational efficiency and workforce well-
being. One of the primary challenges is the high initial
capital investment required. Establishing an assembly
line involves significant expenditure on the design,
installation and maintenance of specialised equipment
and infrastructure, Another limitation is the inherent
lack of flexibility in assembly line systems. These lines are
typically configured for the production of specific items,
making it difficult and costly to adapt them to new product
designs or to accommodate customisation. This rigidity
can hinder innovation and responsiveness to changing
market demands, placing firms at a disadvantage in
industries where product variation and rapid development
are key competitive factors.
The nature of assembly line work can also lead to monotony
and worker dissatisfaction. Employees are often assigned
to perform tepetitive tasks for extended periods, which
can result in boredom, reduced engagement and a sense
of alienation from the final product, Over time, this can
contribute to higher rates of absenteeism, staff turnover
and decreased overall morale, posing challenges for
workforce management and retention.
Moreover, assembly lines are particularly vulnerable to
disruptions. Because the production process is highly
interdependent, a malfunction or delay at any single
point can halt the entire line. This susceptibility to
breakdowns or supply chain interruptions can lead to
significant inefficiencies, production delays and increased
operational costs.
HYBRID PRODUCTION
A hybrid production system represents a strategic
integration of craft, mechanised and automated production
methods, designed to leverage the unique strengths of
each while mitigating their respective limitations. This
approach allows manufacturers to specify a process that
balances sometimes conflicting needs. Considerations
such as flexibility, efficiency, and quality make this
approach particularly well-suited to the demands of
modern, rapidly changing markets.
A key feature of hybrid production is its ability to
combine the customisation and adaptability of craft
production with the speed, precision and consistency offered by mechanised and automated systems. This enables manufacturers to produce both standardised and bespoke products efficiently, catering to a broader range of consumer needs without sacrificing quality or responsiveness.
Human-machine collaboration is another feature of hybrid systems. Here, workers are typically assigned tasks that
require creativity, fine motor skills, or complex decision-making, while machines handle repetitive, high-volume, or precision-driven dirty or dangerous operations. This division of labour enhances productivity, preserves the value of human craftsmanship and assures the appropriate health and safety in areas where OH&S' issues may arise.
Hybrid systems are also inherently scalable and adaptable.
They can be configured to support large-scale mass production or adjusted for small-batch and custom manufacturing, responding to market demand.
Hybrid production systems often incorporate advanced technologies and robotics. Enabling real-time data collection, predictive analytics and intelligent automation, creating a data-driven production environment. Hybrid systems, therefore, may improve efficiency and enhance strategic planning across the production lifecycle.
Hybrid manufacturing systems offer a blend of benefits, including the ability to balance cost and quality. The strategic application of automation, where it is most efficient while retaining human input where precision or creativity is required, can deliver high-quality products without incurring the full costs associated with either extreme.
A key benefit is the system’s ability to respond to market
changes. Hybrid systems are more easily reconfigured
than fully automated lines, allowing manufacturers
to respond to shifts in consumer demand or product
specifications. The system’s capacity to support a wide
variety of products makes it ideal for industries requiring
frequent customisation or product variation.
Resource efficiency is also another feature of hybrid
manufacturing. Through the considered allocation of tasks
between humans and machines, reduced waste, improved
energy use, and streamlined production processes are
achieved. These benefits support sustainability goals and
contribute to long-term cost savings.
Hybrid systems are not without their challenges.
Designing and managing such systems is inherently
complex, requiring careful coordination between diverse
technologies and workflows. The initial setup costs can
be substantial, particularly when integrating advanced
technologies like robotics, AI*, or IoT” infrastructure.
Hybrid systems also demand a highly skilled workforce
capable of operating both manual and automated
components. This requires a technically proficient
workforce with the ability to maintain and adapt to
evolving technologies. Integration of multiple manual
and automated processes can generate issues of efficiency
and compatibility if not carefully selected and tuned.
The benefits attributed to this approach can enly be
achieved through seamless communication and workflow
continuity across the range of system components
employed.
COMPUTER-INTEGRATED MANUFACTURING
With the advent of the technological age, computers and
numerical devices heralded a new era of more sophisticated
control of machinery and processes. Robotic arms replaced
human operators for the movement of parts. Using this
approach, computer-integrated manufacturing (CIM)
systems incorporate technologies or methodologies such
as computers, robots, and Al to increase productivity.
CIM is a programmable computer-based manufacturing
system linking design, engineering, and manufacturing
processes and industrial robots, using automated
workstations. CIM systems can monitor operations from
raw materials intake to final product marketing. CIM
allows for the storage, retrieval and analysis of complex
data, providing manufacturers with valuable information
relating to equipment maintenance scheduling, process
optimisation, time and resource management. Major
motivations for the implementation of a CIM systems
approach are the need for manufacturing industries to
quickly and efficiently respond to changes. CIM systems
control and link the following components:
« personnel
« marketing
« materials handling
« product design (CAD)
« computer management
« machine tools and equipment (CAM).
A key consideration when implementing CIM is its
compatibility with existing systems and infrastructure.
Implementation of CIM may be best handled through
a gradual or staged process instead of wholesale
restructuring. Components of CIM may be added as
deemed necessary as part of a plant overhaul.
Many automobile manufacturers have chosen CIM
systems to manage and coordinate their already high level
of automation. Integrating the ordering stage into the
process involves considering customers with very specific
requirements. Incorporating incoming orders with
materials and third-party component inventories improves
and flexibility and reduces warehousing, allowing for JIT
(just-in-time) manufacture. On the production & floor, a
variety of computer-controlled machines may be arranged
to sequentially perform arange of operations. Components
are often transferred automatically from station to station,
Production Systems
where machines, in teams, perform their programmed
tasks, e.g., holding, rotating, welding, painting, etc. These
processes are performed along what are known as ‘flow’ or
‘transfer’ lines, the control of which is flexibly managed by a
computer. Employing this networked system, components
are transferred between stations using robot assistance. 
Movement of components through their various stages of assembly is managed by a computer ‘hands off” system, and assemblies are monitored for quality control at every stage, reducing wastage and improving consistency. Data from process control points is also logged and stored for reporting and review purposes.
IM systems provide many advantages and benefits to manufacturers, including lower inventories and reduced warehousing requirements, Computer-managed machinery improves machine utilisation, offers greater scheduling & flexibility and reduces manufacturing and lead times. Labour and its associated on-costs should be reduced and have a flow-on effect to overall cost efficiencies. These systems also allow greater control of complex and sometimes dangerous tasks using their ability to act in environments potentially hazardous to human health. Improved access to production data also allows managerial reviews for historical comparison and analysis.
As early as 2001, Gunasekaran et al. recognised that “companies undertake projects in automation and CIM for a variety of reasons, not the least of which is to achieve a competitive advantage” In a connected world, cooperation and collaboration twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week, are an imperative. The use of CIM systems enables real-time data exchange between design and manufacturing teams at both planning and execution stages. CIM systems also monitor production quotas and quality control, thus ensuring the meeting of deadlines while minimising waste. The interconnected nature of these systems means that this data is not only available to manufacturers but distributors and clients around the world.
Manufacturers, however, may find some disadvantages associated with CIM systems, including high initial setup costs. The challenges of computerisation and staff restructuring, however, may have negative effects on staff and morale. Customers should benefit from price reductions and improved quality. Mass customisation is easier within CIM production plants. Consumers may even be able to follow the production of their order through online interfaces with the plant.
Production system selection criteria
The selection of a production system involves a wide number of interconnected considerations such as: materials, process flow, employee training, manufacturing efficiency, health and safety, process control and flexibility, waste and emissions controls, etc., to create an effective, efficient and robust production system.

B4.1.3 and B4.1.4
Handmade furniture introduces variations in skill
levels of craftsmen, quality of design, selection of and understanding of materials, etc. Traditional furniture- making practices involve handcrafting of individual pieces by skilled artisans. Handcrafted items are unique.
No two pieces of wood are the same, and craftsmen take
great care in selecting exactly the right piece of wood for
a particular use, taking into consideration colour, grain
density. Even pieces created as parts of matched sets will
have minor variations. While they may look similar, on
closer inspection, minor variations in grain, colour and ornamental detail will be evident. Figure B.4.1.4 shows an antique, highly ornate, handmade chestnut ceremonial chair.
Consumers may purchase expensive one-of-a-kind,
handcrafted pieces of furniture as an investment, with
plans to keep the furniture for an extended period of time,
or hand it down through generations, as is the case with
antique pieces. Consumers often have a closer relationship
with craftsmen manufacturers than their mass production
counterparts. As such, consumers can have more say in
the specification of their work and may even have input
into the work at various stages of construction.
In terms of life cycle analysis, local artisans utilising native
timbers may have less impact on the environment than an
overseas-sourced mass-produced item.
Fixed costs for manual or craft production are generally
low but considerably higher for mechanisation and
automation due to the purchase of capital equipment.
Variable costs for automation are lower than craft
production due to the cost of wages.
Key influencing factors for mass-
produced items
The type of product must contain standardised parts and
be of high demand, reinforcing the need for high volumes
of output produced quickly. Quality control must be
consistently effective due to the thousands of units being
produced.
Materials selected must be compatible with automated
systems, that is, they must be easy to handle, shape and assemble. Raw materials must be readily available through
reliable supply chains.
Advanced technologies integrating robots, conveyor
transport systems, and CNC machines maximise speed
and reduce labour hire and costs. Lean manufacturing and
just-in-time practices reduce waste and parts inventories.
At the same time, an emphasis is placed on efficiency and
constant improvement.
High capital expenditure in the initial setup is covered by
thelarge volumes produced. Economies of scale reduce the
overall product unit cost. Machines must be maintained
to reduce downtime, keep production running and reduce
any costly interruptions.
Energy efficiency is critical to success due to the high
production volumes. This also assists in lowering the
carbon footprint. Manufacturing processes such as
injection moulding are often chosen not just for their
automation but also for their their low generation of
waste. Sustainable materials also reduce environmental
impacts.
Clearly, from the relationships outlined, the scale of
production would most likely determine the method of
production chosen. Low-volume work would most likely
be produced by craftsmen, whereas high-volume work
would be more suited to mechanised and/or automated
processes. Materials selection also has a part to play
in production method choices. Some materials and
production methods are only suited to mechanisation/
automation.

B4.1.5
Deconstructing and analysing multi-component products
is an important skill in engineering, product design, and
reverse engineering. Reverse engineering has always
been associated with competitors attempting to discover
valuable insights into another company’s intellectual
property in terms of design, materials, functionality and
manufacturing processes. It has, however, developed into
a corporate strategy to assist with market competitiveness
and innovation.
The methodology involves disassembling a product to
understand its design, manufacturing methods, assembly
and functionality. This method is commeonly used to help
PropucTioN SYSTEMS
appreciate product complexity and design decisions. The
meticulous deconstruction and examination of an existing
product to understand functionality, materials and
manufacturing processes can significantly reduce the time
required to complete product development and, at the
same time, reduce costs and improve time-to-market.
'The following process is one approach that maybe adopted
to reverse El'lgil'lEEl' a product.
External visual examination: This process allows for
identification of materials, joining techniques, use of
fasteners and potential ways to disassemble the device.
Check for seams that may open, connecting fasteners and
hardware that may be concealed in recesses, spot facings
or structures under removable caps. Use this time to
develop a plan to approach disassembly.
Disassembly: Care must be taken not to damage
connections through the use of inappropriate tools. The
whole disassembly process should be recorded by video
or a series of snapshots. All parts should be labelled and
numbered to assist with reassembly.
Component analysis: What does each component do, i.e.
is it functional, included for aesthetic value, have safety
ramifications, etc.? How does it aid in the functionality
of the entire device? How does this component interact
with others, and are there considerations for wear in the
materials selection or component design? Lastly, could
this component be made redundant?
Manufacturing Analysis: Analyse the manufacturing process for each component thoroughly and identify alternative methods where applicable, or recognise how the current process enhances the material properties or functionality, such as through work hardening. Be aware of the cost implications associated with these decisions.
Clearly define the chosen materials and justify your selections; materials should be categorised by their mass,
colour and magnetism. Additionally, confirm whether any surface finishes, e.g. paint or anodising, have been applied.
Assembly analysis: record the assembly sequence. How was the product assembled, by hand or machine? Is the product even designed to be disassembled or is it single-use only?
Redundancy: Are there unnecessary parts? Are some parts overdesigned?
Tools that may help in the process include tweezers, a magnifying glass, a magnet, screwdrivers and a voltmeter.
Patent mining
Another technique commonly used forreverse engineering is patent mining.
Patent mining involves: searching patent databases (like
Google Patents, WIPO, USPTO) to analyse potential innovations and product trends to extract insights into the technical knowledge behind new developments. Through the examination of technical illustrations, a product’s inner workings may be revealed or, at least, give some guide to future product trends. Through the study of these documents, a company may plan a development strategy that avoids legal issues.
Businesses employ this strategy to collect competitive
intelligence and identify trends in innovation.
Examples of this approach include pharmaceutical firms reviewing pending expiring drug patents to allow time to develop a generic alternative, thus avoiding costly research and development. Automotive companies exploring battery patents to examine new formulations and manufacturing methodologies that extend battery life and reduce costs. New applications may also reveal
approaches that allow for diversification.

Production Systems
Car Body Panels
Stamping of sheet metal provides strong, work-
hardened, impact-resistant panels with a smooth
surface finish, Stamping allows the production of
aerodynamic shapes with consistent curves that may
lower a car’s coefficient of drag and improve fuel
Ecnnomy.
Carbon fibre layup panels are extremely lightweight,
strong and have extreme levels of dimensional
stiffness. They may be moulded into complex shapes
with smooth and colourful surface finishes or have an
exposed woven texture visible to generate a high-tech
aesthetic.
Cooking Pan
Die-cast aluminium provides excellent heat distribution and a lightweight product. The material provides a sleek and modern finish.
Hand-hammered copper produces superior heat conductivity, but requires constant maintenance to maintain its appearance. Beaten copper lends itself to a rustic, artisanal appearance with visible hammer marks.
Cast iron provides excellent and evenly distributed heat retention. Cast iron is extremely robust and has excellent durability. A well-seasoned cast-iron pan develops a natural non-stick surface over time, eliminating the need for additional surface treatments. The material itself embodies a traditional rustic appearance with a textured surface whose patina ages over time.

C4.1.1
The design for manufacturing process canbe deconstructed
into three complementary components: design for process
(materials and methods), design for assembly, and design
for disassembly.
Design for process involves reductions in: the amount
of energy consumed, the number of production
processes employed, waste generated and emissions
produced. Designers may simplify the design to make the
manufacturing process easier and more efficient, It may
also involve reducing the number of parts that require
additional operations, including: plating, painting,
printing, labelling, insertion of screws, caulking, riveting
and welding.
Specifying standardised components reduces costs,
improves availability and makes repairs and maintenance
easier, Adopting the principles of design for process at the
design stage provides a framework for selecting materials
and fastening techniques, as well as designing modular
assemblies, The selection of appropriate materials reduces
toxic substances, hazardous waste and polluting emissions,
as well as the quantity of materials required. Wherever
possible, single-component materials may be specified
for moulding and recyclable materials identified and
marked for sorting and recycling. These measures assist
in the later disassembly and recycling processes. Similar
specifications are also applied to packaging.
An example of a product designed under the principles
of design for process is the Apple Unibody MacBook.
The MacBook body was carefully designed to allow a
CNC machine to carve the body from a single block of
aluminium. This process was conducted in one action and
did not require the body to join or weld parts together.
This single structure is stronger and more rigid than
multi-part assemblies.
Precision machining generates finer tolerances and a
smooth, seamless finish. The reduction in assembly
complexity saved time and simplified the internal layout
of the case. The key differences between the traditional
manufacturing methods and a unibody construction are
shown in the table (Figure C 4.1.1b) below.
Figure C 4.1.1b Table comparing the differences between
the traditional manufacturing methods and a unibody
construction.
Design for assembly is an approach used by designers to
analyse components and sub-assemblies, reducing costs
by minimising the number of parts and maximising the
efficiency of assembly processes. Fewer parts most often
translates into faster assembly times. A by-product of part
reduction is also smaller inventories and lower storage
costs for manufacturers.
Consideration of tools to be used during assembly
may also be important. A reduction in the number and
variety of fasteners and tool types used during assembly
is preferable. Better product value and longevity can be
embedded at the design stage using modular design
principles. Modular design involves breaking down
complex systems into sets of distinct, independent
components or sub-assemblies that can then be assembled
in various ways. Replaceable modules enable assemblies to
be removed for part replacement, due to failure, or when
modification by updating is required. Waste can also be
reduced if a large product does not have to be scrapped
because a small part fails or becomes obsolete.
An example of a product redesigned using design for
assembly principles is the Bosch circular saw. The original
Bosch circular consisted of more than 100 parts. Assembly
was time-consuming and often error-prone. The assembly
was complicated by the fact that many parts required
manual alignment and fastening.
Using design for assembly principles, several key changes
were made. The part count was reduced by combining
multiple components into a single moulded part. This
also reduced the number of fasteners required, and snap-
fit connections were integrated. The assembly process
was further simplified by the inclusion of self-locating,
symmetrical parts to prevent incorrect assembly. A
vertical axis of assembly was employed, enabling parts
to be dropped into place. Custom fasteners were also
replaced with standard screws and connectors. 
Design for disassembly is a process that facilitates ease of
repair, reuse, remanufacturing or recycling of a product.
It has become increasingly popular with manufacturers as
they strive to meet Waste from Electrical and Electronic
Equipment (WEEE) and Restriction of Hazardous
Substances in Electrical and Electronic Equipment (RoHS)
legislative requirements while maintaining profitability.
Manufacturers also see the benefits associated with
design for disassembly through the potential to reduce
production costs.
To be effective, the disassembly of a product into its
parts, in preparation for recycling, can only be achieved
if it is considered early in the design process through the
application of appropriate guidelines. broken down into
three categories:
« materials selection
« fastening techniques
« design and specification of components, assemblies
and sub-assemblies.
In their 2023 study, Giovanni Formentini and Devarajan
Ramanujan critically analysed the role of design for
disassembly methodologies in advancing sustainability
principles, particularly within the framework of the
circular economy. Their findings indicate that design
for disassembly is predominantly employed to optimise
sustainability outcomes. The concept of sustainability
optimisation encompasses strategies aimed at enhancing
product remanufacturing, facilitating efficient recycling
processes, improving reparability and bolstering overall
circularity performance.
An example of design for disassembly in practice is the
Yurt, a portable dwelling used by the nomadic people
of Mongolia. Constructed using readily available local
materials, it uses an expanding wooden circular frame
covered with felt made from sheep wool, providing
insulation and durability. It is well known as a structure
designed for disassembly and reassembly. 
Careful choice of materials may also negate the need
for coatings or inform the choice of coating, with ease
of removal as a prerequisite. Coatings can contaminate
a recycled resource and may require costly additional
removal processing. Use of glues and adhesives needs
to be eliminated or minimised. If necessary, the
use of thermoplastic adhesives may be appropriate.
Thermoplastic adhesives enable components or assemblies
to be easily separated by applying heat. Incorporation of
semi-permanent snap fittings, connectors, screws, bolts
and clips instead of more permanent methods of securing,
such as welding, brazing and soldering, also facilitates the
disassembly process.
The strategic selection of readily recyclable materials
significantly enhances the efficiency of future reprocessing
operations once a product reaches the end of its functional
lifespan. Minimising material diversity simplifies the
sorting process for recovery, while the incorporation
of polymer identification codes facilitates accurate
classification. Additionally, designers can optimise
material selection based on the recycling technologies
available, thereby ensuring alignment with sustainable
processing methods. This approach not only streamlines
product disassembly but also mitigates the reliance on
virgin resources necessary for manufacturing replacement
components. Moreover, products designed for ease of
disassembly inherently support improved reparability,
as well as facilitate reconditioning and technological
upgrades, further extending their lifecycle and improving
sustainability outcomes.
The design and specification of components, modules
and sub-assemblies should prioritise ease of access for
removal and replacement. This approach reduces the
need for advanced technical expertise at the regional
level. Furthermore, facilitating local servicing and
customisation becomes more feasible when modifications
are simplified. Ensuring the local sourcing of sensitive,
frequently replaced items that require routine maintenance
allows for synchronised servicing schedules and prevents
disruptions in the supply of parts.
Standardisation of fasteners throughout the product
means similar tools and skill sets are required for
DESIGN FOR MAUFACTURE STRATEGIES
disassembly, e.g. standard circlips of the same size used
throughout an assembly would require only one tool for
maintenance. Part standardisation also allows spare parts
to be easily sourced.
Adaptation of designs
Design for manufacturing
The design refinement process entails a comprehensive
review aimed at standardising components wherever
feasible, thereby replacing custom-manufactured elements
with commercially available, cost-effective alternatives.
'This strategic substitution minimises the need to manage a
diverse inventory of specialised parts and tools, enhancing
overall manufacturing efficiency.
Subsequent design adaptation involves a meticulous
analysis to identify avenues for simplification and
optimisation. This process reduces the total number of
components incorporated into the product while adopting
amodular construction framework to improve adaptability
and maintainability. Additionally, design configurations
must ensure that all connections inherently prevent
incorrect assembly orientations, thereby mitigating
potential errors in product construction.
Poka-Yoke is a Japanese concept that means ‘mistake-
proofing’ or ‘error-proofing’ is also applicable in this
context. An example of this approach is the design of
Type C USB connections introduced in 2014, which are
symmetrical, meaning the connection does not rely on
orientation.
Consideration of design for assembly must be intrinsically
linked to the final disassembly phase, facilitating
streamlined end-of-life processing. This includes
systematically marking components to enable efficient
identification and sorting during recycling or waste
disposal operations, thereby reinforcing sustainable
material recovery practices.
Design for manufacturing conflicts
On occasions, design for manufacturing approaches may
be in conflict. In every case, a holistic assessment may be
required to determine the most advantageous result.
Materials selection: The materials chosen for
manufacturing operations can present challenges for
disassembly and recycling. In the automotive industry,
aluminium is often specified for body panels due to
its lightweight nature, corrosion resistance and ease of
formability. During the product use phase, lighter vehicles
also benefit from improved fuel economy, reduced
emissions and decreased brake wear. However, despite
aluminium’s 100% recyclability, its disassembly can be
complex, and the environmental issues associated with the
aluminium smelting process pose a significant conflict.
Reducing complexity: In pursuit of design simplification,
manufacturers frequently consolidate components into
integrated assemblies. While this aggregation streamlines
production, it often introduces greater complexity and
reduces accessibility when attempting to disassemble the
component into its constituent parts.
Joining methods: Permanent joining techniques are widely
recognised for their speed, effectiveness and efficiency in
manufacturing processes. However, these methods often
pose significant challenges to disassembly, sometimes
rendering the separation of components impractical
or unfeasible. This issue is particularly prevalent in the
furniture industry, where the extensive use of adhesives
and glues impedes efforts to disassemble products for
recycling, maintenance or repair.
Cost: Designing cost-effective solutions that align with
design for manufacturing principles presents a complex
challenge. In the construction sector, modular building
systems offer notable advantages, including streamlined
assembly processes, mitigation of weather-related delays
and reduced overall construction timelines. However,
integration of features that enable disassembly—essential
for adaptability, maintenance or end-of-life recycling—
can introduce additional design complexity and elevate
production costs, potentially offsetting the economic
benefits of modularity.
Design for manufacture example: Michael Thonet’s 19th-
century No. 14 or bistro chair (shown in Figure C 4.1 Af)is
one of the most recognisable pieces of furniture ever made.
It was originally designed as a durable cafe chair. The seat,
in particular, was purposefully made from woven cane to
allow the draining of spilled liquids. Earning a gold medal
at the 1867 Paris World Exposition, the chair has been
reproduced millions of times, with over 50 million sold
between 1859 and 1930. Over the years, the original No.14
chair has inspired a range of variations and improvements.
Noted Swiss-French architect and designer, Le Corbusier,
is quoted as saying, “Never was a better and more elegant
design, and a more precisely crafted and practical item
created”.
Instantly identifiable by its simple design and bentwood
construction, this chair is one of the best-selling pieces of
furniture ever produced. What is not readily appreciated is
that this may be the first example of flat-pack furniture.
Eight screws and a pair of nuts and bolts allowed the chair
to be easily assembled and disassembled—a precursor
to today’s flat-pack designs
Also known as the bistro chair, the Thonet No.14 chair
is a classic example of industrial design that excels in
design for process, design for assembly and design for
disassembly. Here’s how it meets the criteria for each:
Originally produced using only six pieces of steam-
bent beechwood, formed in cast iron moulds, the mass
production process reduced the requirement for skilled
labour and minimised waste. The number of parts
was reduced to six wooden pieces, while the hardware
consisted of ten screws and two nuts.
‘The chair is quick and easy to assemble due to the minimal
number of parts. Screws and nuts replace the traditional
use of glue, thus allowing for ease of assembly and
disassembly. All components were symmetrical, allowing
for interchangeability and reduced assembly errors.
The design supports easy disassembly for repair, recycling
or end-of-life processing. It uses mechanical fasteners such as screws and nuts (instead of adhesives), allowing the chair to be taken apart easily. Modular components are easily replaced, extending
the product’s life, and the wood and metal parts can be
simply separated for recycling or reuse.

C4.1.2
Design for process strategies focuses on optimising
production processes to improve efficiency, quality and
sustainability. Some key advantages are:
« Careful materials selection reduces polluting emissions
and poisonous waste by-products. Eliminating toxic
materials as part of the selection process, coupled with
a design that reduces waste and environmental gains
are achieved before starting manufacturing.
« Designs are developed with a specific manufacturing
process in mind to minimise errors and enhance
product reliability.
« Efficient designs reduce material waste and material
usage, lowering overall production costs.
DEesIGN FOR MAUFACTURE STRATEGIES
« Environmental impacts are reduced through lower
energy consumption, materials use and material
waste.
« Well-designed, flexible manufacturing processes allow
for scalability, i.e. manufacturing may be ramped up or
scaled down based on need.
Design for process example:
Simple, inexpensive smartphone stand
Define requirements: A device to hold a smartphone
(and potentially accessories) securely to allow hands-free
viewing, portrait or landscape, see Figure C4.1.2a.
Simplify design: Use a single-piece design requiring no
assembly.
Material selection: Choice of durable aluminium sheet for
formability, stability, strength, recyclability and corrosion
resistance.
Optimise processes: One
manufacturing.
operation only for
Aluminium sheet: cold folded, no waste, form increases
in strength due to work hardening, surface finish straight
from folding. Less energy is used than in hot forming
processes, and no surface treatment is required after
manufacturing. Reduces time and handling, thus speeding

C4.1.3
Designers may employ various strategies when using a
design for assembly approach.
Modular sub-assemblies reduce the number of parts by
combining the function of multiple pieces into a single
standardised component or module. This may result in
assembly efficiencies and lower inventories. Minimising
part numbers frequently reduces overall product weight.
Modern construction methods producing modular
designs include everything from ships (utilising block
construction and prefabrication to divide ships into
functional interchangeable modules that are assembled
on land and then integrated for construction and future
upgrades) to computer modules and home construction.
Effective and efficient product assembly minimises the
number of axes of assembly. Wherever possible, assembly
operations should be in one direction, reducing handling,
time-consuming movement and re-orientation of the
product. The item should be easy to assemble manually,
and where the scale of production warrants it, with
automation. Automated assembly processes are more
consistent, produce a more reliable product and generate
a consistently higher build quality. To assist with assembly,
locating pins should be chamfered or tapered.
Standardising parts means readily available off-the-shelf
components are specified, which makes procurement
easier and may reduce the need for large inventories.
Building bricks, nuts and bolts, electronic parts such as
resistors, etc, are all examples of considerations in this
context.
Design for accessibility ensures that all parts are easily
accessible during assembly, reducing the need for complex
tools or maneuvers.
Minimising and simplifying fastening methods through
the use of snap-fittings, clips, etc, enables assembly and
disassembly without the need for special tools. A reduction
in fasteners not only reduces costs but also assembly and
repair times. If fasteners are specified, standard types and
sizes should be used.
Design for assembly example:
IKEA furniture is a good example of the design for
assembly principles. It achieves this by applying multiple
key principles of design for assembly.
Simplified components: the number of parts is reduced
to the bare minimum, and joining hardware consists of
standardised components such as dowels, screws, hinges
and fasteners, etc. that are consistent throughout their
catalogue. Panels are often symmetrical and reversible,
reducing the complexity of manufacturing and the
potential for customer assembly errors.
Flat pack design: reduces volume, packaging and shipping
costs as well as making transport easier for the domestic
consumer.
Simplicity of Assembly: Ikea products are assembled
using a comprehensive, universally understandable, step-
by-step pictorial guide, eliminating language barriers and
reducing assembly errors. Where tools are required, they
are often provided, eg Allen keys or screwdrivers. Knock-
down fittings with precise snap-fit specifications or cam-
lock devices reduce assembly times. Incorporation of this
hardware facilitates rapid, non-specialist assembly with
minimal technical skill required.
Modular designs: IKEA often uses standardised shared
components and medular units across various product
lines. Shared modules reduce manufacturing costs, and the
modular units allow for ease of customer customisation to
expand the product or reconfigure module placements,
see Figure C 4.1.3a.
It should be noted that while flat pack furniture became
widely popular in the mid-20th century thanks to
companies like IKEA, the application of pre-cut, factory-
manufactured components for houses has roots much
earlier, using the same principles of efficient shipping and
on-site assembly.
Flat pack homes using corrugated iron were first shipped
from England to the Californian goldfields in 1849 and in
the 1850s to the Victorian goldfields in Australia, Each flat
pack contained a building plan along with the necessary
frame material (wrought iron or wood, depending on the
manufacturer) and corrugated iron sheeting.
These prefabricated, portable buildings were produced and
shipped by a number of English firms and reassembled to
quickly address the severe housing shortage caused by the
influx of gold prospectors. The flat pack concept was also
applied to houses in the USA starting in the early 20th
century, with Sears Roebuck selling mail-order house kits
in 1908.

C4.1.4
Through careful planning and design, design for
disassembly is a practice that facilitates the deconstruction
processes and procedures. Design for disassembly offers
several significant advantages:
« Time efficiency: minimises the time required for
disassembly.
« Resource recovery: enables the recovery of valuable
resources.
« Cost Reduction: contributes to a reduction in overall
product costs.
« Waste: reduces waste through reuse, repair and
recycling strategies.
» Material identification: ease of identifying materials
for sorting and recycling.
« Environmental impact: lowers disposal costs and
mitigates environmental impacts.
« Resource efficiency: reduces the demand for virgin
materials to manufacture replacement products or
components.
DEesiGN FOR MAUFACTURE STRATEGIES
« Maintenance and upgrades: ensures products can be
easily disassembled, simplifying maintenance, repair,
reconditioning, updating and recycling processes.
« Reduced embodied energy: The energy required to
extract, process, transport and assemble a product
is reduced as materials are reused in new products,
preventing the need to source and process virgin
resources
Design for disassembly example:
Flashlight
« Single axial line of disassembly.
« Minimising part count through the use of modular
sub-assemblies speeds up the disassembly process.
« Casing: Blow moulded casing from a single, recyclable
polymer with snap-fit mechanisms and threaded
ends
« Switch mechanism: module snaps into place on the
body of the torch
« Modular lens, cap and reflector: threads into place
over the end of the torch body. Wiring connects to
the battery and switch modules with standardised
connectors instead of soldering.
« Modular globe assembly: easily accessible for
replacement as necessary
« Standard-sized O-rings seal the unit and provide ‘soft
seating’ of mating parts, making disassembly easier
« End cap screws are in place to hold all parts together
and complete the circuit.
« No special or custom tools required for disassembly
« Marking polymer components with recycling symbols
for ease of identification and sorting
« Reduce/avoid hazardous materials: this technique
reduces contamination of the recycling stream on
disassembly.
esign for disassembly issues
Implementing design for disassembly, however, can
be fraught with challenges. The intricacies of design,
compatibility of materials—especially concerning jeining
techniques—and adherence to regulatory guidelines and
legislation when utilising standardised components pose
distinct issues. Additionally, design for disassembly may
incur supplementary costs, which are often passed on to
consumers.
‘The smartphone is a marvel of miniaturisation and material
diversity, but it’s also notoriously difficult to disassemble
for recycling or repair due to a variety of reasons.
Material complexity
« Plastics: Used for casings and insulation, often mixed
with flame retardants or pigments that complicate
recycling.
« Metals: Includes aluminium (frames), copper (wiring),
tin (solder), and precious metals like gold and
silver (connectors). These all require identification,
separation and extraction.
« Rare Earths: Neodymium (speakers), dysprosium and
terbium (vibration motors), and lanthanum (camera
lenses) are embedded in tiny quantities, making
recovery extremely difficult.
Joining Techniques
« Adhesives and soldering: Components are often glued
or soldered in place, which impedes mechanical
separation and risks damaging parts during
disassembly.
« Micro fasteners and proprietary screws: These require
specialised tools and knowledge, limiting accessibility
for third-party recyclers or repairers.
412
« Laminated layers: Touchscreens and batteries are often
laminated or fused, making separation hazardous or
impossible without damaging the materials.
Regulatory and standardisation challenges
« Lack of universal standards: Manufacturers use
proprietary designs and materials, making it difficult
to establish consistent disassembly protocols.
« Hazardous materials: Devices may contain lead,
mercury or halogenated compounds that require
careful handling under environmental regulations.
« Legislation gaps: While some regions enforce
Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR), others lack
enforceable mandates for DfD or material recovery.
Circular design vs. reality
Despite the push for circularity, many devices are still
shredded en masse, recovering only high-value metals
while plastics and rare earths are incinerated or landfilled.
This not only wastes resources but also undermines the
environmental and ethical goals of sustainable design.
Emerging Solutions
Al-powered robotics: Systems like Fraunhofer IFF’s
iDEAR project use machine learning and computer vision
to automate disassembly, improving recovery rates.
Smart disassembly: Techniques involving 3D imaging
and selective chemical delamination are being explored
for printed circuit boards (PCBs), which contain up to
50% metals by weight. QR code to the Fraunhofer iDEAR
site.
The Fraunhofer iDEAR project (Intelligent Disassembly of
Electronics for Remanufacturing and Recycling) aims to
develop automated, non-destructive disassembly
technologies for electronic waste to efficiently recover
valuable materials rare such as earths and precious metals
while at the same time supporting a circular economy by
keeping raw materials in use longer and reducing landfill
waste.

C4.1.5
Design for manufacturing strategies that benefit the
environment should be incorporated into every stage of
a products life cycle, i.e. preproduction, manufacturing,
packaging, transportation, in-service use and disposal.
Typically, this approach reduces energy and materials
requirements, minimising waste, improving durability
and product life opportunities, allowing for repair and
upgrading and greater use of recycling and recycled
materials in both the packaging and the product.
To maintain consistency of application, continuous
improvement and measurable outcomes, companies
may also integrate standardised quality management
systems such as ISO 9001, using certified environmental
management systems, e.g. ISO 14001.
Consideration of environmental impacts in the earliest
stages of product design and development
Manufacturers employ these principles to be
environmentally responsible, and when applied
appropriately, they provide cost reductions, innovative
products and processes and a competitive edge.
Greater Energy Efficiency: This may be achieved by
sourcing renewable energy suppliers and reviewing
production processes to reduce energy consumption.
DEesiGN FOR MAUFACTURE STRATEGIES
Material Selection: Choosing local, recycled,
biodegradable or sustainably sourced materials to lower carbon footprints.
Durability and longevity: Designing products to last
longer, be easily repaired and or upgraded reduces the
need for frequent replacements.
Maximising energy efficiency: Products that consume
less energy in use or are powered by alternative energy
sources reduce environmental impacts.
Repairable/upgradable products: Modular designs
allow for ease of repair or upgrade, extending product
life, reducing waste and the need for replacement products.
Waste minimisation: Guidelines include provisions
for adopting designs that favour the efficient selection
of materials, ease of assembly/disassembly, and repair, recovery and recycling.
Recyclability: Single-material components are easy to
disassemble and facilitate recycling. However, using
recycling codes for multi-component systems aids the identification process and reduces contamination of the recycling/waste stream.
Biodegradable Materials: The selection and use of compostable materials prevent landfill accumulation and returns valuable nutrients to the soil.
Circular Economy Integration: Parts designed for reuse, recycling or repair extend product life and reduce waste.
Waste minimisation: Strategies can be as simple
as replacing one process with another. Switching
welding operations for mechanical folding provides
for reductions in energy and multi-part assemblies.
Replacing drilling with punching allows for the
recovery of waste punch blanks to be recycled more
easily than the shavings produced by machining.
Joining electronic components with standardised snap connectors rather than soldering reduces assembly and disassembly times, energy efficiency and manufacturer skill levels.
Elimination of toxic materials: This may be best achieved by substituting toxic materials with alternatives that do not compromise product functionality. If removal of hazardous materials is not possible, then measures must be put in place for end-of-life recovery and/or recycling.
